Chapter 1 COURTS, COUNTY 1. OYER AND TERMINER 2. QUARTER SESSIONS 3. POWER OF JUDGES TO ISSUE WAR- RANTS AND SUBPOENAS 4. TERMS OF COURT 5. QUARTER SESSIONS 6. OYER AND TERMINER 

7.

MUNICIPAL COURT OF PHILADELPHIA 8. COUNTY COURT, ALLEGHENY CO. 

1. Oyer and Terminer

The courts of oyer and terminer and general jail delivery have power:

First, To inquire by the oaths and affirmations of good and lawful men of the county, of all crimes committed, or triable in such county. 

Second, To hear, determine and punish the same, and to deliver the jails of such county of all prisoners therein, according to law.

Third, To try indictments found in the quarter sessions and certified by the said court according to law; and the said courts shall have exclusive jurisdiction and power to try and punish all persons charged with any of the crimes herein enumerated which shall be committed within the respective county, to wit: 

1. All persons charged with any murder or manslaughter, or other homicide, and all persons charged with being accessory to any such crime. 

2. All persons charged with treason against the commonwealth. 

3. All persons charged with sodomy, buggery, rape or robbery, their counsellors, aiders and abettors. 

4. All persons charged with the crime of voluntarily and maliciously burning any building, or other thing, made punishable in the same manner as arson. 15.

2 

PENNSYLVANIA CRIMINAL PROCEDURE 

5. All persons charged with mayhem, or with the crime of cutting off the tongue, putting out the.eye, slitting the nose, cutting off a lip, cutting off or disabling, any limb or member of a person, by lying in wait, or with malice aforethought, and with intent in so doing to maim or disfigure such person, and their aiders and abettors and counsellors. 

6. All persons charged with burglary. 

7. Every woman who shall be charged with having endeavored privately, either by herself or the procurement of others, to conceal the death of any issue of her body, male or female, which if it were born alive, would be by law a bastard, so that it may not be known whether such issue was born dead or alive, or whether it was murdered or not. 

8. All persons charged with the second or any subsequent offense of receiving, harboring or concealing any robber, burglar, felon, or thief, or with the crime of receiving or buying any goods or chattels, which have been feloniously taken or stolen, knowing the same to be so taken or stolen. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 31; 17 P. S. 391; Com. v. Torr, 111 Pa. Super. 179, indictment in wrong court, see 

Trials. The judges of the courts of common pleas hold the courts of Oyer and Terminer, Act of April 14, 1834, P. L. 333, Secs, 58, 59, 17 P. S. 372, 371; Phila., Act of March 13, 1867, P. L. 420, 17 P. S. 471. 

2. Quarter Sessions 

The courts of quarter session of the peace shall have jurisdiction and power within the respective counties: 

1. To inquire, by the oaths or affirmations of good and lawful men of the county, of all crimes, misdemeanors and offenses whatsoever against the laws of this commonwealth, which shall be triable in the respective county. 

2. To inquire of, hear, determine and punish in due form of law, all such crimes and misdemeanors and offenses, whereof exclusive jurisdiction is not given, as aforesaid, to the courts of oyer and terminer of such county. 

3. To take, in the name of the commonwealth, all manner of recognizance and obligation heretofore taken and allowed to be taken by any justice of the peace; and they

shall certify such as shall be taken, in relation to any crime not triable therein, to the next court of oyer and terminer having power to take cognizance thereof. 

4. To continue, or discharge, the recognizances and obligations of persons bound to keep the peace, or to be of good behavior, taken as aforesaid, or certified into such court by any justice of the peace of such county, and to inquire of, hear and determine, in the manner hitherto practiced and allowed, all complaints which shall be found thereon. 

5. The courts of quarter sessions shall also have jurisdiction in cases of fines, penalties or punishments, imposed by any act of assembly, for offenses, misdemeanors or delinquencies, except where it shall be otherwise expressly provided and enacted. 

6. The said courts shall also have and exercise such other jurisdiction and powers, not herein enumerated, as may have been heretofore given to them by law. 

Whenever any indictment shall be found in any court of quarter sessions, for any crime or offense not triable therein, it shall be the duty of said court to certify the same into the court of oyer and terminer next to be holden in such county, there to be heard and determined in due course of law. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 32, 17 P. S. 361. 

3. Power of Judges to Issue Warrants and Subpoenas 

The judges of the county courts of oyer and terminer and quarter sessions, and every one of them, shall have power to direct their writs or precepts to all or any of the sheriffs or other officers of any of the counties, cities, boroughs or towns corporate of this commonwealth, to arrest and bring before them persons indicted for felonies and other offenses, and amenable to the respective court; each of said courts shall have power to award process to levy and recover such fines, forfeitures and amercements, as shall be imposed, taxed or adjudged by them, respectively; each of said courts shall have full power and authority to establish such rules for regulating the practice thereof respectively, and for expediting the determination of suits, causes and proceedings therein, as in their discretion they shall

judge necessary or proper; Provided, that such rules shall not be inconsistent with the Constitution and laws of this Commonwealth; each of the said courts is empowered to issue writs of subpoena, under their official seal, into any county of this commonwealth to summon and bring before the respective court any person to give testimony in any cause or matter depending before them, under the penalties hitherto appointed and allowed, in any such case, by the laws of this commonwealth.¹

All the judges of courts of quarter sessions are ex officio committing magistrates, authorized to hold to bail or commit anyone charged with crime.2

4. Terms of Court

The judges of the several courts throughout the Commonwealth shall be authorized at any time hereafter to make an order fixing the number of the regular terms of the said several courts, establishing the time for holding the same, or fixing the times for the jury trials in said courts, as in the opinion of the said judges, the business may require. 

Act of April 27, 1927, P. L. 420, Sec. 1, 17 P. S. 881. 

5. Quarter Sessions 

Courts to be held four times a year.¹ Adjourned,2 Continued or Special courts may be held.

6. Oyer and Terminer

A court of oyer and terminer and general jail delivery shall be held four times annually, at the times appointed for holding the courts of quarter sessions of the peace. 

Act of April 14, 1834, P. L. 333, Sec. 58, 17 P. S. 371. 

7. Municipal Court of Philadelphia 

The municipal court has jurisdiction to try all indictments which may be brought therein for trial by the district attorney, except indictments for arson, burglary, murder, voluntary manslaughter, treason or misprision of treason, or for violation of or conspiracy to violate the election or registration law, or any offense involving breach of official duties by any public officer.

It has exclusive jurisdiction in desertion and nonsupport cases and exercises the jurisdiction of a Juvenile Court.¹ 

It shall be the duty of the district attorney to prosecute all cases in which prosecutions have been begun in the Municipal Court, or which have been returned to court by Magistrates for trial, and which are exclusively triable in the Municipal Court, or which the district attorney elects to try in said court. In desertion and nonsupport cases and in juvenile cases there shall be no preliminary hearings; but the person accused, or children involved, shall be immediately brought before the court for trial.2

Has exclusive jurisdiction and powers of juvenile court,¹ desertion and non-support proceedings; appeals from summary convictions, and from judgments in suits for a penalty before a magistrate or court not of record.2 Desertion and non-support cases are heard without a preliminary hearing

The judges are ex officio justice of the peace.*

1. Burgess, Powers 

The burgess shall have power; 

1. To administer oaths and affirmations in matters pertaining to borough affairs. 

2. To exercise jurisdiction in all disputes between the borough and individuals, under the ordinances, rules and regulations of the borough. 

3. To exercise the powers and jurisdiction of justices of the peace in the enforcement of all ordinances of the borough, and the collection of fines and penalties imposed thereunder, and to sentence any person violating any such ordinance to detention in the lockup, county jail or workhouse. 

4. To exercise the powers and jurisdiction of a Justice of the Peace within the borough for the suppression of riots, tumults, and disorderly meetings, and in all criminal cases for the punishment of vagrants and disorderly persons.¹

Whenever the burgess is absent or incapacitated the duties of his office shall be discharged by the president of council.2 

1 Act of May, 4, 1927, P. L. 519, Art. 10, Sec. 1027 (1028, to remove nuisances and preserve order), 53 P. S. 12939. Kittaning Boro. v. Burns, 47 Pa. Super. 540, jurisdiction of justice. 

2 Act of May 18, 1933, P. L. 818, 53 P. S. 12941. 

2. Record, Certiorari

An ordinance is not a public law, that all must take notice of, but is a thing that when relied on to support a judgment rendered by a tribunal of limited jurisdiction, should either be set out in extenso in the transcript of the record of the judgment, or the substance thereof should be therein given, so that it might therefore appear that any judgment based thereon stood on a legal basis. The legality of the

judgment is the thing being inquired about, in a proceeding of this kind (certiorari), and, in determining that question, the court, without sufficient specification of them in the record, can take no cognizance of the existence of the ordinance, as it might do with respect to the existence of public laws. 

Borough v. Nahos, 13 Mun. L. R. 98 (West), Reversing. 

3. Burgess to Collect Costs and Fees

In all cases where the burgess is given the jurisdiction of a justice of the peace, he shall collect the same costs and fees as are charged by justices of the peace for like services, and, in all other cases, he may collect such costs and fees as are allowed by law to justices of the peace for services of similar character; provided, however that he shall procure a docket or loose-leaf form, wherein he shall enter all cases and charges, which docket shall be open in public inspection at reasonable times. 

Act of June 9, 1931, P. L. 386, Sec. 1026; 53 P. S. 12937. 

4. Council, Modify, Remit Fines

Borough councils have the power to mitigate or remit fines and forfeitures in reasonable cases. 

Act of May 4, 1927, P. L. 519, Art. 10, Sec. 1006; 53 P. S. 12896. 

5. Arrests on View, Commitment, Bail

Policemen of the several boroughs may, without warrant and upon view, arrest and commit for hearing, any person guilty of a breach of the peace, vagrancy, riotous or disorderly conduct or drunkenness; or that may be engaged in the commission of any unlawful act tending to imperil the personal security or endanger the property of citizens, or violating any of the ordinances of such borough for the violation of which a penalty is imposed. Persons so arrested shall be entitled to give bail for their appearance, according to the practice in cases of summary convictions. 

Act of May 4, 1927, P. L. 519, Art. 31, Sec. 3102; 53 P. S. 15192.

6. Warrant or Summons in Proceedings for Violation of Borough Ordinances or for Collection of Fines and Penalties: Service 

All proceedings for the violation of borough ordinances and for the collection of fines and penalties imposed thereby, may be commenced by warrant or by summons, at the discretion of the burgess or justice of the peace before whom the proceedings are commenced, but no warrant shall be issued except on oath or affirmation specifying the ordinance for the violation of which the same is issued, and all process may be directed to and be served by any policeman of the borough, who may execute the same anywhere within the Commonwealth.

Act of May 4, 1927, P. L. 519, Art. XXXI, Sec. 3103; 53 P. S. 15193. 

Ap- 

Warrants shall be returnable forthwith, and upon such return like proceedings shall be had as in cases of summary conviction, with the same right of appeal from any final judgment. 

7. Return of Warrants: Procedure Thereafter: 

peals 

Act of May 4, 1927, P. L. 519, Art. 31, Sec. 3104; 53 P. S. 15194. 

8. Complaint Against Person Arrested on View: 

Procedure Thereafter

When any person is arrested on view, a complaint, on oath or affirmation, shall be immediately made, whereupon like proceedings shall be had as provided in the preceding section. 

Act of May 4, 1927, P. L. 519, Art. 31, Sec. 3105; 53 P. S. 15195. 

9. Commitment Pending Hearing of Persons Arrested for Violations of Ordinances 

Any person arrested for the violation of a borough ordinance may be committed to the borough lockup, pending

hearing or trial, but in case there is not a suitable lockup in which to detain prisoners, the person arrested may be committed to the county jail. 

Act of May 4, 1927, P. L. 519, Art. 31, Sec. 3107; 53 P. S. 15196. 

10. Commitments After Hearing 

Upon judgment against any person by summary conviction, or by proceedings by summons or default of the payment of the fine or penalty imposed and the costs, the defendant may be sentenced and committed to the borough lockup, for a period not exceeding five days, or to the county jail or workhouse for a period not exceeding thirty days. 

Act of May 4, 1927, P. L. 519, Art. 31, Sec. 3107; 53 P. S. 15197. 

11. Penalty Not to Exceed One Hundred Dollars, Collection of Penalties 

No fine or penalty shall exceed one hundred dollars for any single violation of any ordinance. In case the defendant has goods or property of any kind whatsoever, out of which the judgment and costs can be collected by execution or other process, the plaintiff in the action may elect to collect the judgment and costs by such proceedings. 

Act of May 4, 1927, P. L. 519, Art. 31, Sec. 3108; 53 P. S. 15198. 

12. Commitment of Vagrants or Tramps in Default of Payment of Fine

When any person shall have been arrested by authority of the burgess or justice of the peace of any borough, charged with being a vagrant or tramp, and having refused to pay the fine imposed for such offense, the burgess or justice of the peace shall have authority to commit such person to the lockup in such borough, for a term not exceeding five days or compel such person to work upon the public works or streets

period of time not exceeding one day of the borough, for a for each dollar of fine imposed. 

Act of May 4, 1927, P. L. 519, Art. 31, Sec. 3109; 53 R. S

15. Recovery of Fines, Penalties, Costs 

Fines, penalties, and costs made payable by this act, or coverable before the burgess or any justice of the peace of imposed under the ordinances of any borough, shall be rethe borough, in the same manner as debts not exceeding three hundred dollars are recoverable, and when so recovered shall be forthwith paid to the treasurer of the borough.

16. First Class Cities: General Powers to Enact and Enforce Ordinances 

The cities of the first class of this commonwealth shall have power to make all such ordinances, bylaws, rules, and regulations not inconsistent with or restrained by the Constitution and laws of this Commonwealth as may be expedient or necessary for the proper management, care, and control of the city and its finances, and the maintenance of the peace, good government, safety, and welfare of the city-and the exercise of full and complete powers for local self-government in matter of police-and to enforce all ordinances by imposing fines upon inhabitants or other persons for violation thereof, not exceeding one hundred dollars for any one offense, recoverable with costs, together with judgment of imprisonment not exceeding thirty days if the amount of said fine and cost shall not be paid into the court imposing the fine within ten days from the date of the imposition thereof. 

Act of March 25, 1929, P. L. 66, Sec. 1; 53 P. S. 3451. 

17. Summary Proceedings 

Any police officer or constable, upon view of the breach of any ordinance of any city of the first class, is authorized to forthwith arrest the person or persons so offending, without any process, and to take said person or persons forthwith before any police magistrate or alderman of said city, who shall thereupon require bail for the appearance of said person at a time to be fixed for the hearing of said charge, and in default of bail to commit for a hearing; and at said hearing the case shall be proceeded with as if the parties were appearing before said magistrate or alderman upon a summons duly issued and returned served, or if both parties desire it, the case may be entered and determined by the magistrate or alderman in like manner, without requiring bail or further continuance. 

Act of May 3, 1876, P. L. 99, Sec. 1; 53 P. S. 3452. 2

18. Magistrates' Courts, Philadelphia "The Magistrates' Court Act" (Act of May 10, 1927, P. L. 866, 42 P. S. 1042) establishes twenty-eight courts, not of record, of police and civil causes. 

The jurisdiction of each of said magistrates shall extend throughout the city and county of Philadelphia, and they shall be, by virtue of their office, ex officio justices of the peace. They shall have all the powers and shall exercise the same jurisdiction civil and criminal (except as herein otherwise provided) as was formerly by law exercised by alderman of said city.

In all cases of summary conviction before said magistrates, either party may appeal to the court of quarter sessions of said county, upon allowance of any judge thereof, upon cause shown; and either party may also appeal from the judgment of said magistrates in a suit for a penalty to the courts of common pleas of said county, upon the allowance of any of the judges thereof upon cause shown, provided, however, that this enactment shall not affect any right of appeal under existing laws.¹ The Mayor has the power and authority of a justice of the peace.2 

1 Act of May 10, 1927, P. L. 866, Sec. 20; 42 P. S. 1060. 

2 Act of March 11, 1789, 2 Sm. L. 462, Sec. 19; 53 P. S. 6382. 

19. Second Class Cities: Police Power 

The police power for taking informations, making arrests, and preservation of the peace, heretofore vested in the mayor and five police magistrates, shall hereafter vest in the mayor and not less than five nor more than eight police magistrates, as shall be designated by ordinance, all of whom shall not be of the same political faith; to be appointed by the mayor, subject to the approval of the select council in such districts of the city as shall by ordinance be designated, whose term of office shall be during good behavior and until a successor shall be appointed and approved, said magistrate shall receive an annual salary, not exceeding six thousand dollars, to be fixed by councils, and shall serve for

the term of the mayor appointing them, but subject to removal by him. 

Act of April 13, 1927, P. L. 200, Sec. 1; 53 P. S. 9561. 

20. Jurisdiction 

All police magistrates in all cities of the second class in this commonwealth shall have complete and full jurisdiction, power and authority to receive and take criminal informations, on oath or affirmation of and subscribed to by the affiant, accusing any person or persons of the commission of any felony or misdemeanor, where such felony or misdemeanor has been committed within the corporate limits of the city in which such police magistrate resides and is appointed, and to issue warrants for the arrest of such person so accused, administer oaths and hold preliminary hearings in all such cases, and commit to jail, or bind over for trial at the next term of the proper county, or discharge such accused person or persons, as the evidence at such hearing or hearings may warrant. 

Act of June 16, 1891, P. L. 303, Sec. 1; 53 P. S. 9564. 

21. Admission to Bail

In all cases, the person so accused of any felony or misdemeanor, shall be admitted to bail by one or more sufficient sureties to be taken before the police magistrate before whom such information may be made as aforesaid, or before any judge, justice, mayor, recorder or alderman where the offense has been committed, except such persons as are precluded from being bailed by the constitution of this Commonwealth; Provided also, that persons accused as aforesaid of murder or manslaughter, shall only be admitted to bail by the supreme court or one of the judges thereof, or a president judge of the court of oyer and terminer and quarter sessions of the peace; persons so accused as aforesaid of arson, rape, mayhem, sodomy, buggery, robbery, or burglary shall only be bailable by the supreme court, or any of the judges of the court of oyer and terminer and quarter sessions. of the peace, or the mayor or recorder of such city. 

Act of June 16, 1891, P. L. 303, Sec. 2; 53 P. S. 9565.

22. Hearings 

The said police magistrates shall likewise have full and complete power, jurisdiction and authority to administer oaths and examine witnesses, and hear, determine and punish according to laws and ordinances of such city, all cases of arrests upon view, or upon information made and warrant issued, by the police of the city in which such police magistrate may reside or be appointed, of all persons who may be found engaged in or be charged with drunkenness, disorderly conduct, selling liquor contrary to law, maintaining a disorderly house or bawdy house, lewd, indecent or lascivious behavior on the streets or elsewhere, gambling, creating riots or disturbances, vagrants, beggars, prostitutes, disturbers of the public peace, known or reputed pickpockets, burglars, thieves, watch stuffers, cheating, swindling, persons who abuse their families, and suspicious persons who can give no reasonable account of themselves, or violating any of the laws or ordinances of such city. 

Act of June 16, 1891, P. L. 303, Sec. 3; 53 P. S. 9566. 

23. Suits for Fines and Penalties, Summary Convic tion The said police magistrates shall likewise have full and complete jurisdiction of suits for the recovery of fines and penalties imposed by any and all ordinances of the city in which they reside and are appointed, and of all cases of summary conviction arising under the laws and ordinances of such city, with full power to hear the said cases, administer oaths or affirmations therein, decide the same, enforce the penalty, collect the fines, or commit to prison, as the case may be, according to the provisions of the law and ordinances applicable thereto. 

Act of June 16, 1891, P. L. 303, Sec. 4; 53 P. S. 9567. 

24. Commitment of Vagrants

It shall and may be lawful for any and all police magistrates, where vagrants shall be found within the city in which such police magistrate resides or is appointed, to com-

mit such vagrants, (being thereof legally convicted before him on his own view, or by confession of such offenders, or by the oaths or affirmation of one or more credible witnesses,) to the workhouse of the county within which such city is situate, if such there be, otherwise to the common jail of such county, there to be kept at hard labor by the keeper of such workhouse or jail, for any time not less than thirty days nor more than six months. 

Act of June 16, 1891, P. L. 303, Sec. 5; 53 P. S. 9568. 1 

25. Third Class Cities: Execution of Laws: Powers of Sheriff Conferred

It shall be the duty of the mayor to be vigilant and active in causing the ordinances of the city, and the laws of the Commonwealth relating to the government of the city, to be executed and enforced. In order to enable him effectually to preserve the public peace within the city, all the powers which are devolved by the laws of this State upon sheriffs, to prevent and supress mobs, riots, and unlawful and tumultuous assemblies, are hereby conferred upon him. 

Act of June 23, 1931, P. L. 932, Art. 12, Sec. 1203; 53 P. S. 12198-1203. 

26. Criminal and Civil Jurisdiction Docket Fees 

The mayor shall have the criminal jurisdiction of an alderman within the city; and shall have no civil jurisdiction except in relation to actions for fines, penalties, or forfeitures imposed by virtue of the ordinances of the city or the laws of the Commonwealth relating thereto.

He shall have the power of a committing magistrate under the acts of assembly relating to tramps, vagrants, dissolute, disorderly and drunken persons, as may be provided by law.

He shall be empowered to take ackniwledgments of any instruments in writing, solemnize marriages, and administer oaths and affirmations, and shall attest all his acts with his official seal. He shall keep a docket, and shall enter therein all actions and proceedings had before him; and said docket with the entries therein, and duly certified transcripts there-

of, shall be received in evidence in the same manner and with like effect as the docket entries and transcripts of aldermen are by law admissible for similar purposes. 

He shall charge and receive for all official services the same fees and costs as are allowed by law to aldermen of the city for similar service, but shall pay over the same into the city treasury monthly, according to the statements thereof, verified by his oath or affirmation taken before the superintendent of accounts and finance, and filed with him. 

Act of June 23, 1931, P. L. 932, Art. 12, Sec. 1206, 53 P. S. 

12198-1206; Committment of vagrants, Act of June 

26, 1895, P. L. 377, Sec. 6. 61 P. S. 753. 

27. Acting Mayor

The member of city council who shall be designated as the director of the department of accounts and finances shall be vice president of the city council, and acting mayor of the city during the absence or inability of the mayor, he shall exercise all the rights and powers of the mayor. Upon request any alderman may hold police court.

Act of June 23, 1931, P. L. 932, Art. 12, Sec. 1206, 53 P. S. 

12198—1208; Act of June 23, 1931, P. L. 932, Art. X, Sec. 1018, 53 P. S. 12198-1018; city powers, Act of June 23, 1931, P. L. 

932, Art. XXIV, Sec. 2403, 53 P. S. 12198-2402 (54).

Chapter III METHOD OF PROSECUTION

18. ENTERING COMPLAINTS INFORMATION: WHO MAY MAKE HUSBAND AND WIFE OATH OR AFFIRMATION 1. Commencement of Prosecution 

A prosecution may be commenced; 

1. By coroner's inquest finding; 

2. By a constable's return to the court of quarter sessions. 

3. By a criminal information presented by the district attorney to the court having jurisdiction: 

4. By a presentment of the grand jury; 

5. By an indictment presented to the grand jury by the district attorney without a previous binding over (District Attorney's Bill). 

6. By information before a magistrate, alderman, or justice of the peace or other committing magistrates. 19

2. Coroners to Investigate Facts in Deaths under 

Suspicious Circumstances

It shall be the duty of the coroner or the deputy coroner of any county in this Commonwealth, in all cases where death is sudden or violent or is of a suspicious nature and character, to cause a careful investigation (inquest) of the facts concerning said death to be made to ascertain whether the death was due to other than natural causes and to make or cause to be made such an autopsy as the facts of the case may demand. 

Act of July 12, 1935, P. L. 710, Sec. 1, 16 P. S. 3161. 

3. Jury

The number of jurors summoned by coroners and justice of the peace in cases of inquests upon the bodies of deceased persons, as now required to be held by law, shall not be more than six to attend any one inquest. 

Act of May 16, 1857, P. L. 536, Sec. 1; 16 P. S. 3163; 16 P. S. 3163. 

4. Disposition of Body and Effects 

In all cases of sudden or violent death, where it appears the cause of death is not surrounded by any suspicious circumstances the body of the deceased shall be sent immediately by the police or health authorities, if the death shall occur away from the home or residence of the deceased, to the same, or to such relatives of the deceased, or friends who may claim the body; or, in absence of any knowledge as to the person, his or her home, residence, relative or friend, the health board or police authorities of any county, city, borough or township shall take possession of such body, and keep the same in such place as may be provided by said authorities, for the purpose of remaining for a limited time for identification; and any effects found on the body shall be handed over by said police or health authorities to the coroner or deputy coroner who shall be notified of the death and the effects found on the body shall be given by the coroner or deputy coroner to those legally entitled to the same; Provid-

ed, that the coroner shall have the right to view any such body and to investigate the fact or cause of said death, if in his opinion the same is required. 

Act of July 12, 1935, P. L. 710, Sec. 2, 16 P. S. 3162. 

5. Hearing, Commitment

The coroner should never hear a homicide case without the presence of the district attorney and should only discharge persons accused of homicide on instructions from the district attorney.¹ He does not have the power of a committing magistrate.² 

1 Coroner's Duties, 20 Dist. Repts. 685. 

2 Walker v. McKean County, 15 Dist. Repts. 577. 

6. Constables' Returns

At common law constables were bound to present to the term or last court all offenses inquirable in these courts. Such a return is in the nature of an official information, and being made under oath is the equivalent of a charge before a magistrate, and is sufficient grounds on which to found indictments. 1 The returns required by law to be made by constables to the court of quarter sessions may, in the discretion of the court, be abolished, or be made at such time and relating to such subjects as the court may require. No constable shall hereafter be entitled to any fees or mileage for making any such return, except such as are required by the court.2 

1 Com. v. Hamilton, 74 Pa. Super. 419, 422; Com. v. 

P. R. R. Co. 72 Pa. Super. 353, nuisance; Com. v. Scranton, 21 

Lack. Jur. 106. 

2 Act of Feb. 28, 1933, P. L. 5; 13 P. S. 73. 

7. Criminal Information 

"No person shall for any indictible offense be proceeded against criminally by information, except in cases arising in the land or naval forces, or in the militia, when in actual service, in time of war or public danger; or by leave of court for oppression or misdemeanor in office."

A proceeding by "information" in the sense in which the term is used in Art. 1, Sec. 10, of the Constitution of

Pennsylvania, has reference exclusively to practice such as were formerly used in England whereby upon information made in the King's Court by some one or by some of the king's officers, ex officio, the accused person was put on trial without further inquiry or investigation.2 

1 Art. 1, Sec. 10, Const. Pa.; Com. v. Francis, 250 Pa. 496; 

Com. v. Fletcher, 208 Pa. 137; Com. v. Norris, 87 Pa. Super., 66, misdemeanor in office, district attorney's bill. 

2 Com. ex rel. v. Francis, 250 Pa. 496. 

8. Presentment of the Grand Jury 

A grand jury can only act upon and present offenses of public notoriety, such as are within their own knowledge; such as are given to them by the court and such as are sent to them by the district attorney; and in no other cases can they indict without a previous prosecution before a magistrate according to the terms of the Bill of Rights. 

The power of the court of its own motion to direct an investigation by a grand jury will only be exercised touching matters largely affecting the rights of the people of the state, such as great riots and flagrant vices.¹ 

Where an investigation is desired as to matter of general concern where there is no particular person charged, the grand jury is the proper party to make the investigation.2 

1 Com. v. Hurd, 177 Pa. 481; Com. v. Green, 126 Pa. 531; 

Charge to the grand jury, 62 P. L. J., 105; Com. v. Kenehan, 30 Lack. J. 58; Com. v. Hackney, 117 Pa. Super. 519. 

2 Com. v. Smith, 185 Pa. 553; Com. ex rel. Farley v. Rotan, 82 Pa. Super. 172; Com. v. Sunderlin, 31 Pa. Super. 349; Dist. 

Attorney's Petition, 50 Pa. C. C. 347, 22 Lack. Jur., 99; Com. v. 

Kenehan, 30 Lack. J. 58. 

9. District Attorney's Bills

The district attorney may prefer an indictment before a grand jury without a previous information, hearing, binding over or commitment by a magistrate, but this power is properly exercised only where there is occasion for great haste in applying the machinery of the law, or where the exi-

gencies of the case and the public interest may reasonably require such action to be taken.

When exercised without such necessity, or merely to suit the convenience, the pleasure of other private purpose of the prosecutor, it is the duty of the court to set the officer's act aside. Where the indictment is sent up without first obtaining the leave of the court, the discretion of the court may be invoked, and is exercisable upon a motion to quash. 

The court of quarter sessions has discretionary and revisory powers over what are called district attorney bills, and where the sanction of the court to sending up a bill has been obtained by deception, whether wilful or unintentional, or has resulted from a misapprehension of the facts by the judge, he may revise his action even after the return of an indictment. The action of the court in revoking its leave to send a district attorney's bill to the grand jury, by quashing the indictment, is not reviewable on appeal, except for manifest and flagrant injustice. 

Com. v. Green, 126 Pa. 531; Com. v. Chirico, 117 Pa. Super. 

199; Com. v. Feurstein, 98 Pa. Super., 201; Com. v. Norris, 87 

Pa. Super., 66, misdemeanor in office; Com. v. Sheppard, 20 Pa. 

Super. 417, indictment quashed, bar of statute of limitation, fugitive from justice; Com. v. Strauss, 89 Pa. Super. 82; Com. v. 

Nathias, 89 Pa. Super. 98, District Attorney need not give reason for action; Com. v. Rothensies, 64 Pa. Super. 395, 416; 

Com. v. Hackney, 117 Pa. Super. 519, tax collector. 

10. Information: Who May Make 

As a general rule every person who is capable of taking an oath in a court of justice is competent to become a prosecutor. It is not necessary that the informer or prosecutor should have personal knowledge of the facts necessary to convict the defendant. If he is able to swear that he is informed of them and believes the facts stated in the information to be true, this is all that has ever been required to justify a magistrate or justice of the peace in issuing a warrant.¹ 

Those only are disqualified from becoming prosecutors who either from religious scruples or infidelity, which renders them incapable of affirming or taking an oath, or from infamy, which presumes them unworthy of credit, and are

generally incompetent to become witnesses; to these classes may be added idiots, lunatics and children too young to understand the sanctity of an oath.2 

1 Com. v. Campbell, 22 Pa. Super. 98. 

2 Com. v. Cartuseiello, 100 Pa. Super. 473, 483; Com. v. 

Hamilton, 74 Pa. Super., 419, 422; Com. v. Barr, 25 Pa. Super., 609. 

11. Husband and Wife 

Husband or wife may make information against the other for adultery or bigamy, or against paramour. 

Com. v. Cartusciello, 100 Pa. Super. 473; Com. v. Barr, 25 Pa. Super. 609; Com. v. Snyder, 21 Dist. Repts. 731; Com. v. 

Bomberger, 16 Dist. Repts. 371; Com. v. Marge, 19 Del. Co. 180. 

12. Oath or Affirmation

Persons may qualify themselves according to their conscientious persuasion, either by taking the solemn affirmation. or any oath in the usual or common form, by laying the hand upon an open copy of the Holy Bible, or by lifting up the right hand and pronouncing or assenting to the following words, I, A. B., do swear by Almighty God, the searcher of all hearts, that I will and that as I shall answer to God at the last great day. 

Com. v. Thomas, 74 Pa. Super. 7, Before Notary Public, not attested, valid; Com. v. Kipnis, 46 Pa. C. C. 104. 

13. On Information and Belief

One about to make a complaint before a justice of the peace in order to obtain a warrant of arrest can pursue either of two courses; 

1st. If the information he has received is of such character as to produce conviction in his own mind after investigation, that probable cause for charging the defendant with the commission of a crime exists, he can, "Upon his oath, depose and say, that the facts showing probable cause are true or are, to the best of his knowledge, information and belief, true."

2nd. If he wishes to avoid the responsibility of a positive oath he should, "upon his oath, depose and say that he is informed" (stating the facts which show probable cause of which he is informed, and who informed him), and then state that he believes the facts of which he has been informed to be true, and he expects to be able to prove them true. 

Com. v. Green, 185 Pa. 641; Com. v. Mallini, 214 Pa. 50; 

Com. v. Clutter, 17 Dist. Repts. 160. 

14. Charging the Offense

The charge need not be made with the formality and particularity of an indictment; it is enough if it contains the essential elements of an offense, and it need not negative matters of defense arising from statutory exceptions.¹

It must contain a direct and positive charge against the defendnt; and not merely facts amounting to a presumption of guilt, however sufficient such facts may be as prima facie evidence against him.2

The complaint is the foundation of the proceeding and the jurisdictional facts must appear on its face by a plain statement of them, neither the warrant nor the docket entries can supply them or dispense with their presence there.³ 

The purpose of a preliminary written charge and hearing before a justice is to inform the defendant as to the offense with which he is charged and to ascertain whether there are sufficient grounds for holding him for trial. The office of the written information is to identify the crime charged, and this it may do by employing a general term, or in the words by which the crime is designated in the common language of the people, it is not necessary that it charge the offense with the detail and technical accuracy required in an indictment. 

Committing magistrates are not usually learned in the law, and all that should be required of them, is a plain statement of the charge laid. The putting of it in legal phraseology thereafter becomes the duty of the district attorney. An information charging that the defendant "did unlawfully attempt to break and enter the building of A. & Co" will support an indictment charging attempt to break and enter with

intent to commit a felony. Charging violation of specified sections of The Motor Vehicle Code is not sufficient." 

1 Com. v. Gilbert, 170 Pa. 426, Sunday Law, information insufficient; Com. v. Beatty, 91 Pa. Super. 37; Com. v. Miller, 77 Pa. Super. 469; Com. v. Dingman, 26 Pa. Super, 615; Com. v. 

Campbell, 22 Pa. Super., 98; Com. v. Waxman, 26 Dauph. Co. 

205; Com. ex rel. v. Warden of jail, 9 Wash. Co. 87; Com. v. 

Waint, 1 Somer, Co. 604; Com. v. Loreman, 46 Pa. C. C. 250. 

2 Com. v. Gilbert, 170 Pa. 426; Com. v. Kane, 65 Pa. Super. 

258, disorderly conduct, insufficient. 

Com. v. Gilbert, 170 Pa. 426; Com. v. Beatty, 91 Pa. 

Super. 37; Com. v. Campbell, 22 Pa. Super. 98; Com. v. Herr, 27 Dist. Repts. 976. 

4 Com. v. Grego, 116 Pa. Super. 295. 

5 Com. v. Harned, 25 D. & C. 578. 

15. Several Crimes, Same Transaction

When the defendant may fairly be accused of one crime or another, or of more crimes than one growing out of the same transaction, it is plainly the duty of the magistrate taking the complaint to see that the accusation and the commitment thereon are broad enough to cover the whole, leaving to the grand jury or the prosecuting officer to elect between the several methods of pursuing the defendant. 

Com. v. Danner, 79 Pa. Super. 556; Com. v. Barr, 25 Pa. 

Super. 609. 

16. Several Defendants

When the crime charged was committed by several defendants at one and the same time, they should all be included in one information, and one warrant of arrest only should be issued. 

Act of March 10, 1905, P. L. 35, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 1293, 1294; 

Barry v. McPherson, 24 Dist. Repts. 118. 

17. Information, Violations, "Vehicle Code" 

Informations, charging violations of any of the summary provisions of the "Vehicle Code," shall be brought be fore the magistrate nearest to the point where the alleged violation occurred, or a magistrate within the city, borough,

incorporated town, or township where the alleged violation occurred, within fifteen (15) days after the commission of the alleged offense and not thereafter, except that where an information is filed against a person prima facie guilty of a summary offense, and it subsequently appears that a person other than the person named in the information was the offender, an information may be filed against such other person within fifteen (15) days after his or her identity shall have been discovered. Information under the act regulating trucks and tractors shall be brought before a magistrate within the city, borough, incorporated town or township where the alleged violation occurred.¹

Where the offense committed is designated a felony or misdemeanor information may be filed as now provided by law.2 

1 Act of June 22, 1931, P. L. 815, Sec. 2, 75 P. S. 1091. 

2 Act of June 22, 1931, P. L. 751, Sec. 2, 75 P. S. 731. 

18. Entering Complaints 

It is the duty of all Magistrates, upon complaint being made upon oath in criminal cases to enter such complaint upon their dockets, with the name, residence and occupation if any, of all defendants, bail and witnesses. 

Act of June 11, 1885, P. L. 110, Sec. 1; 42 P. S. 722.

Chapter IV WARRANTS 1. FORM AND CONTENTS OF WARRANTS 2. WHO MAY SERVE 3. WHERE SERVED, BAIL WHEN GIVEN OUTSIDE THE COUNTY

SEARCH WARRANTS CONSTITUTIONAL PROVISIONS PROBABLE CAUSE CASES IN WHICH ISSUED GOODS SUPPOSED TO HAVE BEEN STOLEN, SECURITY 10. PUBLIC MAY INSPECT AFFIDAVIT 1. Form and Contents of Warrants 

The warrant of arrest must be in writing and in the name of "The Commonwealth of Pennsylvania";¹ set forth substantially the nature of the offense;2 command that a definite person named or described in the warrant be arrested and brought before the magistrate issuing the warrant; state the time when issued and the county or city where issued; be signed and sealed by the magistrate; should be directed to a peace officer; and should state the amount of bail required, if the offense is bailable by a magistrate.5 

1 Const. of Pa., Art. 1, Sec. 8.

4 Keech v. County of York, 3 Somerset, 234.

2. Who May Serve

A justice of the peace may authorize any person to be his officer, but it is advisable that process be directed to a con- 28

stable. A deputy sheriff, not salaried, may serve and collect costs on a warrant.

A warrant may be served by any constable or police officer generally, in addition to any particular constable designated specially in the warrant. 

Com. v. Keeper of Jail, 1 Ashmead 183; Com. v. Fitz, 8 

Wash. Co. 40; Keech v. County of York, 3 Somerset 234. 

3. Where Served, Bail When Given Outside the County

A warrant may be served in any county for an offense committed in the county in which the warrant issued, and where the offense is bailable by an alderman or justice of the peace, bail may be given in the county where the arrest is made which bail shall be for defendant's appearance at the next court in the county where the offense was committed. 

The bond is to be transmitted to the clerk of the court of the county in which the defendant is to appear. 

Act of May 2, 1899, P. L. 173, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 3. 

4. Vehicle Code 

Within the period of seven days after an information has been made for violation of a summary provision of the Vehicle Code the magistrate shall send by registered mail, to the person charged, at the address shown by the records of the department, a notice in writing of the filing of the information, together with a copy thereof and a notice to appear within ten days of the date of the writing.

If the person named in the information shall not voluntarily appear within ten (10) days of the date of the written notice, a warrant shall then issue and may be served by a peace officer having authority to serve warrants in the county in which the alleged violation has been committed. If the person charged cannot be served within such county, then the magistrate shall deputize a peace officer, having authority to serve warrants in the county wherein the person charged resides, or may be found, to serve such warrant.

The peace office serving such warrant shall take the defendant before a magistrate, within the city, borough, incor- 3

porated town, or township in which the defendant is found, who shall take bail, either for the defendant's appearance before the magistrate who issued the warrant, or for his appearance for trial in the proper court, if a summary hearing is waived. 

Act of June 22, 1931, P. L. 751, Sec. 2, 75 P. S. 732; Act of 

June 22, 1931, P. L. 815, Sec. 2, 75 P. S. 1092, tractors, trucks. 

5. Bench Warrants, Fugitives from Other Counties

The judge of the supreme court, of the court of oyer and terminer and general jail delivery, of the court of quarter sessions, or any of them, shall and may direct their writs and precepts to the sheriffs and coroners of the several counties within the commonwealth to take person indicted for felonies or other offenses before them.1

Any judge of the common pleas court may issue his warrant for the arrest of a fugitive from another county charged with the commission of a felony.2 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 1; judges of quarter sessions may act as committing magistrates, Com. v. 

McKeehan, 93 Pa. Super. 348. 

2 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 2.

SEARCH WARRANTS 6. Constitutional Provisions 

"The people shall be secure in their persons, houses, papers and possessions from unreasonable searches and seizures; and no warrant to search any place or to seize any person or thing shall issue without describing them as nearly as may be, nor without probable cause supported by oath or affirmation subscribed to by the affiant."¹ 

The constitutional prohibition against unreasonable search and seizure is not violated by the search of a person lawfully arrested, nor by the search of one who consents to

the search,2 nor by a search for liquor when serving a warrant for keeping a disorderly house.³ 

1 Const. Pa. Art. 1, Sec. 8; Com. v. Schwartz, 82 Pa. Super. 

369; Com. v. Kekic, 26 Dauph. Co., 148, cited for review of

Const. Rights; it is enough to describe a definite ascertainable place excluding all others; describing a farm by adjoining premises is sufficient; Com. v. Orwig, 96 Pa. Super. 383; "two story frame building outbuildings and premises" is not sufficient where the location is not given, Com. v. Hunsinger, 290 Pa. 

185; Com. v. Connolly, 290 Pa. 181. 

2 Com. v. Eitler, 2 D. & C. 33. 

3 Com. v. Vanderpool, 84 Pa. Super. 552. 

7. Probable Cause

The affiant is not restricted to matters within his own knowledge nor is he bound to set forth the names of witnesses or the details as to what they would testify. Probable cause does not import absolute certainty. It only implies reasonable grounds for belief, and the justice issuing the search warrant is the authority to be satisfied that probable cause exists. Whether search and seizure is or is not unreasonable must be determined from the facts in each particular case. 

Com. v. Hunsinger, 89 Pa. Super. 238; Com. v. Dabbiero, 89 Pa. Super. 435. 

8. Cases in Which Issued Search warrants are only issued in special cases mentioned by the acts of assembly,¹ i. e., burglary, robbery, larceny,2 gambling instruments, (money cannot be confiscated), beverage or medical containers, milk, ice cream containers, marked bottles," obscene literature, for an act of cruelty to animals." 

1 Snyder v. Davis, 27 Dist. Repts. 832. 

2 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 5; 19 P. S. 982. 

3 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 59; 18 P. S. 1444; 

Com. v. Kaiser, 16 Del. Co. 153, procedure; Mill's Novelty Co's. 

App. 316 Pa. 449. 

4 Com. v. Milloy, 70 P. L. J. 682; Rosen v. Supt. of Police, 120 Pa. Super. 59.

5 Act of June 15, 1911, P. L. 975, Sec. 1; 73 P. S. 32; Com. v. Barbono, 56 Pa. Super., 637; Com. v. Wall, 25 Dist. Repts., 1097. 

Act of March 27, 1903, P. L. 75, Sec. 3; 73 P. S. 63. 

7 Act of April 20, 1853, P. L. 643, Sec. 3; 18 P. S. 2981. · 

8 Act of May 12, 1897, P. L. 63, Sec. 3; 18 P. S. 783. 

9 Act of June 3, 1911, P. L. 654, Sec. 1; 18 P. S. 3120. 

9. Goods Supposed to Have Been Stolen, Security 

When in the execution of a warrant for burglary, robbery or stealing, or of a search warrant for stolen goods, there is probable cause, supported by oath, to suspect that other goods which may be discovered on such search are stolen, the magistrate may direct the seizure of such goods, and secure the same in his own custody, unless the person in whose custody the goods were found gives surety to produce them at the trial.

The magistrate shall file an inventory of the goods with the clerk of courts, and shall before the trial, advertise the inventory, the time of the trial and the name of the person charged, in the newspapers, or in three public places in the city or county where the offense is charged to have been committed.

If at the trial the accused is acquitted, and no one shall claim the goods, at the expiration of three months the said goods shall be returned to the accused, and the county shall be liable for the costs. 

If after conviction of stealing only, restitution made to the owner, and compliance with the sentence of the court, the accused shall claim the remainder of the goods, and no claimant appears within nine months, he shall be entitled to the remainder, or the value thereof, if they have been sold. 

Upon an attainder of burglary or robbery the goods shall be sold at a public sale by the county commissioners, and cla nant may within one year prove ownership as provided by law. 

Act of March 31, 1860 P. L. 427, Sec. 5, 19 P. S. 982; Com. v. Volz, 20 Lack. 154; 28 Dist. 1016, third party in whose possession goods found may give bond.

10. Public May Inspect Affidavit

It is the duty of a magistrate, alderman or justice of the peace issuing a search warrant, to keep in his possession for inspection by the public, the affidavit or complaint on which the search warrant issues.

Dersin v. Rooch, 75 Pitts. L. J. 651.

Chapter V ARREST 

1. 

DEFINITION, HOW MADE 2. WAIVING IRREGULARITIES PRIVILEGE FROM ARREST 

3. 4.

ARREST BY OFFICER WITHOUT WARRANT 5. ARREST, VIOLATION VEHICLE CODE 

6.

PRIVATE PERSON ARRESTING WITHOUT A WARRANT 

7. 8. 9.

PRIVATE PERSON ASSISTING OFFICER KILLING MAKING ARREST TEMPORARY DETENTION OF PRISONERS IN LOCKUPS 10. UPON APPLICATION OFFICER MUST GIVE NAME OF PRISONER AND AMOUNT OF BAIL CUSTODY AFTER ARREST, AWAITING TRIAL 12. ARREST ON BAIL PIECE 

11. 

13. ARREST ON SUNDAY, BREACH OF THE PEACE 14. TAKING PROPERTY FROM PRISONER 15. REWARD 16. FINGER PRINTS, PHOTOGRAPHS 1. Definition, How Made 

Arrest is the taking of a person into custody in order that he may be forthcoming to answer for the commission of a public offense.

An arrest is made by an actual restraint of the person to be arrested, or by his submission to the custody of the person making the arrest. 

No unnecessary or unreasonable force should be used in making an arrest, and the person arrested should not be subjected to any greater restraint than is necessary for his detention. 

Sec. 18, 19, Code Crim. Proc. Am. Law Inst.; McAleer v. 

Good, 216 Pa. 473. 34

35. 

2. Waiving Irregularities 

Before, not after, indictment found the defendant can question the legality of his arrest and his being held for trial, by a proceeding to be discharged from custody, or to be released from his recognizance and discharged without day. 

Com. v. Sullivan, 91 Pa. Super. 544; Com. v. Fedula, 89 

Pa. Super. 244; Com. v. Keegan, 70 Pa. Super. 436; Com. v. 

Williams, 25 Lackawana, 240; Com. v. Gates, 98 Pa. Super. 

591, habeas corpus. 

3. Privilege from Arrest

The members of the general Assembly shall in all cases, except treason, felony, violation of their oaths of office, and breach or surety of the peace, be privileged from arrest during their attendance at a session of their respective houses, and in going and returning from the same. This exemption extends only to arrest on civil process.¹ 

No officer or enlisted man (Pa. National Guard) shall be arrested on any warrant, except for treason or felony, while going to, remaining at, or returning from a place where he is ordered to attend military duty.2

A party or a witness attending, coming to, or returning from a session of court is not privileged from arrest upon criminal process.3 

1 Const. of Penna. Art. 11, Sec. 15. 

2 Act of May 17, 1921, P. L. 869, Sec. 30; 51 P. S. 60. 

3 Com. v. Orowski, 29 Dist. Repts., 834; Com. v. Turner, 17 Berks, Co., 197. 

4. Arrest by Officer without Warrant

The general rule is that upon reasonable suspicion, founded either on his own knowledge or the information of others, that a felony, or such a breach of the peace as will probably prove to be a felony, has been committed, an officer may arrest without a warrant, but he cannot arrest for an ordinary misdemeanor, unless present at the commission of the offense.¹ (See "Uniform Extradition Act.” Act of 

Apr. 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 14, 19 P. S. 101)

The police have a discretion, which should be most pru dently exercised, of dispersing all unwieldy assemblages of persons on the public highways, or other assemblages of persons that are disorderly or noisy, though their conduct falls short of a breach of the peace. While arrest for such noisy demonstrations or minor disorders cannot be made without a warrant, disobedience of an order to disperse is a breach of the peace and justifies arrest without a warrant. 

The police have the right to enter parks, theatres or other places in which large numbers of persons are invited to assemble, whenever, in the judgment of the police authority, the presence of such officers of the law is necessary to protect the public against accident, sudden disorder, criminal practice for example, for pocket picking-or other improper purposes. 2 After arrest on view, or on suspicion, the defendant should be at once taken before a magistrate and information made.3 

Opinion in Com. v. Doe, 109 Pa. Super. 187, reversing lower court:

A verdict of guilty of assault and battery was found against the defendant. The evidence of the prosecution showed that a constable of the city of Philadelphia saw lodged in the back seat of a parked automobile what is known as a "number book" often used in the operating of a lottery. He did not know who the owner of the automobile was, but knew that he had seen the defendant driving it on other occasions. The defendant came to the car and got in. 

The constable asked him about the number business, and he replied he knew nothing about it. The constable then took the key to the car and refused to allow the defendant to start the machine. The defendant then left the car and started to walk away, saying he did not have to explain anything, that he was going. The constable then stepped in front of him and the defendant shoved him out of the way, using no more force than was necessary to push him aside, whereupon the constable arrested him for assault and battery. 

The officer testified that he had received complaints against the defendant, and was looking to see if he operated a lottery. He also testified that he did not approach him with

the intention of arresting him, but only to ask him some questions. He further testified that he had never seen the defendant operating a lottery.

We need not inquire as to whether the constable had any right to search the car for that is not the question which is before us.

The constable's own testimony is that he stopped the defendant when he was getting into the car, he prevented him from starting the car by turning off the ignition, preventing him from putting the key in and his reason for doing so was because the defendant refused to explain to him "something of this number business." The defendant then stepped out of the car and started to walk away. The constable got in front of him and stopped him, whereupon the defendant pushed the constable twice with just enough force to push him out of the way. The constable weighed 

180 pounds.

Under the old English law in a case of affray, or any other action, which would tend to a breach of the peace, constables are authorized to arrest, without warrant. Our law recognizes the same right in them, and under our statutes they have the right to seize gambling devices, or arrest, on view, persons violating certain provisions of the Game and Forest Laws, and those regarding the pollution of streams, etc.

We have here in this case before us, a man walking away from a car which he had attempted to enter. He had committed no crime within sight of the officer, he had a right to go wherever he pleased, and the constable had no right to stop him. The officer by interposing his body sought to stop him, and thus prevent defendant's further progress, the latter has the right to push him aside using as much force as was reasonably necessary. He was not required to remain and submit to the inquiries of the officer. Freedom of locomotion, although subject to proper restriction, is included in the "liberty" guaranteed by our Constitution. 

The judgment of the lower court is reversed, and the defendant is discharged without day.¹

5. Arrest, Violation Vehicle Code 

Police officers, when in uniform and displaying a badge or other sign of authority, may arrest, upon view, any person violating any of the provisions of the "Vehicle Code" (Act of June 22, 1931, P. L. 815, Sec. 2, 75 P. S. 1093, and Act of 

June 22, 1931, P. L. 751, Sec. 2, 75 P. S. 733). Where the offense is designated a felony or a misdemeanor, or in cases causing or contributing to an accident resulting in injury or death of any person the officer shall forthwith make and file with the magistrate before whom the arrested person is taken, an information setting forth in detail the offense, and at once furnish a copy thereof to the person arrested. 

Act of June 22, 1931, P. L. 815, Sec. 2; 75 P. S. 1092; Act of June 22, 1931, P. L. 751, Sec. 2; 75 P. S. 733; Com. v. Charles, 114 Pa. Super. 473. 

6. Private Person Arresting without a Warrant

Any individual has the power to arrest without warrant a person known to have committed a felony, or where there

is probable cause of suspicion of such a crime, though in the latter case the arrest is made at the risk of the party making the same that no crime has in fact been committed. So an individual may arrest, without a warrant, one committing a breach of the peace in his view, but not after the offense has been committed. 

Volunteer Police Officers, 46 Pa. C. C. 231; Com. v.

Micusco, 273 Pa. 474, may not kill or wound felon unless necessary to prevent escape. 

7. Private Person Assisting Officer

It is a misdemeanor for any person to neglect or refuse to assist an officer in the execution of his office in a criminal case, or in the preservation of the peace or in apprehending and securing any person for a breach of the peace. Person assisting is protected although the officer may be acting wrongfully. 

Com. v. Sadowsky, 80 Pa. Super. 496. 

8. Killing Making Arrest 

In making an arrest an officer (or private citizen) may not shoot the felon, either to kill or wound, unless it is necessary to prevent his escape. To excuse the taking of life in making an arrest it must appear that the killing was necessary to effect the object, and that the arrest could not have been made without it; or that the officer reasonably believed, and had grounds to believe, that it was necessary to effect his purpose. Even a felon cannot be killed in an effort to arrest him, without criminal responsibility, unless he cannot otherwise be captured.

Com. v. Micusco, 273 Pa. 474; general rule is that in case of a misdemeanor officers must not shoot in making arrest or preventing escape, 4 Am. Jur. 54. 

9. Temporary Detention of Prisoners in Lockups 

Sheriffs, constables, members of the State Constabulary or other persons authorized by the law of this Commonwealth to make arrest, hereafter shall have the use, for a pe-

riod not to exceed forty-eight hours, of borough and township lockups and city or county prisons, for the detention of prisoners until they can be disposed of according to law, if found necessary by the officer in charge, at a cost of fifty cents per day.¹ Proper procedure for refusal is mandamus.2 

1 Act of April 23, 1909, P. L. 141; 19 P. S. 4, 5. 

2 Shaffer v. Sewickley Borough, 76 Pgh. L. J. 207; Atty. 

Genl. Op., 13 Dauph. 49, boroughs have discretionary power to some extent. 

10. Upon Application Officer Must Give Name of 

Prisoner and Amount of Bail

Officer arresting or having charge of a prisoner, must upon application by counsel or any citizen, issue a certificate stating the name of the prisoner, the charge and the amount of the bail, if the amount has been fixed. Refusal to give the certificate is a misdemeanor. 

Act of April 9, 1915, P. L. 76 Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 54; Opinion 

Atty. Genl., 25 Dist. Repts., 397. 

11. Custody after Arrest, Awaiting Trial 

Defendants, without bail, are committed to jail solely for safe keeping, to insure their attendance at trial, and neither the district attorney nor police officers have the right to remove them, even temporarily, to any other place than the courtroom for trial. While in jail defendants shall have an opportunity of a full and free communication with their counsel. 

Com. v. Trunk, et al. 105 Pa. Super., 569, State Police, third degree; Com. v. Brines, 29 Dist. Repts., 1091, district attorney may not take prisoners from jail for examination; Act of Feb. 

18, 1785, 2 Sm. L. 275, Sec. 12; 12 P. S. 1887, penalty of £ 200 (533.32) for removal by illegal process; prisoners not to be taken to taverns, Act of Feb. 14, 1730, 1 Sm. L. 181, Sec. 14; 19 

P. S. 7. 

12. Arrest on Bail Piece

The principal is regarded as being delivered into the custody of the bail, and the latter has the right to arrest or take the principal at any time or place and remove him to the

forum from which he has been released and to which he has obligated himself to surrender, and then give him up; and such an arrest is not made by virtue of the process of a court, but is the exercise of a right arising from the relation which the parties have established between themselves, and consequently is not confined to any locality or jurisdiction; and the bail piece is not the authority for such an arrest, but is simply evidence of a relationship between the parties. 

Com. v. Shugars, 7 Berks, Co. 23; In re. petition Von Der 

Ohe., 7 Dist. Repts., 131. may arrest on Sunday. 

13. Arrest on Sunday, Breach of the Peace 

The act of Jan. 12, 1705, 1 Sm. Laws 25, (44 P. S. 1) prohibits the execution of warrants, or arresting without warrant, on Sunday except in the case of treason, felony or breach of the peace.

Public peace is that invisible sense of security which every man feels so necessary to his comfort and for which all governments are instituted.

A breach of the peace is an offense well known to the common law. It is a disturbance of public order by an act of violence, or by an act liable to produce violence, or which, by causing consternation and alarm, disturbs the peace and quiet of the community.

Acts injurious to private persons, which tend to excite violent resentment and thus produce fighting and disturbance of the peace of society, are breaches of the peace. 

In general terms, a breach of the peace is a violation of public order, disturbance of the public tranquility, by any act or conduct inciting to violence or tending to provoke or excite others to break the peace.2 

1 Wanner v. Buch, 1 D. & C., 665, constable liable; Wanner v. Danner, 1 D. & C 666, justice liable; Com. v. Overton, 17 Luz. L. R. 253, liquor violations, bail may be taken on Sunday. 

2 Com. v. Sherman, 14 D. & C. 4, 12, baseball on Sunday, may be unlawful if accompanied with disturbing noise, large crowd, residential section.

14. Taking Property from Prisoner 

It is well settled that an officer making an arrest upon a criminal charge may take into his possession the instruments of the crime, and such other articles as may reasonably be used as evidence upon the trial. The officer not only has the lawful power to do so, but he would be blameworthy if he failed to do so. The maintenance of public order and the protection of society by efficient prosecution of criminals require it.¹

Neither an arresting officer nor the state acquires any title to property taken from a prisoner after he has been duly arrested and searched. A mere accusation does not justify the confiscation of the property of the prisoner, and an officer who in making an arrest searches the prisoner and takes money from him with the intention of keeping it may be guilty of larceny. Money is not, ordinarily, itself an instrumentality of gambling. Usually it is the stake or profit of gambling, not an instrumentality, device or apparatus for gambling.2 

1 Com. v. Pezzi, 284 Pa. 85; Com. v. Bassi, 284 Pa. 81; Com. v. Puntario, 271 Pa. 501, gun found near place of killing; Com. v. DuBoise, 269 Pa. 169; Com. v. Coles, 265 Pa. 362; Com. v. 

Scherer, 266 Pa. 210, letter; Com. v. O'Malley, 81 Pa. Super. 

100; Com. v. Exler, 61 Pa. Super. 423, clothing; Com. v. Eitler, 2 D. & C. 33; Com. v. Horbach, 15 Berks, Co., 265, liquor; Com. v. Heiland, 113 Pa. Super. 534; In re Petition Supt. Police, 113 

Pa. Super. 520, gambling machines; Com. v. Milloy, 70 P. L. J.

2 2 R. C. L. 467; Com. v. Brown, 16 Dist. Repts. 537, held money paid as bribe should be turned over to county treasurer; 

Rosen v Supt. of Police, 120 Pa. Super. 59, money returned because not shown to be stake. 

15. Reward

It is a general principal that it is the duty of peace officers to pursue and arrest offenders against whom they have warrants, and that it is against public policy to permit such

officers to take a reward for performance of such service,¹ but exceptions are recognized where the circumstances are such that it cannot be said that it is the duty of the officer to make the arrest, for example, where the officer was not charged by warrant to make the arrest, or where the arrest was made of a fugitive from justice.2 By statute a reward of twenty dollars is payable for the pursuit and apprehension of horse thieves.3

A reward is payable to one giving the information notwithstanding informant did not know a reward was offered.*

16. Finger Prints, Photographs

Pennsylvania State Police, the persons in charge of State penal institutions, the wardens or keepers of jails, prisons, and workhouses, and all police officers within the several political subdivisions of this Commonwealth, shall have the authority to take, or cause to be taken, the finger prints or photographs of any person in custody, charged with the commission of crime, or who they have reason to believe is a fugitive from justice or a habitual criminal. 

Act of April 27, 1927, P. L. 414, Sec. 3; 19 P. S. 1403.
