38. 

APPEAL FROM ORDER OF FORFEITURES 39. BAIL ON APPEAL BY COMMONWEALTH 40. SECURITY FOR GOOD BEHAVIOR, BAIL AFTER ACQUITTAL 1. Definition 

Bail is the security required and given for the release of a person who is in the custody of the law, or against whom a warrant has been issued or an information made, that he will appear before any court in which his appearance may be required and that he will do, or refrain from doing, such things as are stipulated in the bail-bond or recognizance. 

Uniform Code of Criminal Procedure, Am. Law Inst., Sec. 

61; Com. v. Miller, 105 Pa. Super. 56. 

2. Difference between Bail-Bond, Recognizance

The principal difference between a bail-bond and a recognizance lies in the fact that a recognizance is a matter of record in the nature of a conditional judgment, while a bond is but an evidence of debt. A bail-bond is taken out of court, and a recognizance is taken in open court. 

No form of action is prescribed for recovery on a recognizance. Debt may be used, or a proceeding by scire facias on the recognizance; of the two actions perhaps the scire facias on the recognizance is the more appropriate.¹ 

The record of a recognizance in the Quarter Sessions

may be made in one or other of several ways. It may be entered into and acknowledged in open court, which is the primary common law form of taking it. It may be taken in certain cases before a justice of the peace and certified into and filed in the Quarter Sessions. In such cases it is taken with the purpose and design of making it a part of the court's records, and such purpose and design and the procedure for executing them are authorized by statutory enactment. If taken on an occasion warranted by an act of assembly, it is deemed to be taken by an "officer duly authorized," within the sense of the definition of a recognizance, albeit that officer was not an officer of the court in which the record was lodged. In the third place, it may, pursuant to statute or an order of court, be taken by the clerk of the Court of Quarter Sessions, and, thereby become a part of the court's records. In all three cases we have what is regarded as an obligation of record, entered into before a court or officer duly authorized for that purpose, with condition to do some act required by law.2 

1 Com. v. Gillies, 3 Westmd. Co. 167, may be valid though not signed by principal or sureties; Com. v. Miller, 105 Pa. 

Super. 56; Com. v. Lammar, 32 Pa. Super. 200. 

2 Com. v. Blumberg, 56 Pa. Super. 589, forfeiture before justice; Com. v. Gillies, 3 Westmd. Co. 167. 

3. Who Entitled To 

All persons shall be bailable by sufficient sureties, unless for capital offenses when the proof is evident or presumption great.

Art. 1. Sec. 14, Const. Pa. 

4. Refusal, Reduction, of Bail

If the defendant is in confinement, the right to and amount of bail may be determined by a writ of habeas corpus, or, if the transcript has been returned, the amount may be reduced or increased on motion or application. 

Com. v. Skelly, 8 D. & C. 585; Com. v. Williams, 25 Lack. 

J. 240.

5. Arresting Officers to Issue Certificate. 

Stating Name, Charge, Amount of Bail 

Upon arrest of any person with or without a warrant charged with crime, in order to preserve and protect his, her, or their rights, and to facilitate the entry of bail for his, her or their appearance before a magistrate, coroner, judge or the court of Quarter Sessions and Oyer and Terminer, it shall be the duty of the sheriff, coroner, constable, police, or other official making the arrest, or having charge of the prisoner or prisoners, upon application made therefor either by counsel or a citizen, to issue, without cost to the applicant, a certificate stating the name of the prisoner or prisoners and the charge upon which he, she or they have been arrested and imprisoned, and the amount of bail demanded, if the same has been fixed, so that bail may be entered as authorized by law.1 

Should any sheriff, coroner, constable, police or other official who made such arrest or arrests, or having such prisoner or prisoners in charge, or any mayor or director of public safety of any municipal corporation of this Commonwealth, or subordinate thereof, by whose order or direction said prisoner or prisoners are held, fail, omit, neglect, or refuse to issue a certificate immediately upon demand therefor, he or they shall be guilty of a misdemeanor, and upon convic tion be sentenced to pay a fine of not more than five hundred dollars or imprisonment not exceeding one year.2 

1 Act of April 9, 1915, P. L. 76, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 54. 

2 Act of April 9, 1915, P. L. 76, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 55. 

6. Person on Bail Not to Be Placed in Criminal Bar

No person who may hereafter be arraigned on any indictment, and who shall be bound by recognizance to appear and abide by the judgment of the court, shall be placed within the prisoners' bar to plead to such indictment, or be confined therein during his trial, and all persons shall have an opportunity of a full and free communication with their counsel. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 34; 19 P. S. 56.

7. By Whom Taken 

Persons accused of arson, rape, mayhem, sodomy, buggery, burglary, shall be bailable only by the supreme court, the court of common pleas, or any of the justices thereof, or a mayor or recorder of a city. In all other cases the party accused shall be admitted to bail by one or more sufficient sureties, to be taken before any judge, justice, mayor, recorder, or alderman, where the offense charged has been committed.1

The clerk of the court of quarter sessions may take bail, excepting for treason, murder or voluntary manslaughter, where the judges are absent, sick or otherwise unable to attend to their duties.2

Persons accused of murder, or manslaughter shall be admitted to bail only by the supreme court or one of the judges thereof, or a judge of a court of common pleas. Any magistrate in any city of the first class shall have jurisdiction to release on bail any person committed for a hearing by any other magistrate.* 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 7; 19 P. S. 51; Carney's Petition, 3 D. & C. 690, justice may take bail after transscript is filed; Com. v. Lammar, 32 Pa. Super. 200. 

2 Com. ex rel. v. Smith, 96 Pa. Super. 31; Act of March 14, 1877, P. L. 3; 17 P. S. 415. 

3 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 7; 19 P. S. 51. 

4 Act of April 21, 1915, P. L. 145, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 52. 

8. Where Arrest is Made in Another County 

Where the offense is bailable by a justice of the peace, and the arrest is made in a county other than the one in which the offense was committed and warrant issued, bail can be taken in the other county only for the defendant's appearance at the next court in the county where the offense was committed. The bail-bond is returned to the clerk of the 

Quarter Sessions of the county in which the warrant was issued. 

Act of May 2, 1899, P. L. 173, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 3.

9. Bail on Sunday

The entry of a bail bond is an act of necessity and charity, being required to avoid imprisonment of a presumptively innocent defendant, and the execution of such an instrument on a Sunday does not therefore render it invalid as a violation of the statutes prohibiting secular contracts. 

Com. v. McEntee, 22 D. & C. 376; Com. v. Handley, 5 

D. & C. 716. 

10. Determining Amount

Excessive bail shall not be required, nor excessive fines imposed, nor cruel punishment inflicted.¹

In determining and adjusting bail, the test to be adopted by the court is the probability of the accused's appearing for trial. This probability is to be tested in part by the strength of the evidence against the defendant, in part by the nature of the crime charged, and by the severity of the punishment which may be imposed, and in part by the character and means of the defendant.2 

1 Art. 1, Sec. 12, Const. Penna. 

2 Com. v. Miller, 105 Pa. Super. 56; Com. v. Kardosh, 9 D. & C. 812; Com. v. Williams, 25 Lack. Co. 240. 

11. Sufficiency of Sureties

If there is only one surety he must be worth the amount specified in the bond exclusive of the amount of any other bond on which he may be principal or surety, and exclusive of property exempt from execution and over and above all liabilities; if there are several sureties they must in the aggregate be worth that amount exclusive of the amount of other bail bonds, and the exemptions and liabilities mentioned.¹

A married woman may not become accommodation indorser, maker, guarantor or surety for another.2

Rules of court usually provide that no attorney at law and no official authorized to admit to bail shall become bail. 

1 Uniform Code Criminal Procedure, Am. Law Inst., Sec. 

78; Com. v. Miller, 105 Pa. Super. 56. 

2 Act of June 8, 1893, P. L. 344, Sec. 2; 48 P. S. 32; Com. v. 

Harris, 11 D. & C. 1, minor has no right to return of cash bail.

12. Return, Recognizance 

Alderman and justices of the peace shall be required to return to the clerk of the court of quarter sessions all the recognizances entered into before them by any person or persons charged with the commission of any crime, excepting such cases as may be ended before an alderman or justice of the peace under existing laws, at least ten days before the commencement of the session of court to which they are made returnable respectively; and in all cases where any recognizances are entered into less than ten days before the commencement of the sessions to which they are made returnable, the said aldermen and justice of the peace are required to return the same, in the same manner, as if this act has not been passed. 

Act of May 8, 1854, P. L. 678; 42 P. S. 721; Phila., Act of 

March 30, 1821, P. L. 147; 53 P. S. 5991. 

13. Deposit with Magistrates

Current funds may be deposited by defendants with magistrates, justices of the peace, and alderman, upon the defendant or defendants entering his or their own recognizance. 

Act of April 7, 1921, P. L. 118, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 73. 

14. Making Return of Prosecution and Money 

Upon the binding over of the defendant or defendants, the magistrate, justice of the peace or alderman shall make an immediate return of the prosecution or proceedings to the proper court, and pay the money deposited in lieu of bail to the clerk of said court, taking a receipt therefore. 

Act of April 7, 1921, P. L. 118, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 74. 

15. Vehicle Code, Article of Value 

If the defendant is unable to give bail for violation of the provisions of the "Vehicle Code," for a hearing or for his appearance at court, the magistrate shall accept as bail any article of sufficient value, or, if the defendant is the

owner thereof, shall hold in custody the vehicle found in his possession, and the court or magistrate, after the trial of the defendant or when bail according to law has been given, shall make such order as to the disposition of such vehicle or other article accepted as bail as shall seem just and proper. 

Act of June 22, 1931, P. L. 751, Sec. 2; 75 P. S. 733; Act of 

June 22, 1931, P. L. 815, Sec. 2; 75 P. S. 1093, tractors. 

16. Surety of the Peace, Desertion, Forfeiture of Recognizance

All recognizances taken for the appearance of the defendant in surety of the peace and desertion cases shall be made forthwith, and, upon notice to the defendant of the time of hearing, may be forfeited for default of an appearance; and in case the defendant cannot be found, notice of the time of such hearing shall be given to the surety; and if neither can be found, and the defendant does not appear at the time fixed, the forfeiture shall be valid without service of notice upon either. 

Act of April 27, 1909, P. L. 260, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 28. 

17. Deposit, City or Borough Ordinance 

Money may be deposited as forfeit for appearance on arrest on view for the violation of a city or borough ordinance, the amount not to exceed the maximum fine that could be imposed. There shall be delivered to the person arrested a receipt stating the amount deposited, the purpose for which deposited, and the time and place fixed for hearing. 

The forfeit shall be returned without any deduction if the defendant appears. In case of failure to appear the amount posted shall be forfeited, and after registered notice of forfeiture, and the continued failure to appear for hearing within ten days, the money shall be paid in the city or borough treasury. 

Act of May 23, 1923, P. L. 339, Sec. 1, 2, 3, 4; 19 P. S. 75, 76, 77, 78.

18. Receipt, Record

Upon the posting of any such funds as a forfeit for his appearance at said hearing, there shall be delivered to the person so arrested a receipt for the amount so deposited, which shall plainly state thereon the amount deposited, the purpose for which deposited, and the time and place fixed for hearing; and the person receiving such deposit shall make a record of the posting thereof, and the name and address of the person posting the same. 

Act of May 23, 1923, P. L. 339, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 76. 

19. Return of Deposit

In case of the appearance of any person depositing such forfeit at the time fixed for said hearing, the amount so deposited shall be returned without deduction for any purpose whatever. 

Act of May 23, 1923, P. L. 339, Sec. 3; 19 P. S. 77; Com. v. 

Kormuth, 81 Pitts. L. J. 375. 

20. Forfeit and Disposition of Deposit

In case any person who has deposited such forfeit fails or neglects to appear at the time fixed for said hearing, the amount so posted shall be forfeited, and after notice of said forfeiture, mailed by registered mail to the address given by said person when arrested, and the continued failure or neglect of said person to appear for hearing within ten days after the mailing of said notice, the said sum shall be paid into the city or borough treasury for the use of said city or borough. 

Act of May 23, 1923, P. L. 339, Sec. 4; 19 P. S. 78. 

21. Money May Be Deposited in Courts of Record

In all criminal or quasi-criminal prosecutions, the party or parties required to give and enter bail or recognizances, or any other person or persons in their behalf, are allowed to deposit with the clerk of court, current funds in lieu of bail

or recognizance, upon the defendant's or defendants' entering his or their own bail or recognizance. 

Act of March 19, 1925, P. L. 49, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 71. 

22. Clerk to Furnish Receipt

The clerk shall furnish a receipt to the person or persons making the deposit, deposit the money in some national bank or trust company approved by the court, for the use of the party who may thereafter become entitled thereto, and upon order of court to pay it to the person depositing the same, less his commission of one half of one percent, with a minimum commission of three dollars. A record must be kept of the money receipted for and paid out. 

Act of March 19, 1925, P. L. 49, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 72. 

23. Set Off 

When a defendant depositing cash is sentenced to pay a fine and costs, the county can set off its claim against the cash bail,¹ otherwise when the surety makes the deposit.2 

1 Com. v. Coffin, 11 Leh. Co. 338. 

2 Gill's Petition, 6 D. & C. 662. 

24. Bail if Defendant Not Indicted at 

Next Term after Committed

If any person shall be committed for treason or felony, or other indictible offense, and shall not be indicted and tried some time in the next term after his commitment, the court having jurisdiction of the offense is required to release such person on bail on the last day of the term, unless it appears upon oath or affirmation the commonwealth's witnesses could not be produced. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 54; 19 P. S. 781; Com. v. Halderman, 299 Pa. 198. 

25. Bail after Arrest of Judgment

Where a judgment is arrested it is within the discretion of the court to release the defendant on his own recognizance

pending an appeal, and its action is not reversible except, perhaps, for a gross abuse of discretion. 

Com. v. Stetske, 69 Pa. Super., 15. 

26. Holding Witnesses to Bail

No witness in any case who enters his or her recognizance, in such sum as the magistrate may demand, to appear and testify in such prosecutions as require his testimony, shall be committed to prison by the judge, alderman or magistrate before whom any criminal charge may be preferred; 

Provided however, that in all cases triable in the oyer and terminer, where a positive oath is made, reduced to writing and signed by deponent, setting forth sufficient reasons or facts to induce the firm belief on the part of the judge, magistrate or alderman, that any witness will abscond, elope or refuse to appear upon the trial, that then and in that case the judge, magistrate or alderman may exact bail of said witness to testify.

A witness cannot be held to bail or committed to prison in default thereof, unless the case is exclusively triable in the court of oyer and terminer. (See Absconding Witness; See 

Court, Oyer and Terminer) 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 444, Sec. 56; 19 P. S. 651; Act of April 19, 1933, P. L. 50; 19 P. S. 652, provides for $3.00 per day compensation, payable upon release; Com. v. Mixitch, 2 D. & C. 31; Kramfert v. County, 25 Dist. Repts. 153. 

27. Security for Goods Probably Stolen 

When goods other than those described in a search warrant are found and there is probable cause supported by oath or affirmation to suspect that the goods so found are stolen goods, they may be taken into custody unless the person in whose possession they are found shall give sufficient surety to produce the goods at the time of trial. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 5; 19 P. S. 982. 

28. Court May Require Money as Additional Security 

Court of quarter sessions may require a proposed surety to pay into court a specified sum, not in lieu of bail for

the appearance of defendant, but as collateral to the surety's undertaking as bail if accepted. 

Com. ex rel. v. Smith, 96 Pa. Super., 31. 

29. Forfeiture for Failure to Appear

When an accused is required to appear during a certain term of court, the fact that no indictment is found against him does not relieve him from the necessity for such appearance. If he fails to appear, the recognizance is forfeited. whether or not there is an indictment,¹ and a return of "not a true bill" does not discharge the defendant.2

Where an accused is not produced for trial at the term to which he was bound over, and does not appear, the bail cannot take advantage of the default and claim exemption of liability for a failure on the part of the commonwealth to forfeit the recognizance at that term. 

1 Com. v. Iskoe, 289 Pa. 159. 

2 Com. v. Harvey, 36 Pa. Super. 235. 

30. Forfeiture, Respite, Continuance

Upon the continuance of the case, it is the duty of the court to have the recognizance renewed or a new one taken, or otherwise to commit the defendant to jail. The surety has bound himself only for the defendant's appearance at the next term, and his obligation does not extend to any subsequent term to which the case may be continued without his express consent,¹ and a forfeiture at a subsequent term is null and void.2

After forfeiture at the term for which given, the recognizance may be respited from term to term as this is for the benefit of the surety. Where the defendant does not appear at the term to which he is bound over, the bond is not required to be declared forfeited at that term. Where a defendant is required to appear during a certain term, and does not appear at that term, the bond or recognizance may be forfeited whether or not an indictment has been presented.5 

1 Mishler v. Com., 62 Pa. 55. 

2 Com. v. Czemerda, 16 West. Co. L. J. 51.

14 

3 Com. v. Blumberg, 56 Pa. Super., 589; Com. v. Czemerda,. 

16 Westmd. L. J. 51. 

4 Com. v. Iskoe, 289 Pa. 159. 

5 Com. v. Iskoe, 289 Pa. 159; Com. v. Blumberg, 56 Pa. 

Super. 589; Com. ex rel. v. Huntzinger, 10 D. & C. 99, bond F. and B., directors of the poor. 

31. Forfeited, Recognizance to Be Estreated

All fines and forfeited recognizances shall be certified and estreated by the clerk of courts into the office of the commissioners within ten days after the term at which such fines and forfeitures were imposed, together with the judgmentsand orders on all forfeited recognizances as shall be sued upon. 

Act of March 24, 1818, 7 Sm. L. 120, Sec. 1; 8 P. S. 176. 

32. Sued For in Common Pleas, May Be Remitted

All recognizances forfeited in the court of quarter ses-sions shall be sued for in the court of common pleas, and the common pleas court may order the recognizances to be levied, moderated or remitted, on hearing the circumstances of the case, according to equity and its discretion.¹ The statute of limitations does not apply.2 The governor has power to remit fines and forfeiture.3 

1 Act of Dec. 9, 1783, 2 Sm. L. 84, Sec. 2; 8 P. S. 171 appeal; 

Com. v. Blumberg, 56 Pa. Super., 589; Com. v. Limber, 24 Dist. 

Repts. 726; Com. v. Gillies, 3 Westmd. Co. 167; Com. v. Harvey, 222 Pa. 214. 

2 Com. to use v. Barrett, et al. 304 Pa. 13. 

3 Const. Pa. Art. IV, Sec. 9. 

33. When and How Forfeiture Will Be Remitted 

Either the court which forfeited the recognizance, or the one in which the suit on it is brought is "empowered to order the said recognizance to be levied, moderated or remitted." This cannot be done at the trial, but must be, if at all, as the result of the petition prescribed by the Act of Dec. 

9, 1783, 2 Sm. L. 84, Sec. 2, (8 P. S. 171).

At the trial of an action on the recognizance no evidence is admissible for the defense but what tends to show, either that the recognizance was not forfeited, or that it had been moderated or remitted by lawful authority. The reason for this is that a recognizance is a matter of record, and, when forfeited, it is in the nature, in some respects, of a judgment of record. The process on it, whether scire facias or summons, is rather judicial than original, and is for the purpose of carrying the judgment into effect.¹ 

A forfeited bond may, after arrest, conviction, and sentence, be remitted, but where money on a forfeited bond has been paid into the county treasury the forfeiture cannot be remitted or modified.2

Notice of petition to remit forfeiture should be given to the prosecutor. A forfeiture will be remitted where defendant was confined in another jurisdiction when the bond was forfeited.4 

1 Com. to use v. Barrett, et al. 304 Pa. 13; Com. v. Balsamo, 72 Pa. Super. 182; Com. v. Harvey, 36 Pa. Super. 235. 

2 Com. v. Drocton, 10 D. & C. 380. 

3 Com. v. Reed, 21 Del. 572; Com. v. Myers, 13 Lehigh 

Co. J. 110, but see Com. v. Goldberg, 5 D. & C. 59. 

4 Com. v. McMullin, 11 D. & C. 107. 

34. Surrender or Apprehension of Defendant 

In all cases where the county commissioners of any county shall collect, or have collected, any money upon any forfeited recognizance, duly estreated to such county commissioners by the clerk of the proper court, and the same has been paid into the county treasury, and where the defendant in such case subsequently surrenders himself or herself to the jurisdiction of the proper court, or is subsequently apprehended and returned to the jurisdiction of the court, the county commissioners may (a) with the consent of the district attorney, and (b) shall upon order of court in any case, repay to the party from whom such money was collected the amount so collected on such forfeited recognizance exclusive of all costs paid or incurred in such proceeding. 

Act of May 13, 1931, P. L. 131; 8 P. S. 171.

35. Distribution, Forfeited Recognizances 

When recognizances are forfeited for failure of persons to appear, and are not respited, it shall be lawful for any person having suffered loss or injury by the commission of the crime to institute suit in the name of the Commonwealth, and to proceed to final judgment thereon. Any money collected by execution or otherwise shall be taken into open court and distribution ordered; 1. 

Costs on judgment and magistrate's costs. 

2. Reasonable compensation for the person, his agent or attorney for prosecution to final judgment. 

3. Damages sustained, with interest, by reason of the commission of the crime. 

4. Residue to the county.

Auditors may be appointed to assess the amount of damages, and the decree of the court on the report shall be final and conclusive. The bond shall not be respited or cancelled without notice to complainant. 

Act of July 30, 1842, P. L. 449, Sec. 26; 8 P. S. 180; Com. v. 

Winter, 13 Berks. Co., 48, Phila. Co., Act of April 22, 1846, P. L. 

476, Sec. 6; 53 P. S. 6996; Com. v. Balsamo. 29 Dist. Repts. 581, bastardy; Com. v. Erdis, 19 Dist. Repts. 1; Com. v. Saltzman, 2 Leh. Co. 349, F. B.; Com. v. Deitrich, 2 Leh. Co. 170, fee private counsel; Com. v. Eckert, 50 Pa. C. C. 667, must prove crime was committed, medical services and wages not included in assault. 

36. Persons Injured May Bring Suit

Where recognizances have heretofore or shall hereafter be taken by any competent authority, for the appearance of a person charged with the commission of any crime or misdemeanor before any court, to answer to such charge or to appear before any judge, justice of the peace, alderman, or other officer for further hearing, and the said recognizance shall have been or may hereafter be forfeited on account of the person so charged failing to comply with the condition thereof, or where suits have been commenced for the purpose of recovering the amount of any recognizance heretofore forfeited, and not prosecuted to final judgment, it shall be lawful for any person having suffered loss or

injury by the commission of such crime or misdemeanor, by themselves, their agents or attorney, to institute suit in the name of the Commonwealth, or to prosecute any suit or action now pending and undetermined, and to proceed to final judgment thereon. 

Act of July 30, 1842, P. L. 449, Sec. 25; 8 P. S. 179; Com. v. 

Wenter, 8 West. Co., 9, fornication and bastardy. 

37. Bail Piece 

All sureties, mainpernors, and bail in criminal cases, whether bound in recognizance for a particular matter or for any charges whatsoever, shall be entitled to have a bail piece, duly certified by the proper officer or person before whom or in whose office the recognizance of such surety, mainpernors, or bail shall be or remain, and upon such bail piece, by themselves, or their agents, to arrest and detain, and surrender their principals, with the like effect as in case of bail in civil actions; and such bail piece shall be a sufficient warrant or authority for the proper sheriff or jailer to receive the said principal, and have him forthcoming to answer the matter or matters alleged against him. Provided, that nothing herein contained shall prevent the person thus arrested and detained from giving new bail or sureties for his appearance, who shall have the same right of surrender hereinbefore provided. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 8; 19 P. S. 53; Com. v. 

Blumberg, 56 Pa. Super. 589, forfeiture of recognizance before justice of the peace. 

38. Appeal from Order of Forfeiture 

An appeal from the order of the quarter sessions discharging a rule to show cause why the forfeiture of a recognizance should not be remitted and the judgment stricken off cannot be treated as an appeal under the Act of Dec. 9, 1783, 2 Sm. L. 84, but rather as a substitute for a certiorari, and any application to strike off the judgment can only be sustained on the ground of irregularity appearing on the record. 

Where there is a regular and formal forfeiture of a recognizance the liability of the recognizors is absolutely

fixed thereby, and relief therefrom should be sought by petition to the court to respite the recognizance for cause shown under the Act of Dec. 9, 1783, 2 Sm. L. 84 (8 P. S. 171).

In a suit on a recognizance the entry of a forfeiture stands for proof of all the steps necessary to complete the forfeiture. 

Com. v. Harvey, 36 Pa. Super. 235. 

39. Bail on Appeal by Commonwealth 

When the Commonwealth appeals from an order quashing an indictment, it is within the discretion of the court either to hold the defendant to bail, to hold him on his own recognizance, or discharge him without day. 

Com. v. Bartilson, et al. 85 Pa. 482. 

40. Security for Good Behavior, Bail after Acquittal

After an acquittal upon an indictment, the judge may, upon the evidence presented on the trial, require the defendant to find sureties for the peace and good behavior, and commit him until compliance with the order.¹

Surety for good behavior may be considered in two points of view. It is either required after conviction of some indictable offense, in which case it forms part of the judgment of the court, and is founded on a power incident to courts of record by the common law, or it is demanded by judges or justices of the peace out of court, before the trial of the person charged with an offense, in pursuance of an authority derived from a statute, made in the 34th year of Edward III. 

The statute of 34th Edward III, ch. 1, authorizes justices of the peace "to take of all them that be not of good fame, where they shall be found, sufficient surety and mainprize of their good behavior towards the king, and his people, . . . to the intent that the people be not by such rioters or rebels troubled or endangered, nor the peace blemished, nor merchants, nor others passing by the highway of the realm disturbed, nor put in the peril which may happen to such offenders". The natural meaning of the words "Persons

not of good fame" seems to be, those who by their general evil course and habits of life had acquired a bad reputation, and were supposed to be dangerous to the community. 

Surety for good behavior is more extensive in its nature than surety for the peace, and may be more easily forfeited, and therefore should be exercised with greater caution. 

Under the general words of the statute it has been held that a man may be bound to good behavior for causes of scandal, contra bonos mores, as well as contra pacem; as, for haunting bowdyhouses with women of bad fame, or for keeping such women in one's own house; or for words tending to scandalize the government or abuse the officers of justice, especially in the execution of their office. 

The justice of the peace is vested with a discretionary power to take such surety of all those whom he shall have just cause to suspect to be dangerous, quarrelsome, or scandalous, as of those who sleep in the day, and go abroad in the night, and of such as keep suspicious company and of such as are generally suspected to be robbers. 

The discretion, however, is not an arbitrary one, but it is to be exercised according to the law of the land. One repeatedly guilty of operating a store (in an orderly manner) on Sunday in violation of the Act of April 22, 1794, 3 Sm. L. 

177, may not be required to give security for good behavior.2 

1 Bamber v. Com. 10 Pa. 339; Com. v. Andrews, 211 Pa. 110. 

2 Com. v. Foster, 28 Pa. Super. 400. 19

HEARING ON SUNDAY NO COSTS TO BE PAID BY INNOCENT DEFENDANTS 17. COSTS, DISCHARGED CASES 1. The Right to a Preliminary Hearing 

The right to a preliminary hearing is only set aside in extraordinary cases, as where the crime charged is of a grave public nature, or the defendant in the indictment is a fugitive from justice. In these cases the attorney general of the state, or the district attorneys of the various counties, have a right with the permission of the court to present a bill to the grand jury without a preliminary hearing.¹

In absence of some pressing and adequate necessity, it is improper for the district attorney to send an indictment before the grand jury without a preliminary hearing, or the sanction of the court. Where the indictment is sent up by 62

the district attorney without first obtaining leave of the court, the discretion of the court may be invoked, and is exercisable upon a motion to quash.2 

1 Com. v. McKeehan, 93 Pa. Super., 348; Com. v. Strauss, 89 Pa. Super. 82; Com. v. McCormick, 71 Pa. Super., 567; 

Com. v. Rothensies, 64 Pa. Super., 395; Com. v. Clutter, 17 

Dist. Repts., 160; Com. v. McElroy, 45 Pa. C. C. 681. 

2 Com. v. Feurstein, 98 Pa. Super., 201; Com. v. Sheppard, 20 Pa. Super. 417, quashed; Charge to the Grand Jury, 62 Pitts. 

L. J. 105; Com. v. Clutter, 17 Dist. Repts. 160; Com. v. Sykes, 10 Leh. Co. 237, quashed. 

2. Time of Hearing 

There is no precise length of time, after the arrest of the defendant, within which he must be given a hearing. The theory of the law is that he shall have a hearing immediately. 

In many cases this is impossible in practice, inasmuch as the Commonwealth's witnesses may not be present when the defendant is arrested and first brought to the office of the justice. All that can be said is that the defendant must be given a preliminary hearing just as soon as the nature and circumstances of the particular case will permit. 

Com. v. Sweetlick, 19 Dist. Repts. 397. 

3. Defendant May Waive, Commonwealth May Hold

While the defendant in a criminal prosecution may waive a hearing before the magistrate, so far as his interests are concerned, he cannot by doing so interfere with the right of the Commonwealth to institute such preliminary examination. The rules of public justice may imperatively require such preliminary investigation, and as many meetings and hearings may be held as public justice requires.¹ Entering bail for court amounts to a waiver of preliminary hearing.2

Plea of nolo contendere cannot be entered before a magistrate.3 

1 Com. v. Keck, 148 Pa. 639; Com. v. Maimieri, 25 Luz. 

L. R. 107. 

2 Com. v. Rogowski, 23 Luz. Co. 368. 

3 City of Scranton v. Evans, 72 Pitts. L. J. 19.

LAPO 

4. Fugitives

When a defendant in an indictment is shown by the transcript of the justice to be a fugitive from justice, the indictment will not be quashed for want of preceding arrest and preliminary hearing. 

Com. v. Rogowski, 23 Luz. Co. 368. 

5. Hearing, Defense Witnesses 

If demanded, the person accused and all his witnesses, shall be heard, except when the charge is murder, manslaughter, arson rape, mayhem, sodomy, buggery, robbery, or burglary. 

Act of May 14, 1915, P. L. 499, Sec. 1; 42 P. S. 1080. 

6. Assault and Battery, Costs

In cases of assault and battery the magistrate shall enter into a full hearing, and shall only bind over the defendant when he is satisfied that the prosecution is well founded. The transcript should show that a full hearing was entered into.

If the evidence does not show that the prosecution was well founded the magistrate shall discharge the defendant and determine by whom the costs shall be paid. He may order that the prosecutor or the defendant pay all, or any part thereof, or may assess them on the county. In default of payment the person defaulting shall be committed to the county jail, one day for each dollar of costs, and in such cases the costs shall be paid by the county.¹ It has been held that the provision placing costs on discharged defendants is unconstitutional.2 

The defendant may waive a hearing and give bail for court, but this does not prevent the magistrate holding a hearing for the purpose of hearing the Commonwealth witnesses. 

1 Act of May 27, 1919, P. L. 306, Sec. 1, 2, 19 P. S. 21, 22; 

Com. v. Marawski, 101 Pa. Super. 430, too late after indictment found to raise the question as to a "full hearing and investigation".

2 Com. ex rel. v. Bossler, 29 Dist. Repts. 171; Com. v. 

Maimieri, 25 Luz. Co. L. R. 107. 

3 Com. v. Keeleher, 25 Dauph. 343; Com. v. Grenda, 94 Pa. 

Super, 595, 598. 

7. Surety of the Peace, Information

If any person shall threaten the person of another to wound, kill or destroy him, or do him any harm in person or estate, and the person threatened shall appear before a justice of the peace, and attest, on oath or affirmation, that he believes that by such threatening he is in danger of being hurt in body or estate, such person so threatening as aforesaid shall be bound over, with one sufficient surety, to appear at the next sessions, according to law, and in the meantime to be of his good behavior, and keep the peace towards all citizens of this commonwealth. If any person, not being an officer on duty in the military or naval services of the state or of the United States, shall go armed with a dirk, dagger, sword or pistol, or other offensive or dangerous weapon, without reasonable cause to fear an assault or other injury or violence to his family, person or property, he may, on complaint of any person having reasonable cause to fear a breach of the peace therefrom, be required to find surety of the peace as aforesaid. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 6; 19 P. S. 23. 

8. Surety of the Peace, Hearing, Return 

In a prosecution before a justice of the peace, the justice is required to enter into a full hearing and investigation of the facts; if he fails to do so, and his transcript shows that he only heard the witnesses for the Commonwealth, and not witnesses for the defendant, the proceedings will be quashed. 

A justice must enter into a full hearing before he binds a defendant over in a surety of the peace case, and shall only bind over when the evidence shows that the prosecutor's danger of being hurt in body or estate is actual and that the threats were made maliciously and with the intent to do harm, and in case of a discharge to place the costs on the

prosecutor or the defendant or apportion them between the two, and may commit in default to the county jail. 

The provision relating to disposition of costs has been. held unconstitutional.¹

It is the duty of the justice of the peace to afford an opportunity and to suggest to the parties the propriety of compromising their differences before entering into a hearing.2

If the magistrate shall determine to return the proceeding insteal of being made to the next court of quarter session, the return shall be filed immediately. 

A justice's record of a surety of peace case must show: (a) That the justice suggested to the parties the propriety of compromising their differences; (b) A full hearing and investigation of facts, and (c) That there was actual danger of the prosecutor's being hurt in body or estate and threats were made by defendant to do harm.³ 

1 Com. v. Webster, 23 Luz. 359; Com. v. Reynolds, 26 

Lack. 133. 

2 Act of March 18, 1909, P. L. 42, Sec. 1, 2, 3; 19 P. S. 

23, 28. 

3 Com. v. Hooks, 23 D. & C. 206. 

9. Holding for Court, Misdemeanors and Felonies

If it appears that there is probable cause to believe that the accused is guilty of the offense charged in the information the magistrate must hold him to appear at the next term of court. Surety of the peace and desertion cases shall be returned immediately. 

Act of April 27, 1909, P. L. 260, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 27. 

10. Notice of Time of Trial 

In cases returned for presentation to a grand jury the magistrate shall note on the transcript the names and addresses of any persons whom the defendant wishes notified of the time of trial. The district attorney is obliged to mail

postal card notices of the time to at least one of the named. 

Act of July 10, 1935, P. L. 643. 

11. Review of Testimony by Court, Habeas Corpus

A defendant who has been committed to prison, after hearing, has the right to a common law writ of habeas corpus for the purpose of having the evidence taken at the hearing reheard by the court issuing the writ. The court will hear testimony of witnesses and ascertain the fact as to whether or not a crime has been committed for which the defendant should be held. 

12. Returns, Felonies, Surety of the Peace, Cash Bail persons Com. v. Keeper, 26 Dist. Repts. 511; Com. ex rel. v. Skelly, 8 D. & C. 585, on statutory writ; Scholfield v. Root, 12 Phila. 

33; Com. ex rel. v. Ransley, 26 Dist. Repts. 1043; Com. ex rel. 

Goodman v. Keeper, 19 Dist. Repts. 6.

Alderman and justice of the peace must return a true transcript from the docket within five days after the binding over in felony cases,¹ and immediately in surety of the peace and desertion cases,2 and when cash bail is taken.3

The name, residence and occupation of all defendants, bail and witnesses are to be returned, also names of persons whom defendant wishes notified of the time of trial.4 

1 Act of June 11, 1885, P. L. 110, Sec. 1; 42 P. S. 722; 

In Phila. 48 Hrs., Act of March 13, 1867, P. L. 420, Sec. 5; 

17 P. S. 481; returns made to Dist. Atty. in Philadelphia, Com. v. Donnelly, 5 D. &. C. 657.

13. Misdemeanor 

See Bail, Return of Recognizance. Return must be made immediately when cash bail is taken for court. 

Act of April 7, 1921, P. L. 118, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 74.

14. Return May Be Made to Succeeding Term 

A committing magistrate may bind over a defendant, and make return of the proceeding, to a term of court then pending or to the next term,¹ or it seems, to a term succeeding the next term, where notice is given.2 

1 Com. v. Magid, 91 Pa. Super. 513, Aff. 9 D. & C. 81. 

2 Com. v. Hollinger, 9 D. & C. 380; Com. v. Lowell, 6 

D. & C. 290. 

15. Hearing on Sunday

Judicial as distinguished from ministerial acts cannot lawfully be performed by a court on Sunday, but as to all offenses for which an arrest may be made on Sunday a hearing may also be had. 

Com. v. Hendley, 5 D. & C. 716. 

16. No Costs to Be Paid by Innocent Defendants 

Where any person shall be brought before a court, justice of the peace, or other magistrate of any city or county in this commonwealth, having jurisdiction in the case, on the charge of being a runaway servant or slave, or of having committed a crime, and such charge upon examination, shall appear to be unfounded, no costs shall be paid by such innocent person, but the same shall be chargeable to and paid out of the county stock, by such city or county. 

Act of Sept. 23, 1791, 3 Sm. L. 37, Sec. 13; 19 P. S. 1221; 

Com. v. Kane, 65 Pa. Super. 258. 

17. Costs, Discharged Cases

No county shall be hereafter liable for the payment of any costs in any prosecution brought before any magistrate, alderman, or justice of the peace against any person on the charge of having committed any crime when such charge shall, upon examination at a preliminary hearing, appear to be unfounded and shall be dismissed or discharged by such magistrate, alderman or justice of the peace and the costs thereof directed to be paid by the county, unless there shall

be endorsed upon the transcript of such proceedings the names and specific addresses of the prosecutor, the defendant or defendants, and of all the witnesses who were called and sworn and who testified at such hearing or examination (Sec. 1).

It is unlawful for the county commissioner or the controller of any county to order, authorize, or approve the payment of any costs in any such proceedings unless the transcript thereof shall contain the name and specific address of the prosecutor, the defendant or defendants, and all witnesses, as required by the proceeding section of this act (Sec. 2). Costs are not payable by county in cases of summary conviction unless so provided by statute. 

Act of May 3, 1933, P. L. 242, Sec. 1, 2; 19 P. S. 1235; 

Dougherty v. County, 26 Pa. Super. 610; Com. v. Kane, 65 Pa. 

Super. 258; Seiders v. Franklin Co., 6 Dist. Repts. 787, trespassing on train, county not liable; Price v. Lancaster Co., 24 Pa. C. C. 225.

CERTIORARI, DEPOSITIONS REARREST AFTER DISCHARGE 13. APPEAL TO SUPERIOR COURT 14. APPEAL BY DISTRICT ATTORNEY 15. HEARING MAY BE WAIVED UNDER AUTOMOBILE CODE 

1. Jurisdiction, Magistrates, Aldermen, Justices

In all cases in which an act of Assembly defines an offense, and renders it punishable upon summary conviction, or summary proceeding, but without conferring jurisdiction thereof upon any court, either of record or not of record, jurisdiction is hereby conferred upon any magistrate, alderman, or justice of the peace within any county in which any such offense is committed, to hear and dispose of a prosecution and impose the penalties provided therefor, subject to the right of appeal as in other cases.¹

Where an offense, not indictable at common law, is created, it is entirely competent for the legislature to determine the mode in which the guilt or innocence of one charged should be determined. The use of the word "misdemeanor," in the act, does not necessarily mean that the crime defined was an offense indictable at common law. The law of Penn- 70

sylvania does not attach to the legislative use of the word misdemeanor such an exclusive meaning that it may not include, if the legislature so wills, petty offenses which may properly be tried before a subordinate magistrate.2 

1 Act of April 26, 1929, P. L. 824 ; 42 P. S. 391. 

2 Allen v. Com., 77 Pa. Super. 244, cruelty to animals. 

2. The Information 

The complaint must contain a direct and positive charge against the defendant; it is the foundation of the proceeding and the jurisdictional facts must appear on its face by a plain statement of them (See Return). 

Com. v. Phelps, 170 Pa. 430; City of Johnstown v. Perry, 77 Pitts. L. J. 280; Com. v. Kane, 65 Pa. Super. 258. 

3. Magistrate May Collect Fine Where 

Another Magistrate Has Committed 

A person committed by an alderman, magistrate, or justice of the peace to a county prison, or city or borough or township lockup, in default of payment of a fine and costs, may pay such fine and costs to any duly elected alderman, magistrate, or justice of the peace of said county, and shall be discharged and released by the warden or keeper of any prison, stationhouse, or any place wherein said defendant is incarcerated, upon the presentation of a discharge signed and sealed by the said alderman, or justice of the peace. 

Act of June 4, 1915, P. L. 845, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 952. 

4. Appeals

In all cases of summary conviction, or in judgment in suit for a penalty before a magistrate, or court not of record, either party may appeal to such court of record as may be prescribed by law upon allowance of the appellate court or judge thereof upon cause shown.

Art. V. Sec. 14, Const. Pa. P. S. Const.; Com. v. Luckey, 31. 

Pa. Super. 441.

LAP 

5. Appeal within Five Days, Bail

In all cases of summary conviction in this commonwealth before a magistrate or court not of record, either party, even though any fine imposed has already been paid, may, within five days after such conviction, appeal to the court of quarter sessions of the county, upon allowance of said court, or any judge thereof, upon cause shown; and either party may also appeal from the judgment of a magistrate or court not of record, in a suit for a penalty, to the court of common pleas, upon allowance of said court, or any judge thereof, upon. cause shown, provided, that pending the taking of an appeal by either party, or the allowance or refusal thereof by the court or judge, the fine or penalty, and costs imposed by the magistrate or court not of record, need not be paid if bail is entered with one or more sufficient sureties in double the amount of such fine, or penalty, and costs, for the payment thereof, on the refusal of such appeal; or if allowed, on the final disposal of such appeal. If the defendant pays the fine or penalty and costs imposed and wishes to take an appeal he shall give bail in double the probable amount of costs that may accrue in the final disposition of the appeal. 

Act of April 1, 1925, P. L. 98, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 1189 (cases digested); Com. v. Zurn, 7 D. & C. 277; Com. v. Peacock, 118 

Pa. Super. 168, appeal after five days, hearing held. 

6. Allowance

Appeal from summary conviction before a magistrate or court not of record should not be allowed save for cause shown. To ascertain the cause, reference must be had to the petition presented. Ordinarily an appeal should not be permitted, if the party desiring it has had an opportunity to fully and fairly present his case before the magistrate, unless a doubtful legal question is involved or there is something to indicate oppression, corruption, disregard of law, unfairness, partiality, errors of law on the part of the magistrate, or after discovered evidence which would justify a new trial.¹

While the time to consider and determine whether there is such probable cause for the appeal as would justify a re-

trial of the case is before the appeal is allowed, the court may, upon cause shown, vacate or strike off the appeal.2 

It is doubtful if one judge has the authority to review the discretion of another member of the court and vacate or strike off an appeal allowed by him. A petition or motion to strike off or vacate the appeal allowed by one judge should be considered by him or by the court in banc.3 

1 Art. V. Sec. 14, Const. Pa.; Com. v. Long, 276 Pa. 154; 

Com. v. MacDonald, 94 Pa. Super., 486; Com. v. Climenti, 89 

Pa. Super., 195; Com. v. Luckey, 31 Pa. Super., 441; Thompson v. Preston, 5 Pa. Super. 154; Com. v. Serawicz, 23 Luz. Co. 47. 

2 Com. v. MacDonald, 94 Pa. Super. 486. 

3 Com. v. MacDonald, 94 Pa. Super. 486. 

7. Proceedings on Appeal 

When the appeal has been allowed, the charge or cause of action remains the same, but the proceedings to determine the guilt or innocence of the defendant are de novo. The parties are entitled to a hearing upon such relevant and material evidence pertaining to the charge as they produce and to the judgment of the court based on the facts established thereby, and the law applicable to those facts. The judgment should be "Guilty" or "Not Guilty." 

Com. v. Congdon, 74 Pa. Super. 286; Com. v. Fischel, 99 

Pa. Super. 352; Com. v. Peacock, 118 Pa. Super. 168; Com. v. 

Pahlman, 118 Pa. Super. 175. 

8. Appeals, Certiorari, Practice 

The practice relating to appeals from summary convictions and the review of summary convictions on certiorari is well settled. If it is desired to attack only the regularity of the proceedings before the justice or alderman, this is done by writ of certiorari to the court of common pleas, and the assignment of the alleged errors relied on, in which event the judgment of the inferior magistrate is sustained or set aside depending on whether the proceedings objected to are found to be legal and regular or not, and whether they sustain the conviction or not. But if an appeal is asked for this is directed to the court of quarter sessions and if allowed, the

12 case is heard de nova before a judge of that court. In such case, the court of quarter sessions does not sustain or reverse the judgment of the inferior magistrate; it does not review such judgment or the matters alleged as ground for appeal. 

It finds the defendant either guilty or not guilty, it either convicts or acquits him. The judgment on writs of certiorari from a summary conviction before an alderman or justice of the peace is to sustain or set aside the judgment; on appeals from such conviction the judgment is "guilty" or "not guilty" conviction or acquittal. 

Com. v. Fischel, 99 Pa. Super, 352; Com. v. Benson, 94 

Pa. Super. 10; Com. v. Beatty, 91 Pa. Super., 37; Com. v. 

Clark, 75 Pitts. L. J. 612, hearing de nova on appeal. 

9. Special Allowance, Certiorari 

A special allowance from the court of common pleas must be had for a writ of certiorari in a summary conviction, but the writ may be allowed nunc pro tunc. 

Com. v. Barbono, 56 Pa. Super. 637; Com. v. Butler, 39 

Pa. Super., 125; Com. v. Antone, 22 Pa. Super., 412; Com. v. 

Wissler, 24 Lanc. 118. 

10. Return

It is essential that a summary conviction shall contain a finding that a special act has been performed by the defendant; and that it shall describe or define it, in such a way as to individuate it, and show that it falls within such an unlawful class of acts, without this a judgment that the law has been violated goes for naught. 

Com. v. Barden, 61 Pa. 272; Com. v. Barbono, 56 Pa. Super. 

637; Com. v. Divoskein, 49 Pa. Super. 614. 

11. Certiorari, Depositions

On the question of allowing depositions in cases of certiorari the general rule is that the court is not to look beyond the transcript. However, the cases are numerous where, to prevent injustice, evidence by deposition is allowed. Deposi-

tions may be heard to show fraud on the part of the magistrate; to show partiality, and to show a false record. 

City of Scranton, v. Evans, 72 Pitts. L. J. 19. 

12. Rearrest After Discharge

In summary proceedings where the defendant is convicted, and prior to having served his sentence the sentence is reversed, and the defendant is discharged on a habeas corpus, he may be rearrested and retried, where: 

1. The justice or other trial officer has no jurisdiction. 

2. The information was not sufficient to sustain a conviction. 

3. Such reversal or discharge was occasioned by improper procedure, such as an insufficient record or transcript, etc. The first and second reasons are also sufficient to justify a rearrest and retrial of the defendant even where the defendant on the hearing was acquitted. 

Summary Hearings, 44 Pa. C. C. 538. 

13. Appeal to Superior Court

On appeal the superior court will not retry the case, but determine whether there is evidence to sustain conviction and whether the law has been applied properly.¹ An order which merely affirms the judgment of the magistrate without an opinion or finding will be reversed.2

The provision in section 22 of the Act of March 20, 1810, Sm. L. 161, 172, (42 P. S. 957) that the judgment of the court of common pleas shall be final on all proceedings removed to it by certiorari, and that no writ of error shall issue thereon, only applies to the jurisdiction given by the act and not to summary convictions. Hence an appeal properly lies to the superior court, but will operate only as a certiorari, and the review will be limited to such assignments of error as were filed in the court below by way of exceptions to the proceeding before the alderman.3 

1 Com. v. Cooper, 95 Pa. Super. 382; Com. v. Benson, 94 

Pa. Super. 10; Com. v. Devenney, 103 Pa. Super. 83.

1.48 

2 Com. v. Sesso, 95 Pa. Super. 552; Com. v. Benson, 94 

Pa. Super. 10; Com. v. Congdon, 74 Pa. Super. 286. 

3 Com. v. Beatty, 91 Pa. Super. 37; Com. v. Climenti, 89 

Pa. Super. 195; Com. v. Burkhart, 23 Pa. 521. 

14. Appeal by District Attorney 

No one but the attorney general or the district attorney may appeal on behalf of the commonwealth to the superior court from a judgment reversing a summary conviction. 

Com. v. Benson, 94 Pa. Super., 10; Com. v. Peacock, 118 

Pa. Super. 168; Com. v. Pahlman, 118 Pa. Super. 175. 

15. Hearing May Be Waived under Automobile Code 

Any person charged with violating any of the summary provisions of the act may waive summary hearing, and give bond in a sum equal to double the amount of the fine and costs that might be imposed, for appearance for trial before a judge of the court of quarter sessions, or in the county court, or the municipal court, in counties wherein such court exists, and thereupon the magistrate shall, within fifteen (15) days, return the information to the said court. 

Act of June 22, 1931, P. L. 751, Sec. 2, 75 P. S. 734.

CHALLENGE 14. IMPOSING COSTS MAY MEET PRIOR TO TERM, HOLDING OVER COMPOSITION, TRUE BILL 1. Oyer and Terminer and Quarter Sessions 

The court of oyer and terminer and the court of quarter sessions have the power to inquire by the oaths and affirmations of good and lawful men of the county, of all crimes. committed or triable in the county. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 31; 17 P. S. 391; 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 32; 17 P. S. 361. 

2. Drawing

The jury commissioners and the sheriff of the respective county, or any two of them shall draw from the proper jury wheel, panels of jurors, as grand jurors of the proper county, and as petit and traverse jurors, for the trial of issues of fact which may be taken in any action, in any of the courts, civil and criminal, of the several counties, in the manner now practiced and allowed; but before the said jury commissioners and sheriff shall proceed to select or draw jurors in the manner aforesaid, they shall severally take the oath ما 77

or affirmation now prescribed by law to be taken by the sheriff and the county commissioners before selecting and drawing jurors. 

Act of April 10, 1867, P. L. 62, Sec. 3; 17 P. S. 934. In

Philadelphia a grand jury is summoned each month, Act of 

March 13, 1867, P. L. 420, Sec. 2; 17 P. S. 472. 

3. Powers 

The grand jury can only act upon and present offenses of public notoriety and such as are within their own knowledge, such as are given them in charge by the court, and such as are sent to them by the district attorney, and in no other cases can they indict without a previous hearing before a magistrate, according to the terms of the Bill of Rights. 

Com. v. Green, 126, Pa., 531 presentment; McCullegh_v. 

Com., 67 Pa. 30, constable's return; Com. v. Strauss, 89 Pa. 

Super. 82, district attorney's bill; Com. v. Klein, 40 Pa. Super. 

352, Court may direct grand jury to investigate crime; Com. v. Dietrich, 7 Pa. Super. 515. 

4. May Meet Prior to Term, Holding Over 

The grand jury may be summoned to meet prior to the holding of the term and may be held over until the grand jury for the next succeeding term is assembled, and may be called to assemble in session without the issuing of a new venire.1 

An indictment will be quashed where it is found by a grand jury summoned for a later term than that named in the defendant's recognizance, where the record shows no notice of or consent to the presentation by the defendant.2 

1 Act of April 27, 1927, P. L. 420, Sec. 2; 17 P. S. 1351; 

Com. v. Kenehan, 30 Lack. J., 58. 

2 Com. v. Hollinger, 9 D. & C. 380, and cases cited. 

5. Composition, True Bill

A grand jury is composed of not less than twelve nor more than twenty-three, and talesmen shall be summoned only when the number is reduced below the minimum num-

ber required to make a legally constituted jury.¹ Unless at least twelve of the grand jury agree to a true bill it will be ignored.2 

1 Com. v. Little, 42 Pa. C. C. 536. 

2 Charge to Grand Jury, 62 P. L. J. 105; Com. v. Dietrich, 7 Pa. Super. 515. 

6. Finding Indictments, Probable Cause 

Probable cause as the basis of finding a true bill, is a reasonable ground of suspicion sufficient to warrant an ordinarily prudent man in believing the accused guilty of the charge. A reasonable ground of suspicion is based, not upon a guess, but upon facts from which guilt may reasonably be inferred. 

Charge to the Grand Jury, 62 Pitts. L. J. 105. 

7. Ignoring Indictments 

A bill may not be ignored until all the witnesses whose names are endorsed on the indictment have been called and examined. 

Com. v. Lute, 2 D. & C. 224 (West). 

8. Habeas Corpus, Indictment

If the judge at the hearing on a habeas corpus finds in favor of the defendant, the grand jury is bound by his finding, and when the grand jury find against the defendant, the judge should defer to their finding, as the grand jury is a constituent part of the court. 

Com. v. McCarthy, 11 Dist. Repts., 161. 

9. Who May Be Present

In the presentation of indictments no one shall come before the grand jury during their examination except witnesses (one at a time), the district attorney or an assistant, stenographer, and interpreter. During the deliberation of the grand jury upon any indictment, no outsider should be present, not even the district attorney.¹ Probation officers of

juvenile courts may attend any session at which a male minor under the age of sixteen or any female minor is present.2 

1 Maginnis's Case, 269 Pa. 186; Com. v. Garletts, 81 Pa. 

Super. 271; Com. v. Stetzka, 26 Dist. Repts., 460, quashed; interpreter also witness, affirmed, 69 Pa. Super. 15; all jurors must be present when bill being considered, Com. v. Bradney, 126 Pa. 199; Com. v. Hagdus, 44 Pa. Super. 157. 

2 Act of July 11, 1923, P. L. 994; 11 P. S. 103. 

10. Duty of District Attorney 

The duty of the district attorney is to see that the evidence of the prosecution is sufficiently produced, and to give the jurors such general instructions on the law as they may require of him. Where, after drawing all inferences in favor of the commonwealth, the evidence fails, in his opinion, to show a prima facie case against an accused, but a true bill is found, he may ask leave to enter a nol pros. He should not be present at the deliberations of the grand jury nor express an opinion on the evidence to the jurors.¹ 

It is for the grand jury alone to consider the evidence and to apply it to the case in hand; and any attempt on the part of the district attorney to influence their action or to give effect to the evidence adduced, is in the highest degree improper and impertinent. 

Maginnis's Case, 269 Pa. 186; Com. v. Bradney, 126 Pa. 199. 

11. Jurors Setting Aside Findings 

No member of the grand jury can be permitted to testify how he or any member of the grand jury voted, nor what opinion any of them expressed in relation thereto, nor the act of either which might invalidate the finding of the grand jury. His action and the actions of his fellow jurors, must be shown only by the return which they make to the court.¹ 

The rule forbidding disclosure by members of the grand jury of their official proceedings does not apply to the disclosure of the testimony of a witness who is asked concerning contradictory statements made before the grand jury.2

1 Com. v. Gerstman, 64 Pa. Super., 484; Com.,v,, Klee, 12 

D. & C. 203; Com. v. Fennix, 6 D. & C. 15; Com. v. Hastings, 19 Del. Co. 81. 

2 Com. v. Fotti, 93 Pa. Super. 365; Com. v. Campbell, 116 

Pa. Super. 180. 

12. Resubmission of Bill

Grand jurors, for some purpose and to a certain extent, constitute a part of the court. If there returns are in proper form and there is no evidence of misconduct or irregularity, or no allegation or proof of oversight, mistake or fraud, and no grave emergency or public need require it, a bill should not be resubmitted to a grand jury, nor a new one to a subsequent grand jury. 

Com. v. Andruchek, 26 Dist. Repts. 923; Com. v. Priestly, 10 Dist. Repts. 217; Com. v. Allen, 14 P. C. C. 546. 

13. Challenge to Array

The array of grand jurors may be challenged before or after the jurors are sworn, or by motion to quash the indictment before plea entered. 

Roland v. Com. 82 Pa. 306; Brown v. Com. 78 Pa. 321; 

Com. v. Magid, 91 Pa. Super. 513; Com. v. Haines, 57 Pa. 

Super. 616; Com. v. Daugherty, 42 Pa. C. C. 305; Commonwealth may challenge, 91 Pa. Super. 229; seals, custody of wheel and key, Kittaning Ins. Co. v. Adams, 110 Pa. 553, array quashed. 

14. Imposing Costs

In all prosecutions, cases of felony excepted, if the bill of indictment shall be returned ignoramus, the grand jury returning the same shall decide and certify on such bill whether the county or the prosecutor shall pay the costs of prosecution.1

Before a grand jury is permitted to impose costs upon a person who is not a prosecutor of record, the said person must have testified as a witness before the grand jury. As the hearing in a particular case progresses, if it should appear that another than the nominal prosecutor is the real prosecutor, the grand jury may then forthwith have summoned the

LAP 

apparent real prosecutor before it, and take his testimony; and after all the summoned witnesses are heard, the grand jury must then dispose of the costs in accordance with the facts and the law and, if necessary, may determine whether the prosecutor shall pay the costs and, who the real prosecutor is. 2

In all prosecutions for assault or assault and battery where the prosecutor has made complaint, under oath, that the assault was made with intent to maim, disfigure or disable such prosecutor or other person, or to commit the crime of murder, or where the bill of indictment in cases of assault or assault and battery charges the defendant with a felony, if the bill of indictment shall be returned ignoramus, the grand jury returning the same, if they shall believe from the evidence that the prosecutor had no reasonable ground for making the charge of felony, shall decide and certify on such bill whether the county or the prosecutor shall pay the costs of prosecution. 3

2 Com. v. Himler, 19 West. Co. 25, no justice of the peace costs to be paid. 

3 Act of May 25, 1897, P. L. 89, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 1228. 1 ..
