1. Two Alternate Jurors

The trial judge has power to allow the drawing of two additional jurors to act as alternates where in his opinion the trial is likely to be protracted, and the district attorney or counsel for the defendant so requests. The alternates do not retire with the jury of twelve after the case is submitted 83

unless one or two of the jurors shall have died or, in the opinion of the trial judge, become ill so as to be unable to perform their duty. When two alternates are drawn each side shall have one additional peremptory challenge. 

Act of May 1, 1935, P. L. 127, Sec. 1; 17 P. S. 1153. 

2. Selection and Drawing 

The method of selecting and drawing jurors is not general. In Philadelphia the act of April 20, 1858, P. L. 354, 17 P. S. 1234—1248, applies; counties of the second class are governed by the acts of June 12, 1931, P. L. 538, Sec. 

1, 2, 3, 17 P. S. 1271, 1283 and the Act of May 1, 1933, P. L. 

209, Sec. 1, 17 P. S. 1281; counties of the third class come under the act of April 16, 1925, P. L. 244, 17 P. S. 1321. 

1351, other counties are provided for by the act of April 10, 1867, P. L. 62; 17 P. S. 941, and its supplements. As many jurors as the court of Common Pleas designates are selected yearly, by the jury commissioners and a judge, or a jury board (Phila., and third class counties, or a commission, second class counties), from the "whole qualified electors," "whole electorate" or "list of taxables." The names are written on slips which are deposited in a jury wheel, and a list of the names deposited is posted in the prothonotary's office for inspection. 

Com. v. McCormick, 71 Pa. Super. 567; Com. v. Ross, 58 Pa. 

Super. 412; Com. v. Dutter, 15 D. & C. 635, quashing for failure to file list. 

3. Drawing

Upon the order of the court of quarter sessions and oyer and terminer writs of venire are issued from time to time to the proper officers, commanding that a certain number of jurors be drawn from the names in the jury wheel, and they be summoned for service during a specified period. 

4. Talesmen "By section 41 of the Criminal Procedure Act of 1860, 

(17 P. S. 1152) it is enacted that all courts of criminal juris-

diction of this Commonwealth shall be and are hereby authorized and required, when occasion shall render the same necessary, to order a tales de circumstantibus, either for the grand or petit jury." This is a summary of much contained in section 145 of said act; but these sections are not repealed. 

By itself, the summary authorizes the calling of talesmen in no case when a venire has not been issued and returned with a panel of jurors present. A tales, by its very name, signifies a returning of so many as will make up the full complement; and therefore, it is not granted where there is a total default, but only where the number is deficient. "The first process for convening the jury is the venire facias, and thereupon then issue the habeas corpora and distraingas juratores. If all the jury do not attend, or if so many be challenged and drawn there do not remain a sufficient number to make a jury, there are at common law the writs of undecim, decem, or octo tales, according as the number was deficient, or by statute, the plaintiff may pray a tales de circumstantibus to prevent the delay of the decum tales. 

But if the whole of the jury be challenged off, then a new venire facias, and if none appear, then a distringas juratores shall issue and no tales." "By the statute of this state, ample provision is made for the impartial selecting, summoning and returning of jurors in the courts of criminal jurisdiction. The court of quarter sessions has power, whenever the public business shall require it, to fix adjourned or special courts for trial of criminal cases; but if this were not so, and a cause be continued and it cannot be tried till the next term, there is not authority for calling a jury forthwith from bystanders, or the body of the county, to try a prisoner who has not challenged the array. Both letter and spirit of the statute secure to persons charged with crime a trial when a regular panel of jurors is in attendance, and only for deficiency in the number can talesmen be called. When the array is quashed there is no jury—that is no more a necessary occasion to order a tales de circumstantibus than it would be had none be drawn, summoned and returned. The next consideration desideratum to the pure administration of justice is the giving satis-

faction to the suitors, that their causes have been fairly and impartially decided."

A court may not discharge the panel, and then against objection, treat the panel not as discharged but exhausted, and summon talesmen. 

Williams v. Com. 91 Pa. 493; Com. v. Brennan, 100 Pa. 

Super. 94; second class counties, Act of May 11, 1925, P. L. 561, Sec. 16; 17 P. S. 1286; Com. v. Bruno, 324 Pa (1936), elisors. 

5. Excusing Jurors

In practice, attorneys and physicians are not listed for jury service, and the following persons are usually excused upon application to the court: nurses in active practice, school teachers, police, firemen, county, state or federal employees, undertakers, ministers, persons who have served within a year,¹ national guardsmen, 2 and, on cause shown, person may be excused because of illness or business engagements.3 

1 Act of April 14, 1834, P. L. 333, Sec. 136; 17 P. S. 1101; 

Act of April 20, 1858, P. L. 354, Sec. 8; 17 P. S. 1241, Phila.; Act of April 7, 1927, P. L. 172, Sec. 1, 17 P. S. 1279, counties second class. 

2 Act of May 17, 1921, P. L. 869, Sec. 49; 51 P. S. 76. 

3 Act of April 20, 1858, P. L. 354, Sec. 6; 17 P. S. 1241; 

Act of May 11, 1925, P. L. 561, Sec. 10; 17 P. S. 1279. 

6. Qualifications, Second Class Counties 

American citizens not less than twenty-one years of age, taxed, and residing in the county, who do not have a mental or physical disability that will interfere with jury duties, and have not been convicted of a felony, or have not served upon a jury for three years. 

Act of April 7, 1927, P. L. 172, Sec. 1; 17 P. S. 1279. 

7. Qualifications, Counties of Third Class 

Adult citizens of the United States, residents of the county and able to understand the English language; persons under indictment whether for felonies or misdemeanors

and persons who have been convicted of felonies are ineligible. 

Act of April 16, 1925, P. L. 244, Sec. 2; 17 P. S. 1322. 

8. Qualifications, Other Counties 

Sober, intelligent and judicious persons. 

Act of April 10, 1867, P. L. 62, Sec. 2; 17 P. S. 942. 

9. Challenges, Second Class Counties, Failure to 

Comply with Statutory Requirement Where a juror has been summoned to serve in any of the several courts, any failure to comply with all the provisions of the Act of May 11, 1925, P. L. 561, 17 P. S. 1271 (second class counties, drawing and summoning) shall not constitute grounds for objection, or challenge, unless application for examination is made or objection or challenge is stated before the jury upon which said juror is to serve has been sworn. 

Act of May 11, 1925, P. L. 561, Sec. 15; 17 P. S. 1285. 

10. Waiver of Errors in Drawing

A trial, agreement to try on the merits, pleading guilty or the general issue in any case, shall be a waiver of all errors and defects in or to the precept, venire, drowing, summoning and returning of jurors. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 53; 19 P. S. 832. 

11. Challenge, Third Class Counties 

Any objection or challenge to jurors based on their disqualification for jury service under the Act of April 16, 1925, P. L. 244, or to the array based on any irregularity in the proceedings or procedure in and by which said jurors. were selected, drawn, or summoned for jury service, must be made before said jurors or array of jurors are sworn and cannot be made thereafter. After jurors are sworn without objection, all objection to their qualification as prescribed by this act, or to the manner of their selection, drawing or sum-

moning shall be deemed to have been waived. No indictment can be attacked or verdict challenged by reason of any juror's disqualification under this act, or any irregularity in the manner of selecting, drawing or summoning jurors unless due objection is made before said alleged, disqualified, or illegally selected jurors are sworn. 

Act of April 16, 1925, P. L. 244, Sec. 9; 17 P. S. 1329; Com. v. Shapiro, 78 Pa. Super. 361. 

12. Challenges, How Conducted 

All challenges in criminal proceedings shall be con ducted in the following manner: The Commonwealth shall challenge one person, and then defendant shall challenge one person, and so on alternately, until all the challenges shall be made; but if the commonwealth shall refuse to make any challenge, the defendant shall, nevertheless, have the right to challenge the full number allowed him by law. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 38; 19 P. S. 812. 

13. Time to Challenge

The time to challenge is before the jury is sworn, and if not exercised then the right is waived,¹ but during the selection of the jury and before it is completed and sworn the court may excuse, discharge, or allow the challenging of a juror already accepted.2 Generally, the right to attack a verdict for the disqualification of a juror does not exist, even when the moving party was previously ignorant of the disqualification.3 

1 Com. v. Sydlosky, 305 Pa. 406; Com. v. Walker, 283 

Pa. 468. 

2 Com. v. Curry, 287 Pa. 553; Com. v. Marion, 232 Pa. 

413; Com. v. Brown, 23 Pa. Super. 470. 

3 Com. v. Walker, 283 Pa. 468. 

14. Peremptory Challenges, Number 

The commonwealth and the defendant are each entitled to the same number of peremptory challenges, as follows: In misdemeanors and felonies triable exclusively in

the courts of oyer and terminer, twenty peremptory challenges. Included in these offenses are: murder, manslaughter or other homicide; treason, sodomy, buggery, rape, robbery, arson, mayhem, burglary, concealing death of bastard child, second offense of harboring felons, knowingly receiving stolen goods,¹ and kidnapping;2 other felonies, and perjury and forgery, eight;3 all other misdemeanors, six. 

1 Act of July 9, 1901, P. L. 629, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 811; Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 31; 17 P. Ś. 391. 

2 Act of April 4, 1901, P. L. 65, Secs. 1, 2; 18 P. S. 2183-84. 

3 Com. v. Arcurio, 92 Pa. Super. 404. 

15. Peremptory Challenges, Joint Trials 

In all cases in which two or more persons are indicted for any offense, the defendants shall have the right to the same number of peremptory challenges to which either would be entitled if separately indicted and no more. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 40; 19 P. S. 785; Com. v. Deutsch, 72 Pa. Super. 298. 

16. Ruling upon Challenge for Cause

When challenge for a cause assigned shall be made in any criminal proceeding, the truth of such cause shall be inquired of and determined by the court.¹ The determination of the availability for jury service is largely within the discretion of the trial court, and its action will not be reversed in the absence of palpable error, and only when injury appears.2

In ruling upon challenges for cause, there necessarily resides in the trial judge a wide discretion, and his conclusions are not to be reversed unless there appears some substantial reason to convince the appellate court the action was erroneous; the conclusion arrived at must be the result of the study of the whole testimony elicited on the examination, and the trial court, as well as the reviewing tribunal, must be satisfied there was no state of mind of the proposed

juror that would deny to legal evidence its legitimate effect.3 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 39; 19 P. S. 813. 

2 Com. v. Bentley, 287 Pa. 539; Com. v. Gelf, 282 Pa. 434; 

Com. v. Deutsch, 72 Pa. Super. 298. 

3 Com. v. Crow, 303 Pa. 91; Com. v. Deutsch, 72 Pa. Super. 298. 

17. Examination, Causes for Challenge 

A juror may not be examined upon his understanding of the law. He may be asked the general question, whether if sworn as a juror, he would accept and act upon the law as stated to him by the court; and this is as far as the examination on the voir dire may proceed along that line.¹ 

Anyone whose mind, in any possible way, no matter how honestly, has been warped by any preconceived opinion which may affect his verdict, or has made up his mind what verdict he is to give, is excluded, 2 but knowledge of the facts of a case does not disqualify a juror.

A juror who has conscientious scruples upon any subject, which prevents him from standing indifferent between the government and the accused, and trying the case according to law, is not impartial.³ 

1 Com. v. Calhoun, 238 Pa. 474 (defense insanity). 

2 Com. v. Croson, 246 Pa. 536; Com. v. Ninney, 216 Pa. 

149; Com. v. Spahr, 211 Pa. 542; Com. v. Sullivan, 91 Pa. Super. 

544; Com. v. Collins, 86 Pa.; Super 394. 

3 Com. v. Bentley, 287 Pa. 539. 

18. Opinion 

Where the opinion or impression of the juror is founded on rumors or reports, or even newspaper statements, which the juror feels conscious he can dismiss; where he has no fixed belief or prejudice, and is able to truthfully say he can. fairly try the prisoner on the evidence, freed from the influence of such opinion or impression, he ought not be ex-cluded. 

Com. v. McCloskey, 273 Pa. 456. www

19. Remarks in Presence of Panel 

Remarks made in open court in the presence of a panel of jurors who will try the accused, tending to prejudice the prisoner's cause, do not of themselves disqualify such jurors from serving in his case, unless it appears that the prisoner had no opportunity to develop the jurors' state of mind in relation to the remarks. Where such opportunity exists the trial court will not be convicted of an abuse of discretion in refusing a new trial because of such statements. 

Com. v. Luccitti, 295 Pa. 190; Com. v. Renello, 251 Pa. 

329, reversing. 262. 

20. Citizens of the United States

A new trial will not be granted because one of the jurors was not a citizen of the United States, if it appears that the prisoner had an opportunity to ascertain the qualifications of the juror, and had not been misled or deceived in selecting him. 

Com. v. Sydlosky, 305 Pa. 406; Com. v. Dombek, 268 Pa. 

21. Suggested Grounds for Challenge to 

Individual Jurors for Cause 

(Uniform Code Criminal Procedure, American Law 

Institute, Sec. 277). A challenge for cause to an individual juror may be made only on the ground: (a) That the juror has not the qualifications required by law. (b) That the juror is of unsound mind or has such defect in any organ of the body as renders him incapable of performing the duties of a juror. (c) That the juror entertains such conscientious convictions as would preclude his finding the defendant guilty. (d) That the juror served on the grand jury which found the indictment or on a coroner's jury which inquired into the death of a person whose death is the subject of the indictment or information.

(e) That the juror served on a jury formerly sworn to try the defendant on the same charge. (f) That the juror served on a jury which has tried another person for the offense charged in the indictment or information. (g) That the juror served as a juror in a civil action brought against the defendant for the act charged as an offense. (h) That the juror is a party adverse to the defendant in a civil action, or has complained against or been accused by him in a criminal prosecution. (i) That the juror is related by blood or marriage within the fourth degree to the defendant or to the person alleged to be injured by the offense charged, or on whose complaint the prosecution was instituted. (j) That a juror has a state of mind in reference to the cause or to the defendant or to the person alleged to have been injured by the offense charged, or to the person on whose complaint the prosecution was instituted, which will prevent him from acting with impartiality; but the formation of an opinion or impression regarding the guilt or innocence of the defendant shall not of itself be sufficient ground of challenge to a juror if he declares, and the court is satisfied, that he can render an impartial verdict according to the evidence. (k) That the juror was a witness either for the Commonwealth or the defendant on the preliminary examination, or before the grand jury, or is to be a witness for either party at the trial. 

22. Influencing Jury, Investigation

Whenever it is alleged that an attempt has been made to influence a juror or jurors it is proper procedure for the court to direct an investigation before the cause is proceeded with.1 

The trial judge, on his own motion, may withdraw a juror and continue the case whenever, under the circumstances, there is a manifest necessity for the act, or the ends

of public justice would otherwise be defeated, and an order withdrawing a juror will be affirmed where no abuse of discretion appears.2 

1 Com. v. Deutsch, 72 Pa. Super. 298, money paid; Com. v. 

Clay, 57 Pa. Super. 128, jurors shadowed; Com. v. Clay, 56 Pa. 

Super. 427, reading newspaper, fixed opinion, practice; Com. v. 

Weisel, 9 D. & C. 161; Côm. v. Buzzard, 9 D. & C. 419; Com. v. 

Wood, 14 Del. Co. 500, coaching witness while testifying; 

Com. v. Valenti, 19 Del. Co. 221; Com. v. Shoemaker, 8 Northum. 

Co. 82, applause by spectators. 

2 Com. v. Keeper Phila. Prison, 76 Pa. Super. 98, dense foreman, former acquittal, habeas corpus; Com. v. Hetrich, 1 

Woodward 288.

32. ALLEGING TIME 33. DUPLICITY 34. RESUBMISSION OF BILL AFTER QUASHING, IGNORED BILL 1. Penal Acts Strictly Pursued 

In all cases where a remedy is provided, or duty enjoined, or anything directed to be done by any act or acts of assembly of this commonwealth, the direction of the said acts shall be strictly pursued, and no penalty shall be inflicted, or anything done agreeably to the provisions of the common law in such cases, further than shall be necessary for carrying such act or acts into effect.¹ Suspension of sentence, and probation statutes must be followed.2 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 183; 18 P. S. 3712; 

McElhiney v. Com., 22 Pa. 365; Com. v. Richardson, 229 Pa. 609. 

2 Com. ex rel. v. Morgan, 278 Pa. 395. 

2. Plural and Females Covered by General Term

Wherever anything is forbidden or directed by the provisions of this code, by using the general terms, any one, any person, the person, every person, and such person, or the relative pronoun he, referring to such general term, the same prohibition or direction, if the contrary be not expressed, is extended to more persons than one, and to females as well as males, doing or omitting the same act. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 184, 18 P. S. 3711. 

3. Common Law Offenses 

Laws, acts of assembly, the common law and statute law of England in force May 14, 1776, unless replaced or altered, are binding.

It is not necessary that the commonwealth be able to point to a "precise definition" of an offense in the textbooks or adjudicated cases in order to show that it is an offense at common law. Whatever openly outrages decency and is injurious to public morals in a misdemeanor at common law.

Act of Jan. 28, 1777, 1 Sin. Laws, 429; 46 P. S. 151, 152; 

Com. v. McHale, 97 Pa. 397; Com. v. Mohn, 52 Pa. 243, common scold; Com. v. DeGrange, 97 Pa. Super. 181, indecent assault; Com. v. Ruff, 92 Pa. Super. 530, extortion; Com. v. 

Miller, 94 Pa. Super. 482, inciting to riot; C. O. & C. Co. v. 

Barnsdell's Est. 41 Pa. C. C. 120, champerty; Com. v. Benedict, et al. 114 Pa. Super. 183, bribery. 

4. Charging Offense at Common Law 

The test whether an indictment for a common law offense is sufficient is prescribed by section 1 of the Act of 

March 31, 1860. P. L. 427, 19 P. S. 261, relating to penal proceedings and pleadings. It provides that "every indictment shall be deemed and adjusted sufficient and good in law which charges the crime . . . if at common law, so plainly that the nature of the offense charged may be easily understood by the jury.' 

Com. v. Cartusciello, 100 Pa. Super. 473; Com. v. De- 

Grange, 97 Pa. Super. 181. 

5. Charging Offense under Statute

Every indictment shall be deemed and adjudged sufficient and good in law which charges the crime substantially in the language of the act of assembly prohibiting the crime and prescribing the punishment, if any such there be, or if at common law, so plainly that the nature of the offense charged may be easily understood by the jury. There is no requirement that indictments must pursue the words of the statute. 

When it is made to appear to the court that an injustice is likely to be done the prisoner because of the vagueness of the language of the act of assembly used in the indictment, a bill of particulars may be ordered.

When the indictment charges a statutory offense and states "contrary to the form of the act of assembly in such cases made and provided", if it is a well drawn count for a common law offense, the words may be rejected as surplusage.¹

Since the enactment of the criminal procedure act of 

March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, which provides that every indict-

ment shall be deemed sufficient and good in law which charges the crime substantially in the language of the act of assembly prohibiting it, an indictment will be sustained if the charge be stated with such certainty that the defendant may know what he is called upon to answer, and the court may know how to render the proper judgment thereon, and it is sufficiently precise to protect the defendant from a second prosecution for the same offense. Overnice exceptions are not to be encouraged, especially in cases which do not touch the life of the defendant. In passing upon the sufficiency of criminal pleadings, courts look more to substantial justice than to technicalities.2 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 11; 19 P. S. 261; 

Com. v. DeGrange, 97 Pa. Super. 181; Com. v. Price, 80 Pa. 

Super. 291; Com. v. Cartusciello, 110 Pa. Super. 473; Com. v. 

Ruff, 92 Pa. Super. 530; Com. v. Graff, 29 Dist. Repts. 253; 

Gorman v. Com., 124 Pa. 536, adultery; when "or" is not used in disjunctive sense it is not objectionable, Com. v. Benedict, 45 Lanc. 351. 

2 Williams v. Com. 91 Pa. 493; Com. v. Romesburg, 91 

Pa. Super. 559; Com. v. Lewandowski, 74 Pa. Super. 512; 

Com. v. Michel, 29 Dist. Repts. 423; Com. v. McLaughlin, 30 

Dauph. Co. 351. 

6. Offense Not Charged in the Information,

Cognate Offenses 

Defendants may be indicted for any crime arising out of the transaction described in the information. If there be room for surprise, an application for time to meet the charge will be allowed.¹

When a prosecuting officer is satisfied from his investigation that a higher grade of offense, cognate to the one returned by the committing magistrate, is probable chargeable against a defendant, he may draw a bill accordingly.2

The indictment will be quashed when the offense charged is different from the offense charged in the information.³ 

1 Maginnis's Case, 269 Pa. 186; Com. v. Danner, 79 Pa. 

Super. 556; Com. v. Weber, 67 Pa. Super. 497; Com. v. Moore, 10 D. & C. 694; Com. v. Ely, 29 Dist. Repts. 765.

2 Com. v. Ruff, 92 Pa. Super. 530; Com. v. Weber, 67 Pa. 

Super. 497. 

3 Com. v. Glancy, 9 D. & C. 721; Com. v. Graff, 41 Lanc. 

L. R. 41. 

7. Bill of Particulars 

The right of a defendant to a bill of particulars in some cases is not to be questioned, and where refusal thereof has prejudiced the accused in the trial of a case, relief may be had by exception and appeal. The matter of granting or refusing a bill lies within the sound discretion of the trial judge. 

The indictment in the great majority of cases, even in the short form provided by statute, is entirely sufficient to inform the defendant of the nature of the offense with which he is charged. Bills of particulars are only granted in exceptional cases and are not ordered for the purpose of informing the defendant of the evidence to be adduced by the commonwealth at the trial.¹ When it is made to appear to the court that an injustice is likely to be done the prisoner because of the vagueness of the indictment, a bill of particulars may be ordered.2 

The defendant's claim to a bill of particulars must be founded not upon any right to secure a disclosure of the evidence, nor to fetter or hinder the prosecuting officers, but solely upon his right to be informed of the nature and cause of the accusation brought against him, and to enable him to prepare his defense and thereby have a fair trial. 

A bill of particulars, when ordered and filed, becomes particularly part of the pleadings, and has the effect of further limiting the prosecution in the proof that may be offered as to the crime charged. If the bill of particulars is not sufficiently explicit it is the duty of the defendant to ask for a more specific bill.4

Where the indictment is defective in not setting forth with particularity and detail all the facts constituting the offense, the objection might be raised by motion to quash,

demurrer, or by request for a bill of particulars, but may not be raised after verdict.5 

1 Com. v. Buccieri, 153 Pa. 535; Com. v. Sabo, 83 Pa. 

Super. 166; Com. v. O'Malley, 81 Pa. Super. 100; Com. v. Zuern, 16 Pa. Super. 588; Com. v. Doolittle, I D. & C. 493; Com. v. 

Strauss, 20 Northampton Co. 41. 

2 Com. v. Wilcox, 112 Pa. Super. 240; Com. v. Price, 80 

Pa. Super. 291; Com. v. Kaiser, 24 Dist. Repts. 72; Com. v. 

Yuscavage, 19 Schul. Co. 314. 

8 Com. v. Michel, 29 Dist. Repts. 423, bill ordered; Com. v. Emerson, 29 York L. R. 53; Com. v. Wilcox, 112 Pa. Super. 240. 

4 Com. v. Lenhart, 40 Pa. Super. 572, 590. 

5 Com. v. Shields, 50 Pa. Super. 194, 205; Com. v. McCloy, 10 Pa. Super. 598. 

8. Attempt to Commit Crime

In general, an attempt to commit a crime is an offense whether the offense is created by statute or was an offense at common law. Alleging that the defendant "did attempt to commit an offense prohibited by law," and then describing the offense attempted is sufficient. 

Com. v. Rodman, 34 Pa. Super. 607; Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 50; 18 P. S. 3691, defendant may be convicted of attempt to commit any crime; Com. v. Johnson, 312 

Pa. 140. 

9. Referring to Statute

It is not necessary that an indictment specifically refer either to the statute or the section of the statute which is alleged to have been violated. 

Com. v. Sabo, 83 Pa. Super. 166, two acts; Com. v. Ochman, 79 Pa. Super. 171; Com. v. Lucas, 30 Dist. Repts. 963; Com. v. 

Himmel, 25 Dauph. Co., 449; an information which merely charges violation of specified sections of The Vehicle Code is not sufficient, Com. v. Harned, 25 D. & C. 578. 

10. Caption, Conclusion The style of all process shall be "The Commonwealth of Pennsylvania." All prosecutions shall be carried on in the name and by the authority of the Commonwealth of Penn-

sylvania and concluded "against the peace and dignity of the same", but the conclusion will be rejected as surplusage where the offense is one prohibited by the common law.¹ 

In juvenile court proceedings should not be in the form of a criminal case, an appropriate caption would be, “in re: 

John Doe, an alleged delinquent child."2 

1 Art. V. Sec. 23, Const. Penna.; Com. v. Benedict et al. 

114 Pa. Super. 183, Com. v. Cartusciello, 100 Pa. Super. 473, kidnapping. 

2 In the matter of John Doe, minor, 93 Pa. Super. 232; 

Com. v. Carnes, 82 Pa. Super. 335. 

11. District Attorney, Signing

Bills of indictment are to be signed by the district attorney, but the signature may be added by amendment. 

Act of May 3, 1850, P. L. 654; Sec. 1; 16 P. S. 3431; Com. v. Hamilton, 74 Pa. Super 419. 

12. Names of Witnesses to be Indorsed

It is the duty of the district attorney to place on the back of the indictment all the names of the witnesses for the commonwealth that appear in the subpoena and a bill should not be presented to the grand jury until that is done, and no bill can be ignored without calling all the witnesses.¹

The names of the witnesses other than those who appeared before the magistrates may be added to the indictment by the district attorney, but no witness may appear before the grand jury whose name is not indorsed on the indictment.2 Objection that the names of witnesses are not indorsed on the indictment must be made before plea.³ 

1 Com. v. Lute, 2 D. & C. 224; Com. v. Brungard, 23 Dist. 

Repts. 380. 

2 Maginnis's Case, 269 Pa. 186; Com. v. Lute, 2 D. & C. 

244; Com. v. Wodock, 46 Pa. C. C. 539; Com. v. Doran, 20 Luz. 

L. Reg. 20.

8 Halderman's Petition, 276 Pa. 1; Jillard v. Com. 26 Pa. 169. 

13. Prosecutor's Name to be Indorsed

No person shall be required to answer to any indictment for any offense whatsoever unless the prosecutor's name, if any there be, is indorsed thereon, and if no person shall avow himself the prosecutor, the court may hear witnesses and determine whether there is such a private prosecutor, and if they shall be of opinion that there is such prosecutor, then direct his name be indorsed on the indictment. 

When the name of the prosecutor, if any there be, is not indorsed upon the indictment, the proper practice is for the defendant to offer to prove that there is such a private prosecutor; it then becomes the duty of the court to hear the evidence and if upon such hearing it appears that there is a private prosecutor, or that the indictment is founded upon an information made by a private prosecutor, then his name should, by indorsement upon the indictment, be designated as the prosecutor. An indictment will not be quashed for the omission of the prosecutor's name, and objection must be made before plea entered. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 27; 19 P. S. 262; 

Com. v. Samson, 76 Pa. Super. 226; Halderman's Petition, 276 

Pa. 1; Com. v. Leib. 76 Pa. Super. 413, 419, name may be added after return; Com. v. Rovnianek, 12 Pa. Super. 86; Com. v. 

Schmeck, 8 Berks Co. 207. 

14. Time of Finding, Later Than Time

Named in Recognizance 

An indictment may be returned for an offense committed at any time previous to the finding of the bill and within the period of the statute of limitation, although such offense is committed after the commencement of the term of court at which the bill is returned.¹ 

When an indictment is presented to a grand jury at a term later than that named in the recognizance, notice of presentation must be given to the defendant.2 Where a bill

is quashed because not found at the term to which defendant had entered bail a new indictment may be presented without a preliminary hearing or binding over.³ 

1 Com. v. Magid, 91 Pa. Super. 513; Com. v. Gosnell, 24 

Dist. Repts. 426. 

2 Com. v. Brown, 12 Dist. Repts. 316; Com. v. Leperella, 23 Luz. L. Reg. 312; Com. v. Griffiths, 38 Lanc. L. R. 89; Com. v. Honus, 33 Schu. L. R. 180. 

3 Com. v. Gahagan, 17 D. & C. 564. 

15. Amendment, Lost Indictment

Where during a trial it becomes necessary to amend an indictment, or substitute a copy, the application therefore should be made in open court in the presence of the defendant, and on notice to his counsel. 

Com. v. Corsino, 261 Pa. 593; Com. v. Garletts, 81 Pa. 

Super. 268, lost indictment; Com. v. Colwell, 3 D. & C. 153, indictment amended to conform to evidence; Com. v. Wilt, 46 

Pa. C. C. 316. 

16. Variance Between Indictment and

Evidence Amendable 

By statute variance between the indictment and evidence are amendable, without continuing the proceeding, in the following instances; in the name of any place; name or description of persons or body politic; corporation alleged to be the owner of any property, real or personal, or alleged to be injured or damaged; in the christian or surname or both, or other description whatsoever, of any person; in the names or description of any thing; or in the ownership of property, if the court shall consider such variance immaterial, and that the defendant cannot be jeopardized in his defense. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 13; 19 P. S. 433; 

Com. v. Tassone, 246 Pa. 543; Com. v. Zinkeris, 79 Pa. Super. 85.

17. Variance, Writing

Variance between any matter in writing or in print produced in evidence and the recital in the indictment, may be amended without continuance of the proceeding. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 12; 19 P. S. 432; Com. v. Tassone, 246, Pa. 543. 

18. Two Returns, One Indictment

One indictment may be drawn on two magistrate's returns where the different informations are identical in the description of the offense. 

Com. v. Smith, 62 Pa. Super. 288; Com. v. McKeehan, 93 Pa. Super. 348; Com. v. Trembley, 59 Pa. Super. 182; Com. v. Kreisher, 79 Pa. Super. 428. 

19. Joinder of Parties

Where more than one join in the commission of an offense, all or any of them may be indicted jointly for it, or since each is severally answerable, each may be separately indicted. 

Com. v. Kreisher, 79 Pa. Super. 428; the Act of March 10, 1905, P. L. 35, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1293, applies only to the duplication of costs. 

20. One Return, Separate Indictments 

The district attorney may draw separate indictments on one information,¹ and present them at different times, but costs must not be duplicated.2 

1 Com. v. Stangle, 41 Lanc. L. R. 173. 

2 Com. v. Kreisher, 79 Pa. Super. 428; Com. v. Smith, 62 

Pa. Super. 288; Com. v. O'Brein, 26 Dist. Repts. 787; Act of 

March 10, 1905, P. L. 35, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 1293. 

21. Joinder Felonies, Misdemeanors

A defendant cannot be indicted in one bill for several distinct and unrelated felonies, but it is well settled that a bill of indictment may contain several counts charging dif-

ferent offenses if they all arise out of the same transaction. 

Any number of misdemeanors may be charged in one bill of indictment.

If the defendant is prejudiced by the joinder he should move the court to compel the commonwealth to elect on which count it would try him. 

Com. v. Schoen, 25 Pa. Super. 211; Com. v. Klein, 81 Pa.. 

Super. 551. 

22. One Act Several Crimes 

Where there is but one act or cause of injury or death of a number of persons, there is but one injury to the commonwealth; but where the acts or causes are separate, they are separate injuries to the person and dignity of the com-monwealth.¹

Where separate acts are successive steps in the same transaction, and the one necessarily involves the other, they are merged and constitute but a single offense, i.e., possession and transportation of intoxicating liquor; felonious entry and burglary.

Where the same act or transaction constitutes a violation of two distinct statutory provisions, the test to be applied to determine whether there are two offenses or only one, is whether each provision requires proof of an additional fact which the other does not. 

Where the offenses are misdemeanors of an equal grade and based upon different facts there is no merger.2 

1 Com. v. Ernesto., 93 Pa. Super. 339, explosion of still, six killed; Com. v. Veley, 63 Pa. Super., 489, dam broke, several drowned; Com. v. Valotta, 279 Pa. 84. 

2 Com. v. Fall & Sykes, 107 Pa. Super. 129. 

23. Accessories Before the Fact

If any person shall become an accessory before the fact, to any felony, whether the same be a felony at common law, or by virtue of any act of assembly now in force or hereafter to be in force, such person may be indicted, tried, convicted. and punished in all respects as if he were a principal felon.¹

Statutory provisions enable the commonwealth to disregard the distinction between a principal and an accessory before the fact.2

A defendant charged with a felony may be both an ascessory before the fact and a principal, and an indictment under the statute is the same whether he is one or the other.³ 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 44; 18 P. S. 3672. 

2 Com. v. Jones, 97 Pa. Super., 417; Com. v. Bradley, 16 

Pa. Super. 561, accessory indicted as principal, separate trial; 

Com. v. Petro, 115 Pa. Super. 388. 

8 Com. v. Mendola, 294 Pa. 353. 

24. Accessories After the Fact

If any person shall become an accessory after the fact, to any felony, whether the same be a felony at common law, or by virtue of an act of assembly now in force, or that may be hereafter in force, he may be indicted and convicted as an accessory after the fact to the principal felony, together with the principal felon, or after the conviction of the principal felon, or may be indicted and convicted of a substantive felony, whether the principal felon shall or shall not have been previously convicted, or shall or shall not be amenable to justice, and may therefore be punished in like manner as an accessory after the fact to the same felony if convicted as an accessory, may be punished; and the offense of such person, howsoever indicted, may be inquired of, tried, determined and punished, by any court which shall have jurisdiction to try the principal felon, in the same manner as if the act by reason of which such person shall have become accessory has been committed at the same place as the principal felony; provided always, that no person who shall be once duly tried for any such offense, whether as an accessory after the fact, or as for a substantive felony, shall be liable to be again indicted or tried for the same offense.¹ There is no charge of accessory after the fact to a misdemeanor.2 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 45; 18 P. S. 3673. 

2 Com. v. Harlos, 293 Pa. 180; Com. v. Loomis, 267 Pa. 

438, 444, terrified witness not an accomplice; Com. v. Police,

81 Pa. Super. 69; Com. v. Minnich, 250 Pa., 363, defendant charged as an accessory; Com. v. Gray, 265 Pa. 537. 

25. Negativing Exception

The indictment must negative the exception if it is part of the description of the offense, regardless of its location in the statute.¹

If the exception is in the enacting clause, the indictment must show that the accused is not within the exception, but when the exception is in a subsequent section or statute it is matter of defense and must be shown by the accused.2 

It is a general rule, that every indictment must bring the defendant within all the descriptions mentioned in the body of the act, except they are such as carry with them the bare denial of a matter the affirmation whereof is a proper and natural plea for the defendant.3 

1 Com. v. Beatty, 91 Pa. Super. 37; Com. v. Neal, 78 Pa. 

Super, 216; Com. v. Fusarini, 26 Dist. Repts. 548. 

2 Com. v. Neal, 78 Pa. Super. 216; Com. v. Batch, 120 Pa. 

Super 592. 

3 Com. v. Pastor, 89 Pa. Super. 291; Com. v. Neal, 78 Pa. 

Super. 216. 

26. Tolling Statute of Limitations 

Where an indictment is found after the statute of limitations has run, the better practice is to aver in the indictment the facts relied upon to toll the statute. When the facts showing the defendant is a fugitive are not averred, the commonwealth may, on a motion to quash, offer to show that the defendant had not been an inhabitant or usual resident of the state, and the jury should be allowed to pass upon the proofs submitted. Where, however, the indictment does not allege and no offer on the part of the Commonwealth is made to show facts tolling the statute, the court may quash the indictment. 

Com. v. Bishop, 71 Pa. Super. 255; Com. v. Myers, 23 Dist. 

Repts. 350.

27. Description, Written Instrument 

In any indictment for forgery, uttering, stealing, embezzling, destroying or concealing or obtaining by false pretense, any written instrument, it shall be sufficient to describe such instrument by any name or designation by which the same may be usually known, or by the purport thereof, without setting out any copy or facsimile thereof, or otherwise describing the same or the value thereof.¹

In all other cases it shall be sufficient to describe the written instrument by any name or designation by which the same may be usually known, or by the purport thereof, and in such manner as to sufficiently identify such instrument, without setting out any copy or facsimile of the whole or any part thereof.2 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 17, 18, 19 P. S. 

311, 312. 

2 Com. v. McKeehan, 93 Pa. Super. 348, municipal bonds. 

28. Description, Joint Owners 

An indictment charging a crime with reference to the real or personal property of parties or joint owners, or with intent to defraud partners or joint owners, is sufficient which names any one or more of the owners and others, being partners or joint owners, without naming them. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 14, 15; 19 P. S. 281, 282. 

29. Place

It is sufficient to aver the county as the place of the commission of the offense, if the county be coterminous with the jurisdiction of the court.¹ It must be proved that the crime was committed within the county in which the case is tried. Where a crime is committed during a journey upon any vehicle, the crime may be laid in any county through which such vehicle passed.³ 

1 Com. v. Doolittle, 1 D. & C. 493; Com. v. Heller, 31 

Dauph. Co. 255. 

2 Com. v. Robinson, 1 D. & C. 188; Com. v. Gurley, 45 

Pa. 392.

3 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 49, 19 P. S. 525; 

Com. v. Farrell, 319 Pa. 441. 

30. Laying Property of Municipalities 

Property may be averred to be the property of such county, city, township or district. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 16; 19 P. S. 283. 

31. Description, Larceny, Money

Sufficient to allege the larceny or receiving to be of money, without specifying any particular coin or valuable security. 

Act of April 14, 1905, P. L. 153, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 313. 

32. Alleging Time

The commonwealth is not bound by the date laid in the indictment but can show any date within the statutory period and prior to the finding of the indictment, except in cases when time is of the essence of the offense.¹ 

If the jury is satisfied that the witnesses are mistaken as to the particular day mentioned, but are satisfied beyond a reasonable doubt that the offense was committed on some day about that time, a conviction will be warranted.2 

1 Com. v. Weiss, 284 Pa. 105; Com. v. Ryhal, 274 Pa. 401; 

Com. v. Tassone, 246 Pa. 543, murder, date amendable; Com. v. 

Grove, 91 Pa. Super. 553; Com. v. Bridges, 82 Pa. Super. 92; 

Com. v. Coleman, 60 Pa. Super. 512; Com. v. Nailor, 29 Pa. 

Super. 271; Com. v. Vinisky, 9 D. & C. 754; Com. v. Gosnell, 24 

Dist. Repts. 426, offense same day indictment found. 

2 Com. v. Grove, 91 Pa. Super. 553. 

33. Duplicity

Several distinct misdemeanors, if of like nature and subject to similar punishment, may be charged in different counts of the same indictment, but an indictment which charges distinct and separate offenses in a single count is bad for duplicity, and upon proper application will be quashed.¹

When statute makes two or more distinct acts connected with the same transaction indictable, each one of which may be considered as representing a phase in the same offense, they may be coupled in one count, and under the rules of criminal pleading and the established practice of our criminal courts, it is competent for the jury under this form of indictment, to find the defendant guilty of one or more such distinct acts, as the proofs may warrant.2 

Duplicity may be objected to by special demurrer or, perhaps, general demurrer, or on motion to quash the indictment. The better view is that it cannot be made the subject of a motion in arrest of judgment, or of a writ of error, and it is cured by a verdict of guilty as to one of the offenses, and not guilty as to the other, or by a nolle prosequi as to one member of the count.3 

1 Hutchinson v. Com. 82 Pa. 472, quashed; Com. v. Cook, 98 Pa. Super. 117; Com. v. Beatty, 91 Pa. Super. 37; Com. v. 

Saler, 84 Pa. Super. 281, quashed; Com. v. Basha, 80 Pa. Super. 

320; Com. v. Holstein, 76 Pa. Super. 74; Com. v. Smith, 24 

Dist. Repts. 936; Com. v. Morton, 48 Pa. C. C. 202; Com. v. 

Reichbaum, 3 Erie Co., 224 quashed; Com. v. Starr, 36 P. L. J. 334. 

2 Com. v. Cook, 98 Pa. Super. 117; Com. v. Anagustov, 82 Pa. Super. 156, robbery, Agg. A. & B. 

8 Kilrow v. Com., 89 Pa. 480; Com. v. Lewandewski, 74 

Pa. Super. 512; Com. v. Miller, 18 Schul. Co. 401; Com. v. 

Holstein, 29 Dist. Repts. 787, aff. 76 Pa. Super. 74. 

34. Resubmission of Bill After Quashing, Ignored Bill

It is within the discretion of the court to allow a new bill of indictment to be sent to the grand jury after the original bill has been withdrawn or after motion made to quash.¹ 

The return "ignoramus" on an indictment by the grand jury should be the end of the prosecution originating in the information before the committing magistrate. The defendant has complied with the condition of his recognizance. 

The prosecution has failed with the failure of the bill. The sureties of the defendant are released and he is entitled to

be discharged. If the public interest should require that further action should be taken against him it should be by warrant on a new information, except in those cases in which the district attorney is justified in preferring an indictment without a preliminary hearing. Such a rule would not interfere with the power of the Quarter Sessions, on proper notice to defendant, to recommit to the same or a succeeding grand jury a bill thrown out in consequences of oversight, mistake or fraud.2 

1 Com. v. Bradney, 126 Pa. 199; Com. v. Kintz, 79 Pa. 

Super. 433; Com. v. Bridge, 79 Pa. Super. 437. 

2 Com. v. Stoner, 265 Pa. 139, aff. 70 Pa. Super. 365; 

Rowland v. Com. 82 Pa. 405; Com. v. Ney, 8 D. & C. 594; Com. v. Keeper, 6 D. & C. 215; Com. v. Andruchek, 26 Dist. Repts. 923.

Chapter XII INDICTMENT, PARTICULAR OFFENSES 1. ABDUCTION 2. ABORTION 3. ADULTERY 4. AGGRAVATED ASSAULT AND BATTERY 5. BIGAMY 6. BRIBERY 7. CHAMPERTY 8. COMMON SCOLD 9. CONSPIRACY 10. DESERTION 11. DUELLING, CHALLENGING 12. EMBEZZLEMENT 13. EMBRACERY 14. EXTORTION 15. FORGERY, FALSE PRETENSE 16. FORNICATION AND BASTARDY 17. FRAUDULENT CONVERSION 18. INDECENT ASSAULT . 

19. INVOLUNTARY MANSLAUGHTER 20. KIDNAPPING 21. LARCENY 22. MANSLAUGHTER 23. MANSLAUGHTER, VOLUNTARY, INVOLUNTARY 24. MURDER 25. NON-SUPPORT, ILLEGITIMATE CHILD 26. PERJURY 27. SUBORNATION OF PERJURY 28. TREASON 

29. SWEARING 

30. HOUSES OF PROSTITUTION, INJUNCTION 31. SEIZURE OF GAMBLING DEVICES 32. ARREST, FORFEITURE OF GAMBLING DEVICES 33. NO WRIT OF REPLEVIN TO ISSUE FOR GAMBLING DEVICES 

111

1422 

1. Abduction 

"The abduction of L. H. a female child under the age of sixteen years," without further allegations, does not charge a crime either at common law or under any statute. 

Com. ex rel. v. Francies, 53 Pa. Super. 278, habeas corpus; 

Com. v. Eshleman, 2 D. & C. 115. 

2. Abortion 

Simple abortion is a common law offense.¹ Under the 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 88, 89, 18 P. S. 2071, 2072, a woman cannot be indicted for committing an abortion on herself.2 

1 Com. v. Kelsea, 103 Pa. Super. 399. 

2 Com. v. Weible, 45 Pa. Super. 207. 

3. Adultery

There is no requirement that indictments must pursue the words of the statute, and an indictment for adultery adjudged to be good under the criminal practice and procedure prior to the act of 1860 will be considered sufficient now. An indictment which avers that the defendant is a married man, and the prosecutrix a single woman is sufficient, notwithstanding it does allege that the prosecutrix is not defendant's wife.

Adultery and bastardy may be joined in one count. 

Gorman v. Com., 124 Pa. 536. 

4. Aggravated Assault and Battery

The information and indictment should allege that the defendant inflicted "grievous bodily harm", or did "unlawfully cut, stab or wound." The word "wound", of itself, does not charge aggravated assault and battery. If the words of the act are not followed allegations should be made which clearly show that the harm inflicted was grievous. 

Com. v. Dummire, 38 Pa. Super. 155.

5. Bigamy 

Indictment must be brought or exhibited within two years next after any act of cohabitation between parties to the bigamous marriage, or if there be no act of cohabitation within two years from the date of entering into such big amous marriage. 

Act of March 27, 1903, P. L. 102, Sec. 4; 18 P. S. 613; Com. v. McElroy, 45 Pa. C. C. 681. 

6. Bribery

An indictment for bribing a person designated as a "public officer" who is not a public officer but only a subordinate ministerial officer, may be sustained under the common-law.

At common-law bribery is not confined to the giving and receiving of rewards by a judge or other person concerned in the administration of justice, but includes, as the subjects of it, all persons whose official conduct is in any way connected with the administration of the government. 

Com. v. Benedict, et al., 114 Pa. Super. 183; Com. v. 

Taraborrelli, 19 Dist. Repts. 235, Italian subject bribing Italian consul. 

7. Champerty

Champerty is the unlawful maintenance of a suit in consideration of some bargain to have a part of the thing in dispute or some profit out of it. It is a bargain with a plaintiff or defendant for a portion of the matter sued for in case of a successful termination of the suit, which the champertor undertakes to carry on at his own expense. 

5 R. C. L. 270; C. C. & C. Co. v. Barnsdell's Est., 41 Pa. 

C. C. 120. 

8. Common Scold

Does not consist of a single act or a number of single acts but in an habitual course of conduct. The elements of

continuity is essential, and there must be a habit or practice of scolding.. 

Com. v. Moore, 10 D. & C. 694; Com. v. Smith, 17 Berks 

Co. 40. 

9. Conspiracy

Sufficient if drawn substantially in the language of section 128 act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382 (18 P. S. 2451).

Venue may be laid in any county in which it can be proved that an overt act was done by any of the conspirators in furtherance of their common design. 

Com. v. Barnes, 107 Pa. Super. 46; Com. v. Mack, 19 D. & C. 615, 111 Pa. Super. 494; Com. v. Richardson, 229 Pa. 609, indictment at common law; Com. v. Williams, 102 Pa. Super. 

216; Com. v. Brown, 58 Pa. Super, 300. 

10. Desertion and Non-Support 

Prosecutions under the Act of March 13, 1903, P. L. 

26, Sec. 2 (18 P. S. 1257) and the act of July 12, 1919, P. 

L. 939, Sec. 2 (18 P. S. 2160) for separation without reasonable cause and "wilful neglect" to maintain must be by indictment... Under the Act of April 13, 1867, P. L. 

78, Sec. 1 (18 P. S. 1251) and its supplements, trial is without a jury. 

Com. v. Smith, 117 Pa. Super. 318. 

11. Duelling, Challenging

Sufficient to frame an indictment generally for challenging another to fight with deadly weapons, or accepting a challenge, or acting as second. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 23; 19 P. S. 391. 

12. Embezzlement

Indictment may charge three distinct acts of embezzlement within six calendar months, against the same employee. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427; Sec. 28; 19 P. S. 412.

13. Embracery

The act of May 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 13 (18 P. S. 

231) did not affect the common law offence of embracery, which is any attempt to influence a jury corruptly to one side by promises, threats, persuasions, entreaties, money, entertainment and the like. It applies equally to persons not concerned in the case the decision of which was sought to be affected, as well as to those concerned as parties in it. 

It is not necessary either at common-law or statute to aver that the corrupt attempt to influence the juror was not by the strength of the evidence or by agreement of counsel in open court. 

Com. v. Fahey, 113 Pa. Super. 598. 

14. Extortion

Extortion at common-law is the unlawful taking by any officer, by color of his office, of any money or thing of value that is not due him, or more than is due, or before it is due. 

Com. v. Norris, 87 Pa. Super. 61 indictment not good under statute may be sustained as charging common law offense. 

15. Forgery, False Pretense

It shall be sufficient in any indictment for forgery, uttering, offering, disposing of or putting off, of any instrument whatsoever, or for obtaining or attempting to obtain any property by false pretense, to allege that the defendant did the act with intent to defraud, without alleging or proving the intent to be to defraud any particular person. 

In order to bring a case within Sec. 111 of the act of 

March 31, 1860, P. L. 382 (false pretense) the following things alone are requisite: (a) A false pretense; (b) an obtaining property by it; (c) an intent to defraud; and the correct way to determine whether any particular case falls within it is, not to consider each of the requisites separately, but to look at them together. The pretense must be specifically set forth. But the particularity demanded is only

"certainty to a certain intent in general" or that which, upon a fair and reasonable construction, may be called certain.2 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 19; 19 P. S. 331; 

Com. v. Gouley, 5 D. & C. 41; Com. v. Zimmerman, 41 Pa. 

Super. 246; Com. v. Coleman, 60 Pa. Super. 512. 

2 Com. v. Brady, 101 Pa. Super. 336. 

16. Fornication and Bastardy

Fornication and bastardy may both be charged in one count. The bastardy is but an incident of the fornication, the result of a single act. Hence the commonwealth cannot prosecute for one offense in one county, and for the other in a different county; conviction of a man of fornication in one county where the child was begotten is a bar to his indictment for bastardy, growing out of the same illegal act of sexual intercourse, in the county where the child was born.¹ 

A prosecution for fornication and bastardy begun before the birth of the child is not prematurely brought.2 The statute of limitations in bastardy runs from time of fornication.3 

1 Com. v. Losey, 79 Pa. Super 75; Com. v. Lloyd, 141 Pa. 

28; Com. v. Susanek, 88 Pa. Super. 428. 

2 Com. v. Boeshore, 2 Del. Co. 23. 

3 Com. v. Ruffner, 28 Pa. 259. 

17. Fraudulent Conversion

May be joined with any other offense growing out of the same transaction. 

Act of March 18, 1917, P. L. 241, Sec. 2; 18 P. S. 2487; 

Com. v. Wooden, 94 Pa. Super. 452; Com. v. Gilliam, 82 Pa. 

Super. 75; Com. v. Damico, 23 Luz. L. Reg. 481. 

18. Indecent Assault

Indecent assault was an indictable offense under the common law, and is indictable in Pennsylvania. A precise definition is not necessary, and an indictment is sufficient if the nature of the offense can be easily understood by the jury.¹

Indecent assault is defined as the taking by a man of indecent liberties with the person of a female without her consent and against her will, but with no intent to commit the crime of rape. 

Com. v. DeGrange, 97 Pa. Super. 181, Deft. sentenced to pay costs of prosecution, five of fifty dollars and six months imprisonment, affirmed; Com. v. DeMico, 50 Montg. Co. 1. 

19. Involuntary Manslaughter "Did unlawfully kill and slay" the deceased, charges the offense. 

Act of April 28, 1871, P. L. 244, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 352. 

20. Kidnapping

Kidnapping with intent to extort money is triable exclusively in the court of Oyer and Terminer. 

Act of July 2, 1901, P. L. 605, Sec. 1; 17 P. S. 393; Com. v. 

Boyle, 45 Pa. Super. 10. 

21. Larceny

Larceny and receiving stolen goods may be joined in one indictment. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 24, 19 P. S. 411. 

22. Manslaughter 

Did "feloniously kill and slay" the deceased, sufficiently charges the offense. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 20; 19 P. S. 351. 

23. Manslaughter, Voluntary, Involuntary 

If any person shall be charged with voluntary manslaughter, happening in consequence of an unlawful act, the district attorney may, with leave of the court, waive the felony and proceed against and charge such person with a misdemeanor, and give in evidence any act or acts of man-

WA slaughter, or the district attorney may charge both wilful and involuntary manslaughter in the same indictment. 

Act of April 11, 1929, P. L. 513, Sec. 1; 18 P. S. 2226; 

Com. v. Dreman, 80 Pa. Super. 286. 

24. Murder 

Did "feloniously, wilfully and of his malice aforethought kill and murder" the deceased, charges murder.¹

Concealing death of bastard child and murder may be joined.2 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 20, 19 P. S. 351. 

2 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 89, 18 P. S. 2131. 

25. Non-support, Illegitimate Child 

Proceedings under the Act of June 11, 1917, P. L. 773, 18 P. S. 2161, must be by uniformation and indictment.¹

After discharge under insolvency law defendant may be indicted for nonsupport.2 A conviction of fornication and bastardy does not bar a prosecution under this act.³ 

1 Com. v. Kurtz, 24 Sch. L. R. 112. 

2 Com. v. Morningstar, 82 Pa. Super. 425. 

3 Com. v. Susanek, 88 Pa. Super. 428. 

26. Perjury

It shall be sufficient to set forth the substance of the offense charged and in what court, or before whom the oath or affirmation was taken, averring competent authority to administer the same, together with the proper averment to falsify the matter wherein the perjury is assigned, without setting forth any record or proceeding or authority of the body before whom the perjury was committed. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 21; 19 P. S. 371; 

Com. v. Cauffiel, 84 Pa. Super. 278; Com. v. DeCost, 35 Pa.

Super. 88, exact words of testimony need not be set out; Com. v. Dabbiero, 89 Pa. Super. 435; false testimony must be on material point, Com. v. Ñailor, 29 Pa. Super. 275.

27. Subornation of Perjury 

It shall be sufficient to set forth the substance of the offense without setting forth the record or proceeding or the authority of the body before whom perjury was committed, agreed or promised to be committed. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 22; 19 P. S. 372. 

28. Treason

Every person indicted for treason shall have a copy of the indictment and a list of the jury and witnesses to be produced on the trial for proving such indictment, mentioning the names and places of abode of such jurors and witnesses, delivered to him three whole days before the trial. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 35; 19 P. S. 782. 

29. Swearing

Public swearing is a nuisance at common law, but to be indictable it must be in a public place, and an annoyance to the public. The indictment must charge as an essential and an indispensable fact that the profane language was uttered in the presence, and within hearing, of the citizens present. 

Com. v. Linn, 158 Pa. 22; Blasphemy, Act of April 22, 1794, 3 Sm. L. 177, Sec. 2; 18 P. S. 631; Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 

382, Sec. 30, 18 P. S. 634. 

30. Houses of Prostitution, Injunction

SECTION 1. Be it enacted, &c., That any building or part of a building, used for the purpose of fornication, lewdness, assignation, and or prostitution is hereby declared to be a common nuisance; and any person who maintains such a common nuisance shall be guilty of a misdemeanor, and, upon conviction, shall be sentenced, to imprisonment for not more than one year, or pay a fine not exceeding one thousand dollars, or both, at the discretion of the court. 

SECTION 2. If a person, being the owner of any building, has knowledge of, or reason to believe, that such

building, or a part thereof, is used for the purpose of fornication, lewdness, assignation, and or prostitution and suffers the same to be so used, such building shall be subject to a lien for and may be sold to pay all fines and costs assessed against the person guilty of maintaining such nuisance for such violation of this act; and the total amount of any such fine and costs may be entered, by the filing of a certificate thereof by the clerk of the court in which the same was imposed, in the court of common pleas of the county, and shall remain a lien in favor of such county until paid. Judgment may be obtained thereon and execution issued in the manner provided by law in the case of municipal liens. 

SECTION 3. An action to enjoin any nuisances defined in section one of this act may be brought, in the name of the 

Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, by the Attorney General thereof or by the district attorney of the county concerned. 

Such action shall be brought and tried as an action in equity in the court of common pleas of the county. 

If it is made to appear, by affidavits or otherwise, to the satisfaction of the court, or judge in vacation, that such nuisance exists, a temporary writ of injunction shall forthwith issue, directed to the owner of such building and to the person so using the same, restraining such owner and such person from conducting or permitting the continuance of such nuisance until the conclusion of the hearing and decision thereon. No bond shall be required in instituting such proceedings.

SECTION 4. It shall not be necessary for the court to find the property involved was being unlawfully used, as aforesaid, at the time of the hearing, but on finding that the material allegations of the petition are true, the court shall order that the building nor any part thereof be not used for any of the purposes aforesaid. And upon judgment of the court ordering such nuisance to be abated, the court may order that the building, or any part thereof, shall not be occupied or used for any purpose whatsoever for one year thereafter, but the court may, in its discretion, permit it to be occupied or used if the owner thereof shall give bond, with sufficient surety to be approved by the court making the order,

in the penal and liquidated sum of not less than one thousand dollars nor more than twenty-five hundred dollars, payable to the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, and conditioned that the building, or part thereof, shall not be used for any of the purposes aforesaid, and that he will pay all fines, costs, and damages that may be assessed for any violation of this act upon said property.

SECTION 5. Service of any preliminary temporary or permanent injunctions shall be made personally upon the owner, if such person can be found in the county. If such person cannot be found, a copy thereof shall be delivered to any adult residing in said building or upon the agent, if any, of the owner. If no such adult be found, and service cannot be had upon such agent, then service shall be made as the court shall direct. Any person, other than such owner, who shall be served with any such notice of an injunction, shall, within twenty-four hours thereafter, deliver the same to said owner, or mail it to said owner by registered mail, provided the address of the owner be known to the person served. 

SECTION 6. Any person, or any owner of such build ing, or any agent of such owner, who, after any injunction or order, as aforesaid, has been granted, shall use such building, or any part thereof, as aforesaid, or knowingly permit the same to be so used, shall be subject to summary punishment as for contempt of court, in the manner now provided by law.

SECTION 7. Any person violating any of the pro visions of this act shall be guilty of a misdemeanor, and, upon conviction, shall be sentenced to imprisonment for not more than one year, or pay a fine not exceeding one thousand dollars, or both, at the discretion of the court. 

Act of June 23, 1931, P. L. 1178; 68 P. S. 467. 

31. Seizure of Gambling Devices

If an affidavit be made and filed before any magistrate, before whom complaint has been made, of the commission of either of the crimes provided against in the three preceding

sections, (common gambler enticing) setting forth that the affiant has reason to believe, that the person charged in such complaint has upon his person, or at any other place named in said affidavit, any gambling table, device or apparatus, the discovery of which might lead to establish the truth of such charge, the said magistrate shall, by his warrant, command the officer who is authorized to arrest the person so charged, to make diligent search for such table, device or apparatus, and if found to bring the same before such magistrate; and the officer so seizing shall deliver the same to the magistrate before whom he takes the prisoner, who shall retain possession, and be responsible therefor until the discharge, commitment or letting to bail of the person so charged; after which such officer shall retain such table, device or apparatus, subject to the order of the court before which such offender may be required to appear, until his discharge or conviction; and in case of the conviction of such person, the gaming table, device or apparatus shall, by the direction of the court, be destroyed. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 59, 18 P. S. 1444. 

32. Arrest, Forfeiture of Gambling Devices

It shall and may be lawful for any sheriff, constable or other officer of justice, with or without warrant, to seize upon, secure and remove any device or machine of any kind, character or description whatsoever, used and employed for the purposes of unlawful gaming as aforesaid, and to arrest, with or without warrant, any person setting up the same. 

And it shall be the duty of such sheriff, constable or other officer to make return, in writing, to the next court of quarter sessions of the proper county, setting forth the nature and description of the device or machine so seized upon, and the time, place and circumstances under which such seizure was made; and the said court, upon hearing the parties, if they should appear, if satisfied that such device or machine was employed and used for the purpose of unlawful gaming as aforesaid, shall adjudge the same forfeited, and order it to be publicly destroyed, and at the same time order such reasonable costs and charges to the seizing officer as they shall

deem adequate and just, to be paid by the owner or possessor of such device or machine, or in case of his default, or in case he cannot be found, to be paid as costs are now by law paid upon indictments; and such adjudication shall be conclusive evidence to establish the legality of such seizure, in any court of this commonwealth, in any cause in which the question of its legality shall arise; and in any case in which a decree of forfeiture shall not be pronounced, if said court shall, upon the evidence, be satisfied that there was probable cause for the seizure, they shall certify the same, which certificate shall be a bar to any action brought against the officer for or on account of such seizure, in those cases in which the said officer returns, or offers to return such device or machine; and in all cases shall prevent a recovery in damages, for any sum beyond the real value of the device or machine seized. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, 18 P. S. 1445. 

33. No Writ of Replevin to Issue for Gambling Devices 

No writ of replevin shall issue for any device or machine, seized as aforesaid, nor shall any action be instituted for or on account of such seizure, until the court shall have first adjudicated upon the premises; but such writ or action shall forthwith, on motion, be quashed and abated by the court in which it shall be sued or brought. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 6, 18 P. S. 1446.

Chapter XIII MOTION TO QUASH, DEMURRER 1. DEMURRER, MOTION TO QUASH, DIF- FERENCE 2. MOTION IN WRITING, EFFECT OF QUASHING 3. QUASHING, DEFECTS IN PRELIMINARY 

PROCEEDING 

4. QUASHING FOR EXTRANEOUS MATTERS 5. TIME FOR OBJECTING, FORMAL DEFECTS, AMENDMENTS 

1. Demurrer, Motion to Quash, Difference

Demurrer is the proper mode of objecting to an indictment as insufficient in law or defective in form, for other reasons objection should be made by motion to quash. 

Com. v. Ely, 29 Dist. 765; indictments will not be quashed for matters of defense (former conviction) Com. v. Morningstar, 82 Pa. Super. 425. 

2. Motion in Writing, Effect of Quashing 

A motion to quash an indictment should be in writing.¹ 

Quashing an indictment does not prevent a trial of the case on its merits, a new indictment may be made on the same information.2 

1 Com. v. Hans, 68 Pa. Super. 275. 

2 Com. v. Miller, 24 Dauph. Co. 204, 50 Pa. C. C. 301. 

3. Quashing, Defects in Preliminary Proceeding

After indictment found it is too late to complain of defects in the information, illegality of arrest,¹ or sufficiency or regularity of proceedings prior to the indictment.2 

1 Com. v. Brennan, 193 Pa. 567; Com. v. Fadulla, 89 Pa. 

Super. 244; Com. v. Hans, 68 Pa. Super. 275; Com. v. Taylor, 65 Pa. Super. 113; Com. v. Harbach, 15 Berks Co. 265. 

2 Com. v. Murawski, 101 Pa. Super. 430. 124

4. Quashing for Extraneous Matters 

An indictment may be quashed for matters not apparent upon the face of the record,¹ as when an outside party has interferred with the grand jury during its deliberations upon the case,2 or where a presentment has been made upon the testimony of witnesses examined upon a different charge against another defendant and not from the personal knowledge or observation of the grand jurors,3 or where no witnesses appear before the grand jury, or when the only witnesses who appeared before them were persons whose names were not indorsed upon the bill and therefore could not be sworn by the grand jury, or, perhaps, where the only witnesses appearing before the grand jury were legally incompetent to testify.5 An indictment will not be quashed on the ground of insufficiency of the evidence before the grand jury where some witnesses whose names were indorsed upon the indictment were examined, but it will be quashed where the only witness called gave hearsay testimony." 

1 Com. v. Bradney, 126 Pa. 199; Com. v. Green, 126 Pa. 

531; Com. v. Ross, 58 Pa. Super. 412. 

2 Com. v. Bradney, 126 Pa. 199. 

3 Com. v. Green, 126 Pa. 531. 

4 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 10; 19 P. S. 731; 

Com. v. Hamilton, 71 Pitts. 425. 

5 Com. v. Morris, 91 Pa. Super. 571; Com. v. Dwyer, 79 

Pa. 485. 

6 Com. v. Morris, 91 Pa. Super. 571. 

7 Com. v. Vancel, 99 Pa. Super. 10. 

5. Time for Objecting, Formal Defects, Amendments 

Every objection to any indictment for any formal defect, apparent on the face thereof, shall be taken by demurrer, or on motion to quash such indictment, before the jury shall be sworn and not afterward, and every court before whom any such objection shall be taken for any formal defect, may, if it be thought necessary, cause the indictment to be forthwith amended in such particular, by the clerk or other

officer of the court, and thereupon the trial shall proceed as if no such defect appeared. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 11; 19 P. S. 431;

Com. v. Fickes, 105 Pa. Super. 199, title of office; Com. v. Speiser, 79 Pa. Super. 469, woman's name substituted for man's; 

Com. v. Mayberry, 290 Pa. 195, grammatical error; Myers v. 

Com. 79 Pa. 308, date; Com. v. Streets, 113 Pa. Super. 65, date amended after trial; Com. v. Colwell, 3 D. & C. 153, date; Com. v. Columbine, 46 Pa. C. C. 158, date, typographical error; Com. v. Tassone, 246 Pa. 543, date, after trial.

Chapter XIV SETTLEMENT OF PROSECUTIONS 

1. 

MISDEMEANORS 2. SURETY OF THE PEACE 

3.

FORNICATION AND BASTARDY 4. NOLLE PROSEQUI 1. Misdemeanors 

In all cases where a person shall, on the complaint of another, be bound by recognizance to appear, or shall for want of security be committed, or shall be indicted for larceny, when the value of the goods and chattels alleged to have been stolen is less than one hundred dollars, or for an assault and battery or other misdemeanor, to the injury and damage of the party complaining, and not charged to have been done with intent to commit a felony, or not being an infamous crime, and for which there shall also be a remedy by action, if the party complaining shall appear before the magistrate who may have taken the recognizance, or made the commitment, or before the court in which the indictment shall be, and acknowledge to have received satisfaction of such injury and damage, it shall be lawful for the magistrate to discharge the recognizance, which may have been taken for the appearance of the defendant, or in case of committal, to discharge the prisoner, or for the court also where such proceding has been returned to the court, in their discretion to order a nolle prosequi to be entered on the indictment as the case may require, upon payment of costs; provided; that this act shall not extend to any assault and battery or other misdemeanor, committed by or on any officer or minister of justice.¹

The settlements allowed by this section are limited to those who are exclusively within the words of the statute,2 and can only be made after arrest of the defendants, with 127

the consent of the magistrate or court, and strict compliance with this section. 

1 Act of April 11, 1929, P. L. 514; 19 P. S. 491; Com. v. 

Bernstine, 308 Pa. 394, extortion; Com. v. Julian, 44 Pa. C. C. 

665; Com. v. Meneilly, 15 D. & C. 15, 17. 

2 Com. v. Scott, 7 Pa. Super. 590, directors of the poor should be made parties in the settlement of a bastardy case. 

3 Com. v. Bernstein, 308 Pa. 394, felony; Com. v. Julian, 44 Pa. C. C. 665; Com. v. Radzinowicz, 39 Pa. Super. 173, 183. 

2. Surety of the Peace

It is the duty of the justice of the peace before going into a hearing, to afford the parties an opportunity and to suggest to them the propriety of compromising their differences. 

Act of March 18, 1909, P. L. 42, Sec. 3; 19 P. S. 26. 

3. Fornication and Bastardy

An agreement to pay money in settlement of a fornication and bastardy action is enforceable against the estate of the obligor,¹ but unless a settlement is made in the manner directed by the act of April 11, 1929, P. L. 514, and is fair to the child, it will not be a bar to a prosecution.2 

The mother of an illegitimate child may by a fair settlement with the putative father and for a reasonable consideration release him from further obligation. Such release, however, does not relieve him of his obligation to support the child where the mother subsequently becomes unable to support it and it becomes a public charge.³ 

1 Rohrheimer v. Winters, 126 Pa. 253. 

2 Com. v. Scott, 7 Pa. Super. 590, three to two decision; 

Com. v. Julian, 44 Pa. C. C. 665; Com. v. Hikes, 20 D. & C. 541, bond must be given. 

8 Com. v. Patterson, 106 Pa. Super. 433.

4. Nolle Prosequi

A nolle prosequi is the voluntary withdrawal by the attorney general or the district attorney of further proceedings against a defendant.¹ Its effect is to annul the indictment, but it does not bar a new prosecution.2

The court may not enter a nolle prosequi against the district attorney's protest, and the district attorney shall not enter one without the written consent of the proper court. A nolle prosequi may be entered against one of two or more defendants, or as to a particular count in an indictment.Ⓡ
