Chapter XV CHANGE OF VENUE 1. APPLICATION 2. WHEN MAY BE CHANGED 3. PROCEEDINGS AND TRIAL UPON CHANGE 4. DISPOSITION OF DEFENDANT AND WIT- NESSES 5. WHEN PREJUDICE, RACE, COLOR, CREED 5(a) PETITION FILED WITH PROTHONOTARY OF THE SUPREME COURT 5(b) DUTY OF STATE POLICE 5(c) SUMMARY HEARING 5(d) RESISTING OFFICER 5(e) RESCUING, KILLING 5(f) DEATH INFLICTED BY MOB 5(g) PERSON PARTICIPATING IN MOB, GUILTY OF MURDER 5(h) COUNTY SUBJECT TO FORFEITURE 5(i) TRANSPORTATION OF PERSON THROUGH SEVERAL COUNTIES 5(j) NEGLECT OF OFFICERS TO PREVENT LYNCHING 5(k) OFFICER SUFFERING PERSON TO BE TAK- EN FROM HIS CUSTODY, PENALTY 5(1) PARTICIPATION IN MOB DISQUALIFIES AS 

JUROR 1. Application 

Application for change of venue shall be made before the jury is sworn, to the court in which the indictment is pending in such manner as the court shall direct, and if granted it shall be ordered that the venue be changed to some adjoining or convenient county where the causes alleged for a change do not exist.

Act of March 18, 1875, P. L. 30, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 552. Change of venue is largely within the discretion of the trial court; Com. v. March, 248 Pa. 434. 130

2. When May Be Changed 

In criminal prosecutions the venue may be changed, on application of the defendant or defendants, in the following cases:

First: When the judge, who by law is required to try the same, is a near relative of the prosecutor, or of the defendant, or of the person injured, or has knowledge of facts which make it necessary that he should be a witness in the case.

Second: When upon the application of the defendant in a felony, it is made to appear to the satisfaction of the court that, from undue excitement against the prisoner in the county where the offense was committed, a fair trial cannot be had, or that there exists in that county so great a prejudice against him that he cannot obtain a fair trial, or that there is a combination against him, instigated by influential persons by reason of which he cannot obtain a fair trial. 

Com. v. Buccieri, 153 Pa. 535, prejudice. 

Third: When upon the trial of any criminal case, an unsuccessful effort has been made to procure and impanel a jury for the trial of the defendant, and it shall be made to appear to the court by the written affidavit of some credible witness that a fair trial cannot be had. 

Fourth: When upon a second trial of any felonious homicide the evidence on the former trial thereof shall have been published within the county in which the same is being tried, and the regular panel of jurors shall be exhausted without obtaining a jury.

Venue may be changed on the application of the Commonwealth. 

Act of March 18, 1875, P. L. 30, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 551; Com. v. March, 248, Pa. 434; Com. v. Skawinski, 313 Pa. 453; Com. v. Riggs, 313 Pa. 457; Com. v. Reilly, et al., 324 Pa (1936). 

3. Proceedings and Trial upon Change 

When an order for change of venue has been made. the clerk of the court shall transmit a full and complete transcript of the record and proceedings, together with the

indictment and all other papers on file, to the clerk of the court to which the venue is changed, which transcript shall be entered on the minutes of said court, and the trial shall be conducted as if the indictment had been found in the county to which the venue is changed. Costs accruing from a change of venue shall be paid by the county in which the offense was committed. 

Act of March 18, 1875, P. L. 30, Sec. 3; 19 P. S. 553; Com. v. Deutsch, 72 Pa. Super. 298, district attorney of county to which sent prosecutes. 

4. Disposition of Defendant and Witnesses 

If the offense is bailable the accused shall give bail, to be approved by the court, for his appearance in the court to which venue is changed, at the first day of the next term thereof. If the offense is not bailable a warrant shall be issued to the sheriff to convey the prisoner to the county jail of the county where he is to be tried. 

Commonwealth witnesses shall be bound to appear before the court in which the prisoner is to be tried. 

Act of March 18, 1875, P. L. 30, Sec. 4; 19 P. S. 554. 

5. When Prejudice, Race, Color, Creed 

Whenever any criminal prosecution shall have been instituted, or any warrant of arrest shall have been issued or any arrest shall have been made or attempted with the purpose and intent of criminal prosecution, in the courts of any county against any person within the jurisdiction of the county, whether he be a citizen of the United States or not, and such person shall appeal, as hereinafter provided, for protection of the government upon the ground that he has reasonable cause to apprehend that he will be denied the equal protection of the laws by the county within whose jurisdiction he is, or by any officer or inhabitant of such county, such person shall be entitled to the protection of the courts and officers of the State, to the end that the protection guaranteed by the constitution of this state may be given. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 1; 18 P. S. 331.

5 (a) Petition Filed with Prothonotary of the Supreme Court

Any person charged with felony or other crime, who shall file with the prothonotary of the superior court a duly verified petition showing¹ that he is charged with, or has been arrested for, the alleged commission of or participation in some felonious or criminal act, the nature of which shall be set out in his petition;2 that he has reason to apprehend that because of his race, nationality, or religion, which shall be specifically stated in his petition, the petitioner is likely to be denied the equal protection of the laws either by the courts, the officers of the law, or inhabitants of the county; and that some other person or persons of his race, color, nationality or religion, within the jurisdiction of the county, charged with an offense similar to that with which the petitioner is charged, have been put to death without trial or brutally assaulted or otherwise maltreated, or have been denied trial by due course of law, upon similar charges, because of the race, color, nationality, or religion of such person or persons, he shall be entitled to and shall receive the protection of the commonwealth. The petition may be verified and filed by the person in jeopardy, or by another person in his behalf.

Upon the filing of the petition the prothonotary shall issue a warrant to a member of the state police, commanding him to take the petitioner in his custody, giving a receipt if the petitioner is already in custody. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 2; 18 P. S. 332.

The petitioner shall be entitled to a summary hearing before the superior court, and if his petition is sustained, he shall be kept in custody until he may be tried in some other county to be selected by the superior court. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Secs. 1, 2, 3, 4; 18 P. S. 331, 332, 333, 334.

5 (b) Duty of State Police 

It shall be the duty of such State policeman, upon receipt of such warrant, to arrest and detain the petitioner, and to protect him from assault or injury, and in case such petitioner is in the custody of any officer or person upon a warrant to hold petitioner for prosecution in any county court for felony or other crime, such State policeman shall take such petitioner from such official, receipting to him for the body of the petitioner. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 3, 18 P. S. 333. 

5 (c) Summary Hearing 

When said petitioner shall have been brought into said Superior Court, he shall be entitled to a summary hearing upon his petition before one of the judges thereof; and in case he has been taken from the custody of any officer, he shall, in event his petition is not sustained, be surrendered by the State policeman to the officer from whom he had been taken, and if he has not been taken from the custody of any officer, he shall, in the event his petition is not sustained, be set at liberty, and the costs of the proceedings shall be taxed against him; in case the petition is sustained by the Court, the petitioner shall be remanded to the custody of the Superintendent of State Police for protection, or committed to a proper penal institution, until petitioner may be tried in a court of quarter sessions of the peace or oyer and terminer of some other county, to be selected by the Superior Court, upon such indictment, information, or other charge as may have been or may be made or returned against him, and for the purpose of such trial, such court so selected shall have and possess jurisdiction to try and determine any and all proceedings, upon indictment or information, which may be removed from any other county court under this act. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 4, 18 P. S. 334.

5 (d) Resisting Officer

Whoever shall knowingly and willfully obstruct, resist, or oppose any officer of the Commonwealth, or other person duly authorized, in serving, or attempting to serve or execute, the warrant herein provided for; or shall assault, beat, or wound any officer, or other person duly authorized to serve the same, knowing or having reason to believe him to be such officer or other person so duly authorized, or any person lawfully in the custody of such officer, or other person so duly authorized in serving or executing any such warrant, -shall be fined not more than ten thousand dollars and imprisonment not more than ten years. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 5, 18 P. S. 335. 

5 (e) Rescuing, Killing 

Whoever shall rescue, or attempt to rescue, take, or abduct, from the custody of any officer, or person lawfully assisting him, any person taken upon a warrant under the provisions of this act, or shall, directly or indirectly, aid, abet, or assist any person so arrested to escape or to be taken or abducted from the custody of such officer or other person; or shall harbor or conceal any person for whose arrest such a warrant has been issued, so as to prevent his discovery and arrest after notice or knowledge of the fact that a warrant or process has been issued for the apprehension of such person, shall be fined not more than one thousand dollars, or imprisoned not more than six months, or both; and in case the taking or rescue of any such person in custody, as aforesaid, shall be followed by the killing of the person so taken from custody, all persons engaged in such taking or abduction or killing, or in any way contributing thereto, shall be guilty of murder. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 6, 18 P. S. 336. 

5 (f) Death Inflicted by Mob

The putting to death, within any county, of any person within the jurisdiction of the county by mob or riotous assemblage of three or more persons, openly acting in concert

in violation of law, and in default of protection of such per-son by such county or the officers thereof, shall be deemed a denial to such person by such county of the equal protection of the laws, and a violation of the peace of the Commonwealth and an offense against the same. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 7, 18 P. S. 337. 

5 (g) Person Participating in Mob, Guilty of Murder

Every person participating in such mob or riotous assemblage by which such person is put to death, as described in the section immediately preceding, shall be guilty of murder. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 8, 18 P. S. 338. 

5 (h) County Subject to Forfeiture 

Every county in which such unlawful putting to death occurs shall be subject to a forfeiture of ten thousand dollars, which may be recovered, by action therefor in the name of the Commonwealth, against such county for the use of the dependent family, if any, of the person so put to death, and, if none, for the use of the Commonwealth, which action shall be brought and prosecuted by the Attorney General in a court of common pleas of any county within the Commonwealth, other than the county in which such unlawful putting to death occurred. If such forfeiture is not paid upon recovery of judgment therefor, the Court in which said judgment was recovered shall have jurisdiction to enforce payment thereof by extent of levy of execution upon any property of the county, or may compel the levy and collection of a tax therefor, or otherwise compel payment thereof by mandamus or other appropriate process; and every officer of such county, and every other person, who disobeys or fails to comply with any lawful order of the court in the premises shall be liable to punishment according to law as for contempt, and to any other penalty provided by law therefor. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 9, 18 P. S. 339.

5 (i) Transportation of Person through 

Several Counties

In the event any person so put to death, who shall have been taken from an officer in one county by a mob or riotous assemblage of three or more persons, shall have been taken in one county and transported to another before such killing shall have taken place, each and every county through which such murdered person shall have been transported, during the time intervening between his capture by such mob or riotous assemblage, shall be jointly and severally liable to the forfeiture hereinabove provided. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 10, 18 P. S. 340. 

5 (j) Neglect of Officers to Prevent Lynching

Every officer having the duty or power of preservation or conservation of the peace at the time and place of any such putting to death, as described in section seven hereof, who, having reasonable cause to believe that the same is being or is to be attempted, neglects or omits to make all reasonable efforts to prevent the same; and every officer having the duty or power of prosecuting criminal offenses at such time and place, who neglects or omits to make all reasonable effort to prosecute to judgment, under the laws of this State, all persons participating in such mob or assemblage as hereinabove described, except such, if any, as have been or are held to answer therefor as hereinabove provided, shall be deemed guilty of a felony, and, upon conviction thereof, shall be punished by imprisonment not exceeding five years, or by fine not exceeding five thousand dollars, or by both such fine and imprisonment. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 11, 18 P. S. 341. 

5 (k) Officer Suffering Person to be Taken from His Custody, Penalty

Every officer having the custody within a county of any person charged with, or held to answer for, any crime or offense who suffers such person to be taken from his custody

by a mob or riotous assemblage of three or more persons,openly acting in concert in violation of law, with the purpose of putting such person to death or inflicting bodily violence upon him in default of protection of such person by suchcounty, or the officers thereof, shall be deemed guilty of a felony, and, upon conviction thereof, shall be punished by imprisonment not exceeding five years, or by fine not exceeding five thousand dollars, or by both such fine and imprisonment. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 12, 18 P. S. 342. 

5 (1) Participation in Mob Disqualifies as Juror

In any prosecution for any of the offenses defined herein, and in any action for forfeiture imposed as herein provided, every person who has participated in lynching, or in putting to death of, or of the infliction of great bodily violence upon, any person without authority of law, and every person who entertains or has expressed any opinion in favor of lynching, or in justification or excuse thereof, or whose character, conduct, or opinions have been or are such as in the judgment of the court may tend to disqualify him for the impartial and unprejudiced trial of the cause, shall be disqualified to serve as a juror, and the Attorney General in such action or prosecution shall be entitled to make full inquiry thereof, and to produce evidence thereon; and every person who refuses to answer any inquiry touching his qualifications on the ground that he may thereby criminate himself shall be disqualified as aforesaid. 

Act of May 19, 1923, P. L. 283, Sec. 13, 18 P. S. 343.

Chapter XVI CONTINUANCES CONTINUANCE SUGGESTED FORM OF APPLICATION. 

(Uniform Code, American Law Institute Sec. 300.) 3. APPLICATION ON GROUND OF ABSENT WIT- NESS. (Uniform Code, American Law Institute, 

Sec. 301.) 

4. HEARING OF APPLICATIONS. (Uniform Code, 

American Bar Institute, Sec. 303.) 

5. EFFECT OF ADMISSION OF TESTIMONY 

1. Continuance

An application for a continuance is addressed to the sound discretion of the court. Unless there has been an abuse of discretion in the refusal of the application a new trial will not be granted on appeal. 

Com. v. Meyers, 290 Pa. 573, withdrawal of counsel; Com. v. Hilton, 69 Pa. Super. 222; 265 Pa. 353, trial for perjury continued until case in which it was committed was determined; 

Com. v. Jester, 256 Pa. 441, not time to prepare defense; Com. v. Blakeley, 274 Pa. 100, absent witness; Com. v. Emmett, 77 

Pa. Super. 396, defendant's wife ill; Com. v. Hazlett, 16 Pa. 

Super. 534; Com. v. Rosier, 1 D. & C. 258; Com. v. Lockard, 324 Pa. (1937). 

2. Suggested Form of Application 

(Uniform Code, American Law Institute, Sec. 300.)

An application for continuance need not be in writing unless the court so orders. The application shall specify the ground upon which it is based and, if in writing, shall be signed by the prosecution attorney or by counsel for the defendant, as the case may be, and shall be accompanied by the certificate of the signer that it is made in good faith. 139

3. Application on Ground of Absent Witness 

(Uniform Code, American Law Institute, Sec. 301.) 

If the court directs that an application for continuance on the ground that a witness is absent be in writing the application shall state: (a) that the witness is absent; (b) the facts expected to be proved by the witness; (c) that the testimony of the witness is material;¹ (d) the name and residence of the witness, if known; (e) facts showing that due diligence has been used to obtain the witness; (f) facts showing that the applicant expects to be able to procure the attendance of the witness at a specified time; (g) That the witness is not absent through the connivance or consent of the applicant; (h) that the applicant believes that the case cannot be tried with justice to the party without the evidence of such witness. 

Com. v. Chrostowski, 112 Pa. Super. 466; Com. v. Schwartz, 82 Pa. Super. 369. 

4. Hearing of Applications 

(Uniform Code, American Law Institute, Sec. 302).

The party applying for a continuance may file affidavits in support of his application, and the adverce party may, except as to the facts expected to be proved by the witness, thereupon file counter affidavits. The court in its discretion may require additional affidavits or counter affidavits and shall either grant or refuse the application after considering the allegations thereof and any affidavits or counter-affidavits that may be filed.

5. Effect of Admission of Testimony 

(Uniform Code, American Law Institute, Sec. 303).

If good cause is shown for a continuance on the ground that a witness is absent, the court shall nevertheless refuse the continuance if the adverse party will admit that the witness, if present, would testify to the facts set forth in the application; except that the court, if it is of the opinion that the cause cannot be tried with justice to the applicant by such admission, may require the adverse party to admit the truth of the testimony as a condition of refusing to grant the continuance. If the court refuses to grant the continuance by reason of either admission by the adverse party, the party applying may read to the jury the facts expected to be proved as alleged in the application, so far as they are relevant and competent. If the admission by the adverse party is simply that the absent witness would testify as alleged the adverse party may contradict or impeach this testimony or impeach the witness as if the witness were present. (See local rules of court). 10

Chapter XVII TRIALS 1. RIGHTS OF DEFENDANT 2. PUBLIC TRIAL 3. RIGHT TO BE TRIED BEFORE A PARTICULAR JUDGE 4. RIGHT TO BE HEARD, COUNSEL 5. PRISONER TO BE TRIED WITHIN TWO TERMS AFTER COMMITMENT 6. ARRAIGNMENT 7. APPEARANCE DE BENE ESSE 8. PRESENCE OF DEFENDANT 9. CORPORATION'S APPEARANCE 10. OUTLAWRY 11. INDICTMENT IN WRONG COURT 12. JUVENILE COURT, NO JURY 13. TRIAL WITHOUT JURY, LESS THAN TWELVE 14. JOINT AND SEPARATE TRIALS, SEVERANCE 15. SEPARATE INDICTMENTS, JOINT TRIALS 16. TRIAL ON TWO OR MORE BILLS 17. ONE ACT, DIFFERENT INJURIES 18. DESERTION AND NON-SUPPORT 1. Rights of Defendant Article 1, Section 9, of the Constitution of Pennsylvania, provides that in all criminal prosecutions the accused has a right to be heard by himself and his counsel, to demand the nature of the accusation against him, to meet the witnesses face to face, to have compulsory process for obtaining witnesses in his favor, and, in prosecutions by indictment or information, a speedy public trial by an impartial jury of the vicinage. The Constitution of the United States provides that in all criminal prosecutions the accused shall enjoy the right to a speedy and public trial by an impartial jury of the state and district wherein the crime shall have been committed. 142

It is a denial of the right to a speedy trial for the court to grant an arbitrary continuance when the accused is ready for, demands, and may be lawfully tried, or solely because the prosecuting attorney finds himself unprepared with the evidence to convict because of the disappearance of a material witness, if the defendant is not responsible for the disappearance. 

Com. v. Brown, 309 Pa. 515, counsel; Com. v. Zorambo, 205 Pa. 109, face to face; Com. v. Cassel, 10 D. & C. 59; Sec. 9, Art. 1, Pa. Const.; Com. v. Jester, 256 Pa. 441, trial ten days after bill found, no notice to attorney, new trial granted; Com. v. Richards, 111 Pa. Super. 124; Com. v. Cabel, 79 Pa. Super. 

59, Deft. absent, reversed; Court should appoint counsel, Com. v. Richards, 111 Pa. Super. 124; record must show jury was sworn, Com. v. Robinson, 317 Pa. 321; Com. v. Grant, 121 Pa. 

Super. 399, 406, seven year's delay not violation of right when defendant did not ask for trial. 

2. Public Trial 

A trial is public when it is held in an adequate place, and there is no restriction as to admission except the size of the room where the trial is held.¹ The exclusion of witnesses from the court room is a matter for the discretion of the trial judge.2

There is no statute in Pennsylvania requiring court to be held in the court house, but all legal business to be heard by a judge and jury, or a judge without a jury, should be conducted in the court house at the county seat. The order of a trial judge changing the place of trial to another place in the county seat to take testimony of a material witness unable, because of illness, to attend the regular place for trial, will not be reversed unless it appears there has been an abuse of discretion.3 

1 Com. v. Trinkle, 279 Pa. 564. 

2 Com. v. Principatti, 260 Pa. 587. 

3 Com. v. Trinkle, 279 Pa. 564; Com. v. Corsino, 261 Pa. 

593; Com. v. Boyd, 246 Pa. 529, right to sit beside counsel.

3. Right to be Tried before a Particular Judge

The defendant has no right to be tried by a particular judge, or by a judge of the county having jurisdiction. Courts of law are impersonal, and authority to try cases means the court and not any particular judge. One judge cannot substitute for another after trial has commenced. 

Com. v. Graffius, 67 Pa. Super. 281; Com. v. Claney, 113 

Pa. Super. 439. 

4. Right to be Heard, Counsel

It is the duty of the judge presiding at the trial of a criminal prosecution to inform a defendant, who appears without counsel, that he has a right under Sec. 9 of Art. 1 of the Constitution of Pennsylvania, to be heard by himself and his counsel and to subpoena witnesses who might testify for him. The failure of the court to so inform him, or to appoint counsel for him, is fundamental error.¹

Attorneys who are appointed to defend poor persons are not entitled to a fee from the county. Such services must be regarded as an incident of their official position. One of the burdens, which custom has recognized, is the gratuitous service rendered to a poor prisoner, at the suggestion of the court.2

In homicide cases the court, on petition, appoints counsel to represent indigent defendants. In these cases a fee (not exceeding $200.00) is paid by the county, which must be the exclusive compensation. Provision is made for expenses, and costs of appeal.³

A defendant in a criminal action has the right to sit by his counsel during the trial, and it is the duty of the trial judge to allow him to do so, when a request is made for the privilege, and his refusal is reversable errors, unless shown to be harmless.4

Under some circumstances it is not error to refuse to permit a defendant to try his own case without counsel.

2 Com. v. Henderson, 113 Pa. Super. 348.

3 Act of May 31, 1718, 1 Sm. L. 105, Sec. 4; 19 P. S. 783;' 

Act of March 22, 1907, P. L. 31, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 784; Act of June 

3, 1911, P. L. 627, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1232; Com. v. Warmsley, 294 

Pa. 495; Com. v. Loftus, 292 Pa. 395, expenses; Com. v. Briscoe, 18 D. & C. 615; Com. v. Kosh, 79 P. L. J. 321, counsel may be appointed after sentence, to take appeal.

5. Prisoner to be Tried within Two 

Terms after Commitment

If any person shall be committed for treason or felony, or other indictable offense, and shall not be indicted and tried some time in the next term, session of oyer and terminer, general jail delivery, or other court where the offense is properly cognizable, after such commitment, it shall and may be lawful for the judges or justices thereof, and they are hereby required on the last day of the term, sessions or court, to set at liberty the said prisoner upon bail, unless it shall appear to them, upon oath or affirmation, that the witnesses for the commonwealth, mentioning their names, could not then be produced; and if such prisoner shall not be indicted and tried the second term, session or court after his or her commitment, unless the delay happen on the application or with the assent of the defendant, or upon trial he shall be acquitted, he shall be discharged from imprisonment: Provided always, That nothing in this act shall extend to discharge out of prison any person guilty of, or charged with treason, felony or other high misdemeanor in any other state, and who by the constitution of the United States ought to be delivered up to the executive power of such state, nor any person guilty of, or charged with, a breach or violation of the law of nations. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 54; 19 P. S. 781; the act is only applicable where the prisoner has been confined continuously during the two term period, Com. v. Halderman, 299 Pa. 198; Prisoner will not be discharged where trial has been delayed because of any circumstance of moral, physical, or legal necessity, Com. ex rel. v. Warden, 23 D. & C. 169 decisions reviewed.

6. Arraignment

Arraignment of defendants has been abolished in all cases, except where the indictment charges murder. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 25; 19 P. S. 461. 

7. Appearance De Bene Esse 

There is no right to file an appearance "de bene esse" in our criminal courts. One who has been indicted while he is a fugitive from justice, and has neither been arrested nor surrendered himself, nor entered bail for his appearance, may not appear by counsel and attack the validity of the indictment. Courts deal with the body of the defendant, and until he has been arrested, or has actually surrendered his body, or has entered bail to appear, he is in no position to assert his rights. 

Com. v. Feuerstein, 98 Pa. Super. 201, fugitive from justice. 

8. Presence of Defendant

A defendant, whether indicted for a felony or a misdemeanor, cannot be tried in his absence unless he has expressly or impliedly waived the right to be present.¹ A defendant in a capital case should be present at all times during the trial of the case, though his absence during a part of the argument on a motion for a new trial was held not necessary to the validity of the judgment." 

In cases not capital the defendant need not be present when the verdict is rendered, unless he is confined in jail, in which situation he must be brought into court to hear the verdict.3 

If a defendant does not appear when his name is called for trial, the proper practice is for the court to grant fresh process against him to answer the indictment.4 

1 Com. v. L. V. R. R., 165 Pa. 162; Com. v. Gabel, 79 Pa. 

Super. 59; Com. v. House, 6 Pa. Super. 92. 

2 Com. v. Bednorciki, 264 Pa. 124; Com. v. Corsino, 261

Pa. 593, reversed because lost indictment was replaced during absence of the defendant; Com. v. Westwood, 324 Pa. (1936),

FIRE EN

jury visited scene of crime, defendant not present, his counsel was, held not error. 

3 Com. v. Kintz, 79 Pa. Super. 433; Com. v. Davis, 17 D. & C. 488; Com. v. Gabel, 79 Pa. Super. 59; Lynch v. Com. 88 

Pa. 189. 

4 Com. v. Gabel, 79 Pa. Super. 59. 

9. Corporation's Appearance 

Judgment by default may be entered against a corporation which has failed to enter an appearance when summoned by a writ of venire facias to answer an indictment for nuisance. 

Com. v. L. V. R. R., 165 Pa. 162. 

10. Outlawry 

Any person indicted for treason, felony of death, robbery, burglary, sodomy or buggery, or as accessory before the fact to any of these offenses, who does not appear to answer to such indictment, or having appeared shall escape before trial, may be pronounced and declared to be outlawed and attainted as provided by the Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 73; 19 P. S. 1321. 

11. Indictment in Wrong Court

Whenever any indictment, other than an indictment for murder, shall be found in any court of oyer and terminer or quarter sessions of the peace, of the county where the offense was committed, for any crime not triable under existing law in the court where found, the court may nevertheless proceed with the trial of the accused in the court in which the indictment was found, unless the court shall, upon motion of the accused, certify the indictment to the proper court for trial. No motion made after verdict, or writ of error or appeal, based on failure to indict or try defendant or defendants in the proper court, as provided by existing law, shall be granted or sustained, unless it be affirmatively shown that the

accused was in fact prejudiced in his defense upon the merits and a failure of justice has resulted. 

Act of March 10, 1927, P. L. 879, Sec. 1, 17 P. S. 392; Com. ex rel. v. Reifsteck, 271 Pa. 441; Halderman's Petition, 276 

Pa. 1; Com. v. Torr, 111 Pa. Super. 178. 

12. Juvenile Court, No Jury The act of April 24, 1903 P. L. 274 (Am. 1933) (Ju venile Court Act) does not deprive juveniles charged with crime of their constitutional right of trial by jury, the constitution has never been held to guarantee to the citizen the right to insist that he be tried for crime. 

Com. v. Fisher, 213 Pa. 48; Com. v. Carnes, 82 Pa. Super. 

335, involuntary manslaughter. 

13. Trial without Jury, Less than Twelve 

Except where a sentence of death or imprisonment of ten years or more may be imposed, the defendant shall have the privilege, with the consent of his attorney of record, the judge and district attorney, to waive trial by jury. Such waiver must be in writing signed by the defendant, and shall operate as an election by the defendant to be tried by a judge without a jury. The trial judge shall proceed to hear, try, and determine all issues of law and fact, and to render a general verdict in like manner as if the defendant were being tried, and the trial judge may, after hearing the case, order the withdrawal of the waiver of trial by jury and order and direct that the case be tried before a jury.¹ Except in capital cases, a defendant may consent to be tried by a jury of less than twelve.2 

1 Act of June 11, 1935, Sec. 1, P. L. 319. 

2 Com. ex rel. v. Egan, 281 Pa. 251; Prior to passage of this act defendant could not waive trial by jury. Com. v. Hall, 91 

Pa. Super. 485; Com. v. Robinson, 317 Pa. 321, reversing 

Superior Ct.; Com. ex rel. v. Egan, 281 Pa. 251.

14. Joint and Separate Trials, Severance

It is discretionary with the court whether or not co-defendants shall be jointly or separately tried, except in cases of felonious homicide, in which separate trials may be demanded,¹ and joint trials refused.2

When more than one joins in the commission of an offense all or any of them may be indicted jointly for it, or since each is severally answerable, each may be separately indicted.3 

(Am. L. Inst. Uniform Code of Criminal Procedure, 

Chap. XVIII, Sec. 312: When two or more defendants are jointly charged with any offense, whether felony or misdemeanor, they shall be tried jointly, unless the court in its discretion, on the motion of the prosecuting attorney or any defendant, orders separate trials. In ordering separate trials, the court may order that one or more defendants be each separately tried and the others jointly tried, or may order that the several defendants be jointly tried in one trial and the others jointly tried in another trial, or trials, or may order that each defendant be separately tried.) 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 40; 19 P. S. 785; 

Com. v. Schmidheiser, 111 Pa. Super. 283; Com. v. Widovich, 93 Pa. Super. 323; Com. v. Faulknier, 89 Pa. Super. 454; Com. v. Sonis, 81 Pa. Super. 205; Com. v. Kriesher, 79 Pa. Super. 

428; Com. v. Smith, 27 Dist. Repts. 141. 

2 Com. v. Williams, 307 Pa. 134. 

3 Franklin's Appeal, 163 Pa. 1; Com. v. Kriesher, 79 Pa. 

Super. 428. 

15. Separate Indictments, Joint Trials 

Defendants charged in separate indictments cannot in all cases, against their protests, be tried at the same time. 

The true rule would now appear to be that just as in cases where a defendant is charged in one indictment by separate counts with different offenses, or where one defendant is charged in separate indictments with different offenses, so likewise where two defendants are indicted for the same misdemeanor growing out of the same matters and circumstances so related that the proofs received in one would be

competent in the other, even though the defendants demanded separate trials, whether either will be prejudiced by a joint trial and they are therefore entitled to a severance is a matter for the trial court to determine in the exercise of a sound discretion, and the appellate courts will noe reverse except for a clear abuse of such discretion. 

Com. v. Schmidheiser, 111 Pa. Super. 283; reversing; 

Com. v. Faulknier, 89 Pa. Super. 454; Com. v. Stewart, 65 Pa. 

Super. 402; Com. v. Hartman, 31 Pa. Super. 364; Com. v. 

Portner, 92 Pa. Super. 48, four indictments two defendants, possession of drugs, conspiracy, tried together; Com. v. 

Dauphinee, 121 Pa. Super. 565, 586, reversing. 

16. Trial on Two or More Bills 

It is discretionary with the trial court whether or not a defendant shall be tried at the same time on two or more indictments charging separate and distinct crimes arising out of the same transaction, and the appellate court will not reverse unless it clearly appears that the defendant was prejudiced. 

Com. v. Beattie, 93 Pa. Super. 404; Com. v. Wheeler, 75 

Pa. Super. 84; Com. v. Ernesto, 93 Pa. Super. 339; Com. v. 

Valotta, 279 Pa. 84, trial on two murder indictments; Com. v. 

Daracczk, 85 Pa. Super. 253, rape; Com. v. Dauphinee, 121 Pa. 

Super. 565, reversing. 

17. One Act, Different Injuries 

When there is but one cause of injury or of death of a number of persons, there is but one injury to the commonwealth, and there can be only one trial; but when the acts or causes are separate they are separate offenses. 

Com. v. Valotta, 279 Pa. 84; Com. v. Ernesto, 93 Pa. 

Super. 339; Com. v. McCord, 116 Pa. Super. 480, Agg. A. & B. and involuntary manslaughter in one auto collision; Com. v. 

Baker, 115 Pa. Super. 183, receiving stolen goods, several articles.

18. Desertion and Non-support 

Under the Acts of 1903 and 1919 the offense charged is a misdemeanor, which is to be tried, after the finding of a true bill by the grand jury, as all misdemeanors are tried, by a jury. Under the Act of 1867, and its supplements, the proceedings are returned to the next court of quarter sessions, when the court without an indictment or jury trial may, after hearing, order the defendant to pay such sum as the court shall think reasonable and proper for the support of his wife or children. The Act of 1867 makes no provisions for the court to adjudge the defendant guilty of wilful desertion in such proceeding or do more than make an order, with power to commit, if not complied with. 

Com. v. Smith, 117 Pa. Super. 318.

Chapter XVIII PLACE OF TRIAL 1. ABORTION 2. BASTARDY 3. DEATH 4. KIDNAPPING, MURDER 5. OFFENSE NEAR COUNTY LINES 6. OFFENSES DURING JOURNEY 7. EMBEZZLEMENT 

8. ESCAPES 

9. FRAUDULENT CONVERSION 10. PANDERING 11. RECEIVING GOODS STOLEN IN ANOTHER STATE OR COUNTY 12. TREASON 13. WORTHLESS CHECKS 1. Abortion 

A physician who attempts to procure an abortion in one county, cannot be convicted in another county where delivery takes place as a result of the act committed in the first county. 

Com. v. Bingaman, 51 Pa. Super. 336; Com. v. Heller, 40 

Pa. C. C. 531, death in another state. 

2. Bastardy

Prosecution shall be in the county where the child shall be born. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 38; 18 P. S. 712; 

Act of May 29, 1935, P. L. 249, Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 711, mother's statement in extremity admissible. 

3. Death 

When the cause of death occurs in one county and the death occurs in another county, the court in the county where the cause occurs has jurisdiction.¹ When the death occurs- 152

in another state from a cause occuring in this state, the court of the county in which the cause occurs has jurisdiction.2 

1 Act of May 8, 1889, P. L. 135, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 522; Com. v. Hagen, 16 Del. Co. 339; Com. v. Mull, 316 Pa. 424, proof of place. 

2 Act of March 31, 1860 P. L. 427, Sec. 47; 19 P. S. 523. 

4. Kidnapping, Murder

It shall be sufficient to allege, in any information or indictment for kidnapping, or for murder in the perpetration of kidnapping, that the offense was committed in any county in, though or into which, the defendant carried or conveyed or brought the kidnapped person, and every such offense may be inquired of, tried, and punished in the county within which the same be so alleged to have been committed, in the same manner as if it had been actually committed therein. 

Act of April 24, 1935, P. L. 54, Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 2185. 

5. Offense near County Lines

Offenses committed within five hundred yards of county boundaries may be prosecuted in either county. 

Act of March 31, 1860 P. L. 427 Sec. 48; 19 P. S. 524; 

Com. v. Collins, 268 Pa. 295; Com. v. Jefferson, 2 D. & C. 211, fornication and bastardy; Com. v. Magnelli, 25 Dauph. Co. 

297; Com. v. Swanheart, 70 P. L. J. 1030, statutory rape and bastardy brought in court where child born. 

6. Offenses During Journey 

May be prosecuted in any county through which the vehicle or vessel upon which the offense has been committed, has passed. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 49; 19 P. S. 525; Com. v. Brown, 71 Pa. Super. 575, larceny. 

7. Embezzlement

The defendant may be tried in any county where any part of the embezzlement was committed, or where, upon

being called upon to account, he disowned having received the money. 

Com. v. Sexton, 107 Pa. Super. 69. 

8. Escapes 

Escapes shall be tried in the county where such escape was committed. Any crime or offense committed by a convict following an escape and before apprehension shall be tried in the county where the crime or offense was committed, and offenses committed by prisoners in institutions shall be tried in the county where the institution is located. 

Act of Feb. 28, 1933, P. L. 3; 19 P. S. 526. 

9. Fraudulent Conversion

A defendant charged with fraudulent conversion may be tried in any county where any part of the conversion was committed, or where, upon being called upon to account, he disowned having received the money. 

Com. v. Sexton, 107 Pa. Super. 69; Com. v. Shue, 25 D. & 

C. 680. 

10. Pandering

Prosecution may be in any county in which prostitution was intended to be practiced or in which the offense was consummated, or any overt act committed. 

Act of June 7, 1911, P. L. 698, Sec. 6; 18 P. S. 812. 

11. Receiving Goods Stolen in Another State or County

A person may be prosecuted in this state for receiving or bringing into this state goods stolen in another state,¹ or in one county for goods stolen in another county.2 

1 Act of June 20, 1919, P. L. 542, Sec. 1; 18 P. S. 2862. 

2 Act of April 23, 1909, P. L. 159, Sec. 1; 18 P. S. 2861; 

Com. v. Zilberberg, 21 Berks Co. 245.

12. Treason

When committed out of the state prosecution shall be in the county where the offender is apprehended or into which he shall first be brought. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 43; 19 P. S. 521. 

13. Worthless Checks

Offense of issuing checks without sufficient funds is triable in the county where checks were drawn and delivered. 

Com. v. Heller, 41 York 26.

1. Standing Mute 

If any person shall, upon his arraignment for any offense with which he is indicted, stand mute, or not answer directly, or shall peremptorily challenge above the number of persons summoned as jurors for his trial to which he is by law entitled, the plea of "not guilty" shall be entered for him on the record, the supernumerary challenges shall be disregarded, and the trial shall proceed in the same manner as if he had pleaded "not guilty", and for his trial had put himself upon the country. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 26; 19 P. S. 463; Com. v. Gabel, 79 Pa. Super. 59, plea of not guilty may not be entered when defendant is not present; Com. v. Parry, 45 Pa. C. C. 390; 

Com. v. Feurstein, 98 Pa. Super. 201. 

2. Pleas, Form, Manner

In all cases triable in the court of oyer and terminer pleas may be oral or by writing indorsed on the indictment, and a failure to enter a plea in the court of quarter sessions is a formal defect which is cured by a verdict. 

Act of May 15, 1895, P. L. 71, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 462; Com. v. 

Saler, 84 Pa. Super. 281. 

3. Plea of Guilty

Pleas of guilty may be entered on the indictment before or after presentation to the grand jury, and may be withdrawn at any time before sentence by leave of the court. 

Act of April 15, 1907, P. L. 62, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 241; Com. v. 

Francies, 250 Pa. 496; Com. ex rel. v. Francies, 53 Pa. Super. 

278, 288, there must be an indictment. 

4. Withdrawal of Plea

The withdrawal of the plea of guilty should not be denied in any case when it is in the least evident that the ends of justice will be subserved by permitting "Not guilty" to be pleaded in its place. The court ordinarily will permit a plea of guilty to be withdrawn if it fairly appears that the defendant was in ignorance of his rights and the consequence 11

of his act, or was influenced unduly and improperly, either by hope or by fear, in making it or if it appears that the plea was entered under some mistake or misapprehension.¹ 

Plea of "nolo contendere" or "non volo contendere" may be withdrawn at any time before sentence, and may thereafter be withdrawn by leave of court, and the sentence set aside.2 

1 Com. v. Patch, 98 Pa. Super. 464. 

2 Com. v. Wilmer, 22 Del. Co. 278. 

5. Homicide

The defendant may not plead guilty of murder of the first degree, or plead nolo contendere. He may plead guilty of murder,¹ but it is incumbent on the court to fix the degree from testimony.

Where a judge tries an indictment for murder without a jury under the Act of May 14, 1925, P. L. 759, 18 P. S. 

2222, it is his duty, in view of the gravity of the case, to hear the evidence on the plea of guilty, and at least to make and file a brief memorandum, which will reveal the reason for the sentence imposed.2

The Supreme Court will not modify a death sentence imposed by the trial court on a plea of guilty in the absence of an abuse of discretion.3 

1 Com. v. Berkenbush, 267 Pa. 455; Com. v. Shrope, 264 

Pa. 246; Com. v. Gibson, 67 P. L. J. 233. 

2 Com. v. Garramone, 307 Pa. 507, sentence of death reduced to life imprisonment. 

3 Com. v. Sterling, 314 Pa. 76. 

6. Nolo Contendere. Non Vult Contendere 

This plea, which the court may accept or decline in its discretion, is only allowable in cases of misdemeanor,¹ and though not technically a plea of guilty, it is so substantially, and justifies the court in imposing sentence,2 But when accompanied by a protestation of innocence the plea will not preclude a defendant in a civil court from contesting the facts charged in the indictment. Suspension of sentence on

a plea of nolo contendere is not a "conviction" within the meaning of Art. 2, Sec. 7, Const. Pa.¹ 

1 Com. v. Rousch, 113 Pa. Super. 182; Com. v. McGowan, 45 Montg. Co. 23. 

2 Com. v. Shrope, 264 Pa. 246; Com. v. Austin, 27 Luz.

D. & C. 285.

7. Pleas of Autrefois Acquit and Autrefois Convict 

In any plea of autrefois acquit or autrefois convict, it shall be sufficient for any defendant to state that he has been lawfully convicted or acquitted, as the case may be, of the offense charged in the indictment.¹

The criterion for the ascertainment of the validity of the plea of former acquittal is whether the evidence necessary to support the second charge would have been sufficient to secure a conviction on the first.2 A defendant who enters a plea of autrefois acquit or convict is not confined to the offer of the indictment, nor even to the record. He may prove in addition to the record that the two offenses are identical. The plea of autrefois acquit or convict is of a mixed nature consisting of matter of record and matter of fact; of record, the indictment and verdict; of fact, that the defendant is the same person and the offense is the same, and is in bar of the pending action. Conviction of a lesser degree of homicide is construed as an acquittal of a greater degree, but acquittal of murder is not construed as an acquittal of involuntary manslaughter. A plea of autrefois acquit presupposes a verdict. If there is no verdict rendered by the jury at the first trial a plea of former acquittal cannot be sustained.5 Where a jury considers an indictment containing two or more counts and returns a finding as to one count, but the verdict makes no reference to the other count, such verdict constitutes an acquittal on the counts which are not mentioned in the verdict, and a prior

acquittal may be pleaded as to those counts on which the verdict is silent. 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 30; 19 P. S. 464; 

Com. v. Forney, 88 Pa. Super. 451; Com. v. Doolittle, 1 D. & C. 

493; Com. v. O'Brien, 26 Dist. Repts. 787, demurrer to plea; 

Com. v. Wommer, 31 Schul. L. R. 107; Com. v. Kroekel, 121 Pa. 

Super. 423, 428. 

2 Com. v. Greevy, 271 Pa. 95; Com. v. Sobel, 94 Pa. Super. 

525; Com. v. McEvans, 92 Pa. Super. 124; Com. v. Beatty, 91 

Pa. Super. 37; Com. v. Forney, 88 Pa. Super. 451; Com. v. Exler, 61 Pa. Super. 423, murder rape; Com. v. Grosscup, 44 Pa. 

C. C. 315. 

3 Com. v. Greevy, 271 Pa. 95; Com. v. Dudenhoeffer, 105

Pa. Super. 254, fornication and bastardy, non-support Com. v. 

Romesberg, 91 Pa. Super. 559; Com. v. Epstein, 83 Pa. Super. 

75; Com. v. Campbell, 22 Pa. Super. 98; Com. v. Doolittle, 1 D. & C. 493. 

4 Com. v. Alessio, 313 Pa. 537. 

5 Com. v. Beiderman, 109 Pa. Super. 70; Com. v. Greevy, 271 Pa. 95, reversing. 

6 Com. v. Day, 114 Pa. Super. 511. 

8. Former Conviction or Acquittal Obtained by Fraud

The verdict of a jury upon a valid indictment, in a court of competent jurisdiction, acquitting the defendant, is an absolute bar to any subsequent prosecution for the same offense, notwithstanding basic error upon the part of the court, perverse findings or inproper conduct by the jury or either fraud or coercion by the defendant.¹

A prosecution instituted or carried on by or in the interest of an offender, in order to escape punishment, can never be relied upon to sustain a plea of former acquittal or conviction. It may be treated as void by the state and ignored because of the fraud, or on the ground that the state was not in any sense a party to it. Thus where an offender fraudulently institutes a prosecution against himself in a justice's court and pays or performs the judgment against him for the purpose of preventing an indictment against him which purpose may well be implied from the circum-

stances, he cannot set up his conviction to defeat an indictment subsequently presented.2 

1 Com. v. Koekel, 121 Pa. Super. 423, 428. 

2 Com. v. Bolton, 64 P. L. J. 305; Com. v. Pflueger, 10 

Dist. Repts. 717. 

9. Burden of Proof, Plea of "Not Guilty"

The burden of proof is on the defendant, and must be supported by the preponderance of evidence.¹ Evidence of autrefois acquit or convict may not be given under the plea of "not guilty".2 

1 Com. ex rel. v. Veley, 63 Pa. Super. 489; Com. v. Grosscupp, 6 Leh. L. J. 392, 44 Pa. C. C. 315. 

2 Com. v. Morningstar, 82 Pa. Super, 425; Com. v. Epstein, 83 Pa. Super. 75; Com. v. O'Brien, 26 Dist. Repts. 787. 

10. Former Jeopardy

Pleas of former jeopardy is available as a bar only when defendant has been twice put in jeopardy of life or limb.¹ 

When two persons are killed by separate shots or strokes, although in the same affray, an acquittal or conviction of one homicide is no bar to an indictment for the other, as they are distinct acts, but when one act causes several deaths only one offense has been committed.2

A defendant acquitted of voluntary manslaughter may be proceeded against for involuntary manslaughter.³ Where a jury has been duly sworn to try a defendant upon an indictment for murder, and is discharged before verdict, without defendant's acquiescence, and without absolute necessity, such defendant has been in jeopardy of life and cannot be tried again on the same indictment for murder of the first degree. In such case, the defendant may be tried again for the lesser offense comprehended in the indictment, murder of the second degree and voluntary manslaughter.* 

1 Art. 1, Sec. 10, Pa. Const.; Com. v. Beiderman, 109 Pa. 

Super. 70. 

2 Com. v. Valotta, 279 Pa. 84; Com. v. Barille, 270 Pa. 388; juror withdrawn; Com. v. Fitzpatrick, 121 Pa. 109; Hilands v.

Com. 111 Pa. 1; McCreary v. Com. 29 Pa. 323; Com. v. Commander, 10 D. & C. 275. 

3 Com. v. Mayberry, 290 Pa. 195. 

4 Com. v. Simpson, 310 Pa. 380. 

11. Practice on Plea

The plea of autrefois acquit or convict is of a mixed nature, part matter of record and part matter of fact.¹ It is the duty of the court to declare the legal effect of a record which is offered to sustain the plea.2

If it shows that the evidence necessary to support the conviction on the present indictment would have been insufficient to procure a conviction on the former the plea will not be sustained on a demurrer, or the jury may be directed to return a verdict for the Commonwealth on the plea. 

When there is an issue of fact raised under the plea (i. e., identity) which is submitted to a jury, the jury may first render a verdict on the plea, and if against the defendant, then proceed to a verdict on the plea of "not guilty", or, after instructions from the court on the necessity of considering the evidence separately, the jury may determine the questions raised under both pleas, in which practice a verdict must be returned on each plea. The defense of former conviction must be raised by special plea, and not under a plea of not guilty.¹ 

1 Com. v. Montrose, 8 Pa. Super. 237. 

2 Com. v. Trimmer, 84 Pa. 65; Com. v. McEvans, 92 Pa. 

Super. 124; Com. v. Shoener, 30 Pa. Super. 321, 327. 

3 Altenbury v. Com. 126 Pa. 602, 612; Solisay v. Com. 28 

Pa. 13; Com. v. Dalavich, 4 West. 92. 

4 Com. v. Morningstar, 82 Pa. Super. 425. 

12. Prosecution under Repealed Law

The repeal of a penal statute bars prosecution, or further prosecution, for violations of the act committed before the repeal, unless there is a saving clause, or a general statute providing otherwise. But when a statute is expressly repealed and all, or some, of its provisions, are at the time re-enacted, the re-enactment neutralizes the repeal, and the

repealed provisions that are re-enacted continue in force without interruption. 

Com. v. Beattie, 93 Pa. Super. 404. 

13. Trial for Misdemeanor as Bar to Trial for Felony 

No person tried for a misdemeanor shall be afterwards tried for a felony on the same facts (murder excepted),¹ unless the court in the trial of the misdemeanor discharges the jury and directs that the defendant be indicted for felony.2 

1 Com. v. Ramunno, 219 Pa. 204. 

2 Act of March 31, 1860, 427, Sec. 51; 19 P. S. 831. 

14. Limitation, Statutes 

All indictments and prosecutions (murder and voluntary manslaughter excepted) for treason, arson, sodomy, buggery, robbery, burglary, perjury, counterfeiting, forgery, uttering or publishing any bank note, check or draft, knowing the same to be the counterfeited or forged shall be brought within five years next after the offense shall have been committed, and all indictments for other felonies, and all misdemeanors, perjury excepted, shall be brought within two years next after such felony or misdemeanor shall have been committed; Provided, that if the person against whom such indictment shall be brought shall not have been an inhabitant of this state or a usual resident therein during said respective terms, then such indictment shall be brought within a similar space of time during which he shall be an inhabitant of, or usual resident within the state, provided also, that indictments for misdemeanors committed by any officer of a bank, or other corporation, may be commenced and prosecuted at any time within six years. (See Misdemeanors by Officers of Corporations, Embezzlement by Administrators.) 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 77; 19 P. S. 211; Act of March 23, 1877, P. L. 26, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 212, forgery; Com. v. 

Tassone, 246 Pa. 543, murder; Com. v. Weber, 67 Pa. Super. 

497, aff. 259 Pa. 592; Com. v. DeMaria, 110 Pa. Super. 292.

15. Fugitive

The burden of showing that defendant was not a "usual resident" rests upon the Commonwealth. If the defendant lives outside of the state he is a nonresident, and if he remains in hiding or concealment within the state he is an inhabitant, but not a "usual resident." The question is properly one for the jury. 

Com. v. Weber, 67 Pa. Super. 497, Aff. 259 Pa. 592; Com. v. 

Bishop, 71 Pa. Super. 255; Com. v. Roper, 29 Dist. Repts. 173; 

Com. v. Reiber, 50 Pa. C. C. 565, 24 Dauph. Co. 349. 

16. Misdemeanors by Officers of Corporations 

Indictments for misdemeanors committed by any officer, director, receiver, superintendent, manager, broker, attorney, agent, employee or member of any bank, body corporate or public company, municipal or quasi-municipal corporation, may be commenced and prosecuted at any time within four years from the time the alleged offense shall have been committed. 

Act of June 12, 1878, P. L. 196, Sec. 6; 19 P. S. 213; Com. v. 

Bell, 288 Pa. 29 bank officer. 

17. Embezzlement by Administrator, Guardian, Trustees

All indictments for embezzlement by administrators, executors, guardians and trustees, may be brought or exhibited at any time within five years from the final decree of the court adjudicating the final accounts of said trustees. 

Act of April 23, 1899, P. L. 48, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 214. 

18. Time of Death, Murder 

To constitute the crime of murder or voluntary manslaughter, the death must have occurred within a year and a day from the date of the injury received.

Com. v. Evaul, 5 D. & C. 105, does not apply to involuntary manslaughter.

19. Another Indictment Pending

The pendency of another indictment is not a good ground for a plea in abatement to another indictment in the same court for the same offense. Whenever either of these is tried and judgment pronounced thereon, such judgment will afford a good plea in bar to the other, but nothing short of conviction or acquittal will support a plea. 

Com. v. Harpster, 63 Pa. Super. 74. 

20. Age

At common law a child under seven years is conclusively presumed incapable of crime. Between seven and fourteen, the law also deems the child incapable, but only prima facie so, and evidence may be received to show a criminal capacity. 

Over fourteen, infants, like all other persons are prima facie capable, and he who sets up their incapacity must prove it. 

Com. v. Cavalier, 284 Pa. 311; Com. v. Rispo, 30 Dist.

Repts. 459, juvenile court, homicide. 

21. Insanity at Time of Trial

At common law, which has not been charged by statute, when the question of defendant's sanity at the time of trial was raised, the preliminary question was for the trial judge within his legal discretion; the court, believing doubt exists, may, on his own motion or by request order the fact to be tried by a special jury impanelled for that purpose, or by the jury which is to try the indictment, but the court is not obliged to order a preliminary inquiry. 

The provisions of the "Mental Health Act of 1923” 

(Act of July 11, 1923, P. L. 998, Art. 3, Sec. 308; 50 P. S. 

48) are not mandatory. (see following section) 

Com. v. Scovern, 292 Pa. 26; Com. v. Cilioni, 293 Pa. 208; 

Com. v. Ragone, 317 Pa. 113.

22. "Mental Health Act of 1923," Application for

Commitment, Stay of Criminal Proceedings 

When any prisoner, whether waiting trial or undergoing sentence, or detained for any other reason (e. g. as witness), shall, in the opinion of the superintendent, jail physician, warden, or other chief executive officer of the institution, or other responsible person, be insane, or in such condition as to make it necessary that he be cared for in a hospital for mental diseases, the said superintendent, jail physician, warden, or other chief responsible officer of the institution, or other person, shall immediately make application to a law judge of the court having jurisdiction of the charge against said person, or under whose order he is detained, for commitment of said person to a hospital for mental diseases. The said judge shall forthwith order an inquiry by two qualified physicians, or by a commission, who shall immediately examine the said person and make written report of their findings to the said judge. If, in their opinion, the person so detained is insane, the physician or commission shall so state in a prescribed form. They shall also report whether, in their opinion, such person is of criminal tendency. 

The said judge may, in his discretion, summon other witnesses and secure further evidence. If he is then satisfied that the person thought or alleged to be insane is in fact insane, he shall order the removal of such person to a hospital for mental diseases. If the person is a convict serving sentence, or if he is of criminal tendency, he shall be removed to a state hospital for insane criminals. In any other case, the judge shall commit him to some other hospital for mental diseases.

If the person found to be insane and removed to a hospital is awaiting indictment or trial, or has been arraigned or is being tried, proceedings against him shall be stayed until his recovery. Upon his recovery, he shall be returned to the prison from which he was removed, for the disposition of the charges against him, upon proper order of the court. 

If he is a convict serving sentence, the time during which he is in the hospital shall be computed as part of the term for which he was sentenced.

When on the production or appearance of any person charged with a criminal offense, or on the production or appearance before the court of such person under any other circumstances, it shall appear to the court that such person is insane, or in such condition as to make it necessary that he be observed or cared for in a hospital for mental diseases, proceedings for the commitment of such person to such a hospital shall be had, upon application of some person to be designated by the court. 

Act of July 11, 1923, P. L. 998, Art. 111, Sec. 308; 50 P. S. 

48; All commitments shall be reviewable under writ of habeas corpus, Art. 3, Sec. 317; 50 P. S. 57; Com. v. Cilione, 293 Pa. 

208; Com. v. Scovern, 292 Pa. 26; Com. v. Barnes, 280 Pa. 351; 

Veterans, Act of April 23, 1929, P. L. 635; 50 P. S. 58 deportation, Act of March 26, 1931, P. L. 8 Sec. 1; 50 P. S. 89. 

23. Intoxication as Defense, Murder 

Intoxication of the defendant will not excuse or palliate the offense unless the defendant was in such a state of intoxication as to be incapable of conceiving any intent. If he was, his grade of offense is reduced to murder in the second degree.¹ Intoxication is an affirmative defense and must be proved by the weight of the evidence.2 

1 Com. v. Prescott, 284 Pa. 255; Com. v. Walker, 283 Pa. 

468; Com. v. Troy, 274 Pa. 265; Com. v. Vonalla, 24 Luz. Co. 

439; Jones v. Com. 75 Pa. 403. 

2 Com. v. Troy, 274 Pa. 265; Com. v. Vonella, 24 Luz. Co. 439. 

24. Entrapment

If it appears that the offense originated in the minds of the police and that the idea of its commission had by them been suggested or transferred to defendant who, until then had been innocent of it, and that but for the suggestion or instigation, the thought of the crime would not have occurred to defendant, the defendant would not be guilty.¹

Where the doing of a particular act is a crime regardless of the consent of anyone, the courts are agreed that if the criminal intent originates in the mind of the accused, and the criminal offense is completed, the fact that an op-

portunity is furnished, or that the accused is aided in the commission of the crime in order to secure the evidence necessary to prosecute him therefor, constitutes no defense.2 

1 Com. v. Wright, 85 Pa. Super. 412; Com. v. Wasson, 42 

Pa. Super. 38, 53; Čom. v. Hollister, 157 Pa. 13; Com. v. Hoffecker, 27 Berks 136; Com. v. Patterson, 27 Berks, 217. 

2 Com. v. Earl, 91 Pa. Super. 447; Butts v. U. S. 273 Fed. 

35, 18 A. L. R. 143, note; 8 Ruling Case Law 126; Sorrello v. 

U. S., 287 U. S. 435, 86 A. L. R. 249 and note; 66 A. L. R. 468 and note. 

25. Coercion

It seems that the law will excuse a person, when acting under coercion or compulsion, for committing most, if not all crimes, except taking the life of an innocent person. The fear which the law recognizes as an excuse for the perpetration of an offense must proceed from an immediate and actual danger, threatening the very life of the perpetrator. 

The apprehension of any loss of property by waste or fire, or even an apprehension of a slight or remote injury to the person, furnishes no excuse. The presumption that a wife acted under the compulsion of her husband may be rebutted. 

Respublica v. M'Carty, 2 Dallas 86, Treason; Com. v. 

Shortall, 206 Pa. 165, state militiaman; 8 Ruling Case Law, 125; 106 A. S. R. 721; 16 Cyc 91; Com. v. Saab, 75 Pa. Super. 386.
