1. Opening Address 

Courts may properly restrict counsel, in opening, by refusing to permit questionable features of evidence to be referred to, holding counsel to a narrative of the defense, re-serving further consideration of the matter until it is offered in evidence.1

A conviction will not be set aside because in outlining his case in good faith, the district attorney accidentally refers to some matter as to which the trial judge later excludes. evidence.2 (See Par. 23, Arguments). 

1 Com. v. Quaranta, 295 Pa. 264. 

2 Com. v. Smith, 270 Pa. 583. 

2. Improper Remarks, Exceptions, Record 

Where the remarks of counsel are regarded by the opposing party as prejudicial, objection thereto and request for the withdrawal of a juror should be made at once, as soon as the objectionable language is used. Where objection is made to the remarks of counsel and the withdrawal of a juror is requested, the court should direct the stenographer to place upon the record the remarks as the court understood them; on his failure to do so, counsel may place the remarks on the record by affidavit. 

Com. v. Mika, 317 Pa. 487; Com. v. Tyrell, 296 Pa. 332, withdrawing juror; Com. v. Arnold, 292 Pa. 210; Weiss et ux. v. 

London G. & A. Co. Ltd. 285 Pa. 251; Com. v. Wilhelm, 90 

Pa. Super. 473; Com. v. Chester 77 Pa. Super. 388, reversing, district attorney commented on ruling of court sustaining objection to evidence; Com. v. Kline, 66 Pa. Super. 285; Com. v. Dyminski, 79 Pa. Super. 499; Com. v. Swartz, 65 Pa. Super. 

159; Com. v. Berkowitz, 73 Pa. Super. 367, sarcasm; Com. v. 

Deni, 317 Pa. 289, jurors should not be addressed by name; 

Com. v. Donello, 251 Pa. 329, 338, personal opinion, reversed. 

3. District Attorney, Duties 

It is the duty of the district attorney to present his case fairly, not make misstatements of facts or ask for the drawing of unwarranted deductions, nor assert his personal opinion as to the guilt of the defendant.¹

What is excusable conduct in a prosecuting attorney depends somewhat upon the surroundings and atmosphere of the trial,2 but insulting, prejudicial conduct is ground for a new trial. 3

While a district attorney should avoid stating his personal belief he may properly express his opinion on the evidence narrated in the case. The appellate court will not reverse unless the logical effect of the ill-advised remarks of counsel was to create an improper prejudice in the minds of the jurors and thus tend to prevent a fair trial, yet it is well to keep in mind that the appellate courts can and will, if necessary, grant relief to a defendant whose rights have been disregarded by a district attorney. 

1 Com. v. Hadok, 313 Pa. 110; Com. v. Touri, 295 Pa. 50; 

Com. v. Mazarella, 279 Pa. 465; Com. v. Cicere, 282 Pa. 492; 

Com. v. Widovich, 93 Pa. Super. 323; Com. v. Forgione, 114 

Pa. Super. 275, reversing; Com. v. Ronello, 251 Pa. 329, reversing, personal opinion. 

2 Com. v. Torti, 283 Pa. 43; Com. v. Smith, 270 Pa. 583;

Maginnis's Case, 269 Pa. 186, disbarrment, assistant district attorney. 

3 Com. v. Williams, 309 Pa. 529; Com. v. Baker, 93 Pa.

Super. 360, insulting, prejudicial questions are grounds for a new trial; Com. v. Capalla, 322 Pa. 200, new trial granted; Com. v. Ronello, 251 Pa. 329, reversing. 

4. Neglect to Prosecute, Appointment Private Counsel

The district attorney is a public officer whose duties, powers and authority are defined by statute. When that officer neglects or refuses to prosecute, or there is a disagreement between him and private counsel employed by the prosecutor as to the manner of conducting a trial, it shall be lawful for the prosecutor to present his petition to the proper court setting forth the character of the complaint, verified by affidavit; whereupon, if the court shall be of the opinion that it is a proper case for a criminal proceeding or prosecution, it shall be lawful for it to direct any private counsel employed by such prosecutor to conduct the proceedings. 

Act of March 12, 1866, P. L. 85, 16 P. S. 3432; Com. v. 

Reed, 65 Pa. Super. 91; Com. v. Hamilton, 74 Pa. Super. 419;

Petition of Plamer, 22 Schul. L. R. 242; Act of May 2, 1929, P. L. 1278, Art. 3, Sec. 249, 16 P. S. 249, when a district attorney is charged with any crime in the execution of his duties the court shall appoint an attorney to prosecute. 

5. Calling Witnesses, Homicide Cases 

On the trial of an indictment for murder, it is the duty of the district attorney to present all the testimony on the material facts whether adverse to the defendant or favorable to him, but he is not required to call all eye witnesses to the occurrence. Much is left to his discretion under the discretion of the trial judge. He does his whole duty, if he gives notice to the defendant of his determination not to call a particular eye witness so that the defendant may be afforded the opportunity to call him if he desires. 

Com. v. Zec. 262, Pa. 251; Com. v. Deitrick, 221 Pa. 7; 

Com. v. Koramarkovic, 218 Pa. 405. 

6. Special Attorney for Commonwealth

When requested to do so by the president judge, the attorney general may employ special counsel to represent the commonwealth, who shall supersede the district attorney in cases to which he may be assigned, or generally for a limited period. 

The Administrative Code of 1929, P. L. 177, Sec. 904, 907; 

71 P. S. 294, 297; Com. ex rel. v. Irvin, 110 Pa. Super. 387; Act of May 2, 1905, P. L. 351, Sec. 1, 2; 71 P. S. 817, 818, 819; Com. v. Lehman, 309 Pa. 486, act is constitutional; Com. v. Hamilton, 74 Pa. Super. 419, special prosecutor for Bureau of Medical

Education; when district attorney charged with crime in office court appoints attorney to prosecute, Act of May 2, 1929, P. L. 1278, 16 P. S. 249; Com. v. Reilly, 324 Pa. (1936); appointment of deputy for one term, Act of May 2, 1929, P. L. 

1278, Art. 111, Sec. 261, 16 P. S. 260; appointment for the time being, Act of May 2, 1929, P. L. 1278, Art. 111, Sec. 261, 16 P. S. 261. 

Com. ex rel. Reilly v. Reilly, 188 Atl. 524; Com. v. Ryan, 124 Pa. Super. (1936); Com. v. Pipa, 123 Pa. Super. (1936) state pays costs.

7. Private or Special Counsel 

It is within the discretion of the court to permit counsel to assist the Commonwealth in the trial of an indictment for murder,¹ but special counsel should not be permitted to actually participate in conducting such trial except in extreme cases.2 

1 Com. v. Weisel, 9 D. & C. 161; Com. v. Deutsch, 72 Pa. 

Super, 298. 

2 Com. v. Cicere, 282 Pa. 492. 

8. Conduct of Judge

Any display of partiality or prejudice on the part of the trial judge for or against a party or his attorney must necessarily tend to influence the jury. The trial judge is the person to whom the jurors look for guidance and in whom they expect, and have a right to find, an impartial attitude. Any indication that the judge condemns the language or conduct of one of the parties or his counsel is usually reflected in the jury box. While attorneys are expected to maintain a respectful attitude toward the court, the latter should be equally careful to treat counsel with proper courtesy, and avoid every appearance of prejudice which might influence the jury. 

Com. v. Brown, 309 Pa. 515 reversed, judge's remarks testimonial; Com. v. Stallone, 281 Pa. 41; during deliberation of the jury the court may not inquire how it stands, Com. v. 

Anthony, 91 Pa. Super. 518; Com. v. Robinson, 102 Pa. Super. 

46, juror coerced by judge; Com. v. Wood, 118 Pa. Super. 269 reversing, judge told jury to ignore argument of counsel. 

9. Judge Interrogating Witnesses 

Judges should refrain from extended examination of witnesses; they should not, during the trial, indicate an opinion on the merits, a doubt as to the witnesses' credibility, or do anything to indicate a leaning to one side or the other, without explaining to the jury that all these matters are for them; but a trial judge has a right to interrogate witnesses, and it sometimes becomes his duty to do so. 12

When justice requires, a trial judge has a right to interrogate witnesses. It sometimes becomes his duty to do so, even to the point of recalling a witness to supply an omission. of proof on a material point. But a judge may so conduct an examination as to make it an abuse of discretion, requiring a new trial. It is better to permit counsel to bring out the evidence. 

Com. v. Del Giorno, 303 Pa. 509; Com. v. Myma, 278 Pa. 

505, court suggests adoption of A. B. A. Canon 15, 9 Am. Bar 

Asso. Journal 450; Com. v. Robinson, 102 Pa. Super. 46, 49, judicial poise; Com. v. McKeehan, 93 Pa. Super. 348. 

10. Reading or Sending out Notes of Testimony 

The court may not read, or send out, testimony of certain witnesses, as to do so would give the testimony undue prominence over other testimony. 

Com. v. Brown, 264 Pa. 85; Com. v. Ware, 137 Pa. 465. 

11. Separation and Conduct of Jury 

In a homicide case the mere fact of separation further than is necessarily required to enable the jurors to perform their duties as such, even under the care of a sworn officer, creates a presumption of improper influence, yet the Commonwealth may rebut this, and, if no real injury appears, a conviction will not be set aside for this reason.¹ Men and women jurors may separate for rest or sleep under the charge of tipstaffs.2 

1 Com. v. Blakeley, 274 Pa. 100; Com. v. Insano, 268 Pa. 

1; Com. v. Tenbroeck, 265 Pa. 251; Com. v. Renello, 251 Pa. 

329; Com. v. Fisher, 226 Pa. 189, judgment of guilty set aside. 

2 Com. v. Brue, 284 Pa. 294; Com. v. Crosan, 246 Pa. 536; 

Com. v. Jongrass, 181 Pa. 172, jurors sleeping during trial; 

Act of May 5, 1921, P. L. 348, Sec. 2; 17 P. S. 314. 

12. Locking-up Jury

The court may, in its discretion, in any criminal case direct that the jury be kept together until final determination of the case. 

Com. v. Simon, 44 Pa. Super. 538, bribery; Com. v. Golden, 76 P. L. J. 497, election case.

13. Discharge of Jury

In cases not capital the jury may be discharged where there is reasonable necessity for doing so without affecting the right to try the defendant again. A failure to agree after due efforts to do so, justifies a discharge. 

In capital cases the court does not have power to discharge the jury prior to its agreement, except for reasons which amount to manifest and absolute necessity. The defendant should be present when the jury is discharged in a murder case. 

Com. v. Barille, 270 Pa. 388; Com. v. Davis, 266 Pa. 245; 

Com. v. Tenbrock, 265 Pa. 251; Com. v. Fitzpatrick, 121 Pa. 

109; Com. v. Cook, 6 S. & R. 577; Com. v. Martin, 34 Pa. Super. 

451; Com. v. Nixon, 94 Pa. Super. 333; Com. v. Friedman, 94 

Pa. Super. 401; Com. v. Long, 118 Pa. Super. 357, trial judge may tell jury to make an effort to agree. 

14. Putting Actions of Witnesses on Record 

When witnesses by the action of their hands, or otherwise, undertake to indicate time, space, distance or anything else, counsel eliciting the evidence in question, should be careful to have an intelligible explanation placed upon the record, so that the testimony may be understood on review; otherwise, whenever the notes of testimony are not clear, the interpretation must be given them which suports the verdict. 

Bouser v. C. L. H. & P. Co. 267 Pa. 483; Com. v. Principatti, 260 Pa. 587. 

15. Order of Proof

Generally speaking, the order of proof is in the sound discretion of the trial judge, and in the absence of clear proof of harm done he will not be reversed.¹ It is not error to permit the district attorney to reopen the case and offer evidence.2 

1 Com. v. Carelli, 281 Pa. 602; Com. v. Deutsch, 72 Pa. 

Super. 298; Com. v. Leib, 4 Northumb. L. J. 395. 

2 Com. v. Gormley, 78 Pa. Super. 294.

16. Offers, Objections

While the ordinary rule in civil cases is that when an offer blends irrelevant and inadimssible matters with matter relevant and admissible, its rejection as a whole is not error. 

Yet this principle ought not to be summoned to sustain a ruling prejudicial to the interest of a defendant in a criminal case; in such case so much of an offer as is relevant should be admitted and the fact that some inseparable, incidental, collateral facts, irrelevant in themselves and prejudicial to the prosecution, might thus be caused to appear, would not make the competent part any the less admissible. The possibility of prejudice can be sufficiently cured by restricting the use of the testimony.¹

When an offer is made, either at side bar, or in open court, and the objection thereto is overruled, the evidence which follows should conform to the offer. If the testimony varies in material parts, it is necessary to make a further objection to the evidence, or later move to strike it out. If this is not done, the error, if it is such, will not be considered on appeal.2 

1 Com. v. Marshall, 287 Pa. 512; Com. v. Santos, 275 Pa. 

515; Com. v. Bell, 88 Pa. Super. 216; Com. v. Tetrosky, 313 

Pa. 240; Com. v. Norris, 87 Pa. Super. 66. 

2 Com. v. Lucitti, 295 Pa. 190. 

17. Exception to Admission of Evidence 

On an appeal, complaint cannot be taken to the admission of evidence where no exception was taken in the lower court to such admission. Evidence will not be adjudged erroneous if it was competent for any purpose. 

Com. v. Arnold, 292 Pa. 210, must be set forth in assignment of error; Com. v. Rickerson, 77 Pa. Super. 488; Com. v. 

Dickson, 74 Pa. Super. 200. 

18. Allowance of Exceptions to Rulings Unnecessary

It shall not be necessary on the trial of any case, civil or criminal, in any court of record, for the trial judge to allow an exception to any ruling; but immediately succeeding

such ruling, the official stenographer shall note such exception, and it shall thereafter have all the effect of an exception duly written out, signed and sealed by the trial judge. 

It is the practice for the attorney to direct the court stenographer to note an exception when a ruling has been made against him, if he intends to press it as error. It is too late to object to an answer already made, the proper course is to move to strike it out. 

Act of June 5, 1913, P. L. 421, Sec. 1, 12 P. S. 1196; Lodge v. Stone, 85 Pa. Super. 164. 

19. When Notes of Evidence Shall be Transcribed 

The official stenographer shall transcribe the notes of the evidence taken under the following circumstances and these only: (a) When directed by the court so to do; or (b) when an appeal has been taken to the supreme or superior court; or (c) when he shall be paid for a copy thereof by a person requesting him to transcribe it. 

Act of May 11, 1911, P. L. 279, Sec. 3; 12 P. S. 1198; Com. v. Donato, 87 Pa. Super. 285, lost notes, agreement of counsel. 

20. Filing and Certifying Transcript 

When the evidence in any case is transcribed, it shall be the duty of the official stenographer to lodge the same with the clerk of the court, and notify the parties interested or their counsel that the same will be duly certified and filed, so as to become a part of the record, if no objections be made thereto within fifteen days after such notice. If objections be made, the matter shall be heard by the court, and such order made regarding the same as shall be necessary in order to comport with the occurences at the trial. If no objections be made, or when, after objection, the transcript shall have been made to comport with the occurences at the trial, said transcript shall be duly certified by the official stenographer and by the trial judge, shall be filed of record in the case, and shall be treated as official and part of said record for the purpose of review upon appeal, and shall be considered as prima facie accurate whenever thereafter offered in evi-

dence in the same or any other proceeding, without the necessity of calling the stenographer as a witness to prove the same.1

In case where the procedure outlined by the statute is not followed the reasons for such failure should be stated in any application for corrections which may subsequently be made to the trial court, and that tribunal should be petitioned before the record is removed to an appellate court; but, if the alleged errors are not discovered until after the record is removed, then an application to the appellate court must aver the reasons as above stated, and it must be accompanied by a certificate from the trial judge that the petition has been read by him and he desires an opportunity to consider the matters averred therein, else such a petition will not be considered by the appellate court.2 

1 Act of May 11, 1911, P. L. 279, Sec. 3, 4; 12 P. S. 1198, 1199; Bain v. Hendee, 284 Pa. 538, transcript at least prima facia where no objection made in the court below; Com. v. Bailey, 91 Pa. Super. 17. 

2 Bain v. Hendee, 284 Pa. 538. 

21. Stenographer, Copy of Notes

The law judges of each of the several courts of oyer and terminer and general jail delivery, and of the courts of quarter sessions of the peace, shall employ the official stenographer or stenographers of the courts of common pleas of the particular county, to report the proceedings of the said court, whenever requested so to do by any defendant or defendants, or his, her or their counsel, before or during the trial of any case in said courts; Provided, further, That in all cases tried in the several courts of oyer and terminer and general jail delivery, the defendant or defendants shall be furnished with a copy of the notes of testimony taken at his, her or their request, which said notes shall be paid for by the county in which the said case is tried.¹

Where the evidence has not been taken, and the trial judge has died, a motion for a new trial will be considered on the evidence as stated from the recollection of counsel.2 

1 Act of May 5, 1911, P. L. 161, Sec. 1; 17 P. S. 1802. 

2 Com. v. Dubbs, 29 Dist. Repts. 997.

22.

Daily Transcripts to be Given Court and Coun sel, Appeal in Capital Cases 

During the progress of the trial of any case, upon the order of the court or upon its own motion, or upon motion of counsel and allowance by the court, the official stenographer reporting the proceedings shall, from the stenographic notes thereof, make three typewritten copies, and from day to day as the case progresses supply one copy to the court, one copy to the plaintiff, and one copy to the defendant, or to their respective counsel; and within a reasonable time after the completion of the trial, to certify a complete copy as hereinbefore provided and supply to the parties or their counsel any portion of such copies not previously supplied, as may be necessary to complete the record. But, where the trial judge and counsel representing the parties to the cause so agree, such copies need not be made, unless subsequently directed by the court; Provided, however, that in any case in which an appeal, writ of error, or certiorari has been taken to the supreme or superior court, or in cases of conviction of murder in the first degree, it shall be the duty of the official stenographer forthwith to make, certify, and file of record a typewritten copy of the stenographic notes of trial, without any order of the court; payment for which shall be made in the same manner as if directed by the court, but the court may direct additional copies in such cases to be made at the same time with the filing copy, as hereinbefore provided. In all cases in which copies of the stenographic notes are not made or required as hereinbefore provided, it shall be the duty of the official stenographer to file such stenographic notes in the proper office for the record of said proceedings. If thereafter the court or counsel desire a copy or copies of the stenographic notes so filed, it shall be the duty of the official stenographer to make the same; and if ordered by the court, compensation therefor shall be made as provided in section eight of this act; and, if ordered by counsel, without an order of the court, then the compensation therefor shall be paid by the party ordering the same, at the regular rate for the transcription of notes as provided

in section eight of this act (fifteen cents for one hundred words: five cents each hundred words for copies). 

Act of May 1, 1907, P. L. 135, Sec. 7, 17 P. S. 1809. 

23. Arguments of Counsel 

Arguments of counsel are an integral part of the jury trial. They are not mere trial trappings which a judge is at liberty to dispense with or to instruct a jury to disregard. 

Jurors do not decide issues on naked facts alone that are presented to them; they decide issues both on these facts and on inferences reasonably drawn therefrom. It is the right of opposing counsel to urge jurors to accept as reasonable the inferences which they respectively pass upon them, and it it the duty of the jurors to consider carefully whether they will accept or reject such inferences. 

A charge which plainly imports a direction to the jury to accord the argument of counsel minimal consideration is reversible error.1 The district attorney is a quasi-judicial officer, representing the Commonwealth, which seeks no victims, but only justice, and, since he is invested with these grave responsibilities, he should, at all times, conduct the Commonwealth's case fairly, present it in an impartial manner, and avoid seeking to influence the jury by arousing their prejudices.

A case is not well tried unless fairly tried, and a verdict obtained by incorrect statements or unfair argument or by an appeal to passion or prejudice, stands on but little higher ground than one obtained by false testimony. The winning of the verdict should be a hollow reward to the advocate who has brought it to pass by appeals to a jury's prejudice and not by the strength of the case presented.

Such verdicts are, moreover, worthless, for the courts will not let them stand. It is one of the duties of the trial judge to keep the argument and remarks of the counsel within proper bounds. 2

There is no merit in the position that the intemperate remarks upon the part of counsel for the defendant justifies a reply in kind by the district attorney. The trial judge on

objection, or on his own motion, should require counsel for the defendant to confine his remarks to the evidence, to refrain from the use of intemperate language, and conduct the defense according to the rules of conduct governing attorneys and jury trials. 3 

1 Com. v. Brown, 309 Pa. 515, reversing; Com. v. Shoemaker, 240 Pa. 255; Com. v. Nicely, 130 Pa. 261; Com. v. 

Martin, 47 Pa. Super. 346; Com. v. Swartz, 37 Pa. Super. 507. 

2 Com. v. Williams, 309 Pa. 529, reversing; Com. v. 

Massarelli, 304 Pa. 335; Com. v. Del Vaccio, 299 Pa. 547; Com. v. Smith, 270 Pa. 583; Com. v. Cicere, 282 Pa. 492; Com. v. 

Davison, 99 Pa. Super. 412; Com. v. Bonnem, 95 Pa. Super. 496; 

Com. v. Exler, 61 Pa. Super. 423; Com. v. Kline, 66 Pâ. Super. 285. 

3 Com. v. Forgione, et al. 114 Pa. Super. 275; Com. v. 

Ronello, 251 Pa. 329, reversing, district attorney's personal opinion. 

24. Exception to Decision Unnecessary

Whenever the decision of a court of record shall appear in the proceedings of a case, it shall not be necesasry for the purpose of a review of the decision, to take any exception thereto; but the case shall be heard by the appellate court with the same effect as if an exception had been duly written out, signed and sealed by the court. 

Act of May 11, 1911, P. L. 279, Sec. 6; 12 P. S. 1201; Com. v. Emery, 273 Pa. 517, sentence should be assigned as error if review is desired.

Chapter XXI WITNESSES, TESTIMONY 1. ALL PERSONS SHALL BE COMPETENT WITNESSES EXCEPT IN CERTAIN CASES 2. WITNESSES MAY BE COMPELLED TO TESTIFY, INCRIMINATION 3. GAMBLING, NO WITNESS EXEMPT 4. 43. UNIFORMITY OF INTERPRETATION 1. All Persons Shall Be Competent Witnesses 

Except in Certain Cases 

Except upon a preliminary hearing before a magistrate for the purpose of determining whether a person charged with a criminal offense triable in the court of oyer and terminer ought to be committed for trial, and except also upon a hearing under habeas corpus for the purpose of determining whether bail ought to be taken upon a commitment for murder in the first degree or for the purpose of determining in any case how much bail ought to be required, or for the purpose of determining in any case whether a person committed for trial ought to be further held, and except also upon hearings before a grand jury, in none of which cases shall evidence for the defendant be heard, and except also as provided in section two of this act, all persons shall be fully competent witnesses in any criminal proceeding before any tribunal. (See Husband and Wife). 

Act of May 23, 1887, P. L. 158, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 681.

2. Witnesses May be Compelled to Testify,

Incrimination 

Except defendants actually upon trial in a criminal court, any competent witness may be compelled to testify in any proceeding, civil or criminal, but he may not be compelled to answer any question which, in the opinion of the trial judge, would tend to criminate him; nor may the neglect or refusal of any defendant, actually upon trial in a criminal court, to offer himself as a witness be treated as creating any presumption against him, or be adversely referred to by court or counsel during the trial.¹

To entitle a party called as a witness to the privilege of silence the court must see, from the circumstances of the case and the nature of the evidence which the witness is called to give, that there is reasonable ground to apprehend danger to the witness from his being compelled to answer, although if the fact of the witness's being in danger be once made to appear, great latitude should be allowed to him in judging for himself of the effect of any particular question.2 

A witness may be cross-examined as to conviction of crime for the purpose of testing his credibility.3 (See Par. 8). 

1 Act of May 23, 1887, P. L. 158, Sec. 10; 19 P. S. 631. 

2 Com. v. Bolger, 229 Pa. 597; Contempt of Myers and 

Brei, 83 Pa. Super. 383. 

3 Com. v. Payne, 242 Pa. 394, conviction of robbery. The crime should be such as affects credibility. Com. v. Auerbach, 71 Pa. Super. 54. 

3. Gambling, no Witness Exempt 

No witness shall be excused, under any allegation or pretense whatsoever, in any prosecution or proceeding for unlawful gambling, from giving his testimony touching the same; but no evidence given, or facts divulged by him, shall be used or employed against him in any criminal prosecution whatever. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382; Sec. 58, 18 P. S. 1443.

4. Defendant Need not Testify, Former Testimony 

Sec. 10 of the Act of May 23, 1887, P. L. 158 (19 P. S. 

531), recognizes that the defendant need not testify, and provides that his failure to testify will not create a presumption against him and may not be adversely referred to by court or counsel during the trial. A defendant may waive the privilege and take the stand and testify, but if he testifies he waives the constitutional provision against self-incrimination and must go on and make full disclosures.¹

An instruction that the defendant "has not made any denial of the testimony,"2 or a remark by the district attorney that "there is no denial here that defendant took the money" does not violate the restriction.³

If the defendant testifies in one trial, on a retrial of the same case, if he does not offer himself as a witness, his evidence given in a former trial may be used as evidence against him.4 

1 Com. v. Wilhelm, 90 Pa. Super. 473; Com. v. Karmendi, 324 Pa. (1937). 

2 Com. v. Chickerella, 251 Pa. 160; Com. v. Yollin, 115 Pa. 

Super. 215. 

3 Com. v. Van Dyke, 9 Lack. 259. 

4 Com. v. Wilhelm, 90 Pa. Super. 473. 

5. Confessions

In passing upon the admissibility of a confession, when it is alleged it was not made voluntarily, the court only hears the Commonwealth's witnesses, though the defendant may cross-examine them. If, by them, it is shown that it was made voluntarily, without such threat or intimidation as might occur in a false confession, it must be admitted. If afterwards the defendant testifies, or produces other witnesses who testify, that it was not voluntarily made, it becomes a question for the jury. 

Com. v. Jones, 297 Pa. 326; Com. v. Aston, 227, Pa. 112; 

Com. v. Epps. 298 Pa. 377, made in presence of codefendant is admissible if not denied.

6. Admissions, Silence, Failure to Deny Statement 

When a man, at full liberty to speak and not in the course of a judicial inquiry, is charged with a crime (or allusion being made to it¹) and remains silent, that is, makes no denial of the accusation by word or gesture, his silence is a circumstance which may be left to the jury.2 

1 Com. v. Ford, 86 Pa. Super. 483, but see Com. v. Coyne, 115 Pa. Super. 23, where it is held defendant must be "charged" with crime. 

2 Ettinger v. Com., 98 Pa. 338, 345. 

7. When no Evidence of Good Character In troduced There Can be No Rebuttal When no evidence of good character has been introduced, the Commonwealth may not either by evidence or argument refer to defendant's character or failure to call character witnesses. 

Com. v. Weber, 167 Pa. 153, new trial granted; Com. v. 

Dennison, 20 Luz L. R. 15; Com. v. Corsino, 20 Luz L. R. 407. 

8. Examination of Defendant, Other Offenses, Records to Impeach 

Any person charged with any crime and called as a witness in his own behalf, shall not be asked, and if asked, shall not be required to answer, any question tending to show that he has committed or been charged with, or been convicted of, any offense other than the one wherewith he shall then be charged, or tending to show that he has been of bad character or reputation; unless : 

1. He shall have at such trial, personally or by his advocate, asked questions of the witnesses for the prosecution with a view to establish his own good reputation or character, or has given evidence tending to prove his own good character or reputation; or 

2. He shall have testified at such trial against a defendant charged with the same offense.¹

This act refers to cross-examination only, and its purpose was to prevent cross-examining the defendant concerning the commission of other crimes "where there was neither the ability nor expectation of proving the fact suggested if it were denied by the defendant." Records of conviction of certain crimes, however, continue to be admissible to impeach the credibility of a defendant, but they are confined to felonies and misdemeanors in the nature of crimen falsi. 

Conduct derogatory to a witness's character for veracity may be proved by showing by other witnesses that he has been convicted of an infamous offense. No collateral issue of fact is thus raised, as the record establishes the fact; only crimes of felony or misdemeanor in the nature of crimen falsi are admissible to affect credibility. The term "crimen falsi" is not well defined at common law. Its extent and meaning is nowhere laid down with precision, but it generally applied to crimes which disqualified a person as a witness. "The term involves the element of falsehood, and includes everything which has a tendency to injuriously affect the administration of justice by the introduction of falsehood and fraud." It has been held to include, also, forgery, perjury, subornation of perjury, suppression of testimony by bribery or conspiracy to procure the absence of a witness, barratry, the fraudulent making or alteration of a writing to the perjudice of another man's right, robbery and larceny by bailee.2

This act must be read in connection with the Act of 

May 14, 1925, P. L. 759, 18 P. S. 2222, and, when this is done, the former act is found to be modified as it relates to homicide trials, and a defendant may be cross-examined as to other offenses as an aid to the jury in determining the punishment to be inflicted and in ascertaining whether defendant is entitled to mercy. The trial judge should be very careful to explain and emphasize this limitation in his charge to the jury.3

In homicide cases, evidence, in proper form (by records) of prior conviction of crime is admissible for the sole purpose of aiding the jury in determining the penalty to be inflicted if the jury finds defendant guilty of murder in the first degree.*

When the objection is to asking the question the defendant must request the withdrawal of a juror.5 (See Proof

Of Other Offenses, Par. 9). 

1 Act of March 15, 1911, P. L. 20, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 711; Com. v. Brown, 93 Pa. Super. 190, reversing; Com. v. Anthony, 91 Pa.

Super. 518; a witness called as a character witness for defendant may be asked if he has not heard that the defendant has been accused of crime, Com. v. Engle, 73 Pa. Sup. 138. 

2 Com. v. Schambers, 110 Pa. Super. 61; Com. v. Benedict, 114 Pa. Super. 183; Com. v. Wiswesser, 123 Pa. Super. (1936). 

3 Com. v. Flood, 302 Pa. 190; Com. v. Williams, 307 Pa. 

134, 149. 

4 Com. v. Harris, 314 Pa. 81; Com. v. Stabinsky, 313 Pa. 231. 

5 Com. v. Diagicoble, 85 Pa. Super. 305; Com. v. Heckman, 113 Pa. Super. 70. 

9. Proof of Other Offenses 

As a general rule, at common law, on a prosecution for a particular crime, a distinct crime unconnected with that laid in the indictment cannot be given in evidence against a prisoner as substantive proof of the crime for which he is being tried. Nor can evidence of such crime be received to impeach general character, or to prove a normal disposition to commit the crime for which the accused is on trial. 

There are, however, many well recognized exceptions where the commission of another offense by the defendant may be received in evidence. Prior convictions can be admitted in evidence to show intent, scienter, motive, identity, plan, or the accused to be one of an organization banded together to commit crimes of the sort charged, or that such prior conviction or criminal act formed a part of a chain, or was one of a sequence of acts, or became part of the history of the event on trial, or was part of the natural development of the facts; also to prove the mental condition when the defense is insanity, or to rebut the inference of mistake, or to show a guilty knowledge.¹ Felonies and misdemeanors in the nature of crimen falsi may be proved by records of convictions to impeach defendant's credibility.2 But proof of other crimes may not be established by cross-examination of

the defendant when he has not introduced evidence of good character (3) (See Par. 8). 

1 Com. v. Williams, 307 Pa. 134, 148; Com. v. Bennett, 110 Pa. Super. 303; Com. v. Chalfa, 313 Pa. 175. 

2 Com. v. Schambers, 110 Pa. Super. 61; Com. v. Benedict, 114 Pa. Super. 183. 

3 Com. v. Pezzner, 78 Pa. Super, 286; Act of March 15, 1911, P. L. 20, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 711; a character witness for the defendant may be cross-examined as to other offenses of the defendant to test his knowledge, Com. v. McClelland, 82 Pa. 

Super. 437; Com. v. Wiswesser, 123 Pa. Super. (1936). 

10. Husband and Wife, Adultery, Desertion, Bodily Injury

Nor shall husband or wife be competent or permitted to testify against each other, or in support of a criminal charge of adultery alleged to have been committed by or with the other, except that in proceedings for desertion and maintenance, and in any criminal proceeding against either for bodily injuries, or violence attempted, done, or threatened upon the other, or upon the minor children of such husband and wife, or the minor children of either of them, or any minor child in their care or custody, or in the care of or custody of either of them, each shall be a competent witness against the other; and except, also that either of them shall be competent merely to prove the fact of marriage, in support of a criminal charge of adultery or bigamy alleged to have been committed by or with the other. 

Act of May 11, 1911, P. L. 269, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 683; Com. v. 

Cartusciello, 100 Pa. Super, 473, one spouse may make an information against the other for adultery; both competent in divorce action, Act of May 2, 1929, P. L. 1237, Sec. 50, 23 P. S. 50;

Com. v. D; Matteo, 123 Pa. Super. (1936), wife may not testify to non-access in bastardy proceedings. 

11. When Wife May Testify in Rebuttal 

In any criminal proceeding brought against the husband, if he makes defense at the trial upon any ground which attacks the wife's character or conduct, she shall be a competent witness in rebuttal for the commonwealth. 

Act of April 11, 1899, P. L. 41, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 685; Com. v. 

Garanchoskie, 251 Pa. 247. 13

12. Confidential Communications Between Spouses 

Nor shall either husband or wife be competent or permitted to testify to confidential communications made by one to the other, unless this privilege be waived upon the trial. 

Act of April 27, 1909, P. L. 179, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 684. 

13. Confidential Communications to Attorney

Nor shall counsel be competent to testify to confidential communications made to him by his client, or the client be compelled to disclose the same, unless in either case the privilege be waived upon the trial by the client. 

Act of April 27, 1909, P. L. 179, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 686. 

14. Confidential Relation, Physician and Patient 

At common law a physician was not disqualified from testifying to information acquired while attending patients in a professional capacity. The Act of June 7, 1907, P. L. 

462 (28 P. S. 328) which provides that a physician shall not be allowed to testify to information received by him in a professional capacity, when such information tends to blacken the character of the patient, applies only to civil cases. 

Com. v. Edwards, 318 Pa. 1, jail physician. 

15. Infants

A child of any age capable of distinguishing between good and evil may be examined on oath. The admissibility of children as witnesses depends not merely upon their possessing a competent degree of understanding, but also in part upon their having received such a degree of religious instruction as not to be ignorant of the nature of an oath, or of the consequences of falsehood. The determination of the infant's competency is judicial, the examination as to competency is a part of the trial, it must be made in public, and in court. The decision must be founded upon the opinion

of the judge from the examination which he makes, and cannot be referred to somebody else. 

Com. v. Capero, 35 Pa. Super. 392, child must be sworn; 

Piepke v. P. & R. Ry. Co., 242 Pa. 321, court must examine as to competency; Com. v. Furman, 211 Pa. 549, child eight held competent; test is whether child comprehends difference between truth and falsehood, and understands that it is his duty to tell the truth. 

16. Dying Declarations, Abortion 

The ante-mortem statements of any woman, who shall hereafter die in consequences of any criminal acts producing or intended to produce a miscarriage of such woman, as to the cause of her injuries shall be competent evidence on the trial of any person charged with the commission of such injuries, with like effect and under like circumstances as apply to dying declarations in prosecutions for felonious homicide. Provided, however, that before such statement shall be submitted to the jury as evidence, the Commonwealth shall, by competent and satisfactory evidence, prove that such woman was of sound mind at the time such ante-mortem statements were made; and provided further, that no conviction shall be had upon the uncorrobated declaration of such woman. 

Act of June 26, 1895, P. L. 387, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 583; Com. v. 

Thomas, 94 Pa. Super. 353; Com. v. Shearer, 82 Pa. Super. 355; 

Com. v. Kline, 66 Pa. Super. 285. 

17. Exhumation of Body 

A petition for the exhumation of a body must show what it is proposed to develop through an autopsy in addition to what has been shown by the examination of the coroner's physician. Notice of the application to exhume must be given to near relatives of the deceased. 

Com. v. Marshall, 287 Pa. 512.

18. View by Jury 

A jury is not permitted to view the place where the crime was committed without the knowledge or consent of the court or in the absence of defendant or his counsel. It seems the defendant may waive his right to be present. 

Com. v. Coontz, 288 Pa. 74; Com. v. Corsino, 261 Pa. 593; 

Com. v. Van Horn, 188 Pa. 143; Com. v. Beattie, 9 D. & C. 238; 

Com. v. Westwood, 324 Pa. (1936). 

19. Demonstrations Before Jury 

The making of experiments or demonstrations in the presence of the jury is a matter within the discretion of the trial judge. 

Com. v. Stallone, 281 Pa. 41, reversing; Com. v. Barille, 270 Pa. 388; Com. v. Fry, 198 Pa. 379; Com. v. Wilson, 44 Pa. 

Super. 183. 

20. Photographs, Clothes

The admission of photographs and the use to be made of them must necessarily rest largely in the discretion of the trial judge, but if their use does not aid the jury in its investigation of the crime, and they only serve to arouse the jurors' sympathy and excite their prejudice against the accused they shall not be admitted.¹

It is error to admit in evidence clothes worn by deceased where it serves no legitimate purpose and does not aid in solving any issue in the case, and its only effect is to inflame the minds of the jury,2 otherwise where there is a question of the location of wounds, position of deceased, or other material fact. 

1 Com. v. Sydlosky, 305 Pa. 406; Com. v. Winter, 289 Pa. 

284; Com. v. Ware, 279 Pa. 282; Com. v. Letherman, 320 Pa. 

261, chart used; Com. v. Dreamer, 324 Pa. (1936). 

2 Com. v. Flood, 302 Pa. 190..

21. Sending out Papers

What papers shall go out with the jury is largely in the discretion of the trial judge, with which the appellate court will not interfere, unless there has been a manifest abuse of discretion. 

Com. v. Prescott, 284 Pa. 255, defendant's written statement sent out; Com. v. Torti, 283 Pa. 43; Com. v. Brown, 264 

Pa. 85. 

22. Indictments, Joint Defendants 

The bill of indictment is always sent out with the jury, and in case of joint indictments where severances are granted, it is much better practice to make the entries as to the trials of the different defendants on paper separate from the bill of indictment, or when this is not done, to cover the entries on the bill before it goes out with the jury. 

Com. v. Reinschreiber, 276 Pa. 262. 

23. Fornication, Reputed Father 

Any single or unmarried woman having a child born of her body, same shall be sufficient to convict such single or unmarried woman of fornication; and a man by such woman charged to be the father of such bastard child, shall be the reputed father, and she persisting in said charge, in the time of her extremity of labor or afterwards, in open court, upon the trial of such person so charged, same shall be given in evidence, in order to convict such person of fornication. 

Act of March 31, 1860 P. L. 382, Sec. 37; 18 P. S. 711; Com. v. McFeaters, 100 Pa. Super. 169. 

24. Expert Testimony Forgery, Counterfeiting 

Upon the trial of any indictment for making or passing and uttering, any false, forged or counterfeited coin, or bank note, the court may receive in evidence, to establish either the genuineness or falsity of such coin or note, the oaths or affirmations of witnesses who may, by experience

and habit, have become experts in judging of the genuineness, or otherwise of such coin or paper, and such testimony may be submitted to the jury without first requiring proof of the handwriting or other tests of genuineness, and the court shall not require the commonwealth to produce the bank charter, but the jury may find the fact upon other evidence, under the direction of the court. 

Act of March 31, 1860 P. L. 427, Sec. 55; 19 P. S. 581. 

25. Religious Belief may not be Shown

No witness shall be questioned in any judicial proceeding concerning his religious belief nor shall any evidence be heard upon the subject, for the purpose of affecting either his competency or credibility. 

Act of April 23, 1909, P. L. 140, Sec. 3; 28 P. S. 313. 

26. Religious Opinion Not to Disqualify 

Hereafter the capacity of any person to testify in any judicial proceeding shall be in no wise affected by his opinion on matters of religion. 

Act of April 23, 1909, P. L. 140, Sec. 2; 28 P. S. 312. 

27. Notes of Testimony at Former Trial 

Whenever any person has been summoned as a witness, either for the commonwealth or the defense, in any criminal proceeding before a court of record, and the defendant has been present and has had an opportunity to examine or cross-examine, if such witness shall afterwards die or be too ill to attend court, or be out of the jurisdiction so that he cannot be effectively served with a subpoena, or if he cannot be found, or if he becomes incompetent to testify for any legally sufficient reason properly proved, notes of his examination shall be competent evidence upon a subsequent trial of the same criminal issue; but for the purpose of contradicting a witness, the testimony given by him in another or in a former proceeding may be orally proved. 

Act of May 23, 1887, P. L. 158, Sec. 3; 19 P. S. 582; Com. v. 

McFeaters, 100 Pa. Super. 169; Com. v. Cilione, 293 Pa. 208.

28. Testimony Before Magistrate

The admission of proof of testimony of a deceased or absent witness taken before a magistrate at a preliminary hearing will be approved when the right of cross-examination has been exercised or waived. 

Com. v. Lenousky, 206 Pa. 277; Com. v. Bone, 64 Pa. Super. 

44, when testimony has been given through an interpreter, the interpeter must be called, the notes have no probative value; 

Com. v. Insano, 268 Pa. 1. 

29. Taking Testimony of Non-Residents 

It shall be lawful for any defendant in any action to be tried in any of the criminal courts of this commonwealth, desiring to take the testimony of any witness or witnesses residing without the State of Pennsylvania, but within the United States, upon giving twenty-four hours notice, in writing to the district attorney, to present a petition, verified by affidavit, to said court or a judge thereof, setting forth said facts, that the testimony is material, that the witness or witnesses reside without the State of Pennsylvania, have not been and cannot be subpoenaed, or their attendance otherwise procured, and the name of the witness or witnesses, whereupon the court or a judge thereof, may grant a rule to take said testimony, and in case said rule is granted, the court or a judge thereof shall, at the same time, make an order providing what notice of time and place is to be given to the district attorney. 

Act of Apr. 27, 1909, P. L. 258, 19 P. S. 611-615. 

30. Entry and Service of Rule 

Wherever any rule shall have been granted the said rule shall be entered in the office of the clerk of courts, and a copy thereof, with notice of the time and place and names. of the witness or witnesses, signed by the party or his attorney, shall be served on the district attorney, which notice shall be in accordance with the order of court. 

Act of Apr. 27, 1909, P. L. 258, 19 P. S. 611-615.

31. Authority to Take Depositions

The testimony of witnesses taken may be read in evidence upon the trial in any of the criminal courts of this 

Commonwealth; Provided, however, if it shall be made to appear upon the trial that the witness whose deposition has been taken is in attendance, or has been or can be subpoenaed, or his attendance otherwise procured, the said deposition shall not be competent evidence. 

Act of Apr. 27, 1909, P. L. 258, 19 P. S. 611-615. 

32. Liability for Costs and Fees

The costs, fees, and expenses of taking such testimony shall be born by the party in whose behalf the same is taken, but the actual expenses of the district attorney in connection therewith shall be paid by the proper county. 

Act of April 27, 1909, P. L. 258. Sec. 1, 2, 3, 4, 5; 19 P. S. 

611-615; Com. v. Sabo, 83 Pa. Super. 166. 

33. Abscoring Witnesses

If any person who shall have been required by virtue of any writ of subpoena or other legal process, to attend and testify in any prosecution for forgery, perjury, or felony before any criminal court, judge, justice, or other judicial tribunal in this Commonwealth, or who may have been recognized or held to bail to attend as a witness on behalf of the commonwealth or defendant, before any court having jurisdiction, to testify in any prosecution as aforesaid, shall unlawfully and wilfully, from this commonwealth, or from the jurisdiction of such court, and with intent to defeat the ends of public justice, abscond, elope or conceal himself, and refuse to appear as required by said subpoena or other legal process or recognizance of bail, shall be guilty of a misdemeanor, and being thereof convicted, shall be sentenced to pay a fine not exceeding two thousand dollars, or undergo an imprisonment not exceeding two years, or both, or either, at the discretion of the court. (See Bail, Material witness). 

Act of Feb. 24, 1870 P. L. 34, Sec. 1; 18 P. S. 382.

34. Dissuading Witnesses from Testifying 

If any person shall unlawfully dissuade, hinder or prevent any witness from attending and testifying, who may have been required to attend and testify, either before any committee of the legislature of this state, or any civil or criminal court, judge, justice or other judicial tribunal thereof, by virtue of any writ of subpoena or other legal process, or may have been recognized to attend as a witness on behalf of the commonwealth or any defendant, before any court having jurisdiction, to testify in any case depending or about to be tried in such court, any person so offending shall be guilty of a misdemeanor and, being thereof convicted shall be sentenced to pay a fine not exceeding five hundred dollars, and undergo an imprisonment not exceeding one year. 

Act of March 31, 1860 P. L. 382, Sec. 11; 18 P. S. 381; indictment, Com. v. Herr, 27 Dist. Repts. 976. 

35. Jurors as Witnesses, Witnesses as Jurors 

Jurors are not incompetent as witnesses in either criminal or civil issues. They have no interest that disqualifies them, and there is no rule of public policy that excludes them. 

Knowledge of the facts of a case does not disqualify jurors.¹

Every juror, impanelled in any case, who shall know anything relative to the matter in controversy in such case, shall disclose the same in open court, before the jury shall retire to consider their verdict.2 

1 Com. v. Sullivan, 91 Pa. Super. 544. 

2 Act of April 14, 1834, P. L. 333, Sec. 160; 17 P. S. 915; 

Com. v. Kulp, 5 Dist. Repts. 468; Com. v. Reber, 10 Dist. Repts. 683. 

36. Disqualification by Perjury

A person who has been convicted in a court of this commonwealth of perjury, which term is hereby declared to include subornation of perjury, shall not be a competent witness for any purpose, although his sentence may have been fully complied with, unless the judgment of conviction has been judicially set aside or reversed, or unless the pro-

ceedings be one to punish or prevent injury or violence attempted, done, or threatened to his person or property; in which case he shall be competent to testify. 

Act of April 27, 1909, P. L. 179, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 682; Com. v. Arcurio, 92 Pa. Super. 404; Act of March 31, 1860 P. L. 382, Sec. 14. 

37. Subpoenaing Witness to Testify in Another State 

If a judge of a court of record in any state, which by its laws has made provisions for commanding persons within that state to attend and testify in criminal prosecutions in this Commonwealth, certifies under the seal of such court that there is a criminal prosecution pending in such court, that a person being within this Commonwealth is a material witness in such prosecution, and that his prosence will be required for a specified number of days, any judge of a criminal court of record in the county in which such person is, upon presentation of such certificate, shall fix a time and place for hearing and shall notify the witness of such time and place. 

Act of July 15, 1935, P. L. 1017; Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 616. 

38. Hearing and Issuance of Subpoena 

If at the hearing, the judge determines that the witness is material and necesasry, that it will not cause undue hardship to the witness to be compelled to attend and testify in the prosecution in the other state, that the witness will not be compelled to travel more than one thousand miles to reach the place of trial, and that the laws of the state in which the prosecution is pending and of any other state through which the witness may be required to pass will give to him protection from arrest and the service of civil and criminal process, he shall issue a subpoena, with a copy of the certificate attached, directing the witness to attend and testify in the court where the prosecution is pending at a time and place specified in the subpoena. 

Act of July 15, 1935, P. L. 1017, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 617.

39. Failure to Obey Subpoena

If the witness who is subpoenaed, as above provided, after being paid or tendered by some properly authorized person the sum of ten cents a mile for each mile which he shall be required to travel to and from the court where the prosecution is pending, and five dollars for each day that he is required to travel and attend as a witness, fails, without good cause, to attend and testify as directed in the subpoena, The shall be punished in the manner provided for the punishment of any witness who disobeys a subpoena issued from a criminal court of record in this Commonwealth. 

Act of July 15, 1935, P. L. 1017, Sec. 3, 19 P. S. 618. 

40. Subpoenaing Witness in Another State to Testify in This State 

If a person in any state, which by its laws has made provisions for commanding persons within its borders to attend and testify in criminal prosecutions in this Commonwealth, is a material witness in a prosecution pending in a criminal court of record in this Commonwealth, a pudge of such court may issue a certificate, under the seal of the court, stating these facts and specifying the number of days the witness will be required. This certificate shall be presented to a judge of a court of record in the county in which the witness is found. 

Act of July 15, 1935, P. L. 1017, Sec. 4, 19 P. S. 619. 

41. Payment of Witnesses 

If the witness is subpoenaed to attend and testify in the criminal prosecution in this Commonwealth, he shall be tendered the sum of ten cents a mile for each mile which he shall be required to travel to and from the court where the prosecution is pending, and five dollars for each day that he is required to travel and attend as a witness, which sum shall be advanced by the proper authorities upon requisition by the district attorney. A witness who has appeared in accordance with the provisions of the subpoena shall not be re-

quired to remain within this Commonwealth a longer period of time than the period mentioned in the certificate. 

Act of July 15, 1935, P. L. 1017, Sec. 5, 19 P. S. 620. 

42. Exemption from Arrest and Service of Process 

If a person comes into this Commonwealth in obedience to a subpoena directing him to attend and testify in a criminal prosecution in this Commonwealth, he shall not, while in this Commonwealth pursuant to such subpoena, be subject to arrest or the service of process, civil or criminal, in connection with matters which arose before his entrance into this Commonwealth under the subpoena.

If a person passes through this Commonwealth while going to another state in obedience to a subpoena to attend and testify in a criminal prosecution in that state or while returning therefrom, he shall not, while so passing through, be subject to arrest or the service of proceess, civil or criminal, in connection with matters which arose before his entrance into this Commonwealth under the subpoena. 

Act of July 15, 1935, P. L. 1017, Sec. 6, 19 P. S. 621. 

43. Uniformity of Interpretation

This section shall be so interpreted and construed as to effectuate its general purpose to make uniform the law of the states which enact it. 

Act of July 15, 1935, P. L. 1017, Sec. 7, 19 P. S. 622.

Chapter XXII CHARGE, DEMURRER TO EVIDENCE 1. DEMURRER TO EVIDENCE 2. PURPOSE OF CHARGE 3. REVIEWING EVIDENCE 4. EXPRESSION OF OPINION BY COURT 5. JURORS DETERMINE FACTS 6. REQUEST FOR INSTRUCTIONS 7. HOMICIDE CASES, POINTS AND OPINION TO BE FILED 8. POINTS, RIGHT TO 9. NOT NECESSARY FOR THE EXACT POINT TO BE USED 10. BINDING INSTRUCTIONS 11. ADDITIONAL INSTRUCTIONS 12. ERRORS OF LAW 13. ABSENCE OF PRISONER AND COUNSEL, HARMLESS ERROR 14. GENERAL EXCEPTION TO CHARGE 

1. Demurrer to Evidence

A demurrer admits all facts which the evidence of the commonwealth tends to prove, and all inferences reasonably deducible therefrom. Upon joinder of the commonwealth therein it becomes the duty of the trial judge to discharge the jury, and enter such judgment as the evidence warrants, and it is error for the court to hear additional testimony. The commonwealth may refuse to join in the demurrer and insist upon a jury trial. Though unusual, a demurrer may be entered after the case is closed, and the jury may then be discharged and the question of guilt or innocence of the defendant left to the determination of the court. The demurrer should be presented at the close of the Commonwealth's case. 

Com. v. Robinson, et al. 317 Pa. 321, 334; Com. v. Greevy, 271 Pa. 95; Com. v. Pent, 112 Pa. Super. 215, agreed statement of facts; Com. v. Kolsky, 100 Pa. Super. 596; Com. v. Spohn, 201

95 Pa. Super. 261; Com. v. Ernesto, 93 Pa. Super. 339; Com. v.. 

Sonis, 81 Pa. Super. 205; Com. v. Williams, 71 Pa. Super. 311;: 

Com. v. Smith, 97 Pa. Super. 157. 

2. Purpose of Charge 

The main purpose of a charge is to state and explain. the law, not to carry on a process of general reasoning which tends to influence the jury in either direction. A chargeshould not be justly subject to the criticism of containing an over-abundance of argument, or reasoning, which might be better left to counsel in the case. 

Com. v. Green, 292 Pa. 579. 

3. Reviewing Evidence 

Upon a trial for murder the judge should give a general review of the evidence on the one side and the other, which fairly and adequately presents the respective contentions. of the parties; but the manner in which the evidence shall be dealt with must depend upon the circumstances of each case, and to a degree upon the line of arguments pursued by counsel in addressing the jury. 

Com. v. Russogulo, 263 Pa. 93. 

4. Expression of Opinion by Court 

It is the undoubted right of a judge, and often it is his duty, to express to the jury his opinion of the weight and the effect of the evidence. The one limitation of the right is that there must be a reasonable ground for his statement and that it is not made as a binding direction, but leaves the jury free to act. 

Com. v. Edwards, 318 Pa. 1; Com. v. Nafus, 303 Pa. 418; 

Com. v. Thompkins, 267 Pa. 541, reversing; Com. v. Hoffman, 103 Pa. Super. 433; Com. v. Gross, 89 Pa. Super. 387, dissenting opinion; Com. v. Jones, 89 Pa. Super. 219; Com. v. Stabinsky, 313 Pa. 231; Com. v. Pursel, 110 Pa. Super. 110; Com. v. Watson, 117 Pa. Super. 594 reversing; Com. v. Marcinko, 242 Pa.. 

388, reversing.

5. Jurors Determine Facts 

Jurors have the right to determine the law and the facts under the direction of the court, which means only that they have the right to determine, by a general verdict, the result of applying the law, as stated by the court, to the facts as they find them.¹

The trial judge cannot be charged with error where in answer to a question by a juryman as to the penalty he tells the jury that they have nothing to do with the penalty, that they should not consider it, or enter into any discussion about it, but that any recommendation of mercy or severity would be considered.2 

1 Com v. Castlelana, 277 Pa. 117; Com. v. Bryson, 276 

Pa. 566; Com. v. Bednorciki, 264 Pa. 124; Com. v. Fahey, 113 

Pa. Super. 598; Com. v. Long, 100 Pa. Super. 150; Com. v. 

Susanek, 88 Pa. Super. 428. 

2 Com. v. Martin, 34 Pa. Super. 451; Com. v. Switzer, 134 

Pa. 383. 

6. Request for Instructions

If the court fails to charge as fully upon some points as counsel thinks proper, attention should be called to the fact before the jury leaves the box so that the court may correct any omission, and if counsel fails to make such request the court cannot be held in error, unless it clearly appears that defendant has been materially prejudiced. 

Com. v. Delfino, 259 Pa. 272; Com. v. Wilt, 76 Pa. Super. 

106; Com. v. Wilston, 73 Pa. Super. 161; Com. v. Gray, 72 Pa. 

Super. 279; Com. v. Booker, 72 Pa. Super. 395; Com. v. Keegan, 70 Pa. Super. 436. 

7. Homicide Cases, Points and Opinion to be Filled 

If during the trial upon any indictment for murder or voluntary manslaughter, the court shall be requested by the defendant or defendants to give an opinion upon any point submitted and stated in writing, it shall be the duty of the court to answer the same fully, and file the point and answer with the records of the case. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 58; 19 P. S. 1183.

8. Points, Right to

The defendant is entitled to a clear statement of the law in answer to every point properly drawn that is material and applicable to the case, and warranted by the facts in evidence, unless the point is fully covered in a general charge. 

Com. v. Lockett, 291 Pa. 319; Com. v. Hand, 59 Pa. Super. 

286; Com. v. Nathan, 93 Pa. Super. 193, points must be presented in writing. 

9. Not Necessary for the Exact Point to be Used

It is not necessary that the exact point be used, but the trial judge may chose his own form of expression, if it fully and with substantial accuracy expresses the law contained in the point. When the matter contained in the point is properly covered and answered in a general charge it is not necessary to read the point to the jury. 

Com. v. Lewis, 222 Pa. 302; Miller v. Smith Woolen Mach. 

Co., 220 Pa. 181; Creashen v. Bromley Bros., 214 Pa. 15; Mc- 

Henry v. Bulifant, 207 Pa. 15; Com. v. Barner, 199 Pa. 335; 

Fleming v. Dixon, 194 Pa. 67; Com. v. Evans, 36 Lanc. L. R. 25. 

10. Binding Instructions 

A point for binding instructions is the proper method of questioning the sufficiency of the commonwealth's evidence to sustain a conviction.¹

When at the conclusion of the commonwealth's case the defendant moves for binding instructions but does not rest, the refusal of the motion is not error and the motion must be renewed after the evidence is all in, if the defendant desires to raise the question of refusal on appeal.2

The court should in a proper case direct an acquittal,³ but where the evidence, if believed, is sufficient to sustain a verdict of guilty the case should not be taken from the jury.¹ 

The request for binding instructions and points must be made in writing.5 

1 Com. v. Nathan, 93 Pa. Super. 193, request must be made in writing; Com. v. Verner, 74 Pa. Super. 529; Com. v. P. R. R.

Co., 72 Pa. 353, commonwealth may not ask the court to direct a verdict in its favor; Com. v. Gamble, 36 Pa. Super. 146; Com. v. Havrilla, 38 Pa. Super. 292. 

2 Com. v. DeGrange, 97 Pa. Super. 181; Com. v. Hanley, 15 Pa. Super. 271; Com. v. Popp, 87 Pa. Super. 193; Com. v. 

Sonis, 81 Pa. Super. 205; Com. v. Kline, 9 D. & C. 448. 

3 Krause v. Com. 93 Pa. Super. 418; Com. v. Widovich, 93 

Pa. Super. 323. 

4 Com. v. Pane, 110 Pa. Super. 367; Com. v. Beati, 86 Pa. 

Super. 567; Com. v. Kline, 9 D. & C. 448. 

5 Act of March 24, 1877, P. L. 38, 12 P. S. 678; Act of May 

24, 1923, P. L. 439, 12 P. S. 1197; Com. v. Nathan, 93 Pa. Super. 

193; Com. v. Deibert; 106 Pa. Super. 497. 

11. Additional Instructions 

Where counsel for defendant fails to ask for further and fuller instructions or for corrections in the charge, although opportunity is given him, he cannot on appeal, complain of errors in the statement of facts by the trial judge either on the ground of inaccuracy or insufficiency. 

Com. v. Mendola, 294 Pa. 353. 

12. Errors of Law

Errors of law in a charge are not rectified by asking counsel if anything has been omitted or if there is anything to which they desire the court to refer. It is only inadvertent misstatements of facts and objections to the insufficiency of a charge which are waived by the silence of counsel in such circumstances.¹

Misstatement of law in one part of the charge is not necessarily cured by a correct statement in another part, or by answer to points, unless the erroneous instruction is expressly withdrawn from the jury.

The use of extracts from appellate court decisions to illustrate the principle stated in the charge, while not reversable error, is a practice to be discouraged.³ 

1 Com. v. Morris, 87 Pa. Super. 66. 

2 Com. v. Prescott, 284 Pa. 255; Com. v. Daily, 280 Pa. 57; 

Com. v. Ross, 266 Pa. 580; Com. v. Divomte, 262 Pa. 504; Com. v. Wooley, 259 Pa. 249; Com. v. Corsino, 261 Pa. 593.. 

3 Com. v. Mendola, 294 Pa. 353. 14

13. Absence of Prisoner and Counsel, Harmless Error

For a trial judge to communicate any instructions to the jury in the absence of both the prisoner and his counsel is bad practice, and has in some cases been held to be error; but under the modern view, after a criminal prosecution has ended in a conviction, and the evidence sustains the verdict, even where an error in practice has occurred, an ap pellate court should not reverse unless it "feels not simply that the mistake in question possibly influenced the jury against the defendant, but also that it is strongly probable the verdict reflects such adverse influence, in other words, unless the court is convinced not only that error was committed but also that such error did in all probability harm the defendant."1

When error is committed which may have been harmful, the sentence must be reversed.2 

1 Com. v. Kelly, 292 Pa. 418. 

2 Com. v. Gray, 265 Pa. 537, reversing Superior court, erroneous charge in larceny; Com. v. Opolka, 69 Pa. Super. 230; 

Com. v. Mango, 101 Pa. Super. 385. 

14. General Exception to Charge 

A general exception may be taken to the charge without allowance by the trial judge, and without the assignment of any reason therefore, at any time before the verdict is rendered, or thereafter by leave of court; and it shall be thereupon noted by the official stenographer, and shall have all the effect of exceptions duly written out, signed, and sealed by the trial judge, at the time of the trial. Such exception shall operate and be construed to be an exception to the charge as a whole and to each and every part thereof, and to the answer of the court to each and every point where requests for charge are filed, and to the failure or refusal of the court to answer any requests filed, but it shall not operate or be construed to be an exception to any matter of fact inadvertently misstated by the court unless the court's attention is called to the alleged misstatement prior to the taking of such exception.¹

When an exception is so taken, the charge, notes of testimony, points, and answers shall be filed and become part of the record for the purpose of the assignment of errors, without any request that the charge and notes of testimony be reduced to writing and filed of record, and without any request that the points and answers be filed of record with the charge.2

Errors cannot be assigned as to what was not said without a request to say it,³ and ordinarily the supreme court will not reverse when inadequacy of charge is assigned as error. The charge must be considered as a whole. However, the Supreme Court said in Com. v. O'Brien, 312 Pa. 543, "A man is not to be deprived of his liberty and reputation because of the inadvertence of a trial judge or the carelessness of his counsel in failing to call the attention of the trial court to palpable error which offends against the fundamentals of a fair and impartial trial." 

1 Sautter v. Rowland, 285 Pa. 212; Weiss v. London G. & 

A. Co. Ltd., 285 Pa. 251; Com. v. Bell, 88 Pa. Super. 216; Com. v. Norris, 87 Pa. Super. 66, misstatements of fact and insufficiency not reversible error in absence of request; Gilbert v. 

Yellow Cab. Co. 87 Pa. Super. 365; Com. v. Ford, 86 Pa. Super. 483. 

2 Act of May 24, 1923, P. L. 439, Sec. 1; 12 P. S. 1197.

100; Com. v. Millien, 291 Pa. 291; Com. v. DePalmo, 286 Pa. 434.
