33. MOTIVE 34. PERJURY 35. PRESUMPTION OF LAW, OF FACT 36. REASONABLE DOUBT 37. RES GESTAE 38. RIOT 39. RIOT, INCITING TO 40. SELF-DEFENSE 41. SELF-DEFENSE, VOLUNTARY MANSLAUGH- TER, DIFFERENCE 42. SELF-DEFENSE, ASSAULT AND BATTERY 43. DEFENSE OF HABITATION, FAMILY, SERV- ANT, PREVENTION OF FELONY 44. STOLEN PROPERTY, POSSESSION OF 45. UNWRITTEN LAW 1. Abandonment of Criminal Enterprise 

The defendant could have abandoned the enterprise, and given sufficient time to the others to do likewise, and thus have relieved himself from liability for subsequent acts of his fellows. But he cannot escape responsibility for an act which is the probable consequence of the criminal scheme which he has helped to devise and carry forward. To have this result, there must be an actual and effective voluntary withdrawal before the act in question has become so imminent that its avoidance is practically out of the question. 

Whatever may be the other requirements of an effective abandonment of a criminal enterprise there must be some appreciable interval between the alleged abandonment and the act from responsibility for which escape is sought. The process of detachment must be such as to show not only a determination upon the part of the accused to go no further, but also such as to give his coconspirators a reasonable opportunity if they desire, to follow his example and refrain from further action before the act in question is committed. 

Com. v. Doris, 287 Pa. 547, 552; Com. v. Earl, 91 Pa. Super. 447.

2. Abortion 

No conviction shall be had upon the uncorroborated dying declaration of the woman. 

Act of June 26, 1895, P. L. 387; 19 P. S. 583; Com. v. Shearer, 82 Pa. Super. 355; Com. v. Heffelfinger, 82 Pa. Super. 351. 

3. Accident, Mistake

The evidence of the commonwealth, in order to convict, must exclude beyond a reasonable doubt the hypothesis that the death occurred by accident, mistake, or the act of a person other than the defendant.¹

Evidence offered by an accused of the commission of a crime by another must be limited to such facts as are inconsistent with his own guilt and that raise an inference or presumption of his own innocence.

In a criminal case, a defendant will not be permitted to establish facts which raise mere conjectures; his admissible proof must be such as is calculated to fairly raise a doubt of his guilt.2

The burden of proof in homicide cases, where the defense of accidental killing is set up, does not shift but rests on the commonwealth to show that the killing was wilful and intentional.³ 

1 Com. v. Marshall, 287 Pa. 512. 

2 Com. v. Winegrad, 119 Pa. Super. 78. 

3 Com. v. Deitrick, 218 Pa. 36. 

4. Accessory After the Fact 

;

An accessory after the fact is one who, knowing a felony to have been committed by another, receives, relieves, comforts or assists the felon, or in any manner aids him to escape arrest or punishment. In order to fix the guilt of a party charged as accessory after the fact, it is essential that a felony has been committed and that it is complete. 

Another requisite to the conviction of one as an accessory after the fact is that he must know of the felony

having been committed, and that the person aided is the party who committed it, and intend to shield him from the law. 

Com. v. Costa, 2 D. & C. 612; Com. v. Mazarella, 279 Pa. 

465, mere failure to report crime does not make one an accessory. 

5. Accomplice, Testimony of

The rule is that it is not considered safe to convict a defendant on the uncorroborated testimony of an accomplice, but the jury has the power to convict if his testimony satisfies them beyond a reasonable doubt of the defendant's guilt. 

The court should point out, in a general way, not only the testimony of the accomplice but wherein it is claimed to be contradicted and corroborated, and the jury should be told that they must closely scrutinize such testimony and accept it with caution.¹ The testimony of one accomplice can not be taken as corroboration of another accomplice.2 

Accomplice means any criminal connection with the matter on trial either as principal in the first or second degree or accomplice or accessory. When there is corroborating testimony sufficient to sustain conviction it is not necessary to call the attention of the jury to the degree of caution to be exercised in weighing the testimony of an accomplice.* 

The general rule is that when the facts, with respect to the participation of a witness in the crime for which a defendant is on trial, are clear and undisputed, it is for the court to determine whether or not he was an accomplice, but where the facts are in dispute, or different inferences. might reasonably be drawn therefrom, the question whether or not a witness was an accomplice is for the jury.5 

1 Com. v. Elliott, 292 Pa. 16; Com. v. Spardute 278 Pa. 37;

Com. v. Williams, 275 Pa. 58, reversing; Com. v. McCloskey, 273 Pa., 456; Com. v. Haines, 257 Pa. 289; Com. v. Bell, 88 Pa. 

Super. 216; Com. v. Viscosky, 83 Pa. Super. 96; Com. v. Levine, 74 Pa. Super. 491, failure to caution when not requested is not error; Com. v. Vitale, 32 Lanc. L. R. 153; one who joins in the commission of a crime for the purpose of exposing it is not an accomplice, Com. v. Hollister, 157 Pa. 13; Com. v. Earl 91 Pa. 

Super. 447. 

2 Com. v. Chrostowski, 112 Pa. Super. 466.

8 Com. v. Bell, 88 Pa. Super. 216; Com. v. Polise, 81 Pa. 

Super. 69; Com. v. Vitale, 32 Lanc. L. R. 153. 

4 Com. v. Dougherty, 95 Pa. Super. 305. 

5 Com. v. Brown, 116 Pa. Super. 1, 12. 

6. Alibi

The defendant has a right, even though no request is made for the instruction, to have the jury fully advised as to the difference between the burden resting upon the Commonwealth to establish guilt and that resting on the defendant with respect to the alibi set up. 

If the jury find from the weight of the testimony, from the preponderance of the evidence, that the defendant was not at the scene of the crime at the time of its commission, the verdict should be not guilty.¹

The measure of proof, when the sufficiency of evidence offered to establish an alibi is the question, is simply that it shall be satisfactory. It need not be beyond a reasonable doubt. When the evidence raises a balancing question, and the mind is brought to determine its preponderance, there may be a doubt still existing in the mind, yet the actual weight may be with the prisoner, and this proof will be considered satisfactory.

Proof of an alibi is as much a traverse of the crime charged as any other defense, and proof tending to establish it, though not clear, may, nevertheless, with other facts in the case, raise doubt enough to produce an acquittal.2 But if determined by the jury to be false it may weigh against the defendant when considered with all the other evidence. 

1 Com. v. Stein, 305 Pa. 567; Com. v. Delfino, 259 Pa. 272; 

Com. v. Stewart, 106 Pa. Super. 388. 

2 Com. v. Barrish, 297 Pa. 160; Com. v. Andrews, 234 Pa. 

597; Com. v. Arcurio, 92 Pa. Super. 404; Com. v. Friedman, 86 

Pa. Super. 488; Com. v. Duca, 312 Pa. 101. 

8 Com. v. Arcurio, 92 Pa. Super. 404.

7. Assault and Battery, Aggravated Assault and 

Battery (Motor Car)

In order to warrant a verdict of assault and battery or aggravated assault and battery, it is incumbent on the Commonwealth to prove that the operator of the car was wilfully driving at a high rate of speed, or in a manner which involved a reckless disregard for the safety of others lawfully using the highway. 

Com. v. Donnelly, 113 Pa. Super. 173. 

8. Assault and Battery, Intent to Commit Murder

There is no burden upon the commonwealth to establish by evidence that the defendant intended to commit the crime of murder of the first degree. It may show that the intention of the defendant covered either of the degrees of murder recognized by the penal statute. The intent to commit murder is the felonious element, and the overt act in execution of that intent completes the offense. The commonwealth is not required to prove that the wound is dangerous to life. 

Act of March 31, 1860 P. L. 382 Sec. 81, 18 P. S. 2227; Com. v. Matz, 161 Pa. 207; Com. v. Vanchaski, 42 Pa. Super. 294; 

Com. v. Berardino, 100 Pa. Super. 58; Attempt; Act of May 1, 1876, P. L. 92 Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 2228. 

9. Attempt, Preparation

In many cases it is quite difficult to determine the difference between preparation and attempt to commit crime. 

So long as the acts are confined to preparation only, and can be abandoned before any transgression of the law, or of others rights, they are within the sphere of intent and do not amount to an attempt.

An attempt is a deliberate crime, which is begun, but through circumstances independent of the will of the actor, left unfinished. More strictly, it is such an intentional preliminary guilty act as will apparently result, in the usual course of natural events, if not hindered by cause outside of the actor's will, in a deliberate crime.

An attempt, in general, is an overt act done in pursuance of an intent to do a specific thing, tending to the end but falling short of complete accomplishment of it. In law, the definition must have this further qualification, that the overt act must be sufficiently proximate to the intended crime to form one of the natural series of acts which the intent requires for its full execution.¹

To attempt is to make an effort to effect some object, to make a trial or experiment, to endeavor, to use exertion for some purpose. A man may make an attempt, an effort, a trial to steal, by breaking open a trunk, and be disappointed in not finding the object of pursuit, and so not steal in fact. 

Still he remains, nevertheless, chargeable with the attempt and with the act done toward the commission of the theft. 

So a man may make an attempt, an experiment, to pick a pocket, by thrusting his hand into it, and not succeed, because there happens to be nothing in the pocket. Still he has clearly made the attempt and done the act towards the commission of the offense.2 

1 Com. v. Berardino, 100 Pa. Super. 58; Com. v. Puretta, 74 Pa. Super. 463; Com. v. Forker, 20 Berks Co. 125. 

2 Com. v. Dougherty, 18 Dist. Repts. 857; Com. v. Johnson, 312 Pa. 140. 

10. Circumstantial Evidence 

Such evidence must be incompatable with defendant's innocence upon any rational theory, and incapable of explanation upon any other reasonable hypothesis than that of guilt, otherwise the defendant should be acquitted.¹ 

When a crime is sought to be sustained wholly by circumstantial evidence, the hypothesis of guilt should flow naturally from the facts and circumstances proved, and be consistent with them all. The evidence of facts and circumstances must be such as to exclude to a moral certainty, every hypothesis but that of guilt of the offense imputed, or in other words, the facts and circumstances must not only all be consistent with and point to the guilt of the accused, but they must be inconsistent with his innocence.2

PENNSYLVANIA. CRIMINAL PROCEDURE 

215 Circumstantial evidence is, in the abstract, nearly, though perhaps not altogether, as strong as positive evidence; in the concrete, it may be infinitely stronger. 

1 Com. v. Coontz, 288 Pa. 74; Com. v. McTague, 112 Pa. 

Super. 471. 

2 Com. v. Andrews, 234 Pa. 597; Com. v. Braunfeldt, 72 

Pa. Super. 25; Com. v. Bone, 64 Pa. Super. 44; Com. v. Pane, 110 Pa. Super. 367. 

3 Com. v. Arnold, 292 Pa. 210. 

11. Corpus Delicti

In a general way the term corpus delicti means, when applied to any particular offense, that the specific crime charged has actually been committed by some one, and it is made up of two elements; first, that a certain result has been produced, as that a man has died or a building has burned; second, that some one is criminally responsible for the result. The corpus delicti is to be proved like other facts, and it may be shown by circumstantial evidence, such as confession, with the caution that before a confession is received the corpus delicti ought to be proved beyond a reasonable doubt by evidence independent of the confession. 

7 R. C. L. 774; Com. v. Marshall, 287 Pa. 512; Com. v. 

Gardner, 282 Pa. 458, homicide; Com. v. Walker, 3 Som. Co. 

129, adultery. 

12. Costs, Disposition

The court should instruct the jury as to the law of costs in the case of acquittal in misdemeanor, but a failure to do so is not reversible error. 

Com. v. Clymer, 217 Pa. 302; Com. v. Minker, 17 Berks 118. 

13. Deadly Weapons, What is

Primarily the determination of the character of the weapon, whether deadly or not, is for the court. The circumstance under which employed, the manner of using, and the place on which the weapon, not ordinarily a deadly one, is

used may be enough to justify a jury in finding that quoad that use, the weapon was deadly in character.

Com. v. Scott, 281 Pa. 548, and cases cited; Com. v. Blake-ley, 274 Pa. 100. 

14. Delusion

In order to acquit on the theory (delusion that the deceased intended to take the life of the defendant) the jury must find from the fair preponderance of the evidence that the defendant believed (though a delusion) that a certain state of facts existed at the time which, if true, would have justified the killing, or, at least, would have justified the thought on the defendant's part that the deed was necessary to preserve his own life and that his belief impelled him tothe homicide. 

Com. v. Calhoun, 238 Pa. 474. 

15. Embracery

In the trial of an indictment for embracery, it is reversible error for the trial court to merely read the statutory definition of the offense and state the allegation of the Commonwealth that the defendant called upon one who had been drawn as a grand juror and engaged in conversation with him about a case which was to come before the grand jury.

Such a charge did not properly insruct the jury in respect to the burden on the Commonwealth. The court should have charged the jury that it must find as a fact before it could convict, that the defendant knew that the person with: whom he conversed was a grand juror and that he attempted to corrupt or influence the latter.

To speak of a case accidentally in the presence of jurors, without knowing their character, is no offense; but if the defendant knew they were jurors, and knowingly used language in their hearing calculated to influence their verdict,. the offense is complete. 

Com. v. Riley, 113 Pa. Super. 77..

16. Falsus in Uno, Falsus in Omnibus 

The rule is that if a witness wilfully and corruptly swears falsely to any material fact in the case, the jury is at liberty to disregard the whole of his testimony. But the correct principle goes no further than to say that the jury may disregard the testimony, not that they must disregard it.¹ 

If a witness at a former trial or elsewhere has made statements contradictory of his statement at a second trial, such statements affect his credibility but do not warrant an instruction to the jury not to believe him.2 

1 Com. v. Levine, 74 Pa. Super. 491. 

2 Com. v. Alessio, 313 Pa. 537. 

17. Forgery 

Sec. 169, Act of March 31, 1860 P. L. 382, 18 P. S. 

3631, A definition very generally accepted describes forgery as the false making or material alteration with intent to defraud, of any writing, which if genuine, might apparently be of legal efficacy or the foundation of a legal liability. 

The essential elements of the offense are, first, the false making of some instrument of writing, second, the instru ment must be apparently capable of effecting a fraud and working an injury to another, and, third, there must be a fraudulent intent. 

Com. v. Powers, 110 Pa. Super. 319. 

18. Gambling Devices 

Under our act of assembly there is nothing to indicate what the value of the property gambled for must be. An apparatus is a gambling device where there is anything of value to be won or lost, no matter how small the intrinsic value, as the result of chance.

Any contrivance or apparatus by which it is determined who is the winner or loser in a chance or contest on which the money or value is staked or risked. 

Mill's Novelty Company's App., 316 Pa. 449; Com. v. Stahl, 115 Pa. Super. 506; Forfeiture, Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 

382, Sec. 60, 18 P. S. 1445.

19. Good Character 

Evidence of good reputation, produced in behalf of the defendant, is substantial evidence, to be weighed and considered by the jury in connection with the other evidence in the case. If evidence of good character combined with all the other evidence in the case, creates a reasonable doubt of the defendant's guilt in the minds of the jury, then the defendant should be acquitted. Such evidence of good char-acter produced in behalf of the defendant, is offered not simply to raise a reasonable doubt as to the defendant's guilt, but to establish in the minds of the jury the innocence of the defendant, and is to be regarded as a fact like any other fact tending to establish the innocence of the defendant, and must so be regarded by the jury. Such evidence does not raise a distinct issue and is not brought into the case as a mere makeweight. It must be taken into account with all the other evidence in the case, and if the whole of that evidence is sufficient, in the judgment of the jury, to show that the defendant is not guilty, or to create a reasonable doubt on that point, the defendant must be acquited. 

Good character is of importance in this; that it may be of itself, in spite of all evidence to the contrary, sufficient to raise a reasonable doubt in the minds of the jury and so produce an acquittal. 

It must be understood that evidence of good character is substantial evidence, material and essential to the guilt or innocence of the defendant; it could have the effect to create a reasonable doubt, when without it there might be a conviction.¹

Such evidence is applicable as well to the degree of the crime as to the general question of guilt under the entire indictment. It may, therefore, where the proof of guilt has been established, lead the jury to doubt whether a first degree crime was committed, and thus bring the jury to a verdict of second degree or even manslaughter, and where it continues in the mind as to guilt in any degree, or of any crime, it should lead to general acquittal.2

Evidence of good character is always admissible for the defendant in a criminal case; it is to be weighed and con-

sidered in connection with the other evidence in the case, it may of itself, in some instances, create a reasonable doubt which would entitle the accused to an acquittal. The rule itself is not merely merciful, it is both reasonable and just. 

There may be cases in which, owing to the peculiar circumstances in which a man is placed, evidence of good character may be all he can offer in answer to a charge of crime. Of what avail is a good character, which a man may be a life time in acquiring, if it is to benefit him nothing in the hour of peril ?3

Evidence of good moral character offered by the defendant in a criminal prosecution must be limited to the particular trait of character involved in the commission of the crime charged. So in like manner the cross-examination of his witnesses must be limited to the same trait.4 

1 Com. v. Tenbroeck, 265 Pa. 251; Com. v. Matteo, 100 Pa. 

Super. 115; Com. v. Motsko, 70 Pa. Super. 209; Com. v. Stoner, 70 Pa. Super. 365, aff. 265 Pa. 139; Com. v. Andrews, 234 Pa. 

597, reversing; Com. v. Cate, 220 Pa. 138, reversing. 

2 Com. v. Corsino, 261 Pa. 593; Com. v. Ronello, 251 Pa. 

329, reversing; Com. v. Chester, 77 Pa. Super. 388. 

3 Com. v. Garanchoskie, 251 Pa. 247; Com. v. Andrews, 234 Pa. 597; Com. v. Altieri, 78 Pa. Super. 80; Com. v. Dattala, 77 Pa. Super. 320, reversing; Com. v. Chester, 77 Pa. Super. 

388; Com. v. Kester, 58 Pa. Super. 509. 

4 Com. v. Stefanowicz, 118 Pa. Super. 79; Com. v. Thomas, 282 Pa. 20, reversing for improper cross-examination; a character witness may be cross-examined as to other offenses of the defendant to test his knowledge, Com. v. McClelland, 82 Pa. 

Super. 437. 

20. Involuntary Manslaughter, Auto Accident

Is the killing of another without malice and unintentionally, but in doing some unlawful act not amounting to a felony nor naturally tending to cause death or great bodily harm, or in negligently doing some act lawful in itself, or by the negligent omission to perform a legal duty. 

Negligence, as an element in the definition of involuntary manslaughter, denotes the absence of due care in the circumstances; an act done in an unlawful manner and without due caution.

(AUTO)-Jury can convict only if from all the evidence they find beyond a reasonable doubt, defendant operated his automobile in a careless, negligent, and reckless manner and without due regard to the circumstances. 

Com. v. Mayberry, 290 Pa. 195; Com. v. Micuso, 273 Pa. 

474; Com. v. Ochs, 91 Pa. Super. 528, auto collision, may be felonious; Com. v. Gill, 120 Pa. Super. 22, involuntary manslaughter by auto collision. 

21. Innocence, Presumption of

It is the duty of the Commonwealth to convince the jury beyond a reasonable doubt of the guilt of the defendant. 

He starts with the presumption of law in his favor that he is innocent, and that presumption goes with him throughout the trial until it is overcome by evidence on the part of the 

Commonwealth beyond a reasonable doubt. 

Com. v. Booker, 72 Pa. Super. 395; Com. v. Russogulo, 263 Pa. 93. 

22. Insanity

The defendant has made out a defense entitling him to an acquittal, if the jury find from the fair preponderance of the evidence that at the time of the homicide, he was so mentally defective that he did not know what he was doing, or that he did not realize the nature or appreciate the consequences of the act he was committing. The test of the defendant's responsibility is his ability to determine between right and wrong.2

On the trial of an indictment for murder, evidence of mental irresponsibility or weakness of the defendant, is admissible in possible mitigation of the penalty under the Act of May 14, 1925, P. L. 759. Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 2222, though it fails to approximate the legal standard of insanity.³ 

1 Com. v. Williams, 307 Pa. 134; Com. v. Santos, 275 Pa. 

515; Com. v. Thompkins, 267 Pa. 541; Com. v. Thompkins, 265 Pa. 97. 

2 Com. v. Cavalier, 284 Pa. 311. 

3 Com. v. Stabinsky, 313 Pa. 231; Com. v. Williams 307 

Pa. 134.

23. Intoxication (See Pleas, Defenses, Par. 23) 

24. Mania, Homicide

The doctrine which acknowledges this mania is dangerous in its relations, and can be recognized only in the clearest cases. It ought to be shown to have been habitual, or at least to have evinced itself on more than a single instance. To establish it as a justification in any particular case it is necessary to show, by clear proof, its contemporaneous existence by present circumstances, or the existence of an habitual tendency developed in previous cases, becoming in itself a second nature. 

Com. v. Calhoun, 238 Pa. 474; Com. v. Webb, 252 Pa. 187, uncontrolled temper. 

25. Murder at Common Law

Murder as defined by the common law, consists of the unlawful killing of a human being with malice aforethought, express or implied. Malice is a legal term which comprehends not only particular ill will, but every case where there is wickedness of disposition, hardness of heart, cruelty, recklessness of consequence, or a mind regardless of social duty. 

Com. v. McLaughlin, 293 Pa. 218; Com. v. Drum, 58 Pa. 9. 

26. Murder, Statutory

All murder which shall be perpetrated by means of poison, or by lying in wait, or by any other kind of wilful, deliberate and premeditated killing, or which shall be committed in the perpetration of, or attempting to perpetrate any arson, rape, robbery, burglary, or kidnapping, shall be deemed murder in the first degree, and all other kinds of murder shall be deemed murder in the second degree; and the jury before whom any person indicted for murder shall be tried, shall, if they find such person guilty thereof, ascertain in their verdict whether it be murder of the first or second degree; but if such person shall be convicted by confession, the court shall proceed, by examination of witnesses, to 15

determine the degree of the crime, and to give sentence accordingly. 

Act of May 22, 1923, P. L. 306, Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 2221. 

27. First Degree, Defined 

A specific and malicious intention to kill is the essence of wilful, deliberate, and premeditated killing. An intent to kill is the distinction of murder in the first degree from that of the second degree. If a malicious intention to kill exists it is wilful, and the intent to kill distinctly formed, even for a moment, is sufficient; if the intention be accompanied by such circumstances as evidence a mind fully conscious of its own purpose and design, it is deliberate; and if sufficient time be afforded to enable the mind fully to frame the design to kill and to select the instrument, it is premeditated. Suddenness is opposed to premeditation, and crimes done suddenly, are usually the result of passion or anger. The deliberation and premeditation required is not upon the intent but upon the killing. The killing must not only be intentional or wilfull but also deliberate and also premeditated. 

Com. v. Daynarowicz, 275 Pa. 235; Com. v. Gibson, 275 Pa. 

338; Com. v. Šantos, 275 Pa. 515, 529, reversing; Com. v. 

Dreher, 274 Pa. 325; Com. v. Divomte, 262 Pa. 504; Com. v. 

Grimm, 5 D. & C. 287; Com. v. Drum, 58 Pa. 9. 

28. Murder, Death Penalty 

The law (Act of May 14, 1925, P. L. 759) contemplates first, a finding of guilt of first degree murder, and, thereafter, the exercise of discretion by the jurors. It does not provide that the jury may fix only the death penalty in case they are convinced beyond a reasonable doubt that it is proper. It leaves to them the duty of determining fairly under all the proven facts, what is right and just under the circumstances, and it is their discretion which is to be exercised after the guilt is determined.¹

On the trial of an indictment for murder, the trial judge, whether requested or not, must instruct the jury, that, should they find the prisoner guilty of murder of the first

degree, they must fix the penalty at either death or life imprisonment. 2 

1 Com. v. Curry, 287 Pa. 553; Act of May 14, 1925, P. L. 

759, Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 2222. 

2 Com. v. Madaffer, 291 Pa. 270. 

29. Second Degree 

The one and only distinction between the grades of murder of the first and second degree is that in first degree murder an intent to take life is an essential element, and when this element is lacking in the case of the Commonwealth, though all the other elements of murder of the first degree be established beyond a reasonable doubt, the defendant's guilt would be murder in the second degree. Second degree includes all unlawful killing' under circumstances of depravity of heart, and a disposition of mind regardless of social duty, but where no intent to kill exists or can reasonably be inferred.1 

If the defendant only intended to inflict grevious bodily injury, and had no, purpose of taking life, and the jury cannot infer an intent to take life from the circumstances, the killing would be murder of the second degree.? ! 

1 Com. v. McLaughlin, 293 Pa. 218; Com. v. Divomte, 262 

Pa. 504; Com. v. Grimm, 5 D. & C. 293; Com. v. Drum, 58 Pa. 9. 

2 Com. v. Green, 294 Pa. 573; Com. v. Marshall, 287 Pa. 512. 

30. Voluntary Manslaughter 

Voluntary manslaughter is a homicide intentionally committed under the influence of passion but without legal malice. Passion means any of the emotions of the mind known as anger, rage, sudden resentment, fear, or terror, rendering the mind incapable of calm reflection. 

The terror or fear from the belief on the part of the slayer that he is in imminent danger of death or great bodily harm, will reduce the crime to voluntary manslaughter, although his belief is not reasonable. A killing committed under an apprehension (one that one's life is in danger), if the apprehension exists, but is not reasonable, is manslaughter

only. If the apprehension is reasonable the killing is excusable as done in self-defense. 

To reduce an intentional blow, resulting, in death, to voluntary manslaughter, there must be sufficient cause of provocation, and a state of rage or passion without time to cool, placing the defendant beyond control of his reason, and suddenly impelling him to the deed. What is sufficient provocation has not been exactly defined, and is probably incapable of exact definition, for it must vary with the myriad shifting circumstances of men's temper and quarrels. No words nor mere gestures however false, foul or insulting, will free a party killing from the guilt of murder. (See Selfdefense) 

Com. v. Miller, 313 Pa. 567; Com. v. Newson, 277 Pa. 48; 

Com. v. Webb, 252 Pa. 187; Com. v. Principatti, 260 Pa. 587; 

Com. v. Colandro, 231 Pa. 343; Com. v. Drum, 58 Pa. 9; Com. v. Grimm, 5 D. & C. 293. 

31. Unlawful Killing, Presumption 

The legal presumption from an unlawful killing arises only to second degree murder, and it is incumbent on the Commonwealth to establish the intent to kill in order to constitute the crime of murder in the first degree. (See Deadly Weapon, Vital Part). 

Com. v. Green, 294 Pa. 573; Com. v. Thompkins, 267 Pa. 

541, reversing; Com. v. Berkenbush, 267 Pa. 455; Com. v. 

Channell, 40 Lanc. Co., 385. 

32. Deadly Weapon, Vital Part 

Where one without sufficient provocation unlawfully kills another by the use of a deadly weapon upon a vital part of the body, with a manifest intent so to use it, the presumption of fact (not of law) arises, in the absence of qualifying circumstances, that he intended the consequences of his act and intended to kill his victim.¹ 

When one intentionally uses a deadly weapon upon a vital part of the body of another, there is a legal presumption of an intent to kill which cannot be rebutted by the

assailant's own testimony that he did not so intend. But whether the assailant intended to use the weapon upon a vital part is a question of fact, and he may deny that he intended to so use it, or that he intended to take life.2 

1 Com. v. Green, 294 Pa. 573; Com. v. Arnold, 292 Pa. 

210; Com. v. Chapler, 228 Pa. 630. 

2 Com. v. Millien, 291 Pa. 291; Com. v. Zec. 262 Pa. 251. 

33. Motive

There can be no escape from punishment for crime when all the elements of it are proved, whether the evidence be positive or circumstantial, simply because no motive is shown.1

Where the court upon the trial of an indictment for murder makes a statement from which the jury might infer that the Commonwealth had furnished a motive for killing, where the evidence did not warrant the finding of such motive, the defendant will be granted a new trial.2 

Com. v. Henderson, 242 Pa. 372; Com. v. Danz, 211 Pa. 

507; Com. v. Gormley, 78 Pa. Super. 294; Com. v. Daniele, 3 

Wash. Co. 102; Com. v. Keegan, 70 Pa. Super. 436; Com. v.

Williams, 307 Pa. 134, 155, no motive is sufficient to account for a wilful, deliberate, premeditated killing. 

2 Com. v. Evans, 3 Northumb. L. J. 390. 

34. Perjury

On the trial of a prosecution of perjury it is error for the trial judge to instruct the jury that the single question for their determination is whether the defendant testified falsely in the particulars complained of. The question should have been submitted as to whether he testified falsely, wilfully, knowingly, and corruptly, and the omission is sufficient ground for a new trial. 

Com. v. Hilton, 34 Lanc. L. R. 288. 

35. Presumption of Law, of Fact 

A presumption of law is conclusive, and is an inference which the court will draw from the proofs, which no evidence however strong, will be permitted to overturn. A

presumption of fact is an inference of fact, based on probability which cannot be made without the intervention of the jury. .. 

Com. v. Green, 294 Pa. 573. 

36. Reasonable Doubt

In a criminal case it is desirable that the court should explain to the jury the legal meaning of the expression "reasonable doubt," but it is not reversible error unless the charge is murder for the court to instruct the jury in the very language of the law and to stop at that, in the absence of a request for an explanation of the expression.¹

A reasonable doubt may exist in the mind of a juror without his being able to formulate a reason. An accepted definition of reasonable doubt is that "it must be an honest doubt, such a difficulty as fairly strikes a conscientious mind and clouds the judgment. If the mind be fairly satisfied of a fact, on the evidence as much so as would induce a man of reasonable firmness and judgment to take the fact as true, and to act upon it in a matter of importance to himself, it would be sufficient to rest a verdict upon it."2 

If, in an endeavor to determine from the evidence any point essential to the commonwealth's case, the juror hesitates as between two conclusions and finds himself thinking, as to one of them "such is, or may be, the fact", and then is mentally reluctant to so conclude, and, after considering the evidence from all angles, such hesitancy still persists, that is what the law terms a reasonable doubt, and the defendant is entitled to the benefit of it. In other words, when a juror is in the mental condition just described he is bound to draw inferences and find facts whichever way is most favorable to the defendant; and this is equally so whether the point of doubt concerns a prime fact in the case, or only a minor matter necessary to consider on the way to determine a prime fact, or whether it has to do with the jurors ultimate conclusion on the question of the guilt of the defendant. The doubt may arise out of the evidence on an isolated point, essential to the Commonwealth's case, or out of the whole body of the evidence. The reluctance to reach a conclusion

is not one caused by sympathy or compassion for the defendant or others who may be affected by a verdict against him; it must not be a doubt which comes out of the juror's heart, but one which arises in his mind after a consideration of the evidence; he is not at any time, in his deliberations on the case, to allow himself to conjure up a doubt in order to avoid the performance of a disagreeable duty, or to be controlled by a hesitancy over the decision of a point which he would not consider a matter of doubt or hesitate to decide if it arose in an affair of importance to himself. But a juror ought not to condemn unless he is so convinced by the evidence that he would venture to act upon that conviction in matters of importance to his own interest.3 

1 Com. v. Quaranta, 295 Pa. 264; Com. v. Berney, 262 Pa. 

176; Com. v. Dougherty, 95 Pa. Super. 305; Com. v. Kelsey, 76 Pa. Super. 193. 

2 Com. v. Barrish, 297 Pa. 160; Com. v. Baker, 93 Pa. 

Super. 360; Com. v. Drum, 58 Pa. 9. 

3 Com. v. Green, 292 Pa. 579, reversing; Com. v. Bryson, 276 Pa. 566; Com. v. Disalvo, 275 Pa. 70; Com. v. Wilson, 266 

Pa. 236; Com. v. Zec, 262 Pa. 251; Com. v. Weiner, 101 Pa. 

Super. 295, reversing; Com. v. Baker, 93 Pa. Super. 360, reversing; Com. v. Taylor, 78 Pa. Super. 386; Com. v. Wolfe, 24 Dauph. Co. 173. 

37. Res Gestae

The res gestae are the circumstances which are undesigned incidents of the litigated act, which are admissible when illustrative of such act. These incidents may be separate from the act by the lapse of time more or less appreciable. Their sole distinguishing feature is that they should be necessary incidents of the litigated act; necessary in this sense, that they are part of the immediate preparation for or emanations of such act, and are not produced by the calculated policy of the actors. The criterion is whether the declarations are made under such circumstances as will raise a reasonable presumption that they were spontaneous utterances created by the transaction itself and so soon thereafter as to exclude the presumption of premeditations and design. 

Com. v. Gardner, 282 Pa. 458; Com. v. Stallone, 281 Pa. 41; 

Com. v. Puntario, 271 Pa. 501; Smith v. Stoner, 243 Pa. 57;

Com. v. Werntz, 161 Pa. 591; Com. v. Bradowski, 3 D. & C. 647. 

38. Riot 

A riot is the tumultuous assembly, the tumultuous disturbance of the public peace, by the unlawful assembly of three or more persons in the execution of some private object. If three or more persons so assemble together for an unlawful purpose, every man is guilty of all acts done in execution thereof or contributing or tending to the purpose. 

If they meet for a lawful purpose, and proceed to an unlawful act, it is a riot, provided it results in a breach of the peace. It will be noticed, a riot is a tumultuous disturbance of the peace by an unlawful assembly of three or more persons in the execution of some private object. It is a tumultuous disturbance of the public peace. "Tumultuous" means a noisy, boisterous, violent disturbance of the peace by an unlawful assembly of three or more persons in the execution of some private object.

A riot may occur either in execution of a lawful private enterprise in a violent and turbulent manner, to the terror of the people, or in executing an unlawful enterprise in a violent and turbulent manner.

All persons who are present (not those actually assisting in suppressing the riot) where their presence is intentional, and where it tends to the encouragement of the rioters, shall be prima facia inferred to be participants and the obligation is cast upon a person so circumstanced, and it must be his defense, to prove his actual noninterference. 

Com. v. Brletic, 113 Pa. Super. 508; Com. v. Merrick, 65 

Pa. Super. 482. 

39. Riot, Inciting to

Inciting to riot means such a course of conduct, by the use of words, signs or language, or any other means by which one can be urged on to action, as would naturally lead to, or urge other men to engage in, or enter upon, conduct which, if completed, would make it a riot.

If any men or set of men combine and arrange to so agitate the community to such a pitch, that the natural, and to be expected result of such agitation, would be a riot, that would be inciting to riot.

No man has a right to trifle with the feelings of any large class of men, so as to provoke them to a breach of the peace.

The words uttered or acts done must be proved to have been such as to support a finding that they were said or done, as the case may be, with intent to provoke a riot or with a reckless and wilful disregard of their probable outcome in that respect. 

Com. v. Egan, 113 Pa. Super. 375; Com. v. Frankfald, 114 

Pa. Super. 262; Com. v. Spantaco, 104 Pa. Super. 1, form of indictment. 

40. Self Defense 

Where a person has reasonable ground to believe that he is in imminent danger of loss of life or of suffering serious bodily harm he may, in self-defense kill his assailant, but before taking life in self-defense it is the defendant's duty to retreat and avail himself of any opportunity of escape which a reasonable person could see.¹ Self-defense is an affirmative defense which must be established by the weight of the evidence, by a "fair preponderance" of the evidence.2 

A killing committed under an apprehension that one's life is in danger, is, if the apprehension is reasonable, excusable as done in self-defense; if the apprehension exists, but is not reasonable, it is manslaughter only. 

1 Com. v. Nelson, 294 Pa. 544; Com. v. Russogulo, 263 

Pa. 93; Com. v. Mitchka, 209 Pa. 274; Com. v. Channell, 40 

Lanc. Co. 385. 

2 Com. v. Lockett, 291 Pa. 319; Com. v. Walker, 283 Pa. 

468; Com. v. Baker, 285 Pa. 77; Com. v. Bryson, 276 Pa. 566, insanity; Com. v. Weinberg, 276 Pa. 255; Com. v. Ross, 266 

Pa. 580. 

3 Com. v. Miller, 313 Pa. 567.

41. Self Defense, Voluntary Manslaughter, Difference 

The dividing line between self-defense and voluntary manslaughter, brought about through the influence of a passion of fear is:

Self-defense. A reasonably founded belief of either imminent peril to life or great bodily harm. If the circumstances are both adequate to raise and sufficient to justify a belief in the necessity to take life in order to save one's self from such danger, where the belief exists and is acted upon, the homicide is excusable upon the theory of self-defense. 

Voluntary Manslaughter.-If the act is committed under the influence of an uncontrollable fear of either death or great bodily harm caused by the immediate circumstances, but the fear is not reasonably justified by the circumstances, the killing is voluntary manslaughter. 

Com. v. Principatti, 260 Pa. 587, 596; Com. v. Colandro, 231 Pa. 343. 

42. Self Defense, Assault and Battery

The defendant submitted points, in several different forms, requesting the court to charge that if at the time he struck the prosecutor with the hammer the latter was making an attack upon him, the jury should acquit. The vice of these points is that they left entirely out of view any question as to whether the violent act of the defendant in using a weapon was or was not, under the circumstances as they then reasonably appeared to him, wholly unnecessary to protect him from bodily harm, in other words, whether the counter attack which he delivered was wholly disproportioned to that which was being made upon him. 

One who is assaulted in such a way as to induce in him. a reasonable and well-grounded belief that he is actually in danger of losing his life, or of receiving great bodily harm, under the influence of such apprehension, will be justified in defending himself, whether the danger was real or only apparent; and though he be mistaken in his reasonable conviction, he will not be held criminally liable. The good faith of his assertion of such a conviction is, however, always a

question for the jury. Even if the prosecutor in this case was the original assailant, the testimony with regard to which was conflicting, it is very clear that the evidence would have warranted a finding that the defendant repeatedly used his weapon with great violence, that he reduced his adversary to a helpless condition and continued to hammer him after the prosecutor was lying upon the floor with the defendant seated upon his stomach. The right of self-defense does not include the right to indefinitely beat and wound the original assailant after he has been reduced to a helpless condition. 

Com. v. Sutton, 51 Pa. Super. 191. 

43. Defense of Habitation, Family, Servant, 

Prevention of Felony

There are cases where retreat may not be necessary before using a deadly weapon upon an assailant, as in defense of one's habitation or person, or the life or chastity of one's family from violent attack and imminent danger. The rule is that a person may repeal force in defense of his person, habitation or property against one who manifestly endeavors by violence or surprise, to commit a felony such as rape, robbery, arson, burglary or the like, and is not bound to retreat, but may pursue his adversary until he has freed himself from all danger, and in such a case a homicide committed by a person in defense of his habitation or property is justifiable.

The general rule of law is "a bona fide belief by the defendant that a felony is in progress of commission, which can only be averted by the death of the supposed felon, makes the killing excusable homicide; though, if such belief be negligently adopted by the defendant, then the killing is manslaughter". With reference to this rule reliance must be had on the ancient and settled right to interfere to prevent felony, with its well-guarded limitations, however, that the injury to be prevented must be serious, must be imminent and not past, the quarrel in actual progress and the necessity for the use of a deadly weapon clear of doubt. "A mere bystander" may not interfere in a stranger's quarrel to the extent of killing with a deadly weapon, without being guilty of

more than manslaughter, and the more generous rule, applicable when the interference is to protect a near relative, did not extend beyond cases where a serious injury was threatened to a "wife or husband, child or servant." Finally, it is well-established that "to warrant taking life in self-defense there must be a reasonable ground for belief, upon the part of the slayer, that he is in imminent danger of loss of his. own life or of suffering serious bodily harm, at the hands of the person killed," and in so far as the right to take human life is dependent upon the surrounding circumstances, persons. acting in defense of others are in the same situation, and upon the same plane, as those who act in defense of themselves, and if it appear that the accused knew that the person defended had been the aggressor, he is not excusable for his homicidal act. 

Com. v. Nelson, 294 Pa. 544, 548; Com. v. Russogulo, 263 

Pa. 93; Com. v. Johnson, 213 Pa. 432, 434; Com. v. Bouchet, 5 Dist. Repts. 343; Com. v. Brown, 14 Erie, 63, 69. 

44. Stolen Property, Possession of

The possession of recently stolen property by a person is evidence from which it can be found he is the thief, but the presumption is one of fact and not of law, and the jurors must pass on it as part of the evidence against the accused. 

In doing this, they must also judge of his explanation, which, even though not entirely believed, may raise a reasonable doubt of the defendant's guilt, or if accepted as true, may overcome the presumption, or inference, against him and require further proof from the prosecutor; on the other hand, if the explanation is rejected in whole or in part becauseunworthy of belief, this may serve to strengthen the case of the commonwealth accordingly. 

Com. v. Newman, 276 Pa. 534; Com. v. Chester, 77 Pa. 

Super. 388, reversing; Com. v. Baker, 115 Pa. Super. 183, receiving stolen goods, reversed.

233 

. Unwritten Law 

There is no such defense in the jurisprudence of Pennsylvania known as the "Unwritten Law" which justifies a husband in killing his wife's paramour. 

Com. v. Scherer, 266 Pa. 210; Com. v. Grimm, 5 D. & C. 287.

Chapter XXIV VERDICT 1. VERDICT, WHAT IS 2. CONVICTION 3. SPECIAL VERDICT 4. ATTEMPT 5. NO ACQUITTAL FOR MISDEMEANOR BECAUSE EVIDENCE SHOWS FELONY 6. INCLUDED OFFENSE 7. SEVERAL COUNTS, FINDING ON ONE 8. INCONSISTENT VERDICT 9. MOULDING VERDICT INTO PROPER FORM 10. ACCESSORIES 11. MURDER 12. FELONIOUS ASSAULT 13. FALSE PRETENSE, VERDICT WHEN LARCENY PROVED 14. ACQUITTAL ON THE GROUND OF INSANITY 15. COURT DIRECTING FORM OF VERDICT 16. POLLING JURY, HOMICIDE CASES 17. JURORS, INVALIDATING VERDICT 

1. Verdict, What is

The verdict is what is announced in open court and recorded as such; not what may have been written and handed to the trial judge; and, until so announced and recorded, the jury may correct any mistake inadvertently made therein, and the court may request them to retire for that purpose. 

Com. v. Micuso, 273 Pa. 474; Com. v. Huston, 232 Pa. 209; 

Com. v. Chathams, 50 Pa. 181; Com. v. Dudley, 46 Pa. Super. 337. 

2. Conviction

The word "conviction" has a popular as well as a technical meaning. As popularly used it implies nothing more than a finding of guilty by a jury, but as technically under- 234

stood it means the ascertainment of the guilt of the accused and judgment thereon by the court, implying not only a verdict but judgment of sentence thereon. 

Com. v. Minnich, 250 Pa. 363. 

3. Special Verdict 

A special verdict may be rendered in a criminal case, wherein the facts are found and the legal inference of guilt or innocence is referred to the court; but where this unusual mode of procedure is adopted, a conviction cannot be sustained unless the guilt of the defendant is the inevitable legal conclusion from the facts stated in the special verdict. 

The jury need not follow the technical language used in the indictment, yet as the court is not at liberty to draw inferences of fact, any ingredient of the offense that is not conclusively established by the facts found by the jury and stated in the verdict, must be taken not to exist. 

Com. v. Channing, 55 Pa. Super. 510; Com. v. George, 27 

Dist. Repts., 845. 

4. Attempt

If the evidence shows that the defendant did not complete the felony or misdemeanor charged, but was guilty only of an attempt, he shall not be entitled to an acquittal, but the jury shall be at liberty to return a verdict that the defendant is not guilty of the felony or misdemeanor charged, but is guilty of an attempt to commit the same. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 50, 18 P. S. 3691; 

Com. v. Cordurso, 12 D. & C. 169; Com. v. State Loan Corp., 116 Pa. Super. 365. 

5. No Aquittal for Misdemeanor Because Evidence Shows Felony 

If upon the trial of any person for any misdemeanor, it shall appear that the facts given in evidence amount in law to a felony, such person shall not by reason thereof be entitled to be acquitted for such misdemeanor; and no person tried for such misdemeanor shall be liable to be afterwards

prosecuted for felony on the same facts, unless the court before whom such trial may be had shall think fit, in its discretion, to discharge the jury from giving any verdict upon such trial, and direct such person to be indicted for felony, in which case such person may be dealt with in all respects as if he had not been put upon his trial for such misdemeanor. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 51; 19 P. S. 831. 

6. Included Offense

On an indictment containing one count charging felony, a defendant may be convicted of a misdemeanor which is a constituent of, or included within, the greater offense.¹ 

When an indictment charges an offense that includes within the description another offense of less grade or lower degree, the jury properly may find the accused guilty of a less offense.2 

1 Com. v. Anagustov, 82 Pa. Super. 156, verdict Agg. A. & B. on indictment for robbery. 

2 Com. v. Kellyon, 278 Pa. 59, adultery, fornication; rape, fornication; homicide, voluntary manslaughter; Com. v. Bailey, 92 Pa. Super. 581; Com. v. Anagustov, 82 Pa. Super. 156, robbery, Agg. A. & B. 

7. Several Counts, Finding on One

Where a jury considers an indictment containing two or more counts, and returns a finding as to one count, but the verdict makes no reference to the other counts, such verdict constitutes an acquittal on the counts which are not mentioned in the verdict. 

Com. v. Day, 114 Pa. Super. 511. 

8. Inconsistent Verdict

Consistency in the verdict is not necessary. Each count in an indictment is regarded as if it were a separate indictment. "The most that can be said in such cases is, that the verdict shows that either in the acquittal or the conviction the jury did not speak their real conclusion, but that does not

show that they were not convinced of the defendant's guilt. 

We interpret the acquittal as no more than their assumption of a power which they had no right to exercise, but to which they were disposed through lenity." In the case of Dunn v. 

United States, 284 U. S. 390, and cited by the court in Com. v. Kline, 107 Pa. Super. 594, the defendant was indicted on three counts, first, maintaining a common nuisance by keeping for sale at a specified place intoxicating liquor, second, for unlawful possession of intoxicating liquor, and, third, for the unlawful sale of such liquor. The jury acquitted on the second and third counts and found the defendant guilty on the first. The verdict was sustained. 

In the case of Brown et al. v. United States, 284 U. S. 

596 (cited in Com. v. Kline) the question was, "the certificate set forth an indictment in four counts charging the three defendants with murder in the first degree. The first count named one of the defendants, the second another, and the third the last as having held the pistol with which the crime was committed. The fourth count alleged that the weapon was held by one of the defendants but that his name was to the grand jurors unknown. The certificate also set out a part of the instructions of the trial judge, and showed that all three defendants were found not guilty under the first three counts but guilty under the fourth and were sentenced to death." The question propounded was: "Can the judgment based upon the conviction of the defendants on the fourth count of the indictment be sustained in view of the acquittal of each and all of the defendants of the charge of murder in the first degree as contained in the first three counts of the indictment?" The supreme court answered "Yes." 

In Com. v. Kline, the superior court says: "There is some difficulty in formulating a general statement which will be true with reference to all cases of inconsistency, repugnancy, or incongruity by reason of the fact that these expressions as commonly used have a broad meaning and sometimes may be taken to comprehend cases which involve the violation of other principles of law. For example, where two persons are charged in a count or counts with conspiracy between themselves and not involving third parties, a jury 11 Ca Ba 50 16

or different juries could not find one guilty and the other not guilty and the verdict of guilty be enforced. It perhaps would not be improper to refer to this as an inconsistency, but the fact is that such a verdict could not be sustained because of a violation of the substantive law with relation to conspiracy. We take another example from the case of 

Mills v. Com. 13 Pa., 634, where the court stated: "If the counts, on which there was conviction of the defendant, were repugnant and could not stand together, and there was a general verdict, it would be error. That case held that an acquittal on one count does not require the setting aside of a verdict of guilty on another count, even though the two counts related to the same charge; but that a general verdict of guilty on two inconsistent counts could not stand, because it would be impossible for the count to determine from the verdict on which of the two counts the jury intended to convict. We do hold that where a defendant is charged in separate counts of an indictment with different offenses arising out of the same transaction, or in different language is charged with the same offense, the mere fact that the defendant is found guilty upon one count and not guilty upon another will not sustain a motion in arrest of judgment as to the count on which a verdict of guilty is found. We are also of the opinion that the principal of res judicata does not have any application. 

Com. v. Kline, 107 Pa. Super. 594; Com. v. Campbell, 116 

Pa. Super. 180. 

9. Moulding Verdict into Proper Form

The court has power to mould the verdict into proper form in the presence and with the acquiescence of the jury.¹ 

When the verdict is so inconsistent that it cannot be amended or moulded into form by the court, a new trial will be granted.2

A recommendation of mercy is not part of the verdict, but may be a proper matter for consideration by the board of pardons.³ 

1 Com. v. Rosser, 102 Pa. 78, no findings on some counts, opportunity given to correct; Com. v. Hartley, 89 Pa. Super. 230.

2 Com. v. Micuso, 273 Pa. 474, "involuntary" changed to "voluntary"; Com. v. Huston, 46 Pa. Super. 172; Com. v. 

Miller, 77 Pa. Super. 469; Com. v. Cooke, 8 D. & C. 763; Com. v. Fronhiser, 6 D. & C. 710. བད 

3 Com. v. Bishop, 285 Pa. 49; Com. v. McManus, 282 Pa. 

25; Com. v. Zec, 262 Pa. 251. 

10. Accessories

An accessory before the fact, may, under the Act of 

March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 44 (18 P. S. 3672) relating to felonies, be convicted as principal. The rule that guilt of the principal must first be shown before an accessory can be convicted does not apply where defendant is indicted as a principal. 

Com. v. Mendola, 294 Pa. 353; Com. v. Minnich, 250 Pa. 

363; Com. v. Heffelfinger, 82 Pa. Super. 351; Com. v. Gray, 265 Pa. 537, larceny. 

11. Murder

In the trial of an indictment for murder, the trial judge, whether requested or not, must instruct the jury, that, should they find the prisoner guilty of murder of the first degree they must fix the penalty at either death or life imprisonment. 

Com. v. Madaffer, 291 Pa. 270; Act of May 14, 1925, P. L. 

759, 18 P. S. 2222. 

12. Felonious Assault

The jury may acquit on charge of felonious assault and find the defendant guilty of a misdemeanor, in unlawful cutting, stabbing, wounding. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 99, 19 P. S. 833. 

13. False Pretense Verdict When Larceny Proved 

If, upon the trial of any person for cheating by fraudulent pretense, it shall be proved that the defendant obtained the property in question in such manner as to amount in law to larceny, he shall not, by reason thereof, be en-

titled to be acquitted of such misdemeanor; and no person tried for such misdemeanor shall be liable to be afterwards prosecuted for larceny upon the same facts. 

Act of Apr. 30, 1925, P. L. 386, Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 2631. 

14. Acquittal on the Ground of Insanity 

When any person charged with any crime or misdemeanor whatever, shall be acquitted on the ground of insanity or having been insane at the time the offense was committed, the jury shall set out this fact in their verdict; and the court or judge before whom such person is tried, may thereupon order such person to be committed to a hospital for mental diseases. 

Act of July 11, 1923, P. L. 998, Art. 3, Sec. 308; 50 P. S. 

48; Act of April 17, 1929, P. L. 532, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 1351. 

15. Court Directing Form of Verdict

When a form of verdict is submitted by the court to the jury, it should be so comprehensive as to include every kind of verdict the jury would be warranted in returning. 

It may well be that jurors would sometimes be relieved of much anxiety, if the trial judge would specifically direct them as to the form of verdict they should return, but it is better that jurors, in every case, should suffer some slight inconvenience and discomfort, rather than that countenance should be given to the practice of directing the form of verdict, which followed to its logical result, would destroy one of the safeguards of the accused, which reason and experience combine to show is of the highest value. 

Com. v. Mandela, 48 Pa. Super. 56, reversing; Com. v. 

Kloss, 38 Pa. Super. 307, form directed, several counts, numerous charges. 

16. Polling Jury, Homicide Cases 

In homicide cases the proper time to request a poll of the jury is before the verdict is entered. 

Com. v. Scovern, 292 Pa. 26, 35; Com. v. Bishop, 285 Pa. 

49; Com. v. Schmous, 162 Pa. 326, practice.

17. Jurors, Invalidating Verdict

It is well settled that jurors, after they have rendered a verdict, ought not to be admitted to give testimony to invalidate that verdict,¹ and public policy forbids examination of jurors as to the reason for their verdict.2 

1 Com. v. Clay, 56 Pa. Super. 427; Com. v. Clay, 57 Pa. 

Super. 128. 

2 Com. v. Greevy, 271 Pa. 95.

LAY Chapter XXV NEW TRIAL: ARREST OF JUDGMENT 

1. POWER TO GRANT 2. TIME TO MAKE 3. JOINING MOTION FOR NEW TRIAL WITH MOTION IN ARREST OF JUDGMENT 4. WEIGHT AND SUFFICIENCY OF EVIDENCE 5. CIRCUMSTANTIAL EVIDENCE 6. AFTER DISCOVERED EVIDENCE 7. AFTER DISCOVERED EVIDENCE, HOMICIDE 7(a) DISCHARGE OF RULE 7(b) IF EVIDENCE RULE TO BE MADE ABSOLUTE 8. PERJURED EVIDENCE 9. DISQUALIFICATION OF JURORS, MISCONDUCT 10. ARREST OF JUDGMENT DOES NOT OPERATE AS AN ACQUITTAL 16. REFUSING MOTION, GRANTING NEW TRIAL 

1. Power to Grant 

The courts have wide discretion in granting new trials.

In very intricate and doubtful cases, notwithstanding the finding of the jury, the court will, in the exercise of its extraordinary discretion, order a new trial for the better attainment of justice. 

At common law, new trials may be granted by courts of general jurisdiction upon their own motion. The right of a court to order a new trial of its own motion is indisputable. 

It is one of the essential functions of a judge sitting with a 242

.jury. This authority is incident to the trial by jury, by the principles of the common law. The entry of judgment overrules a pending motion for a new trial. 

Com. v. Whaley, 85 Pa. Super. 320; new trial may not be granted on verdict of acquittal, defendant to pay costs, Com. v. 

Qjáy, 16 D. & C. 49; Com. v. Kroekel, 121 Pa. Super. 423, 428, Commonwealth may not have new trial, Com. v. Grow, 48 Pa.

Super. 373. See Chap. XIX Par. 8. 

2. Time to Make 

The power of the court to set aside a judgment obtained adversely, and grant a new trial, in the absence of fraud, expires with the end of the term at which the judgment was entered.¹ Rules of court usually provide that a motion for a new trial must be made within four days after rendition of the verdict.

The courts have a discretionary power to permit a motion for a new trial to be filed nunc pro tunc at any time within the term at which the judgment was entered.2 

1 Com. v. Grow, 48 Pa. Super. 373. 

2 Wickel v. Mertz, 49 Pa. Super. 472; Com. .v. Grow, 48 

Pa. Super. 373. 

3. Joining Motion for New Trial with Motion in Arrest of Judgment 

It is not good practice to join a motion for a new trial with a motion in arrest of judgment. There is a clear distinction between the two. (See Arrest of Judgment, Par. 14) 

Com. v. Yuscavage, 19 Schul. Co. 314. 

4. Weight and Sufficiency of Evidence 

The quality and weight of the Commonwealth's evidence may be called in question by demurrer to the evidence at the close of the Commonwealth's case, by request for binding instructions for the defendant, or by a motion for a new trial. 3... 

Com. v. Jones, 303 Pa. 551; Com. v. Nathan, 93 Pa. Super. 

193; Com. v. Varner, 74 Pa. Super. 529; Com. v. Yuscavage, 19 

Schul. Co. 314.

5. Circumstantial Evidence 

A new trial will be granted where the evidence was largely circumstantial and left doubt as to the defendant's guilt. 

Com. v. Supansie, 93 Pa. Super. 111; Com. v. Burdge, 30 

Dist. Repts. 289; Com. v. Seidel, 28 Dist. Repts. 604; Com. v. 

Greth, 14 Berks Co. 13; Com. v. Oxenford, 14 Berks L. J. 162; 

Com. v. Reed, 3 Som. Co. 124. 

6. After Discovered Evidence 

The evidence must have been discovered since the trial, and be such as could not have been obtained at the trial by the use of reasonable diligence; it must not be simply corroborative or cumulative, or merely to impeach the credibility of witnesses, and it must be such as to likely result in a different verdict if a new trial were granted. 

Com. v. Fragassa, 278 Pa. 1; Com. v. Carter, 272 Pa. 551; 

Com. v. Patterson, 272 Pa. 522; Com. v. Greenfield, 103 Pa. 

Super. 489; Com. v. Walker, 94 Pa. Super. 586; Com. v. Brady, 76 Pa. Super. 488; Com. v. Hetrick, 30 Dist. Repts. 326, granted; 

Com. v. Mitchell, 28 Dist. Repts, 388, granted; Com. v. Eberly, 34 Lanc. L. R. 105, granted; Com. v. Stewart, 110 Pa. Super. 

279, Com. v. Delfino, 259 Pa. 272. 

7. After Discovered Evidence, Homicide 

Whenever by petition, supported by after discovered evidence, it shall be made to appear to the supreme court that there is ground for substantial doubt as to the guilt of any prisoner convicted of murder of the first degree, the said court shall have power to authorize the court of oyer and terminer in which such prisoner has been convicted to grant a rule for a new trial, nunc pro tunc, notwithstanding the expiration of term in which such prisoner was convicted and sentenced; and thereupon the said court of oyer and terminer may, in its discretion, grant and proceed to hear such rule, as in other cases. (See Par. 6). 

Act of April 22, 1903, P. L. 245, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 861; Com. v. 

Branhan, 283 Pa. 392; Com. v. Carter, 272 Pa. 551.

7(a) Discharge of Rule

Upon the termination of the hearing of such rule, if the court of oyer and terminer shall not deem the grounds sufficient it shall thereupon discharge said rule, and the proceedings shall terminate, and the judgment and sentence theretofore entered of record shall remain unaffected. 

Act of April 22, 1903, P. L. 245, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 862; except on question of jurisdiction; Com. v. Cicere, 286 Pa. 296; Com. v. Carter, 272 Pa. 551, rule discharged. 

7(b) If Evidence Sufficient Rule to be Made Absolute

But if said court of oyer and terminer shall be of opinion that, of right and justice, a new trial should be granted, it shall set forth its opinion to that effect, and thereupon the prisoner may bring the opinion, together with the evidence, before the supreme court, as if upon appeal; and if the supreme court shall, after hearing, concur with the court of oyer and terminer that of right and justice a new trial should be had, it shall have power to authorize the court of oyer and terminer to make the rule for new trial absolute; and thereupon the case shall proceed in said court as if a new trial had been granted in due course, at the instance of the prisoner during the term. 

Act of April 22, 1903, P. L. 245, Sec. 3, 19 P. S. 863; Com. v. 

Carter, 272 Pa. 551. 

8. Perjured Evidence

Usually a new trial will not be granted because of perjury on an immaterial issue or a collateral issue, nor generally when the false testimony may be eliminated without depriving the verdict of sufficient evidentiary support.¹ 

After the expiration of the term at which sentence has been imposed, and the affirmance of the conviction and sentence by the Supreme Court, the quarter sessions has no power to grant a new trial on the ground that the conviction was 31 +1 بلہ ལ་རྔ་ཕྱི

PENNSYLVANIA CRIMINAL : 

PROCEDURE 

procured by perjured evidence, the defendant must look to the pardoning power for relief.2 

1 Com. v. Daily, 280 Pa, 59; Com. v. Ruff, 92 Pa. Super. 

530; Wickel v. Mertz, 49 Pa. Super. 472, granted; Com. v. Yet 

Sing, 7 Kulp 349, granted; Com. v. Stoy, 25 Dist. Repts, 937, granted; Com. v. Nicholas, 23 Lanc. L. R. 91, granted; Com. v. 

Cantanzariti, 38 Montg. L. R. 171, granted. 

2 Com. v. Brady, 50 Pa. C. C. 613. 

9. Disqualification of Jurors, Misconduct 

The right to attack a verdict for the disqualification of a juror does not exist, even when the moving party was viously ignorant of such disqualification. It is the duty of parties to ascertain, by proper examination, at the time the jury is impaneled, the existence of any reason for objection to jurors.¹

New trial will not be granted merely because of irregularities in the conduct of jurors or misconduct of others in their presence. It must be made to appear that the alleged misconduct was prejudicial to the rights of the accused.2 

The mere fact that it is discovered after a trial that a juror has expressed an opinion before being sworn is not ground for a new trial. If, however, he has prejudged the case and stated that he would find the defendant guilty, no matter what the evidence was, that would be ground for a new trial. 

1 Com. v. Walker, 283 Pa. 468; Com. v. Dombek, 268 Pa. 262. 

2 Com. v. Buzzard, 9 D. & C. 145, 419, opinion, prejudging; 

Com. v. Blakeley, 274 Pa. 100, separation of jurors, entertainment. 

10. All Defendants do not have the Same Right

A new trial of one conspirator does not entail a new trial for all; the question in each case being whether special treatment of one defendant in any way prejudiced the ather. 

Com. v. Bruno, 324 Pa. (1936; see Com. v. Faulknier, 89 

Pa. Super. 454.)

11. Hearing Argument 

Though it is the proper practice, no statute requires that a motion for a new trial be heard in banc, but where request is made and refused the appellate court may return the record for hearing in banc. 

Com. v. Parker, 294 Pa. 144; Com. v. Robinson, 102 Pa.

Super. 46 before as many judges as may be available; In 

Phila. Co. request to be heard in banc must be made, Act of 

February 3, 1843, P. L. 8, Sec. 5, 17 P. S. 477. 

12. Disposing of Motion Without Argument 

If the court below is satisfied that the reasons for a new trial presented by a convicted defendant are trivial and without legal justification, and raise no question which requires further consideration by it, it may dispose of the motion forthwith and without argument. In fact, in clear cases such course is to be recommended in the interest of speedy justice. 

Com. v. Montpleiser, 100 Pa. Super. 91. 

13. Refusal, Discretion

The refusal of a new trial is not ground for reversal where no abuse of discretion by the lower court is shown. 

Com. v. Nelson, 294 Pa. 544; Com. v. Arnold, 292 Pa. 210; 

Com. v. Jester, 256 Pa. 441, reversing Superior; Com. v. Croce, 89 Pa. Super 249; Com. v. Crawford, 79 Pa. Super. 483. 

14. Arrest of Judgment 

Judgment can only be arrested in criminal cases, for causes appearing on the face of the record; this is a general rule and is well settled; an exception exists when a pardon is pleaded before sentence. The record to be considered consists of the indictment, plea, issue and verdict.¹

Neither the evidence in the case nor the charge of the court will be considered on the motion.2 The entry of judg ment overrules a pending motion in arrest of judgment.3 

1 Com. v. Jones, 303 Pa. 551; Com. v. Nathan, 93 Pa. Super. 

193; Com. v. Bateman 92 Pa. Super. 53; Com. v. Smith, 116 Pa.

Super. 146 a defect of substance is not cured by a verdict or a plea. 

2 Com. v. Nathan, 93 Pa. Super. 193; Com. v. P. R. R. Co.,. 

72 Pa. Super. 353; Com. v. Shaefer, 11 D. & C. 552; Com. v. 

Vinisky, 9 D. & C. 754; Com. v. Popkave, 29 Dist. Repts. 787. 

See Com. v. Stetska, 69 Pa. Super. 15, judgment arrested because interpreter appeared as a witness before grand jury. 

3 Com. v. Grow, 48 Pa. Super. 373. 

15. Arrest of Judgment does not Operate as an Acquittal

A decree arresting judgment does not operate as an acquittal, but only places the defendant in the same situation in which he was before the prosecution was begun.¹ The entry of judgment overrules any pending motion in arrest of judgment or for a new trial.2 

1 P. S. B. of J. V. Stogosh, 15 West. Co. 115. 

2 Com. v. Grow, 48 Pa. Super. 373. 

16. Refusing Motion, Granting New Trial 

When the court grants a new trial and refuses a motion in arrest of judgment, this sets aside the verdict and the proceeding in arrest of judgment falls with it.1 

When the lower court errs in arresting judgment and in not giving due consideration to the motion for a new trial, the record will be remitted with direction to reinstate the motion for a new trial.2 

1 Lance, v. Bennell, 105 Pa. 46; Com. v. Bishop, 69 Pa. 

Super. 435; appeal quashed. 

2 Com. v. P. R. R. Co. 72 Pa. Super. 353.
