2. Penalty for Common Law Offenses 

Every felony, misdemeanor or offense whatever, not especially provided for by the act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 

382, Sec. 178 (19 P. S. 374), may and shall be punished as heretofore.¹ If the offense charged was indictable under the portion of the common law of England which has been adopted in this Commonwealth it is preserved by this section, described by the codifiers as "a saving section." When a specific punishment is not prescribed it is uniformly and universally understood to be that annexed to common-law misdemeanors, viz., fine and imprisonment. 

Com. v. McHale, 97 Pa. 397; Com. v. Mohn, 52 Pa. 243; 

Com. v. Kelsea, 103 Pa. Super. 399, simple abortion, common law offense; Com. v. Rosser, 102 Pa. Super. 78, misbehavior in office; Com. v. Degrange, 97 Pa. Super. 181; accessories, see 

Act of June 3, 1893, P. L. 286, Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 3671. 

3. Indefinite Sentence, Penitentiary 

Under the Act of June 29, 1923, P. L. 975, Sec. 1, (19 

P. S. 1057), whenever any person is sentenced for any crime punishable in a state penitentiary, the court shall pronounce a sentence for an indefinite term, stating in the sentence the minimum and the maximum limits. The maximum shall not exceed the maximum prescribed as a penalty for such offense, and the minimum shall not exceed one-half of the maximum sentence imposed. Provided, that before any parole shall be granted, ten days notice shall be given, by the board of prison inspectors, to the judge who imposed sentence, or his successor, and to the district attorney. Persons sentenced under this act are not entitled to benefits under the Act of 

May 11, 1901, P. L. 166, 61 P. S. 271; this act does not affect the power of the court to parole under the Act of May 

11, 1923, P. L. 204, 61 P. S. 314. 

Com. v. Renya, 91 Pa. Super. 90; Com. v. Hefferman, 20 

Del. Co. 333; Com. v. Prince, 25 D. & C. 485, rape, penitentiary or workhouse.

4. Penalty upon Second Conviction 

If any person who has been convicted of any offense, other than murder of the second degree, for which the punishment prescribed by this code is imprisonment by separate or solitary confinement at labor, shall, after such conviction, be guilty of a similar offense, or of any offense for which such punishment is directed, he shall in either case, upon conviction, be sentenced to undergo an imprisonment, and be kept at labor, not exceeding double the whole period of time which may, by the penal laws of this commonwealth, be prescribed for the crime of which he is convicted. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 182, 18 P. S. 3731. 

5. Indeterminate Sentence, Third Conviction

Whenever any person, convicted in any court of this commonwealth of any crime, shall be sentenced to imprisonment in either the Eastern or Western Penitentiary, the Court, instead of pronouncing upon such convict a definite or fixed term of imprisonment, shall pronounce upon such convict a sentence of imprisonment for an indefinite term; stating in such sentence the minimum and maximum limits thereof; fixing as the minimum time of such imprisonment, the term now or hereafter prescribed as the minimum imprisonment for the punishment of such offense; but if there be no minimum time so prescribed, the court shall determine the same, but it shall not exceed one-fourth of the maximum time, and the maximum limit shall be the maximum time now or hereafter prescribed as a penalty for such offense; Provided, however, that when a person shall have twice before been convicted, sentenced, and imprisoned in a penitentiary for a term of not less than one year, for any crime committed in this state, or elsewhere within the limits of the United States, the court shall sentence said person to a maximum of thirty years; And provided further, that no person sentenced for an indeterminate term shall be entitled to any benefit under the Act of May 11, 1901, P. L. 166, Sec. 1, 61 P. S. 271 (commutation for good behavior). 

Act of May 10, 1909, P. L. 495, Sec. 6, 19 P. S. 1086.

6. Second or Subsequent Offense, Certain Crimes 

Any person who, after having been convicted within or without this Commonwealth of the crime, or attempt to commit the crime, of treason, murder, voluntary manslaughter, sodomy, buggery, burglary, entering with intent to steal, robbery, arson, mayhem, kidnapping, sale of narcotics, perjury, abortion, pandering, incest, or any offense committed or attempted to be committed through the instrumentality of or with the aid of a deadly weapon or gunpowder or other explosive substance or corrosive fluid, may, upon conviction of any of such crimes for a second offense committed within five years after the first offense, or subsequent offense committed within five years after the prior offense, be sentenced to imprisonment for a term the maximum of which shall not be more than twice the longest term prescribed upon a first conviction for the crime in question. Sec. 1. 

(6a) Fourth Offense 

A person who, after having been three times convicted, within or without this Commonwealth, of crimes of the character above set forth, or of attempt to commit any such crime, shall, upon conviction of any such crimes for a fourth or subsequent offense committed within five years after the prior offense, be sentenced, in the discretion of the judge trying the case, to imprisonment in a State penitentiary for the term of his natural life. Sec. 2. 

(6b) Computation of Period between Convictions 

In computing the period of time between convictions, as provided in sections one and two of this act, any period of servitude by a person in a penal institution, within or without this Commonwealth, shall not be included in the computation of any of said five year periods. It is hereby declared that the intent of said sections is that said five-year period shall run only during the time any such person was at liberty. 

Sec. 3. 17

(6c) Information Accusing Offender of

Previous Convictions 

If at any time within two years, either after conviction or sentence, it shall appear that a person convicted of any of the crimes set forth in section one of this act has previously been convicted of any such crimes three or more times, it shall be the duty of the district attorney of the county in which the last conviction was had to file an information accusing the said person of such previous convictions, and to serve a copy of such information upon the defendant thirty days before taking any further proceedings in court as hereinafter set forth. Thereupon the court in which the last conviction was had shall cause the said person, whether confined in prison, penitentiary or otherwise, to be brought before it, and shall inform him of the allegations contained in such information and of his right to be tried as to the truth thereof according to law, and shall require such offender to say whether he is the same person as charged in such information or not. If he says he is not the same person, or refuses to answer, or remains silent, his plea or the fact of his silence shall be entered on record, and a jury shall be impaneled to inquire whether the offender is the same person mentioned in the several records as set forth in such information. If the jury finds that he is the same person, or if he acknowledges or confesses in open court after being duly cautioned as to his rights that he is the same person, the court may sentence him to imprisonment for life prescribed in section two of this act, and shall vacate any previous sentence notwithstanding that the term of court in which the last sentence was imposed may have expired. Such sentence shall be reviewable on appeal by the Supreme or Superior Courts, not only as to alleged legal errors but also as to the justice thereof, and, where any such defendant is indigent, the costs of appeal, together with reasonable counsel fee, shall be paid by the Commonwealth. Whenever it shall become known to any warden or prison, probation, parole or police officer, or other peace officer, that any person charged with, or convicted of, crime has been previously convicted, within the mean-

ing of this act, it shall become his duty forthwith to report the facts to the district attorney of the county. Sec. 4. (6d) Indictment

A person need not be formally indicted and convicted as a previous offender in order to be sentenced under this act. Sec. 5. 

Act of April 29, 1929, P. L. 854, 19 P. S. 921; Com. v. Woodward, 110 Pa. Super. 478. 

7. Sentence under Unconstitutional 

Law, Repealed Law 

Whenever any person shall be sentenced under an unconstitutional law, he shall be resentenced under the provisions of the law in force at the time of the commission of the crime. 1

Where part of a repealed act is re-enacted after a crime has been committed, and there is no saving clause, or general saving clause, or general saving statute, the sentence may not exceed the penalty left in force.2 

1 Act of Feb. 28, 1913, P. L. 2, 19 P. S. 892. 

2 Com. v. Caravella, 113 Pa. Super. 263; Com. v. Beattie, 93 Pa. Super. 404. 

8. Court may not Impose Part of Sentence 

Court may not impose part of sentence at one term and an additional sentence at a subsequent term. 

Halderman's Petition, 276 Pa. 1; Com. v. Carelli, 90 Pa. 

Super. 416; Com. v. Ciccone, 84 Pa. Super. 224. 

9. One Act Different Offenses 

!

Where there is but one cause of injury or death of a number of persons, there is but one injury to the Commonwealth and there can be only one sentence, but where the acts or causes are separate, they are separate injuries.' 

A single act may be an offense against two statutes, and if each statute requires proof of an additional fact which the ་ ན་ The te. Hus

IL 31 12 15 12 1 "sa 102 119 Ni

other does not, an acquittal or conviction under either statute does not exempt the defendant from prosecution and punishment under the other.2 

1 Com. v. Valotta, 279 Pa. 84; Com. v. Ernesto, 93 Pa. 

Super. 339, six killed; Com. v. Baker, 115 Pa. Super. 183, receiving stolen goods. 

2 Com. v. Falls and Sikes, 107 Pa. Super. 129, indictment for malfeasance in office did not include extortion, bribery and violation of liquor law upon the commission of which offense it was based; Com. v. Kline, 107 Pa. Super. 594; Com. v. Berman, 119 Pa. Super. 315, conspiracy. 

10. Different Counts, Concurrent,

Cumulative, Included Offenses 

Ordinarily, when different counts charge, in effect, the same offense the sentence shall be limited to the punishment for the one most serious, though distinct sentences may be ordered when different violations of law are charged in different counts, 1 but in the latter case it is essential that not more than the maximum period of confinement for each crime be imposed, with the direction that one sentence take effect at the expiration of that preceding. If this does not appear the terms run concurrently.2 Additional sentence cannot be imposed for included offenses; the greater includes the less. 3 

1 Com. ex rel. v. Ashe, 293 Pa. 322; Com. v. Clark, 123 

Pa. Super. 277. 

2 Com. ex rel. Holinko, v. Ash, 290 Pa. 534; Halderman's 

Petition, 276 Pa. 1; Com. ex rel. v. Ashe, 114 Pa. Super. 332. 

3 Com. ex rel v. Heston, 292 Pa. 501, possession merged in sale of narcotics; Com. v. Bailey, 92 Pa. Super. 581, entering included in burglary; larceny included in breaking and entering, Com. ex rel. Wendell v. Smith, 123 Pa. Super. 113. 

11. Changing Sentence to Cumulative

After the expiration of the term the court may not alter its order by directing that the imprisonment be cumulative, since this would result in an increase of the time of incarceration. 

Halderman's Petition, 276 Pa. 1.

12. Specifying on which Count Sentence Imposed 

Where an indictment contains two counts and the defendant pleads guilty, the court is not bound to state specifically that it sentenced on the second count rather than the first, so long as the term of the sentence is not in excess of the maximum permitted on convictions of the former. If there be one count in an indictment to sustain a sentence imposed it is enough. 

Com. ex rel. Biscetti v. Leslie, 290 Pa. 530; Com. v. Gayton, 69 Pa. Super. 513; Com. v. Clark, 123 Pa. Super. 277. 

13. Insane Prisoners After Acquittal 

The court may order defendants acquitted on the ground of insanity to be committed to a hospital for mental diseases,¹ or released on parole after investigation.2 

1 Act of July 11, 1923, P. L. 998, Art. 3 Sec. 308; 50 P. S. 

48; Act of April 17, 1929, P. L. 532, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 1351. 

2 Act of May 23, 1913, P. L. 335, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 1381; Act of May 11, 1911, P. L. 273, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 1382; Com. v. Ward, 15 D. & C. 213. 

14. Mental Defectives, After Conviction

In case of the conviction of any person for any offense, the trial judge may, on his own initiative or on the application of the district attorney, the defendant, or counsel for the defendant or other person acting for the defendant, defer sentence until the report of a mental examination of the defendant can be secured to guide the judge in determining what disposition shall be made of the defendant. 

Act of May 2, 1933, P. L. 224, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1153.

The trial judge shall have power to require such mental examination, and report thereon, to be made by a psychiatrist employed by the state Department of Welfare or by a psychiatrist employed in any state hospital or in any mental hospital maintained by the county. Such report, when furnished to the judge, shall be available to counsel for the defendant and to the district attorney.

The psychiatrist making such examination shall not be entitled to any compensation for making such examination and report, but shall be paid by the county his actual expenses, on bills approved by the trial judge. 

Act of May 2, 1933, P. L. 224, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 1154.

If the report of the examination by the psychiatrist shows that the defendant though not insane is so mentally ill or mentally deficient as to make it advisable for the welfare of the defendant or the protection of the community that he or she be committed to some institution other than the county prison, workhouse or a penitentiary, the trial judge shall have power by virtue of this act to commit such defendant to any state or county institution provided for the reception, care, treatment and maintenance of such cases or similar mental cases, in lieu of a sentence to a county prison, workhouse or penitentiary where required by a relevant act of assembly or by law, and to direct the detaining of the defendant in such institution until further order of the court. 

The trial judge shall, at the time of such commitment, make an order upon the defendant, or such person or persons responsible for the support of the defendant, or upon the county or the Commonwealth, as may be proper in such case, for the cost of admission, care and discharge of such defendant.

Nothing contained in this act shall be construed to conflict with or repeal any portion of the Mental Health Act of 

1923, and the amendments thereto. 

Act of May 2, 1933, P. L. 224, Secs. 3, 4; 19 P. S. 11531156; Com. v. Iacobino, 319 Pa. 65. 

15. Accessories, Principals in the Second Degree, and Abettors of Misdemeanors Every principal in the second degree, or accessory before the fact, to any felony punishable under any act of assembly of this Commonwealth for whom no punishment is provided, shall be punishable in the same manner as the principal in the first degree is by such act punishable; every accessory after the fact to any felony punishable under any

act of assembly of this Commonwealth for whom no punishment is provided, shall, on conviction, be sentenced to pay a fine not exceeding five hundred dollars and to undergo an imprisonment, with or without labor, at the discretion of the court, not exceeding two years, and every person who shall counsel, aid or abet the commission of any misdemeanor punishable under any act of assembly of this Commonwealth for whom no punishment is provided shall be liable to be proceeded against and punished as the principal offender. 

Act of June 3, 1893, P. L. 286 Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 3671; Com. v. 

Gray, 265 Pa. 537; Com. v. Williams, 116 Pa. Super. 177, misdemeanor. 

16. Adultery

Sentence to penitentiary is not proper. 

Com. ex rel. v. Ashe, 295 Pa. 379. 

17. Bigamy 

Second marriage may be declared void. 

Com. v. Spohn, 95 Pa. Super. 261; Com. v. Walinski, 18 

Dist. Repts. 504. 

18. Conspiracy

One or two defendants may be convicted and sentenced after death of co-conspirator. 

Com. v. Bonnem, 95 Pa. Super. 496. 

19. Desertion, Non-Support 

In addition to the remedies now provided by law, if any husband or father, being within the limits of this commonwealth, has, or hereafter shall, separate himself from his wife or from his children, or from wife and children, without reasonable cause, or shall neglect to maintain his wife or children, it shall be lawful for any alderman, justice of the peace, or magistrate of this Commonwealth, upon information made before him under oath or affirmation, by his wife or children, or either of them, or by any other person or per-

sons, to issue his warrant to the sheriff or to any constable for the arrest of the person against whom the information shall be made, as aforesaid, and bind him over, with one sufficient surety, to appear at the next court of quarter sessions, there to answer the said charge of desertion. 

Act of April 13, 1867, P. L. 78, Sec. 1; 18 P. S. 1251. 

20. Residence of Defendant 

If a delinquent husband can be reached by a warrant, it is sufficient to give the court jurisdiction and compel him to support his wife and children, without regard to his residence or settlement and without reference to where the original desertion or neglect to maintain his family took place. Furthermore, the residence and citizenship of the wife is immaterial.1

An order of support may be made where a husband deserts his wife or children or both without reasonable cause, and where a husband neglects to maintain his wife or children, although he may continue to reside in the same house.2 

1 Com. ex rel. v. Sanders, 111 Pa. Super. 202; Com. v. 

Carson, 82 Pa. Super. 291. 

2 Com. ex rel. v. Cunningham, 102 Pa. Super. 104; an order under Act of 1867 does not bar a proceeding under Act of 

1903, Com. v. Carson, 82 Pa. Super. 291. 

21. Court Order for Support, Enforcement 

The information, proceedings thereon, and warrant shall be returned to the next court of quarter sessions, or other court having jurisdiction thereof, when it shall be lawful for said court, after hearing, to order the person against whom complaint has been made, being of sufficient ability, to pay such sum as said court shall think reasonable and proper for the comfortable support and maintenance of the said wife or children, or both, and to commit such person to the county prison, there to remain until he comply with such order, or give security, by one or more sureties, to the Commonwealth, and in such sum as the court shall direct for the compliance therewith; and to further enforce compliance with the said

order, the court may issue the appropriate writ of execution against any property, real or personal, belonging to the defendant, and its writ of attachment execution against any money or property to which he may be in any way entitled, whether under what is known as a spendthrift trust or otherwise; and its said writ of attachment execution shall become a lien and continuing levy upon any money or property to which he may be in any way entitled, whether under what is known as a spendthrift trust or otherwise, which shall not exceed fifty per cent thereof, and shall remain a continuing levy until the order has been paid in full with costs. And it is further provided that the person against whom an order is made shall not be entitled to the benefits of any exemption law now in force or hereafter passed. And it is further provided that the provisions of this act shall apply to any trust, whether it is known as a spendthrift trust or otherwise, whether such trust was created or came into existence before or after the passage of this act. Where an attachment execution is issued the further proceedings thereon shall be in the manner heretofore practiced and allowed in the case of foreign attachments. 

Act of June 15, 1917, P. L. 614, Sec. 1; 18 P. S. 1252; Act of May 10, 1921, P. L. 434, Sec. 1; 48 P. S. 136; Com. v. Cleary, 95 Pa. Super. 592, amount of payments, minor, second marriage; Com. v. Gilleland, 93 Pa. Super. 307, amount awarded wife should not exceed one-third the income from property and labor; Com. v. Leonard, 93 Pa. Super. 21, income $60.00 a week, order $25.00 weekly; Com. ex rel. v. O'Malley, 105 Pa. Super. 

232, duty to support continues beyond age of twenty-one when child is mentally or physically feeble; Com. v. Gillen, 102 Pa. 

Super. 136, college expenses; Com. v. Hayes, 18 Del. Co. 440, agreement with wife not to prosecute is no defense where agreement is not carried out; Com. v. McClelland, 109 Pa. Super 211, non-support is a continuing offense, plea of former acquittal no bar; Everhart v. Everhart, 87 Pa. Super. 184, spendthrift trust; order effective from date on which made, Com. v. Ehrhart, 118 

Pa. Super. 293. 

22. Non-Support, Imprisonment at Labor

Whenever in any proceedings brought against any man, wherein it is charged that he has, without reasonable cause, separated himself from his wife or children, or from both, NO 4016 319 BG REN 

SITE 

1

or has neglected to maintain his wife or children; or in any proceedings where any father of an illegitimate child has neglected to comply with the order of court made against him, in fornication and bastardy proceedings, or in any other proceedings for the support of such child, for the payment to the mother of expenses incurred at the birth of the child; or in any proceedings where any child of full age has neglected or shall neglect to maintain his or her parents, not able to work or of sufficient ability to maintain themselves,-the court having jurisdiction shall commit the defendant to imprisonment, for want of a bond with security; or, otherwise, he court may order the defendant to be imprisoned at hard labor under existing laws, or laws that may hereafter be passed, in such penal or reformatory institution in this Commonwealth as the court shall direct; or the court may discharge a defendant upon his own recognizance, in the custody of a desertion probation officer or other person, subject to such conditions as the court may, in its descretion, impose. 

Whenever any defendant shall be ordered to be imprisoned at hard labor, under the provisions of this act, there shall be paid by the official in charge of the penal or reformatory institution in which such defendant is imprisoned, to the person designated in the order of the court as the proper recipient of such money, to be disbursed by the said recipient as the order of the court may direct, the sum of sixty-five cents for each day, Sundays and legal holidays only excepted, during which he remains imprisoned. Such sum shall be paid as wages, and shall be paid at such times and in such manner as other wages are paid by cities and counties, and shall be charged as one of the general running expenses of such institution; and, if the labor done in such institution is not sufficient to pay the running expenses of such institution, such sum shall be charged to and paid by the county from which such defendant was committed. 

Act of May 24, 1917, P. L. 268, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 1151; Com. v. McClelland, 109 Pa. Super. 211.

Fot

23. Forfeiture of Bond or Recognizance 

If the defendant in any such proceeding shall violate the terms of the order of court, the court may issue an attachment upon the petition of such defendant's parents, wife, child or children, or of any other person or persons having knowledge of the facts.

In case of the forfeiture of a bail-bond in any such proceedings, the court may order that any sum collected, by suit or otherwise, shall be paid, in whole or in part, to such parent, wife, child or children. In the case of the forfeiture of any bond or recognizance with or without surety, given as security under any order of court, any sum collected thereon, by suit or otherwise, shall be paid to such parent, wife, child or children. Such payment shall not bar or in any way affect the power of the court to enforce its orders against the defendant by attachment or otherwise. 

Act of May 24, 1917, P. L. 268, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 1152. 

24. Attachment for Contempt

Where an order of support has not been complied with for a period of thirty days, upon affidavit or petition filed setting forth that the person on whom the order has been made has not complied with it, the court shall issue an attachment directed to the sheriff or other proper officers of the county, directing and commanding that the person named as having failed to comply with the order be brought before the court forthwith, or at such other time as the court may in its discretion direct. If it shall appear after hearing that the defendant has wilfully neglected to comply with the order the court may adjudge him in contempt of the court. 

Act of May 14, 1925, P. L. 762, Sec. 1013; 62 P. S. 1013, act does not apply to first and second class counties; Com. v. Varner, 103 Pa. Super. 149, where petition in proper form is presented it is mandatory that court issue the attachment (or rule to show cause why attachment should not issue); Act of June 

15, 1911, P. L. 973, Sec. 1; 62 P. S. 1960; Com. ex rel. v. Berardino, 99 Pa. Super. 537, giving of bond does not bar issuing of attachment. 12 10 11

25. Non-Compliance with Order, Discharge 

Whenever the court of quarter sessions shall, under 

Sec. 2, Act of April 13, 1867, P. L. 78 (Par. 21), commit the person complained of to the county jail, there to remain until he comply with their order or give security, et cetera, it shall be lawful for the said court, at any time after threemonths, if they shall be satisfied of the inability of such person to comply with the said order, and give such security, to discharge him from imprisonment. 

Act of April 15, 1869, P. L. 75, Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 1255. 

26. Desertion and Non-Support, Misdemeanor 

If any husband or father, being within the limits of this. commonwealth, shall hereafter separate himself from his wife or from his children, or from wife and children, without reasonable cause, and shall wilfully neglect to maintain his wife or children, such wife or children being destitute, or being dependent wholly or in part on their earnings for adequate support, he shall be guilty of a misdemeanor; and on conviction thereof be sentenced to imprisonment not exceeding one year, and to pay a fine not exceeding one hundred dollars, or either, or both, at the discretion of the court; such fine, if any, to be paid or applied in whole or in part to the wife or children, as the court may direct.

Provided, that no such conviction, payment of fine, or undergoing imprisonment shall, in any manner, affect the obligation of any order for support theretofore made against the defendant in the court of quarter session, (or the municipal court) in the manner now provided by law; and provided further, that upon conviction, the court may suspend sentence, upon and during compliance by the defendant with any order for support therefore made against him, as already made or as may thereafter be modified, in the manner now provided by law; and if no such order shall have been made, then the court trying the defendant may make such order for the support by the defendant of his wife and children, or either of them, which order shall be subject to modification by the court on cause shown, and may suspend

sentence, upon and during the compliance by defendant with such order then made or as thereafter modified and entry of bond by defendant, with surety approved by the court, conditioned on compliance with such order. 

Act of March 13, 1903, P. L. 26, Sec. 2; 18 P. S. 1257; Act of July 12, 1919, P. L. 939, Sec. 2; 18 P. S. 2160, municipal court;

Com. v. McClelland, 109 Pa. Super. 211; wife is a competent witness, Act of March 13, 1903, P. L. 26, Sec. 3, 18 P. S. 1258, this act did not repeal or supersede Act of April 13, 1867, P. L. 

78; Act of July 11, 1917, P. L. 773, Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 2161, illegitimate child, Com. v. Allen, 15 D. & C. 731, procedure; Com. v. 

Smith, 117 Pa. Super. 318, jury trial. 

27. "Children" and "Separation" Defined 

As used in this act, (Par. 26) the word "children" shall be limited to mean children under sixteen years of age, and also such children over sixteen and under twenty-one years of age as, by reason of infirmity, are incapable of supporting themselves; and further, that "separation," within the meaning of this act, shall include every case where a husband has caused his wife to leave him by conduct on his part which would be ground for divorce. 

Act of March 13, 1903, P. L. 26, Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 1256; Act of July 12, 1919, P. L. 939, Sec. 1, 18 P. S. 2159; defines "children," "separation" and "non-support." 

28. False Pretense 

Restitution as part of the sentence is proper. 

Com. v. Williams, 102 Pa. Super. 216; Com. v. Schmidt, 95 Pa. Super. 102; Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382. Sec. 111, 18 P. S. 2631. 

29. Fornication and Bastardy

If any person shall commit fornication, and be thereof convicted he or she shall be sentenced to pay a fine not exceeding one hundred dollars to the guardians, directors or overseers of the poor of the city, county or township where the offense was committed, for the use of the poor of such city, county or township; and any single or unmarried wom- 1 it 4 10 ·7 17 4 25 12 00%

ATEN 2

an having a child born of her body, the same shall be sufficient to convict such single or unmarried woman of fornication; and the man by such woman charged to be the father of such bastard child, shall be the reputed father, and she persisting in the said charge, in the time of her extremity of labor or afterwards, in open court, upon the trial of such person so charged, the same shall be given in evidence, in order to convict such person of fornication; and such person, being thereof convicted, shall be sentenced, in addition to the fine aforesaid, to pay the expenses incurred at the birth of such child, and if such child shall be born dead or shall die during the continuance of the order for the maintenance of said child, to pay the reasonable funeral expenses thereof, and to give security, by one or more sureties, and in such sum as the court shall direct, to the guardians, directors, or overseers of the poor of the city, county or township where such child was born, to perform such order for the maintenance of the said child, as the court before which such conviction is had shall direct and appoint. 

Act of May 29, 1935, P. L. 249. 18 P. S. 711. Com. v. Losey, 79 Pa. Super. 75, declarations. 

30. Judgment May Be Entered in Fornication and Bastardy Cases

From and after the passage of this act, when any person shall have been convicted of fornication and bastardy, and sentenced by any court of quarter session to pay the mother of any bastard child any sum or sums of money for the support of such child, it shall be lawful for the mother of such child to file, in the court of common pleas of the county in which such conviction shall have been had, a copy of such sentence, certified by the clerk of the proper court of quarter sessions and under the seal thereof; upon which copy, so filed,. the prothonotary of the court of common pleas shall enter judgment in favor of the mother and against the defendant, for the full amount of the said sentence, payable in the instalments therein provided, with interest thereon from the time they shall respectively become due, and costs of suit. 

Act of June 7, 1907, P. L. 429, Sec. 1, 12 P. S. 1003.

31. Fieri Facias on Default: No Exemption 

If default be made in the payment of any such instalments, and continue for five days, a writ of fieri facias may issue for the collection of all past due instalments, and no exemption of property from levy and sale shall be allowed. 

Act of June 7, 1907, P. L. 429, Sec. 3, 12 P. S. 1004. 

32. Attachment Execution: No Exemption 

In addition to the writ of fieri facias, above provided, attachment execution may be issued, and in addition to such rights and credits as are now attachable, wages and salaries may also be attached thereon, and no exemption of any money, rights, or credits attached thereby shall be allowed. 

Act of June 7, 1907, P. L. 429, Sec. 3, 12 P. S. 1005. 

33. Reissue of Writs

The said writs, either or both, may be issued as often as default occurs, until the whole judgment be paid. 

Act of June 7, 1907; P. L. 429, Sec. 4, 12 P. S. 1006. 

34. Costs

The defendant shall be liable for all costs on any of said writs when properly issued. 

Act of June 7, 1907 P. L. 429, Sec. 5, 12 P. S. 1007.

Electrocution or life at the discretion of the jury, or in cases of pleas of guilty, at the discretion of the court.¹

36. Procedure

The clerk shall transmit a complete record of the trial to the Governor within thirty days after sentence, or within twenty days after final disposition of an appeal. After the receipt of the record the Governor shall issue his warrant to the warden of the Western Penitentiary commanding him to execute the convict within the week to be named in the warrant. The warden shall notify the officer having custody of the convict to deliver him forthwith. While in the custody of the warden no person shall be allowed access to the convict except counsel, a spiritual adviser selected by the convict, and members of the convict's immediate family. Those allowed to witness the execution are the warden, a physician, six reputable adult witnesses selected by the warden, one spiritual adviser when requested, not more than six newspapermen, and such officers of the penitentiary as may be selected by the warden. The warden shall certify the fact of execution to the court of oyer and terminer which sentenced the convict, together with a report of the post-mortem examination. 

Act of June 19, 1913, P. L. 528, 19 P. S. 1122-1127. 

37. Prostitutes

Prostitutes may be committed to an approved private institution adapted to the proper control of women of this class. 

Act of June 30, 1923, P. L. 982, Sec. 3, 18 P. S. 803. 

38. Gambling Devices, Forfeiture

It shall and may be lawful for any sheriff, constable or other officer of justice, with or without a warrant, to seize upon, secure and remove any device or machine of any kind, character or description whatsoever, used and employed for the purpose of unlawful gambling as aforesaid, and to arrest, with or without warrant, any person setting up the same. 

And it shall be the duty of such sheriff, constable or other officer to make return, in writing, to the next court of quarter sessions of the proper county, setting forth the nature

and description of the device or machine so seized upon, and the time, place and circumstances under which such seizure was made; and the said court, upon hearing the parties, if they should appear, if satisfied that such device or machine was employed and used for the purpose of unlawful gambling as aforesaid, shall adjudge the same forfeited, and order it to be publicly destroyed, and at the same time order such reasonable costs and charges to the seizing officer as they shall deem adequate and just, to be paid by the owner or possessor of such device or machine, or in case of his default, or in case he cannot be found, to be paid as costs are now by law paid upon indictments; and such adjudication shall be conclusive evidence to establish the legality of such seizure, in any court of this commonwealth, in any case in which the question of its legality shall arise; and in any case in which a decree of forfeiture shall not be pronounced, if said court shall, upon the evidence, be satisfied that there was probable cause for the seizure, they shall certify the same, which certificate shall be a bar to any action brought against the officer for or on account of such seizure, in those cases in which the said officer returns, or offers to return such device or machine; and in all cases shall prevent a recovery in damages, for any sum beyond the real value of the device or machine seized.¹

No writ of replevin shall issue for any device or machine, seized as aforesaid, nor shall any action be instituted for or on account of such seizure, until the court shall have at first adjudicated upon the premises.2 

1 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 60; 18 P. S. 1445. 

2 Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 61; 18 P. S. 1446; 

Mill's Novelty Company's App., 316 Pa. 449. 

39. When Restitution To Be Made 

On all convictions for robbery, burglary or larceny of any goods, chattels, or other property, made the subject of larceny by the laws of this Commonwealth, or for otherwise unlawfully and fraudulently taking or obtaining the same, or of receiving such goods, chattels or other property, knowing the same to be stolen, the defendant shall, in addition to 1 PAS 110 to 6 5355 14 18

the punishment heretofore prescribed for such offense, be adjudged to restore to the owner the property taken, or to pay the value of the same, or so much thereof as may not be restored. And on all convictions on any indictment for forgery, for uttering, publishing or passing any forged or counterfeit coin, bank notes, check or writing, or any indictment for fraudulently, by means of false tokens or pretences, or otherwise, cheating and defrauding another of his goods, chattels or other property, the defendant in addition to the punishment hereinbefore prescribed for such offenses, shall be adjudged to make similar restitution, or other compensation, as in case of larceny, to the person defrauded; Provided, that nothing herein shall be so construed as to prevent the party aggrieved, and to whom restitution is to be awarded, from being a competent witness on the trial of the offender. 

Act of March 31, 1860 P. L. 382, Sec. 179, 19 P. S. 981; Com. v. Williams, 102 Pa. Super. 216, conspiracy. 

40. Maintenance of Destitute Families, First and Second Class Cities 

Cities of the first and second classes may appropriate money for the maintenance of destitute families of persons sentenced in such cities, and are and were, at the time of conviction domiciled within said city. 

Act of April 12, 1923, P. L. 65, Sec. 2, 53 P. S. 1823. 

41. Suspension, Not a Formal Judgment, Period of

The suspension of sentence is not a final judgment, nor is it equivalent to a nolle prosequi or discontinuance, by which the case is put out of court, but is a mere suspending of active proceedings in the case, and the court may after the expiration of the term bring in the defendant and impose the sentence authorized by law.¹ The suspension can continue no longer than till the expiration of the maximum term of imprisonment, 2 but sentence may be imposed after the expiration of the term. After the expiration of the term the court cannot alter its order by directing that the imprisonment be cumulative since this would result in an increase of

the time of incarceration. The suspension of sentence upon payment of costs is not a sentence or judgment."

Chapter XXVII PLACE OF CONFINEMENT 1. IMPRISONMENT AT LABOR FOR LESS THAN ONE YEAR: SIMPLE IMPRISONMENT 2. CONFINEMENT AT LABOR FOR ONE YEAR OR MORE 3. SIMPLE IMPRISONMENT, AT LABOR, SEP- ARATE, OR SOLITARY CONFINEMENT 4. MALES, FIFTEEN TO TWENTY-FIVE YEARS OF AGE 5. WOMEN NOT TO BE SENTENCED TO PENITENTIARY 6. FEMALES OVER SIXTEEN 7. MALES AND FEMALES UNDER TWENTY- ONE, WESTERN DISTRICT 8. CHILDREN, PHILADELPHIA 9. FIRST CLASS COUNTIES, MISDEMEANORS 10. COUNTIES HAVING INDUSTRIAL FARMS 11. LABOR BY CONVICTS IN COUNTY JAIL 12. INSANE PRISONERS, WHEN SENTENCE EXPIRES BEFORE RECOVERY, RECOV- ERY BEFORE SENTENCE EXPIRES 

1. Imprisonment at Labor for Less than

One Year: Simple Imprisonment 

No person shall be sentenced to imprisonment at labor by separate or solitary confinement for a period of time less than one year, except in the counties where, in the opinion of the court pronouncing the sentence, suitable prisons have been erected for such confinement and labor; and all persons sentenced to simple imprisonment for any period of time shall be confined in the county jail where the conviction shall take place; Provided, that in the counties where suitable prisons for separate or solitary confinement at labor do not exist, and the sentence shall be less than one year, simple imprisonment shall be substituted in all cases for the separate and solitary confinement at labor required by the act to con- TYRE 272

solidate, revise, and amend the penal laws of this commonwealth. 

Act of June 26, 1895, P. L. 374, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 891; Com. v.

Fetterman, 26 Pa. Super. 569, simple imprisonment means imprisonment in the county jail; Com. v. Árbach, 113 Pa. Super. 

137; Com. v. Prince, 25 D. & C., 485; Com. ex rel. v. Francis, 250 Pa. 350; "month" means lunar month of twenty-eight days; 

Com. v. Stanley, 12 P. C. C. 543. 

2. Confinement at Labor for One Year or More 

Whenever any person shall be sentenced to imprisonment at labor, by separate or solitary confinement, for any period not less than one year, the imprisonment and labor shall be had and performed in the state penitentiary for the proper district; Provided, that nothing in this section contained shall prevent such person from being sentenced to imprisonment and labor, by separate or solitary confinement, in the county prisons now or hereafter authorized by law to receive convicts of a like description. 

Act of Feb. 28, 1905, P. L. 25, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1021; Com. v. 

Prince, 25 D. & C. 485, rape, penitentiary or workhouse. 

3. Simple Imprisonment, at Labor, Separate, or Solitary Confinement

When the penalty is simple imprisonment for whatever period, the place of confinement is the county jail or county workhouse. "Imprisonment" or "Simple Imprisonment" means confinement in the county jail; "Imprisonment at labor, by separate or solitary confinement" means imprisonment in the penitentiary or in a suitable county prison, but if for less than one year means county jail or county prison.2

4. Males, Fifteen to Twenty-five Years of Age 

Males between fifteen and twenty-five years of age and not known to have been previously sentenced to a state prison in this state or elsewhere, may be sentenced to the 

Pennsylvania Industrial Reformatory at Huntingdon, for a term to be fixed by the board of managers, not exceeding the term provided by law. 

Act of April 28, 1887 P. L. 63, 61 P. S. 483. 

5. Women Not to be Sentenced to Penitentiary 

Women shall not be sentenced to imprisonment in any state penitentiary, but instead of imprisonment in a penitentiary shall be sentenced to imprisonment in the State In dustrial Home for Women at Muncy. 

Act of July 17, 1935, P. L. 1165, 19 P. S. 1021. 

6. Females Over Sixteen

Any court of record in this Commonwealth, exercising criminal jurisdiction, may, in its discretion, sentence to the

State Industrial Home for Women, any female over sixteen years of age, upon conviction for, or upon pleading guilty of, the commission of any criminal offense punishable under the laws of this state. 

COON Dm? 

Act of June 22, 1931, P. L. 859, Sec. 61, P. S. 566.

7. Males and Females under Twenty-one,

Western District 

Infants (under twenty-one) taken or committed as vagrants or upon any criminal charge within the Western District may be committed to the Pennsylvania Training 

School at Morganza, during their minority. 

Act of April 22, 1850, P. L. 538, Sec. 15, 11 P. S. 561. 

8. Children, Philadelphia

Children guilty of vagrancy or convicted of criminal charges may be committed to the house of refuge (Glen 

Mills Schools). 

Act of March 23, 1826, P. L. 133, Sec. 6, 11 P. S. 491; Act of April 10, 1835, P. L. 133, Sec. 1, 11 P. S. 492. 

9. First Class Counties, Misdemeanors 

For misdemeanors courts in counties of the first class may sentence to a reformatory or house of correction for a period not exceeding one year. No prisoner so sentenced shall be relieved by pardon, parole or probation, except upon order from the court making the commitment. 

Act of May 10, 1921, P. L. 433, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1023. 

10. Counties Having Industrial Farms counties that have complied with the provisions of the Act of June 12, 1931, P. L. 512 (61 P. S. 727-743), establishing industrial farms and workhouses, any person convicted of any offense, crime, misdemeanor, or felony (except murder, voluntary manslaughter, rape and arson) the punishment of which is, or may hereafter be imprisonment in any county jail or prison for a period of ten days or more, shall be sentenced to the industrial farm and workhouse of the district in which said court may have jurisdiction. Courts of record and courts not of record shall commit to the industrial farm and workhouse of the district all persons who might be lawfully committed to the county jail or prison on

charges of vagrancy, drunkenness or disorderly conduct, or for default or nonpayment of any cost, fine or penalty, or for default in complying with any order of court entered for desertion or nonsupport, where, in any such case, the commitment will be for a period of ten days or more. (Sec. 1).

Compensation to be paid at the rate of ten to twenty cents a day for nonproductive labor, and twenty to fifty cents a day for productive labor. The court shall order the disbursement of earnings of persons confined for non-support. (Sec. 9)

The county jails may be retained to confine persons awaiting trial, or sentenced for a period less than ten days, and such number of other convicted persons as may be required to perform the necessary institutional maintenance work. (Sec. 8). 

11. Labor by Convicts in County Jail 

All persons sentenced to simple imprisonment, for any period of time, in the county jails may be required to perform such labor, in the custody of the sheriff, about the county buildings and upon the grounds and property of the county as the commissioners of the county in which the prisoners are confined may specify, and the said commissioners are authorized to allow and pay from the moneys of the county, to the sheriff, for his services in guarding such prisoners while employed, compensation not to exceed twenty-five cents per hour. 

Act of June 26, 1895, P. L. 374, Sec. 1, 61 P. S. 183; first class counties, Act of May 20, 1931, P. L. 138, Sec. 2, 61 P. S. 

256; Com. v. Camwell, 91 Pa. Super. 540. 

12. Insane Prisoners, When Sentence Expires Before Recovery, Recovery Before Sentence Expires 

If the sentence of a person committed to a hospital for mental diseases, under the provisions of this section, shall expire before his recovery, he may be transferred, by the authority of the department, to any other hospital for care until his recovery, when he shall be discharged as in the case of any noncriminal patient. If the person found to be in-

sane and removed to a hospital is a convict and recovers before the expiration of his sentence, or is found, after observation for a sufficient time, not to be insane, the superintendent or chief officer of the hospital to which said person has been committed shall properly certify such fact to the court which has committed him, and he shall be returned, upon order of the said court, to the prison from which he was removed. The expense of removal shall be paid by the county liable for his maintenance in the prison from which he was removed, without recourse against any proper district. 

Act of July 11, 1923, P. L. 998, Art. 3, Sec. 308, 50 P. S. 48.

Chapter XXVIII COMMUTATION 1. COMMUTATION FOR GOOD BEHAVIOR, TERM ONE YEAR OR MORE 2. WHERE PRISONER IS HELD UNDER MORE THAN ONE CONVICTION 3. MONTHLY REPORT OF PRISONERS TO BE DISCHARGED 4. CONDITIONS COMMUTATION 5. PRISON BOARDS TO DETERMINE AMOUNT OF COMMUTATION 6. DISCRETION OF GOVERNOR 7. GOVERNOR NOT TO ACT, EXCEPT UPON RECOMMENDATION 8. ACT TO BE READ TO PRISONERS 

1. Commutation for Good Behavior,

Term One Year or More 

Every convict confined in any state prison, penitentiary, workhouse or county jail, on a conviction of felony or misdemeanor, whether male or female, where the term or terms equal or equals or exceeds one year, exclusive of any term which may be imposed by the court or by statutes as an alternative to the payment of a fine, or term of life imprisonment, may, if the governor shall so direct, and with the approval of the board of inspectors or managers, earn for himself or herself a commutation or diminution of his or her sentence or sentences as follows, namely; two months for the first year, three months for the second year, four months each for the third and fourth years, and five months for each subsequent year, and for every fraction or part of a year the same rate of commutation as is provided for the year in which said fractional part occurs. (See Par. 8). 

Act of May 11, 1901, P. L. 166, Sec. 1, 61 P. S. 271, See act of June 29, 1923, P. L. 975, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1057; Com. ex rel. v. 

Heston, 292 Pa. 501; Com. v. Sweeney, 281 Pa. 550; In re Raymond, 110 Fed. 155; Com. ex rel. v. Shearrer, 7 D. & C., 150, 278

county prison; Com. ex rel. v. Warden, 23 Dist. Repts. 1032; 

Parole and Discharge, 23 Dist. Repts. 585; Commutation Act, 23 Dist. Repts. 100; Commutation of sentence, 22 Dist. Repts. 

206, 42 Pa. C. C. 223; Keepers of prisons to make reports of prisoners to be discharged the following month, Act of May 11, 1901, P. L. 166, Sec. 3, 61 P. S. 273. 

2. Where Prisoner is Held under More 

Than One Conviction

Where any convict in any state prison, penitentiary, workhouse, or county jail in this state is held under more than one conviction, the several terms of imprisonment imposed thereunder shall be construed as one continuous term, for the purpose of estimating the amount of commutation to which he or she may be entitled under the provisions of this act. 

Act of May 11, 1901, P. L. 166, Sec. 2, 61 P. S. 272. 

3. Monthly Report of Prisoners To Be Discharged

On any day not later than the twentieth day of each month the board of inspectors or managers, or the warden, superintendent, or keeper of each of the state prisons, penitentiaries, workhouses, or county jails of this state, shall forward to the governor a report, directed to him, of any convict or convicts who may be discharged the following month by reason of the commutation of his or her sentence or sentences, which shall contain the following information, namely; The full name of the convict, together with any alias which he or she may be known to have; the name of the county where the conviction was had; a brief description of the crime of which the convict was convicted; the name of the presiding judge; the date of the sentence; the date of the reception in the prison or penitentiary; the term and fine; the amount of commutation recommended, and the date for discharge from the prison, penitentiary, workhouse or jail, if allowed. 

Act of May 11, 1901, P. L. 166, Sec. 3, 61 P. S. 273. ་་་་་ི་ཡ 

NE DE 

1498 pe UND 11-

4. Conditions of Commutation 

The governor shall, in commuting the sentence of convicts, annex a condition to the effect that if any convict socommutted shall, during the period between the date of his or her discharge by reason of such commutation and the date of the expiration of the full term for which he or she was sentenced, be convicted of any felony, he or she shall, in addition to the penalty which may be imposed for such felony committed in the interval, as aforesaid, be compelled to serve in the prison, penitentiary or workhouse in which he or she may be confined for the felony for which he or she is convicted, the remainder of the term, without commutation, which he or she would have been compelled to serve but for the commutation of his or her sentence. 

Act of May 11, 1901 P. L. 166, Sec. 4, 61 P. S. 274. 

5. Prison Boards to Determine Amount of Commutation

The boards of inspectors and managers of the stateprisons, penitentiaries, workhouse, and county jails in this state shall meet once every month, before the date fixed for the transmission of their report to the governor, as hereinbefore provided, and proceed to determine the amount of commutation which they shall recommend to be allowed to any convict, which shall not in any case exceed the amount fixed by this act. They shall have full discretion to recommend the withholding of the allowance of commutation for good conduct or of a part thereof, as a punishment for offenses against the discipline of the prison, penitentiary or county jail, or for any attempt to escape therefrom. 

Act of May 11, 1901 P. L. 166, Sec. 5, 61 P. S. 275. 

6. Discretion of Governor 

In all cases, however, where the board shall recommend' the withholding of the allowance of the whole or any part of the commutation for good conduct, they shall forward' with their report to the governor their reasons, in writing,,

for such disallowance, and the governor may, in his discretion, decrease or increase the amount of commutation as recommended by the said board, but he shall not increase the same beyond the amount fixed by this act. 

Act of May 11, 1901, P. L. 166, Sec. 5, 61 P. S. 276. 

7. Governor Not to Act, Except 

Upon Recommendation

Provided, however, that the governor shall not execute any of the rights or powers herein granted unto him until the lieutenant governor, secretary of the commonwealth, attorney general and the secretary of internal affairs, or any three of them, after full hearing, upon due public notice and in open session, according to such rules as they shall provide, shall have recommended the said commutations and diminutions of sentence. 

Act of May 11, 1901, P. L. 166, Sec. 5, 61 P. S. 277. 

8. Act to be Read to Prisoners

Upon the receipt of any convict in any prison, penitentiary or county jail in this state, who shall be entitled to the benefits of this act, the provisions of the same shall be read to him or her, and the meaning of the same shall be fully explained to him or her by the clerk of the prison, penitentiary, workhouse or jail. 

Act of May 11, 1901, P. L. 166 Sec. 6, 61 P. S. 278. +1 ​1110 1190 th ​才 ​4

Chapter XXIX PROBATION, PAROLE 1. FIRST OFFENDERS, SUSPENSION OF SEN- TENCE, PROBATION 2. ORDER OF PROBATION NOT A SENTENCE 3. PAYMENT OF FINE IN INSTALMENTS 4. VIOLATION OF PROBATION, ARREST, SENTENCE 5. DISCHARGE FROM PROBATION, DURATION, 6. VIOLATION, HEARING 7. PAROLE FROM JAIL, WORKHOUSE, HOUSE OF CORRECTION 8. PAROLE FROM PENITENTIARY 9. INSPECTORS TO REPORT REASON FOR NOT RECOMMENDING PAROLE 10. SUPERVISION DURING PERIOD OF PAROLE 11. VIOLATION OF PAROLE, PENITENTIARY 12. CONVICTION DURING PAROLE 13. VIOLATION OF PAROLE, INDUSTRIAL REFORMATORY 14. RECOMMENDING PARDON, BOARD OF INSPECTORS 15. PAROLE AFTER ACQUITTAL BECAUSE OF INSANITY 16. INVESTIGATION BY COURT 17. TERMS AND CONDITIONS OF RELEASE 1. First Offenders, Suspension of 

Sentence, Probation

Whenever any person shall be convicted of any crime (except murder, administering poison, kidnapping, incest, sodomy, buggery, rape, assault and battery with intent to ravish, arson, robbery, or burglary) and it does not appear that the defendant has ever before been imprisoned for crime in this state or elsewhere (detention in any institution for juvenile delinquents shall not be considered) and when the court believes the character of the defendant and the circum- 282

stances of the case are such that the person is not likely again to engage in any offensive course of conduct, and that the public good does not demand or require that the penalty imposed by law should be suffered, the court may suspend the imposing of sentence and place the defendant on probation for a definite period, on such terms and conditions, including the payment of money for the use of the county, not exceeding the fine fixed by law for conviction of such offenses, as it may deem right and proper. No such condition for the payment of money shall be considered as the imposition of a fine or a sentence nor prevent the court from thereafter sentencing any defendant under the act under which he was convicted upon violation of his or her parole. The terms and conditions are to be entered as part of the judgment. 

Com. ex. rel. Wilhelm v. Morgan, 278 Pa. 395; Com. v. 

Renya, 91 Pa. Super. 90; Act of May 7, 1925, P. L. 554, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1051; Act of May 10, 1909, P. L. 495, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 

1081; Com. v. Fotte, 17 D. & C. 453; Com. v. Prince, 25 D. & C. 485. 

2. Order of Probation Not a Sentence 

The order of probation is not a sentence nor a substitution for a sentence. At any time prior to his discharge the defendant may be arrested and sentenced for violating the terms of his probation. 

Com. v. Fox, 69 Pa. Super. 456. 

3. Payment of Fine in Instalments 

In any case where a fine only is imposed, and the defendant might be imprisoned until such fine be paid, the court may direct, as one of the terms of the probation, that such fine shall be paid in certain instalments at certain times; Provided, however, that upon payment of the fine, judgment shall be satisfied and probation ceases. 

Act of June 19, 1911, P. L. 1055, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 1052; Act of May 10, 1909, P. L. 495, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 1082.

4. Violation of Probation, Arrest, Sentence

Whenever a person placed on probation, as aforesaid, shall violate the terms of his or her probation, he or she shall be subject to arrest in the same manner as in the case of an escaped convict; and shall be brought before the court which released him or her on probation, which court may thereupon pronounce upon such defendant such sentence as may be prescribed by law, to begin at such time as the court may direct. 

Act of June 19, 1911, P. L. 1055, Sec. 4, 19 P. S. 1055; Act of May 10, 1909 P. L. 495, Sec. 4, 19 P. S. 1084. 

5. Discharge from Probation, Duration

Whenever it is the judgment of the court that a person on probation has satisfactorily met the conditions of his or her probation, the court shall discharge such defendant and cause record thereof to be made; Provided, that the length of such period of probation shall not be less than the minimum nor more than the maximum term for which the defendant might have been imprisoned; And provided also, that when no minimum term is fixed by the law, it shall in all such cases be one-fourth of the maximum sentence. 

Act of May 10, 1909, P. L. 495, Sec. 5, 19 P. S. 1085; Act of 

June 19, 1911, P. L. 1055, Sec. 5, 19 P. S. 1056; convict need not have sponsor. Act of May 28, 1913, P. L. 363, Sec. 1, 61 P. S. 315. 

6. Violation, Hearing

On failure to perform the conditions the defendant may be sentenced as provided in the act under which the indictment was drawn; it is not obligatory on the court in inquiring into the conduct of the defendant while on probation to proceed according to the forms of law observed at the trial, nor is the evidence to be reviewed by the court limited to that which may come through the channel of a regular examination in court. It is the judgment of the court which is to be informed.¹ It is only when the person on probation has sat-

isfactorily met the conditions of his probation that the court is required to discharge him. 

Com. ex rel. v. Morgan, 278 Pa. 395, period of probation, Com. v. Fox, 69 Pa. Super. 456. 

7. Parole from Jail, Workhouse, House of Correction 

1 Courts of record of criminal jurisdiction are authorized, after due hearing, to parole convicts (including those committed by justice of the peace and magistrates) in the county jail, house of correction, or workhouse. The parole shall extend for a period not to exceed the maximum sentence provided by law for the offense of which the convict was convicted regardless of the sentence first imposed on the prisoner.

Upon presentation of a petition, verified by the prisoner or someone in his behalf, the court shall fix a day for hearing. A copy of the petition, with notice of the time for hearing, must be served on the prosecutor, if residing within the county, and the district attorney, ten days before the date of hearing, and proof of service produced at the hearing. If a parole is granted the prisoner shall be placed under the supervision of a probation officer. The court shall have power to recommit on cause shown by the probation officer that a convict has violated his parole, and to reparole in the same manner as in the original parole. 

Act of May 11, 1923, P. L. 204, Sec. 1, 61 P. S. 314; Com. v. 

Renya, 91 Pa. Super. 90; Com. v. Donovan, 25 Lack. J. 311, prison bounds, ill health; Com. v. Eotte, 17 D. & C. 453; Com. v. Prince, 25 D. & C., 485. 

8. Parole from Penitentiary 

Parole from penitentiaries is upon the recommendations of their boards of trustees to the governor, who may grant it only when the state board of pardons consents. The trustees recommend parole only after notifying the district #1 2 11 11 18 19 1110 Top POP 12.

JA 2 ​Marie 39 AT 1 19

attorney who prosecuted the case and the judge, or his successor, before whom it was heard. 

Com. v. Renya, 91 Pa. Super. 90; Act of June 19, 1911, P. L. 1055, Sec. 9 Sec. 15, 61 P. S. 304, 311; Act of June 29, 1923, P. L. 975, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1057; Com. v. Prince, 25 D. & C. 485. 

9. Inspectors to Report Reason for

Not Recommending Parole 

In any case where a board of prison inspectors does not recommend the release on parole of a convict, at the termination of the minimum term for which such convict may have been sentenced, such board shall report in writing to the governor its reasons, in detail, for not having made such recommendation. 

Act of June 19, 1911, P. L. 1055, Sec. 11, 61 P. S. 306. 

10. Supervision During Period of Parole 

The Board of Pardons has jurisdiction to supervise the conduct, during the period of parole, of all persons released on parole from the state penitentiary and the Pennsylvania Industrial Reformatory, excluding dependent, neglected, incorrigible, and delinquent children. 

Act of May 1, 1929, P. L. 1184, Sec. 5, 6, 71 P. S. 845, 846. 

11. Violation of Parole, Penitentiary

Whenever it shall appear to the state Board of Pardons that a paroled convict of the state penitentiary has violated his parole the board shall cause a warrant to issue for his arrest and return to the penitentiary, where he shall be given an opportunity to appear before the board of trustees of the penitentiary. If the trustees find that the parole has not been broken the prisoner shall be released subject to the terms of the parole, but if it be found that the parole has been broken a full report shall be made to the governor who may recommit the convict for the remainder of a period equal to the unexpired maximum term of such prisoner, as originally sentenced, computing the same from the date of arrest

for breach of parole (unless sooner released on parole or pardon).

If the governor shall disapprove the finding of the trustees, the prisoner shall be released upon the conditions of the original parole.¹ Where the parole of a prisoner sentenced to a state penitentiary has been revoked for violation of the rules of the parole board, he is entitled to an allowance of credit from the time he was out on parole and not delinquent, and must be discharged at the expiration of the maximum period, computed from the date of sentence.2 

1 Act of May 1, 1929, P. L. 1182, Sec. 1, 2, 61 P. S. 309, 310. 

2 Com. ex rel. Crimmins v. Smith, 118 Pa. Super. 255. 

12. Conviction During Parole 

If any convict released on parole as provided for in this act, shall, during the period of his or her parole, or while delinquent on said parole, commit any crime punishable by imprisonment for which he or she is at any time thereafter convicted in any court of record and sentenced to any place of confinement other than the penitentiary from which he or she was released on parole, such convict shall, in addition to the penalty imposed for such crime committed during the said period, and after the expiration of the same, be compelled, by detainer and remand as for an escape, to serve in the penitentiary from which said convict has been released on parole, or any other institution to which he or she may be legally transferred, the remainder of the term (without commutation) which said convict would have been compelled to serve but for the commutation authorizing said parole, and if not in conflict with the terms and conditions of the same as granted by the Governor; but, if sentenced to the penitentiary from which said convict was released on parole, then the service of the remainder of the said term originally imposed shall precede the commencement of the term imposed for said crime. If no new sentence is imposed for such crime or crimes, punishable by imprisonment, committed during period of parole, or while delinquent on parole, for which he or she has been convicted in any court of record, either by plea or trial, said convict shall be compelled, by detainer and re- 18 14 #1 2 2 110 12 2 73 137 A 18 2 

ANET 

10 13

mand as for an escape, to serve in the penitentiary from which said convict had been released on parole, or any other institution to which he or she may be legally transferred, the remainder of the term (without commutation) which said convict would have been compelled to serve but for the commutation authorizing the parole, and if not in conflict with the terms and conditions of the same as granted by the Governor.¹

Where the parole of a prisoner sentenced to a state penitentiary has been revoked for the commission of another crime, he may be imprisoned for another period equalling the remainder of the maximum sentence not served when the parole was granted, without allowance of credit for the time he was out on parole and not delinquent.2 

1 Act of June 22, 1931, P. L. 862, Sec. 1; 61 P. S. 305, Pardon, 27 Dist. Repts. 830; In re Lane's Parole, 27 Dist. Repts. 75; 

O'Gorek's case, 45 Pa. C. C. 404; Clay's case, 47 Pa. C. Č. 47. 

2 Com. ex rel. Menizer v. Smith, 118 Pa. Super. 250. 

13. Violation of Parole, Industrial Reformatory 

When a paroled inmate of the Pennsylvania Industrial Reformatory at Huntingdon is alleged to have broken his parole he shall be given a hearing by the board of trustees, and if the board finds that the parole has not been broken, it shall notify the board of pardons, whereupon the prisoner shall be released and continue subject to the terms of said parole. But if it be found that said parole has been broken, the prisoner may be required to serve the unexpired term of his possible maximum sentence, at the discretion of the board of trustees, and the time from the date of his declared delinquency to the date of his return to the reformatory shall not be counted on any part or portion of such sentence. 

Act of May 11, 1931, P. L. 109, 61 P. S. 521. 

14. Recommending Pardon, Board of Inspectors

If it shall appear to the board of inspectors of any penitentiary, from which a convict has been released on parole, that there is a reasonable probability that such convict will

live and remain at liberty without violating the law, and it will be to the advantage of such convict that he or she be released from the terms of his or her parole, then, upon the application of such convict released on parole, it shall be the duty of the said board to recommend to the governor that such convict be pardoned absolutely. 

Act of June 19, 1911, P. L. 1055, Sec. 13, 61 P. S. 308. 

15. Parole after Acquittal because of Insanity 

When any person charged with any crime or misdemeanor has been acquitted by the jury on the ground of insanity, such person may be kept in strict custody or released from custody on parole, at any time, by the court wherein such person shall have been acquitted. 

Act of May 23, 1913, P. L. 335, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1381, 1382; 

Com. v. Wilcox, 77 Pa. Super. 136; Com. v. Ward, 15 D. & C. 213. 

16. Investigation By Court 

Before the court shall direct the release of any person acquitted on the ground of insanity, it shall ascertain in such manner as it may direct whether it shall be advisable that such person be released on parole.¹ The procedure usually followed, is, a petition for a rule on the commonwealth to show cause why the defendant should not be released on parole, an answer by the district attorney and hearing. Either the commonwealth or the defendant may appeal.2 

1 Act of May 11, 1911, P. L. 273, Sec. 2; 19 P. S. 1382. 

2 Com. v. Baginski, 85 Pa. Super. 47; Com. v. Wilcox, 77 

På. Super. 136. 

17. Terms and Conditions of Release 

The court directing the release on parole of any person acquitted on the grounds of insanity may provide such terms and conditions of said release as to it may seem proper. 

Act of May 11, 1911, P. L. 273, Sec. 3; 19 P. S. 1383; Com. v. Baginski, 85 Pa. Super, 47; Com. v. Wilcox, 77 Pa. Super. 136.

Chapter XXX DISCHARGE 1. PERSONS CONFINED FOR COSTS, BENEFIT OF INSOLVENT LAWS 2. DISCHARGE, FAILURE TO MAKE RESTITU- TION, FORNICATION AND BASTARDY 3. INSOLVENCY ACT, RESIDENTS FOR SIX

THE INSOLVENCY LAW 5. UNDER TWO TERM RULE 6. FULFILLMENT OF SENTENCE EQUIVALENT TO PARDON 

1. Persons Confined for Costs, Benefit of Insolvent Laws

Every person and persons who is, are, or shall be held in confinement, by order or judgment of any court of this `commonwealth, for the costs of prosecution, shall be entitled to the benefit of the several acts of assembly of this commonwealth for the relief of insolvent debtors, and may be discharged from personal imprisonment by the court in which such prosecution was or may be had so far as regards confinement of their bodies for said costs, if such court shall, on consideration of the circumstances of such person or persons, find that he, she or they are or shall be unable to discharge the said costs of prosecution; Provided, that the like previous notice of such application for discharge from confinement be given to the several persons interested in the said costs as the law requires where insolvent debtors in other cases apply for such discharge. (See Par. 4). 

Act of Sept. 23, 1791, 3 Smith L. 37, Sec. 12, 19 P. S. 1292. 290

2. Discharge, Failure to Make Restitution, Fornication and Bastardy

The court of common pleas of any county, in which any person may be confined by sentence or order of any court of this commonwealth until he destore any stolen goods or chattels, or pay the value thereof; or on which any person may be confined for the nonpayment of any fine or of the costs of prosecution, or upon conviction of fornication and bastardy, and for no other cause, shall discharge such person from confinement on his making application and conforming to the provisions herein directed in the case of insolvents, who have been arrested on civil process; Provided, that where such person shall have been sentenced to the payment of fine, or after a conviction of fornication and bastardy, he shall not be entitled to make such application until after he shall have been in actual confinement for a period of not less than three months, except in case of a fine not exceeding fifteen dollars, exclusive of costs, in which event the actual confinement need not exceed thirty days. 

Act of June 4, 1901, P. L. 404, Sec. 6, 39 P. S. 13; a defendant may be discharged under the insolvency act for failure to comply with the order in a fornication and bastardy case, notwithstanding the Act of May 24, 1917, P. L. 268, 19 P. S. 1151, In re Insolvency of Hogan, 83 Pa. Super. 221; Com. ex rel. v. 

Myers, 7 D. & Ć. 150. 

3. Insolvency Act, Residents for Six Months 

Parties who have been residents of the state six months are entitled to be discharged under the insolvent laws as follows; 

1. When sentenced to pay costs only, forthwith and without imprisonment, upon making the application as provided by law. 

2. When sentenced to pay a fine of fifteen dollars or less, without imprisonment, at the end of thirty days. 

3. When sentenced to pay a fine of fifteen dollars, or less, and also to imprisonment, at the end of thirty days after the term of imprisonment shall have been served, in both of

these cases the discharge should be made without awaiting the order of court. 

4. When sentenced to pay a fine of more than fifteen dollars, without any sentence of imprisonment, at the end of three months. 

5. When sentenced to pay a fine and to undergo an imprisonment, and the fine exceeds fifteen dollars, at the end of three months from and after the expiration of the term of imprisonment.¹

A court of quarter sessions which imposes a sentence of fine and imprisonment upon defendant's plea of guilty is without jurisdiction to amend or reform the sentence by remitting the fine, the period of imprisonment having been served in full, after the expiration of the term at which sentence was imposed; such power is vested in the Governor.2 

1 Johnson's case, 2 Dist. Repts. 700; Jury imposing costs, see 1 Dist. 774, Com. v. Hefferman, 20 Del. 333. 

2 Com. v. Smith, 18 D. & C. 460, 48 Montg. Co. 164. 

4. Discharge without Proceeding under the Insolvency Law

The county commissioners, upon the order of the court of quarter sessions, or, in vacation, of a law judge thereof, in the exercise of its or his discretion, and upon such terms as the court or judge may impose, may discharge from prison, without the delay and expenses of any proceedings under the insolvent laws of this Commonwealth, every convict who shall have served out his or her term of imprisonment, or who shall have been committed for nonpayment of costs only; or any person who shall have failed to comply with the order of the court of quarter sessions upon conviction of fornication or bastardy, or for fornication and bastardy, and been confined for a period of more than three months, notwithstanding if he be convicted and shall not have paid the costs of prosecution, fine, or made restitution, or paid the value of stolen goods or property, or failed to comply with the court's order upon conviction of fornication or bastardy, or fornication and bastardy; Provided, that in the opinion of

said commissioners, such person is unable to pay or restore the same; and, provided, that such discharge shall not prevent the commonwealth, or any person interested in such payment or restitution, from proceeding by action to recover the same from the property of such person; but no such person shall be so discharged until he or she shall have made, under oath or affirmation, duplicate schedules of all his or her property, real, personal or mixed, so far as he or she can ascertain the same, one of which shall be filed among the papers of the said prison, and the other with the clerk of the court of quarter sessions; Provided further, that nothing in said proceeding shall either add to or take from the liability of said county for costs, under existing laws of this Commonwealth. 

Act of May 6, 1915, P. L. 266, Sec. 1, 39 P. S. 14; Com. v. 

Schortel, 9 D. & C. 313. 

5. Under Two Term Rule 

If any person shall be committed and not be indicted and tried sometime in the next term, he is entitled to be set at liberty upon bail, unless the Commonwealth's witnesses cannot be produced; and if he is not indicted and tried the second term he shall be discharged, unless he has consented to the delay. This law does not apply to fugitives from other states.

The case remains open for trial thereafter upon due and proper notice given to the defendant of the time when his case will be called. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 54, 19 P. S. 781; Com. v. Zec, 262 Pa. 251; Bunch's Petition, 29 Dist. Repts. 139; Com. v. Sukena, 11 D. & C. 340. 

6. Fulfillment of Sentence Equivalent to Pardon

When a person has been or shall be convicted of any felony, not punishable with death, or of any misdemeanor punishable with imprisonment at labor, and has endured the punishment to which such offender has been adjudged for the same, the punishment so endured shall have the like effect je 10.

N° 10 WEL 

1 INA 2

and consequences as a pardon by the governor, as to the felony or misdemeanor whereof such person was so convicted; 

Provided, that the enduring of such punishment shall not prevent or mitigate any punishment to which the offender might otherwise be lawfully sentenced on a subsequent conviction for any other felony or misdemeanor, and that the provisions hereof shall not extend to the case of a party convicted of wilful and corrupt perjury. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 181, 19 P. S. 893; 

Diehl v. Rodgers, 169 Pa. 316; County v. Copley, 67 Pa. 386;

Pitts v. Lehmer, 7 D. & C. 547; Com. v. Hagan, 10 Pa. C. C. 22.
