26. JURORS, WITNESSES FEES 27. SET OFF, WITNESS FEES 28. INNOCENT DEFENDANTS, MAGISTRATES 29. MAGISTRATE, FINE AND COSTS, TO WHOM MAY BE PAID 30. SETTING ASIDE VERDICT OF COSTS 31. PRACTICE, SETTING ASIDE VERDICT OF COSTS 32. FEES OF JUSTICE OF THE PEACE AND ALDERMAN 1. Fines to be for Use of County 

All fines imposed upon any party, by any court of criminal jurisdiction, shall be decreed to be paid to the commonwealth; but the same shall be collected and received, for the use of the respective counties in which such fines shall have been imposed as aforesaid, as is now directed by law. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 78, 19 P. S. 951. 

2. Payment of Fine or Costs in Instalments 

Any person sentenced to pay a fine or to pay the costs of any criminal proceeding against him, either in addition to or without a term of imprisonment, under any act of assembly or municipal or borough ordinance, may, in the discretion of the sentencing authority, be given leave to pay such fine or costs by instalments. 

Act of May 17, 1917, P. L. 199, Sec. 1, P. S. 953; Act of 

May 11, 1911, P. L. 1055, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 1052. 

3. Court to Fix Instalments 

In giving leave under the foregoing section (Sec. 1), the sentencing authority shall fix the amount of each instalment, and the dates of payment; but no order giving such leave shall prescribe a period longer than twelve months for the completion of payment of the entire fine or costs. 

Act of May 17, 1917, P. L. 199, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 954.

4. Effect of Default 

Upon default in payment of any one instalment, under any such order, the entire unpaid balance of the fine or costs shall at once become due and payable. 

Act of May 17, 1917, P. L. 199, Sec. 3, 19 P. S. 955. 

5. Commitment, Stay

An order under section one of this act (Par. 2), giving leave to pay a fine or costs by instalments, shall not bar the sentencing authority from issuing a warrant or commitment against the defendant, but the execution of such warrant shall be stayed until default occurs in the payment of any instalment. 

Act of May 17, 1917, P. L. 199, Sec. 4, 19 P. S. 956. 

6. County Liable Primarily for Expenses 

Counties have the primary responsibility for the expenses of all criminal proceedings, unless expressly relieved therefrom. Such has been the state of the law since the organization of the Commonwealth. This extends to many cases where no statutory liability exists, as in the case of a prisoner taken ill at the trial or a juror in the box; the expenses of a coroner in a post mortem examination; the lodging and boarding of a jury, and many other instances which might be enumerated. Where no other provision is made for the expenses of the judiciary, the county in which the court sits is primarily liable. All of the expenses incidental to the trial of causes are born by the county except that portion particularly known as costs. 

Com. v. Irvin, 110 Pa. Super. 387; Com. v. Garramone, 115 

Pa. Super. 588.

Costs of prosecution on bills charging felony, returned ignoramus,¹ and all costs of prosecution when the defendant is acquitted or convicted, shall be paid by the county.2 It seems that where a felony count (other than larceny and 1132 14* 10 1 1 xf

8. Misdemeanors, Ignoramus

If the bill of indictment shall be returned ignoramus the grand jury shall decide and certify on such bill whether the county or the prosecutor (naming him) shall pay the costs of prosecution (including such witnesses as the district attorney shall certify where subpoenaed by his order, where in actual attendance, and necessary), and the costs shall be immediately chargeable to and paid by the county, without any formal sentence being passed upon the prosecutor if he is made liable. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 62, 19 P. S. 1222; Act of May 19, 1887, P. L. 138, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1225; Act of May 3, 1933, P. L. 238, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1227; Allen v. County, 161 Pa. 

550; Dougherty v. County, 26 Pa. Super. 610; Charge to Grand 

Jury, 62 Pitts. L. J. 105; larceny and receiving stolen goods where the value shall be less than $100.00, the jury shall determine on acquittal or ignoramus who shall pay the costs; Act of May 3, 1933, P. L. 328, 19 P. S. 1227; Conniff v. Luz. Co. 30

Pa. Super. 383, county liable on defective bill returned ignoramus. 

9. Misdemeanors, Acquittal, Conviction, Larceny 

On acquittal in misdemeanors, larceny and receiving stolen goods when the value of the goods stolen shall be less than 100 dollars (jury determines yalue, Act of May 3, 1933,

P. L. 238, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1227), the jury may direct that the costs of prosecution be paid by the county, or by the defendant, or by the prosecutor, or apportion them between the defendant and the prosecutor, stating in what proportion.

In cases in which the prosecutor is directed to pay the costs or any portion thereof, he must be named in the verdict. 

Where costs are imposed on the defendant or prosecutor, the court shall forthwith pass sentence to that effect, and order him to be committed to the county jail until the costs are paid, unless he gives security to pay the same within ten days. (See Par. 11).

County is liable for costs of prosecution in all cases of misdemeanor where the bill is ignored, or where there is a verdict of guilty and sentence. Only such witnesses as the district attorney shall certify where subpoenaed by him, and were in attendance and necessary, are entitled to costs. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 62, 19 P. S. 1222; 

Com. v. Fell, 31 Pa. Super. 574, costs on corporation when agent prosecutor; Com. v. Dickinson, 62 Pa. Super. 468, game warden; Com. v. Maguire, 1 D. & C. 671, private prosecution, officer, Com. v. Clement, 8 Dist. 705, must be sentence or order of court before costs are payable, Com. v. Bishoff, 13 C. C. 503. 

10. Cost, Joint Offenders

In all cases where two or more persons have committed an indictable offense, the names of all concerned shall be contained in one bill of indictment, for which no more costs shall be allowed than if the name of one person was contained therein; but upon the conviction or acquittal of any defendant named in such bill of indictment, the costs in such case shall be paid by the county, or the prosecutor, or defendant, in the same manner as though the defendant so convicted or acquitted had been alone indicted. 

Act of April 17, 1929, P. L. 532, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1291.

12 URD 。

11. Defendant's Bill when Costs Divided, or Prosecutor to Pay

When the defendant is acquitted, and the prosecutor is directed to pay the costs, or the costs are divided between the defendant and the prosecutor (naming him in the verdict), the defendant's bill for his subpoena, serving the same, and attendance of his material and necessary witnesses shall be included in the costs. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 63, 19 P. S. 1261; Act of April 9, 1873, P. L. 67, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1262; county is not liable to defendant's witnesses where costs are placed on the county, County of Franklin v. Conrad, 36 Pa. 317. 

12. Where Convicted Defendant Discharged 

Without Payment, County Is Liable

In all cases of conviction of any crime, all costs shall be paid by the party convicted; but where such party shall have been discharged, according to law, without payment of costs the costs of prosecution shall be paid by the county. (Discharge from confinement for fines and costs, see Discharge). 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 427, Sec. 64, 19 P. S. 1223; 

Codding v. Bradford Co. 116 Pa. 47, not in summary conviction. 

13. Costs to be First Paid Out of Fines and Forfeited Bail

Whenever, under the provisions of any law, any fines or forfeited bail are payable to the Commonwealth, all costs of prosecution in any such case shall first be paid out of any money realized, whether the same is received as costs in full, or on account of, any fine imposed or on the forfeiture of any bail; and in the event that such moneys received are not sufficient to pay the full amount of such fine and costs, the costs shall be paid in full, and any balance remaining shall be paid into the State Treasurer through the Department of Revenue on account of such fine. 

Act of July 15, 1935, Sec. 1, P. L. 1002, 19 P. S. 1296.

14. Defective Indictment, Etc. Apportionment 

Jury may dispose of the costs in cases of defective indictments;¹ directed verdict of "not guilty," and on an acquittal on a plea of former conviction or acquittal,2 but courts are not agreed as to the power of the jury in imposing costs where the court does not have jurisdiction of the offense charged." 

1 Com. v. Keenan, 67 Pa. 203. 

2 Com. v. Huggins, 2 Dist. Repts. 329; Com. v. Cohen, 102 Pa. Super. 397, where there is both an insufficient indictment and a lack of proof of the offense the jury has nothing to do with the costs; Com. v. Ajay 16 D. & C. 49. 

3 Com. v. Balph, 44 Pitts. L. J. 148. 

15. Nolle Prosequi

When a nolle prosequi is entered the court may make an order directing the county to pay the costs, not including costs of defendant's witnesses, provided the proceeding is one in which the jury could charge the costs to the county. 

Act of April 14, 1905, P. L. 152, Sec. 1; 19 P. S. 1230. 

16. Insanity, Acquittal

If the defendant shall be acquitted on the grounds of insanity, the court before whom the trial is had shall order the costs of prosecution to be paid by the county. 

Act of April 17, 1929, P. L. 532, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 1351. 

17. Escaped Convicts, Trials

The costs of the transportation of escaped convicts, and of the trial of an escaped convict for the commission of any crime or misdemeanor committed after his escape and before his apprehension, or of the trial of offenses committed on the grounds of the institution, shall be paid by the county from whose courts the convict shall have been originally committed, and in case of conviction the cost of maintenance. 

Act of Feb. 28, 1933, P. L. 3, 19 P. S. 1233, 1234. 20

18. Expenses of Investigations by the District Attorney

All necessary expenses incurred by the district attorney in the investigation of crime and the apprehension and prosecution of persons charged with or suspected of the commission of crime, upon the approval of the bill of expenses by the district attorney and the court, shall be considered part of the costs in the event that a prosecution is instituted which results in the conviction and sentence of a defendant to pay the costs.¹ Expenses and transportation of witnesses out of the state, room-rent, meals, and remuneration are not necessary expenses.2 

1 Act of June 29, 1923, P. L. 973, 16 P. S. 345. 

2 Com. v. Wilson, 71 Pitts. 868. 

19. District Attorney, Expenses,

Counsel Fee on Appeal 

On appeals the necessary expenses of the district attorney, including additional compensation for his services, fixed by the court, shall be paid by the county.¹ 

The county in a criminal case is liable to pay for the cost of printing the paper book of a defendant who is successful in the appellate court on appeal from conviction in the quarter sessions and who is acquitted on his second trial.2 

1 Act of May 19, 1877, P. L. 138, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 1263. 

2 Com. v. Trunk, 320 Pa. 270. 

20. Duplicate Informations

When one complaint or information is legally sufficient to serve and promote the due administration of justice, it shall be unlawful to duplicate the complaint, information, warrant and subpoena, and where such duplication occurs, costs shall be taxed upon only one complaint, information and subpoena. 

Act of March 10, 1905, P. L. 35, Sec. 1, 2, 19 P. S. 1293, 1294; Com. v. Avrach, 110 Pa. Super. 438; Com. v. Smith, 62 

Pa. Super. 288, validity of proceedings not affected by duplication; Com. v. Potteiger, 27 Dist. Repts. 781; Com. v. Kram. lick, 27 Dist. Repts. 267; Barry v. McPerson, 24 Dist. Repts. 118.

21. Felonious Assault, No Reasonable Ground

In prosecutions for assault or assault and battery where the charge is that the assault was made with the intent to maime, disfigure or disable another, or to commit the crime of murder, or an assault which constitutes a felony, if the bill of indictment shall be returned ignoramus, the grand jury, if they believe from the evidence that the prosecutor had no reasonable grounds for making the charge of felony, shall decide and certify on such bill whether the prosecutor or the county shall pay the costs of prosecution, and in all cases of acquittal by the petit jury on such indictment, if the jury believes that the prosecutor has no reasonable ground for making the charge of felony, they shall determine by their verdict whether the county, or the prosecutor, or the defendant shall pay the costs, or whether the same shall be apportioned between the prosecutor and the defendant, and in what proportion.¹ The jury should be instructed that they have nothing to do with the costs unless they believe from the evidence that the prosecutor had no reasonable ground for making the charge of felony.2 

1 Act of May 25, 1897, P. L. 89, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 1228; prosecutor must be named in verdict; Com. v. Johnson, 50 Pa. C. C. 257. 

2 Com. v. Donovan, 119 Pa. Super. 544. 

22. House of Assignation

If a person, being the owner of any building, has knowledge of, or reason to believe, that such building, or a part thereof, is used for the purpose of fornication, lewdness, assignation, or prostitution, and suffers the same to be so used, such building shall be subject to a lien for and may be sold to pay all fines and cost assessed against the person guilty of maintaining such nuisance for such violation of this act; and the total amount of such fine and costs may be entered, by the filing of a certificate thereof by the clerk of the court in which the same was imposed, in the court of common pleas and shall remain a lien in favor of the county until paid. Judg-

ment may be obtained thereon and execution issued in the manner provided by law in the case of municipal liens. 

Act of June 23, 1931, P. L. 1178, Sec. 2, 68 P. S. 468. 

23. Surety of the Peace, Desertion

The costs of the officers, including the costs of the justice of the peace and constable, in all cases of wife desertion and surety of the peace, shall be chargeable to and paid by the proper county as soon as the case is disposed of by order of the court. It is the duty of the district attorney and county commissioners to collect the costs from the party made liable by the sentence or order of the court. 

Act of May 19, 1887, P. L. 138, Sec. 2, 19 P. S. 1226; Act of 

April 13, 1867, P. L. 78, Sec. 3, 18 P. S. 1253. 

24. Gambling Devices

Owner or possessor may be ordered to pay the reasonable costs and charges of the seizing officer. 

Act of March 31, 1860, P. L. 382, Sec. 60, 18 P. S. 1145. 

25. Recognizance Paid, Costs To Be Paid

When a recognizance is forfeited and paid, the county shall pay all costs, not exceeding the amount recovered on the recognizance. 

Act of May 2, 1901, P. L. 127, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 1229. 

26. Jurors, Witnesses' Fees

The pay of jurors shall be three dollars per day together with mileage at the rate of six cents for each mile going to and returning from the county seat; and the pay of witnesses shall be two dollars per day, together with mileage at the rate of three cents for each mile going to and returning from the county seat. 

Act of May 22, 1933, P. L. 851, Sec. 1, 17 P. S. 1121a.

27. Set Off, Witness Fees 

County may set off against any money due as witnesses' fees or due whatsoever (jurors' fees and mileage excepted) from the county any sums due the county as costs, fines or other money by reason of a sentence. 

Act of April 28, 1899, P. L. 97, Sec. 1, 2 (taxes), 16 P. S. 

3691, 3692. 

28. Innocent Defendants, Magistrates 

Where a person is brought before a court or magistrate on a charge of having committed a crime, and such charge upon examination shall appear to be unfounded, no costs shall be charged against such innocent person, but the same shall be charged against and paid by the county, except in cases of summary conviction in which case costs are not payable unless so provided by statute. (See Chap. VII Par. 

16, 17; see Par. 32). 

Act of Sept. 23, 1791, 3 Smith Laws, 37, Sec. 13, 19 P. S. 

1221; Com. v. Kane 65 Pa. Super. 258, disorderly conduct; 

Dougherty v. County, 26 Pa. Super. 610, summary convictions. 

See Act of May 3, 1933, P. L. 242, Secs. 1, 2; 19 P. S. 1235; act does not apply to trials, Com. v. Cohen, 102 Pa. Super. 397. 

29. Magistrate, Fine and Costs, To Whom May Be Paid 

Any person committed by an alderman, magistrate, or justice of the peace to a county prison, or city or borough or township lockup, in default of payment of fine and costs, may pay such fine and costs, to any duly elected alderman, magistrate, or justice of the peace of said county, and shall be discharged and released by the warden or keeper of any prison or station house, or any place wherein said defendant is incarcerated, upon the presentation of a discharge signed and sealed by the said alderman, magistrate or justice of the by the said alderman, magistrate or justice of the peace. 

Act of June 15, 1915, P. L. 845, 19 P. S. 952.

11 

30. Setting Aside Verdict of Costs 

The court has the common law supervision of the costs which belong to trial by jury, and it is within the power of the court to set aside that part of a verdict imposing costs on the prosecutor or on the defendant.¹ Verdicts placing costs on prosecutors have been set aside in the following instances: when the wrong party is named as prosecutor; in cases of a serious nature where the prosecutor has not acted maliciously and improperly, and when the prosecutor is a public officer.2 

The court may make an order directing the county to pay the costs, not including costs for defendant's witnesses.

168; Com. v. McCarthy, 67 Pa. Super. 135; Com. v. Bixon, 67 

Pa. Super. 554; Com. v. Mikula, 2 D. & C. 170; Com. v. Maguire, 1 D. & C. 671. Com. v. Cohen, 102 Pa. Super. 397. 

2 Com. v. Bixon, 67 Pa. Super. 554; Com. v. Dickerson, 62 Pa. Super. 468; Com. v. Moore, 26 Dist. Repts. 698; Com. v. 

Brown, 24 Lanc. L. R. 149; Com. v. Stetler, 77 P. L. J. 493, private officer, street railway.

31. Practice, Setting Aside Verdict of Costs 

Should be by petition for rule on the commonwealth to show cause why that part of the verdict imposing costs on the prosecutor or defendant should not be set aside and the costs imposed on the county. Where the prosecutor petitions it would be good practice to serve the rule on the defendant also.

The fees of justice of the peace and alderman shall be as follows; For

Each additional defendant named on information or warrant, ten cents. 

Docket entry of action in behalf of the Commonwealth, seventy-five cents.

Hearing in each criminal case, each day occupied or fraction of day, one dollar. 

Administering oaths in criminal cases, ten cents.

Docket entry of confession of plea of guilty in criminal cases, seventy-five cents.

Making docket entry of testimony in cases of summary conviction, each witness, fifty cents. 

Taking bail for a hearing or for appearance at quarter sessions, each defendant, seventy-five cents. 

Entering judgment on conviction for fine, seventy-five cents. 

Recording conviction, fifty cents. 

Recording sentence, fifty cents. 

Warrant to levy fine or forfeiture, seventy-five cents. 

Bail piece and return, one dollar. 

Commitment of each defendant, seventy-five cents. 

Discharge to jailer, seventy-five cents. 

Entering discontinuance in criminal cases, fifty cents.

Transcript and certificate for any purpose to any one in criminal case, one dollar.

In all cases of summary convictions relating to the use and operation of motor vehicles, and suits for penalties relating to the use and operation of motor vehicles, whether under statute or ordinance, the total fee shall be two dollars and twenty-five cents, which shall include the giving of a transcript to either the defendant or prosecutor, or both, if same be requested. In all cases of summary conviction, in which the evidence is not sufficient to convict, and the defendant is dismissed, the costs of the suit shall be paid by the county.

In all cases of summary convictions, in which the defendant is convicted and sentenced to jail in default of payment of the fine and costs imposed, the costs of prosecution shall be paid by the county.

1. Constitution

The privilege of the writ of habeas corpus shall not be suspended, unless when in case of rebellion or invasion the public safety may require it.

Art. 1, Sec. 14, Const. Penna. 

309

2. What Courts May Issue 

The Supreme,¹ Superior,2 Common Pleas³, and Court of Quarter Sessions of Philadelphia County, may issue writs of habeas corpus. 

The Supreme Court does not issue writs of habeas corpus when other courts are available for the purpose.5 

1 Art. 5, Sec. 3, Const. Penna.; Act of June 16, 1836, P. L. 

784, Sec. 7, 17 P. S. 48. 

2 Act of May 5, 1899, P. L. 248, Sec. 3, 17 P. S. 181.

4 Act of April 4, 1837, P. L. 377, Sec. 2, 17 P. S. 502. 

5 Com. ex rel. v. Keeper, 295 Pa. 252. 

3. Issuing Writ When Defendant In Custody of Law 

If any person shall be, or stand committed or detained for any criminal or supposed criminal matter, unless for treason or felony, the species whereof is plainly and fully set forth in the warrant of commitment, in vacation time and out of term, it shall and may be lawful to and for the person so committed or detained, or any one on his or her behalf, to appeal or complain to any judge of the supreme court, or to the president of the court of common pleas for the county within which the person is so committed or detained; and such judge or justice, upon a view of the copy or copies of the warrant or warrants of commitment or detainer, or otherwise, upon oath or affirmation legally made, that such copy or copies were denied to be given by the person or persons in whose custody the prisoner is detained, is hereby authorized and required, upon request made in writing by such prisoner, or any person on his or her behalf, attested and subscribed by two witnesses, who were present at the delivery of the same, to award and grant a habeas corpus, under the seal of the court, whereof he shall then be a judge or a justice, to be directed to the person or persons in whose custody the prisoner is detained, returnable immediately before the said judge or justice, and to the intent, and that no officer, sheriff, jailer, keeper or other person, to whom such

writ shall be directed, may pretend ignorance of the import thereof, every such writ shall be made in this manner, "By act of assembly, 1785," and shall be signed by the judge or justice who awards the same.¹

Application for the writ should be made in the name of the Commonwealth at the relation of the petitioner, and against the person alleged to wrongfully hold the defendant in custody, and should aver that defendant is now in custody.2 

It is not improper practice to grant a rule to show cause why the writ should not be allowed, and admit the defendant to bail pending a decision on the questions raised.³

It is questionable whether "the county in which the person is so detained", applies to the courts of the county in which a convict is detained by virtue of a sentence of a court of another county." 

1 Act of Feb. 18, 1785, 2 Sm. L. 275, Sec. 1, 12 P. S. 1871. 

2 Com. ex rel. v. Cooper, 277 Pa. 554; failure to attest petition is amendable, Com. ex rel. v. Keeper, 26 Pa. Super. 191. 

8 Com. ex rel. v. Reifsteck, 271 Pa. 441. 

4 Com. ex rel. v. Ash, warden, 10 D. & C. 538; Com. ex rel. v. Mathieu, 107 Pa. Super. 261; in Com. ex rel. v. Ash, 114

Pa. Super. 119, the petition was filed in Allegheny County on behalf of a prisoner in the Western Penitentiary committed from Beaver County. 

4. When Writ May Be Used, Defendant in Custody of Law

The writ is not to be used to fulfill the functions of an appeal, or to bring for review mere errors or irregularities which may have occurred in a trial court having jurisdiction of the person and the subject matter. Except in unusual cases, where the proceeding has been adopted in furtherance of the prompt administration of justice,¹ a writ of habeas corpus can be effectively invoked by one convicted of crime only where it appears the sentencing court was without jurisdiction;2 the record shows no crime was committed; the passing of an illegal sentence; or where there is an improper detention of the relator after the expiration of his term of imprisonment by lapse of time or pardon. The writ will not 1 30 170 1 ار 42 9110 +9

be granted where defendant is not in custody, and will not lie to review a commitment for contempt of court, the proper remedy is appeal." 

1 Com. v. Shortall, 206 Pa. 165. 

2 Com. ex rel. v. Keeper, 295 Pa. 252; Com. v. Ketner,. 

92 Pa. 372. 

3 Halderman's Case, 53 Pa. Super. 554; Com. ex rel. v. 

Skelly, 8 D. & C. 585. 

4 Com. ex rel. v. Morgan, 278 Pa. 395; Halderman's Petition, 276 Pa. 1; Com. ex rel. v. Reifsteck, 271 Pa. 441. 

5 Com. v. Kardosh, 9 D. & C. 812. 

In re Contempt of Myers, 83 Pa. Super. 383; Com. ex rel. v. Weigley, 83 Pa. Super. 189. 

5. Where Person Confined As Lunatic

On a written statement, properly sworn to or affirmed, being addressed by some respectable person to any law judge, that a certain person then confined in a hospital for the insane is not insane, and is thus unjustly deprived of his liberty, the judge shall issue a writ of habeas corpus, commanding that the said alleged lunatic be brought before him for a public hearing, where the question of his or her alleged lunacy may be determined, and where the onus of proving the said alleged lunatic to be insane shall rest upon such persons as are restraining him or her of his or her liberty. 

Act of Apr. 20, 1869 P. L. 78, Sec. 3, 50 P. S. 592. 

6. Habeas Corpus and Certiorari 

Habeas corpus and certiorari may be severally used as ancillary to each other. If a habeas corpus at common law issues, and the return to it shows that the prisoner is held by virtue of proceedings in a court, or before a magistrateover which the court issuing the habeas corpus has a supervisory authority, the said court may issue a certiorari to bring up the record; and may thereupon hear and decide the case, or review and correct the proceedings, in order to give efficiency to the writ of habeas corpus. If a certiorari be issued to bring up a case into a higher court for review or hearing, the court may issue a habeas corpus to bring up the

defendant; and it may in a proper case, admit him to bail to appear at the hearing and abide the event, and the form of the recognizance must be adopted to the exigencies of the case. 

Com. ex rel. v. Veley, 63 Pa. Super. 489. 

7. Service, Return, Proceedings 

And whenever the said writ shall, by any person, be served upon the officer, sheriff, jailer, keeper or other person whatsoever, to whom the same shall be directed, by being brought to him, or by being left with any of his under officers or deputies, at the jail or place where the prisoner is detained, he or some of his under officers or deputies shall, within three days after the service thereof, as aforesaid-make return of such writ, and bring or cause to be brought, the body of the prisoner unto or before the judge or justice before whom the said writ is made returnable; and, in case of his absence, before any other of the judges or justices aforesaid; and shall then likewise specifically and fully certify the true cause or causes of the commitment and detainer of the said prisoner, and when he was committed. And thereupon the judge or justice, before whom the prisoner shall be so brought, shall, within two days, discharge the prisoner from imprisonment, taking his or her recog nizance, with one or more surety or sureties, in any sum, according to his discretion, having regard to the circumstances of the prisoner, and the nature of the offense, for his or her appearance at the next court of oyer and terminer, general jail delivery, or general quarter sessions of or for the county, city or place where the offense was committed, or in such other court where it may be properly cognizable, as the case shall require; and then shall certify the said writ, with the return thereof, and the said recognizance, into the court where such appearance is to be made, unless it shall appear to the said judge or justice that the party so committed is detained upon legal process, order or warrant for such matter or offenses for which, by the law, the said prisoner is not bailable; and that the said judge or justice may, according to the intent and meaning of this act, be enabled, by investi- IN PL #4 125 NB 113 解​: HR 19 Leal

PAR 14

gating the truth of the circumstances of the case, to determine whether, according to law, the said prisoner ought to be bailed, remanded, or discharged; the return may, before or after it is filed, by leave of the said judge or justice, be amended, and also suggestions made against it, that thereby material facts may be ascertained. 

Act of Feb. 18, 1785, 2 Sm. L. 275, Sec. 1, 12 P. S. 1873. 

8. Notice of Application by Convicts 

Hereafter, when application shall be made to any court of this commonwealth, or a judge thereof, by an inmate of either of the penitentiaries of the state, for a writ of habeas corpus for the discharge of such inmate, the court or judge directing such writ to issue, and before whom the application shall be heard, shall, before the hearing of such application and the discharge of any such inmate, have submitted to it or him proof of notice of the intended application to the authority or authorities that may have committed such inmate to the said penitentiary, and it shall not be lawful for such court or judge to order the discharge of such inmate without proof of notice as aforesaid to the said authority or authorities.

Act of June 6, 1893, P. L. 328, Sec. 1, 12 P. S. 1878. 

9. Writ Not Granted within Fifteen Days of Term

And that no person may avoid his or her trial by procuring a removal, so that he or she cannot be brought back in time, be it enacted, That no person shall be removed, upon any habeas corpus granted in pursuance of this act, within fifteen days next preceding the term, sessions of oyer and terminer, general jail delivery or other court, where the offense with which he or she stands charged is properly cognizable; but upon such habeas corpus shall be brought before the judges or justices thereof, who are thereupon to do what to justice shall appertain.¹

The power to issue writs of habeas corpus will not be exercised in such manner as to hinder or delay trials.2 

1 Act of Feb. 18, 1785, 2 Sm. L. 275, Sec. 6, 12 P. S. 1881. 

2 Com. ex rel. v. Keeper, 26 Pa. Super. 191. 

10. Penalty for Refusing Writ

If any judge or justice aforesaid, being appealed or complained to, as aforesaid, upon view of the copy or copies of the warrant or warrants of the commitment or detainer, or upon oath or affirmation made, that such copy or copies where denied as aforesaid, shall refuse or neglect to award any writ of habeas corpus by this act required to be granted, he shall forfeit to the prisoner or party grieved, the sum of three hundred pounds, to be recovered by the said prisoner or party grieved, his or her executors or administrators, against such offender, his executors or administrators, by action of debt, suit, bill, plaint or information, in any court of record, wherein no essoin, protection, privilege, injunction, wager of law or stay of prosecution shall be allowed, or any more than one imparlance. 

Act of Feb. 18, 1785, 2 Sm. L. 275, Sec. 8, 12 P. S. 1883. 

11. Neglect to Execute Writ, Penalty 

If any officer, sheriff, jailer, keeper or other person, to whom any such writ shall be directed as aforesaid, or any of his under officers or deputies, shall refuse or neglect to make the returns aforesaid, or to bring the body of the prisoner, according to the command of the said writ, within the respective times aforesaid, all and every such officer, sheriff, jailer, keeper or other person, under officer or deputy, shall be guilty of a contempt of the court under the seal of which the said writ shall have issued, and shall also, for the first offense, forfeit to the prisoner or party grieved one hundred pounds, and for the second offense two hundred pounds, and shall be and is hereby made incapable to hold or execute his said office; the said forfeiture to be recovered by the prisoner or party grieved, in manner aforesaid. 

Act of Feb. 18, 1785, 2 Sm. L. 275, Sec. 9, 12 P. S. 1884.

12. Refusal to Deliver Copy of

Warrant of Commitment If any officer, sheriff, jailer, keeper or other person, to whom such writ shall be directed as aforesaid, or any of his under officers or deputies, upon demand by the prisoner, or some person in his or her behalf, shall refuse to deliver, or within six hours after demand shall not deliver to the prisoner or person so demanding a true copy or copies of the warrant or warrants of commitment and detainer of such prisoner, which are hereby required to be delivered, all and every such officer, sheriff, jailer, keeper or other person, under officers on deputy, so offending, shall for the first offense, forfeit to the prisoner or party grieved one hundred pounds, and for the second offense two hundred pounds, and shall also be and is hereby made incapable to hold or execute his said office; the said forfeiture to be recovered by the prisoner or party grieved, in manner aforesaid. 

Act of Feb. 18, 1785, 2 Sm. L. 275, Sec. 10, 12 P. S. 1885. 

13. Witnesses, Compulsory Attendance 

The judge or court, before whom any writ of habeas corpus shall be returnable, shall have power to issue subpoenas, and all other process necessary to compel the attendance of witnesses. 

Act of April 17, 1866, P. L. 112, Sec. 1, 12 P. S. 1875. 

14. No Evidence Heard in Behalf of Defendant 

Upon a hearing under habeas corpus for the purpose of determining whether bail ought to be taken upon a commitment for murder in the first degree, or for the purpose of determining in any case how much bail ought to be required, or for the purpose of determining in any case whether a person committed for trial ought to be further held, evidence for the defendant shall not be heard. 

Act of May 23, 1887, P. L. 158, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 681.

15. Person Released Not To Be Recommitted 

And for preventing unjust vexation by reiterated commitments for the same offense, be it enacted . . . That no person who shall be delivered or set at large upon a habeas corpus shall, at any time thereafter, be again committed or imprisoned for the same offense, by any person or persons whatsoever, other than by the legal order and process of such court wherein he or she shall be bound by recognizance to appear, or other court having jurisdiction of the cause, and if any other person or persons shall knowingly, contrary to this act, recommit or imprison or knowingly procure or cause to be recommitted or imprisoned, for the same offense or supposed offense, any person delivered or set at large as aforesaid, or be knowingly aiding or assisting therein, then he or they shall forfeit to the prisoner or party grieved, any pretense of variation in the warrant or warrants of commitment notwithstanding, the sum of five hundred pounds, to be recovered by the prisoner or party grieved, in manner aforesaid. 

Act of Feb. 18, 1785, 2 Sm. L. 275, Sec. 11, 12 P. S. 1886. 

16. Payment of Fees and Costs

It shall be lawful for the court of common pleas, or any judge thereof, before whom any writ of habeas corpus shall be heard, upon the petition of any person or persons charged with any criminal or supposed criminal matter, to make an order for the payment by the proper county, or by the prosecutor or prosecutors in the case, as the said court or the judge hearing the case may determine, of the costs and fees of the witnesses subpoenaed by the district attorney on the part of the commonwealth; Provided, that the fees of witnesses shall be the same as are allowed by law in civil cases. 

Act of April 14, 1868, P. L. 98, Sec. 1, 12 P. S. 1877. 

17. Common-Law Writ

The statutory writ of habeas corpus is of limited application. The cases to which it extends are of two classes only; public restraints. on bailable criminal charges, and private 300 #151 1st g 1.013 17 1700117 74 남​: 단단 ​1414 2 21

restraints on any pretense whatsoever. But the efficacy of the common-law writ of habeas corpus reaches much further, and takes many forms, according to the character of the case to which it is applied. It may issue in all sorts of cases where it is shown to the court that there is probable cause for believing that a person is restrained of his liberty unlawfully or against the due course of law. It is a form of remedy which can be administered only by a court in banc, though a single judge may allow the writ. 

Com. v. Gibbons, 9 Pa. Super. 527; Gosline v. Place, 32 Pa. 

520; Schofield v. Root. 12 Phila. 333; Williamson v. Lewis, 39 Pa. 9; Com. ex rel. v. Skelly, 8 D. & C. 585, form of writ immaterial. 

18. When Detained by Private Individual 

Whenever a person is deprived of the privilege of going when and where he pleases he is restrained of his liberty, and has a right to inquire if that restraint be illegal and wrongful, whether it be exercised by a jailer, constable or private individual. 

Com. v. Reynolds, 26 Lack. J. 133; Com. v. Ridgway, 2 

Ashmead 247. 

19. Habeas Corpus ad Testificandum 

The purpose of the writ is to bring a witness into court who is in legal custody, and there must be a pending suit or a lawful proceeding. The writ is in the nature of a subpoena, the difference between the two being that a subpoena is served upon the person whose presence is desired, while the writ is served upon the one who has the witness in custody. The right of custody is not in question, and the witness remains in charge of the officer who is designated as his custodian. 

Com. ex rel. Fraley v. Rotan, 82 Pa. Super. 172. 

20. Illegal Removal of Prisoners 

Any person being committed to any prison, or in custody of any officer, sheriff, jailer, keeper or other person, or his under officer or deputy, for any criminal or supposed crim-

inal matter, shall not be removed from the said prison or custody, into any other prison or custody, unless it be by habeas corpus or some other legal writ, or where the prisoner is delivered to the constable or other inferior officer, to be carried to some common jail, or where any person is sent by any judge or justice, having proper authority, to some common workhouse or house of correction, or where the prisoner is removed from one place to another within the same county, in order to his or her trial or discharge in due course of law, or in case of sudden fire or infection or other necessity; and if any person or persons shall, after such commitment as aforesaid, make out, sign, countersign and issue any warrant or warrants for such removal, except as before excepted, then he or they shall forfeit to the prisoner or party grieved two hundred pounds, to be recovered by the prisoner or party grieved, in manner aforesaid. 

Act of Feb. 18, 1785, 2 Sm. L. 275, Sec. 12, 12 P. S. 1887. TE 20 اتور RAP 2.

Chapter XXXIII PARDONS 1. THE GOVERNOR 2. BOARD OF PARDONS, MEMBERS 3. POWERS Rules of the Board of Pardons 1. HEARINGS 2. REPRESENTATIVES 3. APPLICATIONS, FORMS AND FEES 4. CAPITOL CASES 5. ORIGINAL CASES 6. REHEARING CASES 7. SERVICE AND ADVERTISEMENT 8. CALENDAR 9. WITHDRAWALS AND CONTINUANCES 10. DEPOSITIONS AND WITNESSES 11. PROTESTS 12. COMMUNICATIONS 13. NOTICE OF BOARD ACTION 14. RECOMMENDATIONS FOR PAROLE 1. The Governor 

He shall have power to remit fines and forfeitures, to grant reprieves, commutation of sentence and pardons, except in cases of impeachment; but no pardon shall be granted, nor sentence commuted, except upon the recommendation in writing of the Lieutenant Governor, Secretary of the 

Commonwealth, Attorney General and Secretary of Internal Affairs, or any three of them, after full hearing, upon due public notice and in open session, and such recommendation, with the reasons therefore at length, shall be recorded and filed in the office of the Secretary of the Commonwealth.

Art. 4, Sec. 9, Const. Pa. 

320

2. Board of Pardons, Members 

The board of pardons shall consist of the Lieutenant 

Governor, Secretary of the Commonwealth, Attorney General, and Secretary of Internal Affairs. Three members of the board shall constitute a quorum. 

The administrative Code of 1929, Act of April 9, 1929,

P. L. 177, Art. 4, Sec. 403, 71 P. S. 113. 

3. Powers

The board of pardons shall have power to hear applications for the remission of fines and forfeitures, and the granting of reprieves, commutations of sentence, and pardons, except in cases of impeachment, and to make recommendations in writing to the Governor thereon, in the manner provided in and under and subject to Article IV, Sec. 9, of the Const. of Pennsylvania.

The administrative Code, 1929. Act of April 9, 1929, P. L. 

177, Art. 9, Sec. 909, 71 P. S. 299. 

RULES OF THE BOARD OF PARDONS 

Adopted February 20, 1935

I. HEARINGS 

(a) The Board will meet in regular session on the third Wednesday of each month except during July and

August. The regular session will be an open session held in the Supreme Court Room, Harrisburg, at nine o'clock a. m. Eastern Standard Time. Special sessions may be held at such times as the Board shall determine. Applications will not be heard or considered by the individual members of the Board. (b) In capital cases, one-half hour will be allowed the applicant and one-half hour will be allowed to any person or persons opposing the application.

In all other cases, fifteen minutes will be allowed the applicant and fifteen minutes will be allowed to any person or persons opposing the application.

In all cases, a reasonable time will be allowed applicant for reply..

II. REPRESENTATIVES 

(a) Anyone may act as the applicant's representative. 

Only attorneys entitled to practice before the Board may charge or accept fees. (b) Attorneys admitted to practice before the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania and in good standing may practice before the Board, subject to the following rules: 

1. The Board reserves the right in every case to inquire into the fees paid to attorneys and if it deems any fee to be excessive, to require a reduction thereof. 

2. Any attorney who refuses to reduce a fee in accordance with the requirement of the Board may not thereafter practice before the Board.

III. APPLICATIONS, FORMS AND FEES 

(a) Applications that have never been before the

Board are original cases. Those that have been previously considered are rehearing cases. A capital case is one in which applicant is under sentence of death. (b) All applications must be on forms supplied by the

Board at a cost of one dollar a set payable in advance and must be typewritten. In ordering forms, it must be stated whether the application is an original, rehearing or capital case. (c) In all, except capital cases, a filing fee of ten dollars is charged and must accompany the application. Filing fees are never returned. (d) Only certified checks or money orders payable to the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania will be accepted. 

IV. CAPITAL CASES 

(a) The original and three copies of the application must be filed with the secretary of the Board at least two full weeks before the day of the regular session at which hearing is desired.

(b) Notice of intention to apply for clemency, accompanied with an exact copy of the application must be served on (1) the judge who tried the case if living (or if the trial judge is dead, on the president judge of the court), (2) on the district attorney (and if he is not the district attorney who prosecuted the case, also on such former district attorney), (3) on the assistant district attorney, if any, who actually tried the case, (4) on the private prosecuting attorney, if any, and (5) on the warden or keeper of the penitentiary or prison in which the applicant is confined. 

Service of such notices and copies of the application must be made before the application is filed. 

Affidavit of proof of service, or acceptances of service of the notices required, must be filed at least five days before the day of regular meeting. (c) Notice of the application must be advertised once a week for two consecutive weeks prior to the regular session of the Board at which the application is to be heard. 

Such advertisement shall appear in a newspaper of general circulation, printed in the English language, in the county in which the applicant was convicted and shall state the true name of the applicant, the name under which he was tried and the crime of which he was convicted, that the applicant was sentenced to death, that he has applied for clemency, and the date on which the application will be heard. 

Proof of publication of the advertisement required by this rule, must be filed before the day of hearing. Proof shall consist of an affidavit by the owner of the newspaper, or his authorized agent, with a copy of the advertisement attached. (d) If an appeal is taken, five copies of all paper books must be filed with the Board at least five days before the day of hearing. (e) A schedule or list of all papers filed shall be delivered to the secretary of the Board not later than the day of hearing.

11 11 

V. ORIGINAL CASES 

(a) Applications may be filed with the secretary at any time. (b) The Board will not hear any application in the case of a person sentenced to a county prison until the applicant has made application for parole to the trial court and such application has been refused and a certification of such refusal from the clerk of the court must accompany the application. (c) Applications filed on or before the fifteenth day of the month shall be heard at the regular session of the Board held the following month. For cause shown, the 

Board, with the approval of two members, may specially list applications for hearing if filed at least two weeks before the regular session at which hearing is desired. The original and eight copies of the application must be filed with the secretary of the Board; the tenth copy to be retained by the applicant or his representative. 

VI. REHEARING CASES 

(a) Applications for rehearing will not be heard until six months have expired after the refusal of the last application. The Board however, on its own motion may order a rehearing at an earlier time. (b) Applications for rehearing filed on or before the fifteenth day of the month shall be heard at the regular session of the Board held the following month. (c) New and substantial reasons must be set forth on form JBP-2 which will be included in sets of rehearing forms. (d) To each rehearing form marked JBP-2 must be attached, but not permanently, a copy of the original application on new original form JBP-1 which will also be included in sets of forms for rehearing cases.

VII. SERVICE AND ADVERTISEMENT 

In all except capital cases, the secretary of the Board shall, upon receiving applications, (a) Transmit one copy of the application to the trial judge, if living, and if not living, to the president judge of the court or of one of the courts of the county in which the applicant was tried; one copy to the district attorney or assistant district attorney who tried the case, and, if the trial district attorney is no longer in office, a copy to the present district attorney; one copy to the president of the board of trustees of the penitentiary in which the applicant is or was confined, or if he is not confined in a penitentiary, then one copy to the president of the board of inspectors of the prison in which the applicant is or was confined; and one copy to the State Supervisor of Paroles; and shall request each of the persons to whom a copy is transmitted to advise the Board of Pardons at their earliest convenience, if in the judgment of such judge, district attorney or assistant district attorney, State Supervisor of Paroles, or in the judgment of the board of trustees or board of inspectors of the institution as the case may be, the application for clemency is meritorious, and if, from such information as is available, he or it believes that the applicant can safely be released from prison, together with any facts bearing on the case which would be of assistance to the Board of Pardons in its deliberation. (b) Give to the Superintendent of State Police a list of names of all applicants for clemency with a request for such information as is available from the records of the State Police with such comment as he or his representative believes would be of assistance to the Board in acting on the applications. (c) Post in his office at Harrisburg two weeks before the regular session a list of all applications to be heard; and on the Friday following the posting of the list, publish in a newspaper of general circulation, in each county where any of the applicants were convicted, a notice stating: (1) the names and aliases of applicants sentenced in the par- 3 10 11 #17 1 3417

ticular county; (2) the crimes of which applicants were convicted; (3) places of confinement and (4) time and place of hearing.

VIII. CALENDAR 

(a) The secretary of the Board shall prepare and cause to be printed, a calendar of the cases to be heard by the Board at each of its regular sessions. Copies of the calendar will be furnished to the applicants or their representatives, the district attorneys for the counties in which the applicants were convicted, to wardens and presidents of the boards of trustees of the several penitentiaries, and the Superintendent of State Police. 

(b) Cases appearing on the calendar will be called in their regular order. If there be no answer upon the first call of the case it will be passed temporarily, and, if not answered when called a second time, the case will be marked "refused," unless a satisfactory reason for failure to answer be presented.

IX. WITHDRAWALS AND CONTINUANCES 

Applications listed on the calendar may be withdrawn or continued at any time and when withdrawn or continued, may be placed on any subsequent calendar. A fee of five dollars shall be charged for the listing of those applications withdrawn or continued within two weeks prior to the regular session. Requests for withdrawals or continuances shall be addressed to the secretary of the Board and shall have attached an acceptance of service of a notice of the withdrawal or continuance or subsequent listing of the application, to the district attorney of the county in which applicant was convicted.

X. DEPOSITIONS AND WITNESSES 

(a) If it should become necessary to establish by deposition, the truth of any disputed matter of fact relative to any issue before the Board, such depositions may be taken under the rules of the court of jurisdiction wherein the

deponents may be, by notice of the time and place of the taking of said depositions, which must be served upon the district attorney and upon the applicant or his attorney. (b) The Board may require at its regular sessions the appearance of witnesses, district attorneys, private counsel for the Commonwealth, police officials or any other persons for interrogation.

XI. PROTESTS 

(a) Persons interested adversely in applications for clemency may appear at the regular session of the Board without notice to the applicants. Formal protests in writing may be filed with the Board at any time. (b) The secretary of the Board shall not be required to notify the applicants of the filing or the substance of any protests.

XII. COMMUNICATIONS 

(a) All correspondence relating to applications, whether addressed to the Board or to individual members thereof, shall be filed with the Board. (b) The individual members of the Board shall not discuss pending cases except with the trial judge, trial district attorney, president judge, district attorney, assistant district attorney, private prosecuting attorney and members of the boards of trustees or boards of inspectors or wardens or keepers of institutions in which the applicants are or were confined.

XIII. NOTICE OF BOARD'S ACTION 

(a) After hearing applications in open session the Board will consider the same in executive session. Announcement of the action of the Board will be made by the secretary as soon as practicable after the adjournment of the executive session. Written notice of the Board's action will be forwarded by mail to the respective applicants or their counsel and to the district attorney of the county in

which any applicant was convicted if the district attorney appeared, or sent a representative to the hearing. (b) When favorable action on an application for clemency is recommended, the finding of the Board, with reasons, will be transmitted to the Governor, and if approved by him and an order for pardon or commutation follows, the charter of pardon or of commutation will in due course be forwarded to the warden of the proper prison. (c) No reason will be given by the Board for its refusal to recommend clemency.

XIV. RECOMMENDATIONS FOR PAROLE 

(a) Recommendations of prison inspectors, wardens and sheriffs for commutation under the Act of 1901 and its supplements, and recommendations for parole with proof of publication and notices, must be filed at least two weeks before the day of regular meeting. Such recommendations will be included on the printed calendar. (b) The Board will not approve any recommendation to the Governor for parole of any inmate of a penitentiary unless the inmate recommended shall (1) have reasonable assurance of employment or sufficient funds for his maintenance for sixty days, or (2) be deported, or (3) require institutional treatment and arrangements for such treatment have been made, or (4) be of advanced years or physically unable to work and have relatives or other persons guaranteeing support or care. However, the Board may approve recommendations for parole when, in the opinion of the board of trustees making the recommendations the circumstances of the particular case may warrant a recommendation without the fulfilment of the aforementioned requirements. (c) The board of trustees of each penitentiary shall append to each parole list submitted to the Board, a statement showing who has agreed to employ the respective applicant for parole; the amount of cash inmate will have on

discharge; that the inmate is to be deported, or that other arrangements made for the care of the applicant for parole. (d) No recommendations for parole shall be approved unless the reasons for recommendation are given. (e) No recommendations for parole shall be approved where the person recommended has been convicted of a crime and sentenced to a penitentiary while on parole from the Pennsylvania Industrial School until such person has served six months additional time over and above his minimum sentence except where such person has been specially recommended for earlier parole by the Board of 

Trustees of the Pennsylvania Industrial School.

Chapter XXXIV EXTRADITION 1. DEFINITION 2. PRISONERS TO BE DELIVERED UPON REQUISITION 3. FORM OF DEMAND 4. GOVERNOR MAY INVESTIGATE CASE 5. WHAT PAPERS MUST SHOW 6. ARREST WITHOUT A WARRANT 15. COMMITMENT TO AWAIT REQUISITION, BAIL 

16. BAIL: EXCEPTION CAPITAL AND LIFE IM- PRISONMENT CASE: CONDITIONS 17. IF NO ARREST MADE ON GOVERNOR'S WAR- RANT BEFORE THE TIME SPECIFIED 18. FORFEITURE OF BAIL 19. IF A PROSECUTION HAS ALREADY BEEN IN- STITUTED IN THIS STATE 20. GUILT OR INNOCENCE OF ACCUSED, WHEN INQUIRED INTO 21. GOVERNOR MAY RECALL WARRANT OR IS- SUE ALIAS 22. FUGITIVES FROM THIS STATE 23. 330

27. CONSTITUTIONALITY 28. CITATION OF ACT 

1. Definition

Where appearing in this act, the term "Governor” includes any person performing the functions of Governor by authority of the law of this State. The term "executive authority" includes the Governor, and any person performing the functions of Governor, in a state other than this state. 

And the term "state", referring to a state other than this

State, refers to any other State or territory, organized or unorganized, of the United States of America. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 101. 

2. Prisoners to be Delivered upon Requisition

Subject to the qualifications of this act, and the provisions of the Constitution of the United States controlling, and acts of Congress in pursuance thereof, it is the duty of the Governor of this State to have arrested and delivered up to the executive authority of any other state of the United States any person charged in that state with treason, felony, or other crime, who has fled from justice and is found in this State. 

Provided, If the executive authority of any other state or district requests the extradition of any person charged in that state with murder, and that person is imprisoned in a penal institution or jail of this State for a term less than imprisonment for life, the Governor of this state may deliver him or her up to the executive authority of the demanding state or district for the purpose of trial in said state or district; Provided, however, that prior to the removal of the person from this State, the executive authority of the demanding state or district shall have agreed that the person so delivered up is to be returned immediately to this State, at the cost of the demanding state or district, to serve the balance of his or her term of imprisonment in the event of his or her acquittal in the demanding state, or in the event of his or her conviction in such state of manslaugh-

ter or any degree of murder the punishment for which is less than death or imprisonment for life. 

Act of May 3, 1933, P. L. 249, Sec. 1, 19 P. S. 121, amending 

Sec. 2, Act of 1929. Com. ex rel. Abbot 71 P. L. J. 191 (1923); 

Com. ex rel. v. Ash, 114 Pa. Super. 119, fugitive a prisoner. 

3. Form of Demand

No demand for the extradition of a person charged with crime in another state shall be recognized by the Governor, unless in writing, and accompanied by a copy of an indictment found, or by an information, supported by affidavit, in the state having jurisdiction of the crime, or by a copy of an affidavit made before a magistrate there, together with a copy of any warrant which was issued thereon. 

The indictment, information, or affidavit made before the magistrate, must substantial charge the person demanded with having committed a crime under the law of that state, and the copy must be authenticated by the executive authority making the demand, which shall be prima facie evidence of its truth. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 3, 19 P. S. 122. Com. v. Steele, 78 Pa. Super. 352 (1923) desertion; Com. v. Supt. 

Prison, 220 Pa. 401. 

4. Governor May Investigate Case 

When a demand shall be made upon the Governor of this State, by the executive authority of another state, for the surrender of a person so charged with crime, the Governor may call upon the attorney general, or any prosecuting officer in this State, to investigate, or assist in investigating, the demand and to report to him the situation and circumstances of the person so demanded and whether he ought to be surrendered. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 4, 19 P. S. 123; Com. v. Supt. Prison, 220 Pa. 401.

5. What Papers Must Show 

A warrant of extradition must not be issued, unless the documents presented by the executive authority making the demand show that the accused was present in the demanding state at the time of the commission of the alleged crime, and that he thereafter fled from justice in that state and is now in this State, and that he is lawfully charged, by indictment found, or by an information filed by a prosecuting officer and supported by affidavit to the facts, or by affidavit made before a magistrate in that state, with having committed a crime under the laws of that state, or that he has been convicted of crime in that state, or has escaped from confinement, or broken his parole. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 5, 19 P. S. 124; Com. v. Tiblerino, 81 Pa. Super. 9 (1923); Com. v. Supt. Prison, 220 

Pa. 401; Com. v. Cooke, 55 Pa. Super. 435. 

6. Persons Guilty of Crime in Another 

State, Though Being in This State The Governor of this State may also surrender, on demand of the executive authority of any other state, any person in this State charged, on indictment found in such other state, with committing an act in this State, intentionally resulting in a crime in such other state, and the provisions of this act, not otherwise inconsistent, shall apply to such cases, notwithstanding that the accused was not in that state at the time of the commission of the crime and has not filed therefrom. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 6, 19 P. S. 125. 

7. Issue of Governor's Warrant of Arrest 

If the Governor shall decide that the demand should be complied with, he shall sign a warrant of arrest which shall be sealed with the State seal and be directed to a sheriff, marshal, coroner, or other person whom he may think fit to entrust with the execution thereof, and the warrant must 22

#1 Mod 71 substantially recite the facts necessary to the validity of its issue. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 7, 19 P. S. 126; Com. v. Tiblerino, 81 Pa. Super. 9 (1923). 

8. Manner and Place of Execution 

Such warrant shall authorize the officer, or other person to whom directed, to arrest the accused at any place where he may be found within the State, and to command the aid of all sheriffs and other peace officers in the execution of the warrant, and to deliver the accused, subject to the provisions of this act, to the duly authorized agent of the demand-ing state. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 8, 19 P. S. 127. 

9. Authority of Arresting Officers 

Every such officer, or other person empowered to make the arrest shall have the same authority, in arresting the accused, to command assistance therein as sheriffs and other officers have by law in the execution of any criminal process directed to them with the like penalties against those who refuse their assistance. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 9, 19 P. S. 128. 

10. Accused May Apply for Writ of Habeas Corpus

No person arrested upon such warrant shall be delivered over to the agent, whom the executive authority demanding him shall have appointed to receive him, unless he has been informed of the demand made for his surrender, and of the crime with which he is charged, and that he has the right to demand legal counsel, and, if the prisoner, his friends or counsel, shall state that he or they desire to test the legality of the arrest, the prisoner shall be taken forthwith before a judge of a court of record in this State, who shall fix a reasonable time to be allowed him within which to apply for a writ of habeas corpus. And when such writ is applied for, notice thereof, and of the time and place of hearing thereon,

shall be given to the public prosecuting officer of the county in which the arrest is made and in which the accused is in custody and to the said agent of the demanding state. (See Paragraph 20) 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 10, 19 P. S. 129. 

11. Penalty for Not Informing Prisoner of His Rights 

Any officer who shall deliver, to the agent for extradition of the demanding state, a person in his custody under the Governor's warrant in disobedience to the last section, shall be guilty of a misdemeanor and, on conviction, shall be fined not more than one thousand dollars or be imprisoned for not more than six months or both. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 11, 19 P. S. 130. 

12. Confinement in Jail, When Necessary 

The officer or person executing the Governor's warrant of arrest, or the agent of the demanding state to whom the prisoner may have been delivered, may, when necessary, confine the prisoner in the jail of any county or city or borough, through which he may pass, and the keeper of such jail must receive and safely keep the prisoner until the person having charge of him is ready to proceed on his route, such person being chargeable with the expense of keeping. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 12, 19 P. S. 131. 

13. Arrest Prior to Requisition 

Whenever any person within this State shall be charged, on the oath of any credible person before any judge or other magistrate of this State, with the commission of any crime in any other state and with having fled from justice, or whenever complaint shall have been made before any judge or other magistrate in this State setting forth on the affidavit of any credible person in another state, that a crime has been committed in such other state, and that the accused has been charged in such state with the commission of the crime and has fled from justice therefrom, and is believed to have been found in this State, the judge or magistrate shall issue a

warrant, directed to the sheriff or other officer of the county in which the oath or complaint is filed, directing him to apprehend the person charged, wherever he may be found in this State, and bring him before the same or any other judge, court, or magistrate, who may be convenient of access to the place where the arrest may be made, to answer the charge or complaint and affidavit, and a certified copy of the sworn charge or complaint and affidavit, upon which the warrant is issued, shall be attached to the warrant. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 13, 19 P. S. 132. 

14. Arrest Without a Warrant

The arrest of a person may be lawfully made also by an officer or private citizen without a warrant, upon reasonable information that the accused stands charged with a crime punishable by death or life imprisonment in the courts of another state, but, when so arrested, the accused must be taken before a judge or magistrate with all practicable speed, and complaint must be made against him, under oath, setting forth the grounds for the arrest, as in the last section, and thereafter his answer shall be heard as if he had been arrested upon a warrant. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 14, 19 P. S. 133. 

15. Commitment to Await Requisition, Bail 

If, from the examination before the judge or magistrate, it appears that the person held is the person charged with having committed the crime alleged, and that he probably committed the crime, and, except in cases arising under section six, that he has fled from justice, the judge or magistrate must commit him to jail, by a warrant reciting the accusation, for such a time specified in the warrant as will enable the arrest of the accused to be made under a warrant of the Governor on a requisition of the executive authority of the state having jurisdiction of the offense, unless the accused give bail, as provided in the next section, or until he shall be legally discharged. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 15, 19 P. S. 134.

16. Bail: Except in Capital and Life 

Imprisonment Cases; Conditions

Unless the offense, with which the prisoner is charged, is shown to be an offense punishable by death or life imprisonment under the laws of the state in which it was committed, the judge or magistrate must admit the person arrested to bail, by bond or undertaking, with sufficient sureties, and in such sum as he deems proper, for his appearance before him at the time at which he receives the warrant of the Governor of this State, not exceeding thirty days thereafter, and for his surrender to be arrested upon the warrant of the Governor of this State. 

Com. v. Bentz, 22 D. & C, 132; Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 

327, Sec. 16, 19 P. S. 135. 

17. If No Arrest Made on Governor's Warrant 

Before the Time Specified

If the accused is not arrested under warrant of the Governor, by the expiration of the time specified in the warrant, bond, or undertaking, the judge or magistrate may discharge him, or many recommit him to a further day, or may again take bail for his appearance and surrender as provided in section sixteen, and at the expiration of the second period of commitment, or, if he has been bailed and appeared according to the terms of his bond or undertaking, the judge or magistrate may either discharge him, or may require him to enter into a new bond or undertaking to appear and surrender himself at another day. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 17, 19 P. S. 136. 

18. Forfeiture of Bail

If the prisoner is admitted to bail and fails to appear and surrender himself according to the conditions of his bond, the court, by proper order, shall declare the bond forfeited, and recovery may be had thereon, in the name of the state, as in the case of other bonds or undertakings given by the accused in criminal proceedings within this State. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 18, 19 P. S. 137.

19. If a Prosecution Has Already Been

Instituted in This State 

If a criminal prosecution has been instituted against such person under the laws of this state, and is still pending, the Governor, at his discretion, either may surrender him on the demand of the executive authority of another state, or may hold him until he has been tried and discharged or convicted and punished in this State. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 19, 19 P. S. 138. 

20. Guilt or Innocence of Accused, When Inquired Into

The guilt or innocence of the accused, as to the crime of which he is charged, may not be inquired into by the Governor, or in any proceeding after the demand for extradition, accompanied by a charge of crime in legal form as above provided, shall have been presented to the Governor, except as it may be involved in identifying the person held as the person charged with the crime. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 20, 19 P. S. 139; guilt of husband, nonsupport, Com. v. Edeburn, 76 P. L. J. 253; 

Contra, Com. v. McNeil, 76 P. L. J. 391; Com. v. Hill, 19 Berks 

Co., 548, burden on relator to show he is not fugitive; Com. v. 

Tiblerino, 81 Pa. Super. 9; Com. v. Steele, 78 Pa. Super. 352; 

Com. v. Weimer, 27 Dist. Repts. 249; Com. ex. rel. v. Abbot, 71 P. L. J. 191; Com. v. Supt. of Prison, 220 Pa. 401; Com. v. 

Matthews, 62 P. L. J. 345. 

21. Governor May Recall Warrant or Issue Alias 

The Governor may recall his warrant of arrest or may issue another warrant whenever he deems proper. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 21, 19 P. S. 140. 

22. Fugitives from This State

Whenever the Governor of this state shall demand a person charged with crime in this state, from the chief executive of any other state, or from the chief justice or an associate justice of the Supreme Court of the District of

Columbia, authorized to receive such demand under the laws of the United States, he shall issue a warrant, under the seal of this State, to some agent, commanding him to receive the person so charged, if delivered to him, and convey him to the proper officer of the county in this State in which the offense was committed. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 22, 19 P. S. 161. 

23. Manner of Applying for Requisition

When the return to this State of a person charged with a crime in this State is required, the prosecuting attorney of the county in which the offense is committed shall present to the Governor his written application for a requisition for the return of the person charged, in which application shall be stated the name of the person so charged, the crime charged against him, and the approximate time, place, and circumstances of its committal, the state in which he is believed to be, including the location of the accused therein at the time the application is made, and certifying that, in the opinion of the said prosecuting attorney, the ends of justice require the arrest and return of the accused to this State for trial, and that the proceeding is not instituted to enforce a private claim. The application shall be verified by affidavit, shall be executed in duplicate, and shall be accompanied by two certified copies of the indictment returned, or information and affidavit filed, or of the complaint made to the magistrate, stating the offense with which the accused is charged. The prosecuting officer may also attach such further affidavits and other documents, in duplicate, as he shall deem proper to be submitted with such application. One copy of the application, with the action of the Governor indicated by endorsement thereon, and one of the certified copies of the indictment or complaint or information and affidavit, shall be filed in the office of the Secretary of the Commonwealth to remain of record in that office, the other copy of all papers shall be forwarded with the Governor's requisition. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 23, 19 P. S. 162.

24. Exemption from Civil Process 

A person, brought into this state on extradition based on a criminal charge, shall not be subject to service of personal process in civil actions, arising out of the same facts as the criminal proceeding to answer which he is returned, until he has been convicted in the criminal proceeding, or, if acquitted, until he has had ample opportunity to return to the state from which he was extradited. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 24, 19 P. S. 163. 

25. No Right of Asylum 

After a person has been brought back to this State upon extradition proceedings, he may be tried in this State for other crimes, which he may be charged with having committed here, as well as that specified in the requisition for his extradition. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 25, 19 P. S. 164. 

26. Interpretation

This act shall be so interpreted and construed as to effectuate its general purpose to make uniform the law of those states which enact it. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 26, 19 P. S. 181. 

27. Constitutionality

If any part of this act is for any reason declared void, such invalidity shall not affect the validity of the remaining portions of this act. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 27, 182. 

28. Citation of Act

This act may be cited as the Uniform Criminal Extradition Act. 

Act of April 21, 1927, P. L. 327, Sec. 29, 19 P. S. 183.
